\begin{abstract}\label{sec:abstract}
 
In 2017, Google Research published \textit{Attention Is All You Need} \cite{vaswani2017attentionneed}.  Their paper introduced the Transformer architecture that reshaped modern AI.   Just five years later, OpenAI's ChatGPT \cite{chatgpt} triggered AI's mainstream adoption.

Yet while our screens flicker with progress, space travel and clean energy breakthroughs remain comparatively rare.

\textbf{Dude, Where's My Spaceship?}

Just as attention led to AI breakthroughs, this paper argues precision aim will let us solve grand challenges in the physical world.

Specifically, we propose spacecraft propulsion via momentum transfer from targeted orbital impacts.  Consider a ``PuffSat,'' a single-use microsatellite with a mass of roughly 25–100 kilograms, comparable to the unofficial mass of a 27U CubeSat \cite{cubesat_advanced_standard}.  When triggered, a PuffSat rapidly converts nearly all its mass into a low-density gas, generated explosively or by atomizing cold solids or liquids (see \autoref{sec:puffsat_design}). Ultra-lightweight microthrusters and electronics let it make small navigation adjustments.  Deployed in sequence, these PuffSats gasify and strike a shock-absorbing pusher plate on a target rocket, each impact delivering a pulse of momentum.

Recent and upcoming missions such as Proba-3 \cite{llorente2013proba3,zhukov2025proba3aspiics} and VISORS \cite{visors_formation_flying} demonstrate that millimeter-scale formation flying works. This suggests that many PuffSats can hold a tight formation with little relative spread. Most PuffSat navigation and control tasks can therefore be handled with robust model predictive control (MPC) \cite{visors_formation_flying} and Kalman filtering (see \autoref{sec:neural_navigation}).  These classical techniques are data-efficient and interpretable, with formal performance and safety guarantees. For the more demanding conditions near low periapsis, classical control methods can be augmented with neural networks that warm-start the controller and learn from residual errors.  They also add resilience against lost PuffSats and other anomalies (see \autoref{sec:neural_navigation}).  Formation flying would be paired with cooperative steering algorithms to guide the target in front of the PuffSat stream (see \autoref{sec:neural_navigation}).

This speculative white paper is a brainstorming exploration that could be followed by more rigorous analysis. If the ideas prove too advanced for near-term implementation, they can instead be fodder for hard science fiction. The paper summarizes and updates some of the writings from my own blog, ``Aim Is All You Need.''\cite{aim2024}

\end{abstract}

\section{Introduction}
Conceived in the 1950s and recently brought to a new audience by \textit{The Three-Body Problem} \cite{liu2014three}, Project Orion remains one of the most compelling rockets ever proposed. It is the only rocket built on mature technology that combines specific impulses comparable to those of electric propulsion with thrust levels on par with chemical rockets \cite{projorion}.   Orion would propel a spacecraft by directing hypervelocity plasma from nuclear explosions onto a pusher plate. Despite its theoretical promise, Orion faced insurmountable challenges related to political feasibility, radioactive fallout, and the impractical mass requirements stemming from the high minimum yield of nuclear explosions \cite{balcomb1970nuclear}. Even so, Orion validated the physics of hypervelocity pulsed propulsion.

Our approach replaces nuclear bombs with precisely aimed, hypervelocity gas puffs from specialized ``PuffSat'' microsatellites (see \autoref{sec:puffsat_design}) sequentially impacting a rocket's pusher plate.  These gas puffs can be scaled down to practical sizes.  Unlike nuclear explosions, small hypervelocity gas impacts could be efficiently contained within a pulsed propulsion chamber. With gravity assists and the Oberth effect \cite{oberth_effect}, hypervelocity PuffSat impacts could achieve energy densities and specific impulses well above those of chemical rockets.

Picture two rockets. The first rocket deploys a series of these fast-moving PuffSats. As shown in \autoref{fig:PuffSat_impact}, the deployed PuffSats follow precise paths to a shock-absorbing pusher plate (see \autoref{sec:lightweight_pusher_plates}) on a separate target rocket. Each one gasifies and strikes the plate in sequence. These collisions deliver concentrated pulses of energy and momentum to the target vehicle.

\begin{figure}[htpb]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{images/Starship_Impact_ellipse.png}
    \caption{PuffSats from a first rocket (not shown) crash their gas puffs into a second target rocket, transferring momentum for propulsion. \cite{earth_image}}
    \label{fig:PuffSat_impact}
\end{figure}

Our goal is to stimulate fresh thinking and new research directions. At minimum, the concept suggests new uses of formation flying.

The sections that follow open with the business case (\autoref{sec:business_case}), then explain how PuffSats work (\autoref{sec:how_puffsats_work}) and assess the near-term architecture's feasibility (\autoref{sec:engineering}) before surveying applications. \autoref{tab:applications} previews the grand challenges each section takes on.
