Tightening the surveyor gate alone lands at \SI{4.99}{\centi\meter}, under the mark with zero margin, so holding \SI{5}{\centi\meter} with any margin takes the camera link tightened as well \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}. That capability is margin, well inside the \SI{2}{\meter} the plate allows. The cost of the looser tolerance falls on the reaction control system, which must cancel the off-center torque from a PuffSat that lands up to \SI{2}{\meter} from center. 

It helps to treat the whole train of PuffSats as a single block. Part of the position error is common-mode, the same for every PuffSat in the block, like a shared bias in the modeled drag or a deployer offset they all inherit. That error moves the block as one, and re-pointing the aim point removes it, a shift of at most a few kilometers that the launch geometry affords cheaply. If every PuffSat is off by the same kilometer, aiming the whole salvo a kilometer the other way lands them all on target. A sacrificial lead PuffSat sharpens this further. Sent a short way ahead of the salvo, it flies past the target, and its miss measures the block's residual offset, so the target makes a small move to take the rest head-on.

What remains is the per-unit scatter, the way the PuffSats differ from one another, and only that has to fall within the catch radius, the largest miss the terminal guidance can still correct. This split between common-mode and per-unit error is the single most important lever in the architecture. It is also why a fluctuating atmosphere and an uncertain drag coefficient are less of a threat than they first appear, since both act on the whole formation nearly identically and so are mostly common-mode. Pulsed optical beacons drive the per-unit scatter down. Each PuffSat strobes a known pattern, and centroiding its image against the star field fixes its angle to a small fraction of a pixel \cite{liebe2002startracker}. A formation held that tightly lands its PuffSats in a small cluster near the plate's center, so the off-center spread at impact stays low. \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture} details the beacons and the trackers that read them.

Operating at a lower interception altitude (\SI{200}{\kilo\meter}) introduces stronger and more variable drag perturbations than at VISORS' \SI{600}{\kilo\meter} orbit. VISORS maintains a near-circular sun-synchronous orbit and is therefore exposed to drag continuously, whereas PuffSats spend only minutes below VISORS' altitude. While PuffSats traverse low altitudes at higher speeds, their brief dwell time at these heights limits the accumulation of drag-induced drift. Timing PuffSat interceptions at night, in colder regions, or during periods of low solar activity can reduce the atmospheric drag PuffSats experience during their descent.

GOCE, a larger satellite, achieved full drag compensation at altitudes as low as \SI{229}{\kilo\meter} using a streamlined aerodynamic profile coupled with \SI{20}{\milli\newton} ion thrusters \cite{goce_dragfree, goce_229km}. Though electric propulsion for CubeSats is actively being developed \cite{busek_iodine}, adapting GOCE's ion propulsion system for a PuffSat remains unrealistic due to mass and power constraints. However, PuffSats could be elongated like GOCE to reduce drag. Torque could be mitigated by modest spin stabilization or GOCE-like fins, combined with cold gas or small chemical thrusters for fine control.

GOCE is an encouraging precedent rather than a proof. It showed that controlled flight deep in the atmosphere is real, but it held its altitude with ion thrusters that a PuffSat cannot carry. The case that a PuffSat can hold adequate precision instead rests on a preliminary closed-loop control simulation, which models the interception directly and finds it within reach of cold-gas thrusters \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}. Based on the crude GOCE-derived modeling discussed in \autoref{sec:estimate_cold_gas}, PuffSats using cold gas thrusters would expend at most \SI{400}{\gram} of propellant during descent through denser atmospheric layers below \SI{600}{\kilo\meter}, even with pessimistic assumptions. Estimated peak thrust requirements of approximately \SI{400}{\milli\newton} appear sufficient to counteract drag forces below VISORS' operational altitude. The simulation puts the drag itself far lower, near \SI{17}{\milli\newton}, and the \SI{400}{\milli\newton} survives as the authority the terminal aiming burn needs rather than as a drag figure. This conservative ``back-of-the-napkin'' estimate provides a very rough upper bound, with propellant for low-altitude drag cancellation comprising less than 2\% of a \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} PuffSat's total mass.

\subsection{PuffSat Control Simulation}\label{sec:puffsat_control_simulation}
A closed-loop Monte Carlo simulation \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026} explores a representative PuffSat scenario. The simulated scenario tracks a bound reference trajectory from deployment near apogee (\SI{150000}{\kilo\meter}) down to interception near perigee (\SI{200}{\kilo\meter}). It does not include the distant injection or escaping arrival selected in \autoref{sec:near_term_disposal}. Under the modeled PuffSat dispersions, the simulation finds that capturing a \SI{5}{\meter} pusher plate is feasible. That result is conditional. It depends on the sensor and thruster grades fed in as inputs, assumes a conservative cannonball drag shape, and has not been cross-checked against an independent astrodynamics tool. It is a simulation result, not a flight result. Along with our analogy to GOCE (see \autoref{sec:estimate_cold_gas}), it supports the idea that adequately precise PuffSat navigation can overcome perturbations and measurement error.

The \SI{5}{\meter} capture is a complete feasibility result, not a downscaled stand-in for a harder target. A \SI{5}{\meter} plate is a design the mission can actually fly, and landing on it within a tolerance near \SI{2}{\meter} is what success requires (see \autoref{sec:formation_challenges_current_missions}). The tighter centering the beacon metrology is sized to reach, roughly \SI{10}{\centi\meter}, is a refinement on top of that, not a prerequisite. So the concept stands on the simulated case alone. Pushing toward \SI{10}{\centi\meter} would not make the guidance loop work harder. It would ask for a tighter terminal measurement, a bench-characterized sensor number rather than a re-run of the descent.

The simulated scenario offloads tracking and computation to three off-board navigation assets. An apogee navigation constellation fixes the PuffSat's position through the GNSS-free coast. A target-side tracker array drives terminal homing. The simulation reuses the launch rocket as a closer co-flying tracker, but it's a hedge that improves measurement margin rather than a requirement. \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture} develops these and explains why the low-orbit case needs no dedicated coordinator node.

The feasibility claim comes in three tiers, kept separate on purpose. Tier 1, the \SI{5}{\meter} capture above, is the load-bearing one, and it already describes a design the mission can fly. The other two reach past it and are held to a lower standard of proof. Tier 2 asks how much smaller the plate could be made, a refinement rather than a requirement, and it is sized rather than simulated. With surveyor-anchored centering (a sacrificial lead PuffSat whose crossing is measured independently at the plate, calibrating out the tracker's fixed bias), a sizing module in the companion repository composes the surveyor and camera error legs and reaches a plate under \SI{6}{\centi\meter} with rendezvous-lidar-class metrology, so \SI{10}{\centi\meter} carries margin \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}. Its binding inputs are bench characterizations of instruments, which no simulation can supply. Simulating this tier would add little, because centimeter trims sit deep inside the catch-radius funnel the Tier 1 simulation already covers. The one assumption a flown loop could still puncture is that execution adds nothing, that the guidance lands PuffSats no wider than its measurement error. At meter scale that assumption fails by roughly threefold, because a loop homing through angular noise that shrinks with range must fire early. At centimeter scale it holds. Flying the actual terminal law with constant centimeter-grade knowledge reproduces the sized plate to within one percent, and the same rig recovers the meter-scale threefold when fed Tier 1 noise, so the pass is a measurement rather than a blind spot \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}. That check ran on a simplified dynamics stand-in, so the tier stays sized rather than simulated. It also surfaced one timing constraint. A block correction issued at the surveyor's crossing takes seconds to execute, so the followers arriving first cross the plate before a decimeter-class shift completes. The correction must go out early, or the first units counted as surveyor-class. What gates \SI{10}{\centi\meter} is how well a calibrated camera holds its distortion floor, a question a bench test settles. We have not run that test. The floor the sizing assumes stays within demonstrated star-tracker performance, with a coarser-detector fallback if it proves optimistic on an impact-hammered airframe (see \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture}). On that basis \SI{10}{\centi\meter} looks reachable with existing hardware rather than new instruments. Tier 3, carrying the same pattern to a near-Sun, Parker-class trajectory, is an architectural sketch with two open sizing numbers. Thermal distortion of the camera is not one of them. At the 4 solar radii extreme the optics ride in the shield's shadow, where the radiative load runs nine orders of magnitude below a sunlit aperture, so the smooth heating never develops (see \autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal}). Only Tier 1 is a simulation result. Tiers 2 and 3 are extrapolations.

\subsection{Fine-Control Thruster Options With Moderate Tank Pressures}\label{sec:fine_control_thrusters}
Cold gas thrusters, like all PuffSat components, must minimize dry mass to reduce impact risk with the pusher plate (see \autoref{sec:coordinator_node_dry_mass_disposal}). A conventional high-pressure cold gas tank would likely need to be embedded within the PuffSat to protect it from micrometeoroid damage, which increases both volume and drag. These tanks are also typically expensive to manufacture and pose some risks of hitting the pusher plate as shrapnel. What follows rules out the several-hundred-bar stored-gas vessel, not every form of containment. A liquefied gas at a few bar is admitted, because its tank mass falls by more than an order of magnitude (see \autoref{sec:cold_gas_fluid_choice}).

Two primary control objectives dictate the requirements for the replacement propulsion system. Compensating for aerodynamic drag and providing torque cancellation requires a throttleable thrust of approximately \SI{400}{\milli\newton}, sustained for several minutes. That thrust figure is the peak drag force derived from GOCE atmospheric modeling (see \autoref{sec:estimate_cold_gas}). This favors a continuous feed of liquid or generated gas. Brief attitude and divert corrections need fast, discrete impulses, which favor a bank of pre-measured solid charges. A single PuffSat can carry more than one of the forms below, matched to these jobs. We baseline the liquefied propane system described first, which covers both. The forms after it are alternatives, kept because each answers something propane does not.

\subsubsection{Liquefied Hydrocarbon Cold Gas}\label{sec:cold_gas_fluid_choice}
The simplest arrangement stores the propellant as a liquid that supplies its own tank pressure. Below its critical temperature a liquefied gas sits in equilibrium with its own vapor, so the tank pressure follows the temperature alone and holds steady as propellant leaves. No separate pressurant is needed, and the tank runs at a few bar rather than the hundreds a stored-gas system needs. Small satellites fly this today. The VISORS formation-flying pair carries a 3D-printed cold-gas system of seven thrusters producing \SIrange{40}{70}{\milli\newton} on R-236fa, a fluorinated refrigerant \cite{hart2022visors_propulsion}. 

PuffSats must be very inexpensive. R-236fa is relatively expensive and at high scales a strong greenhouse gas \cite{ghgprotocol_gwp_values}. Propane, our preferred gas, is cheap and photodissociates in days in space. Propane is a rare CubeSat fuel, but we spend most of our fuel during a \SI{300}{\second} pass countering drag near target interception. The liquid cools itself as it boils, its vapor pressure falls with it, and there is no pause in which to recover. Sustained flow, not total impulse, is the binding requirement here.

Take \SI{2}{\bar} as the feed floor needed to pass \SI{400}{\milli\newton} through a millimeter-scale throat. Propane at \SI{20}{\celsius} can then cool \SI{45.5}{\kelvin} before reaching that floor, isobutane \SI{13.0}{\kelvin}, and n-butane \SI{1.2}{\kelvin} \cite{nist_webbook_propane,nist_webbook_butane,nist_webbook_isobutane}. Butane at room temperature is already sitting on its floor. It would have to be preheated and insulated through a coast of several days, and a heater would move from reserve to critical path. Propane's margin is worth about \SI{57}{\kilo\joule} of self-cooling from the \SI{500}{\gram} load sized below, and a warm start raises that by two thirds. We can achieve additional thermal margin either by simply carrying additional self-heated propane or by using a phase change material like paraffin wax on an aluminum mesh and running the propane over the wax. A black tank with vacuum-gapped greenhouse materials like glass or plastic can naturally heat paraffin to melting at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}. While this wax is not as mass efficient as batteries, it is very cheap, and the mass required is still small simply because total thrust is so low compared to the PuffSat mass. However, some battery power is already required to power electronics, so they can trim the need for self-heating or phase change materials. Propane, like all the other propellants, should be aimed away from the electronics so that the plume doesn't interfere with optical sensors.

Two alternatives were considered but appear inferior. Nitrous oxide matches propane's molar mass at \SI{44}{\gram\per\mole}, so it offers no specific-impulse gain at all, and it loses on everything else. It reaches \SI{50.5}{\bar} at \SI{20}{\celsius} and turns supercritical above \SI{36.4}{\celsius} \cite{nist_webbook_n2o}, which destroys the self-pressurizing liquid the design depends on and drives the tank past \SI{250}{\gram}. It also carries a decomposition hazard the hydrocarbons lack. A nitrous tank failed three seconds into a cold-flow test at Mojave in 2007 and killed three people, with no combustion involved \cite{flightglobal_scaled_n2o}. Its 100-year global warming potential is 265 \cite{ghgprotocol_gwp_values}, and it is the largest ozone-depleting emission of this century \cite{ravishankara2009n2o}, which sits badly beside the stratospheric caution of \autoref{sec:ozone_policy}. High-pressure nitrogen fails on mass instead. Its specific impulse is the best of the practical options. But \SI{500}{\gram} of propellant needs a \SI{1.65}{\liter} vessel at \SI{300}{\bar}, and a flown composite tank of that class alone runs near \SI{400}{\gram} \cite{meyer_copv}. That exhausts the entire dry-mass allowance before anything else is fitted. A blowdown tank also loses feed pressure steadily as it empties, while a two-phase liquid holds its pressure flat at constant temperature.

The tank is cheap and light in aluminum. Propane's saturation pressure reaches \SI{17.1}{\bar} at a \SI{50}{\celsius} qualification temperature. The \SI{500}{\gram} load then needs a \SI{1.15}{\liter} vessel, and burst-rating it to twice that pressure masses about \SI{88}{\gram} in 6061 aluminum against roughly \SI{240}{\gram} in tinplate steel. Steel is the false economy here. Its specific strength is \SI{38}{\kilo\joule\per\kilo\gram} against aluminum's \SI{102}{\kilo\joule\per\kilo\gram}. The metal itself costs about twenty cents either way. The steel version simply arrives \SI{150}{\gram} heavier, and as the densest non-volatile fragment on the vehicle (see \autoref{sec:coordinator_node_dry_mass_disposal}). A \SI{34}{\bar} burst rating is an undemanding class. Threaded carbon dioxide cartridges burst above \SI{200}{\bar} and are made by the billion at well under a dollar each. Forming cost is set by tooling and draw operations, not by wall thickness. The \SI{50}{\celsius} rating is not spare margin here, because the warm start the thermal design relies on runs the tank at that temperature in flight.
 
\subsubsection{Liquid-Fed Gas Generation}
An alternative to propane is chemical propulsion, though it is not worth the complexity until mass manufacturing drives costs and the logistics of energetic chemicals down. Chemicals need a minimal pressure tank, and their burst mode also reaches a specific impulse cold gas cannot. The liquid feed makes gas on demand instead of storing it under pressure. Metered valves release small charges of a gas-generating liquid, which decomposes into warm gas just upstream of a lightweight bladder. The bladder acts as a brief low-pressure reservoir, not a liquid propellant tank, and feeds the fine-control thrusters with gram-scale gas charges. For example, a system might catalytically decompose a gram-scale charge of concentrated hydrogen peroxide mixed with a similar mass of water. The water quenches the hot decomposition products, so the bladder briefly holds moderately heated, pressurized steam and oxygen rather than gas too hot for its thin film. Small amounts of fuel may be added both to supply additional thermal energy and to react with harsh oxidizers in the exhaust stream. Corrosion and residue are challenges in microfluidics, but the system's very short, low-altitude operating times partially mitigate them. Green monopropellant can also feed a higher-thrust hybrid mode. Rather than generating gas for the bladder, it decomposes directly in a thruster chamber to give short, stronger bursts as thrust approaches the \SI{400}{\milli\newton} maximum. The flight-proven green monopropellants suit this burst mode better than the bladder mode. NASA's Green Propellant Infusion Mission flew the HAN-based ASCENT propellant, formerly AF-M315E \cite{spores2013gpim,mclean2020gpim_results}, and ECAPS in Sweden flew the ADN-based LMP-103S earlier on PRISMA \cite{anflo2009prisma,anflo2011hpgp_inspace}. Both are reduced-toxicity hydrazine replacements tuned for high specific impulse, with adiabatic flame temperatures between \SI{1900}{\kelvin} and \SI{2200}{\kelvin} \cite{green_monoprop_review}. That heat fits short, high-thrust pulses but works against the thin polymer bladders and microfluidics. A bipropellant burst mode avoids qualifying a second propellant for that job. Burning the oxygen from the same 60 percent peroxide against a small propane charge gives a stoichiometric mixture that is only about 7 percent fuel by mass (see \autoref{sec:peroxide_propane_biprop}). The adiabatic flame temperature works out near \SI{1900}{\kelvin}, the low end of the HAN and ADN range \cite{green_monoprop_review}, so the same radiation-cooled chamber suits it. The water in the 60 percent solution does that cooling. It holds the flame well below the roughly \SI{2800}{\kelvin} of a dry propane and oxygen flame, and running oxidizer-rich or adding water lowers it further, a temperature knob the monopropellants lack. The ideal specific impulse is near \SI{230}{\second}, comparable to the HAN and ADN propellants, using commodity chemicals and the oxidizer already aboard for the bladder mode.

A platinum catalyst does double duty. It decomposes the peroxide and lights the propane. At 60 percent the decomposition gas reaches only about \SI{410}{\kelvin} (see \autoref{sec:peroxide_propane_biprop}), below both propane's \SI{450}{\celsius} autoignition \cite{cameo_propane} and platinum's catalytic light-off temperature \cite{cho1986catalytic_ignition}. A brief electric preheat lights the catalyst on the first pulse, and the \SI{1900}{\kelvin} flame then keeps it hot, so no spark or pyrotechnic igniter is needed. Going instead to 85 percent peroxide raises the decomposition gas above the autoignition point and lights the propane with no catalyst, at the cost of a hotter chamber and the more restrictive shipping tier that applies above 60 percent. The propane must be the sulfur-free, unodorized grade, since the mercaptan odorant in commercial propane would poison the platinum. That grade is preferred anyway to limit storage fractionation, and the oxidizing exhaust discourages carbon fouling of the bed.

Only milligrams of platinum are required at these flow rates. The bed frontal area follows from the peroxide mass flux, and at the mass fluxes typical of peroxide beds it is only millimeters across \cite{jung2021catalyst_bed_scaling}. A cheaper silver bed can carry the bulk peroxide decomposition \cite{yang2024microchannel}, with the platinum reserved for a thin propane-ignition zone. At those quantities the precious-metal content adds negligible cost per unit, so the substrate and its coating drive the catalyst price rather than the metal itself.

Handling a 60 percent solution is a step below the high-test peroxide of the burst chamber's ancestors. Commercial aqueous peroxide at this concentration cannot be made to propagate a detonation at ambient temperature, and it sits below the roughly 74 percent threshold at which the vapor above the liquid turns detonable below its boiling point \cite{kopacz2022htp_oxidizer}. The governing hazard in manufacturing and in flight is catalytic decomposition and oxygen buildup in a closed volume, controlled by contamination control and a vent path rather than by shock sensitivity \cite{romantsova2015htp_safety}. The peroxide and the propane are stored apart and meet only at the catalyst, because peroxide mixed with an organic fuel can detonate above 30 percent \cite{kopacz2022htp_oxidizer}. The propane self-pressurizes near \SI{8}{\bar} at room temperature \cite{nist_webbook_propane} and can serve as the peroxide's expulsion gas, removing a separate pressurant. It is the same propane the cold-gas system already carries (see \autoref{sec:cold_gas_fluid_choice}). The exhaust is only carbon dioxide and water. A nitrous oxide monopropellant would decompose to a nitrogen and oxygen exhaust. But it turns supercritical above \SI{36}{\celsius} \cite{nist_webbook_n2o}, which destroys the self-pressurizing liquid this design relies on. The tank would then have to hold the whole load as dense gas. Its exhaust is not the cleaner one either. Nitrous oxide is itself a greenhouse gas of 100-year potential 265 \cite{ghgprotocol_gwp_values} and the largest ozone-depleting emission of this century \cite{ravishankara2009n2o}. The bladder path therefore stays on hydrogen peroxide, the classic green monopropellant and still a flight option for small satellites \cite{cassese2023htp}. High-test peroxide delivers a lower specific impulse than the HAN and ADN propellants. But its catalytic decomposition runs far cooler and leaves only water and oxygen, matching the thin-skin, biocompatible design.

Peroxide has long spaceflight heritage as a monopropellant and gas-generator fluid, from the Mercury and X-15 reaction-control systems onward. At CubeSat scale, though, the catalytic thrusters are still at ground and vacuum-chamber test readiness rather than flown, behind the ADN and HAN green monopropellants that already have in-orbit heritage \cite{pasini2025htp_cubesat,kopacz2022htp_oxidizer}. The recurring challenge in that work is the catalyst bed, which must resist poisoning by the stabilizers and trace metals carried in the peroxide and keep decomposing repeatably across the mission \cite{cervone2006htp_rockets}. The PuffSat case eases this in two ways. The bed fires only for the half-orbit of a single use, so slow poisoning has little time to act, and at 60 percent the decomposition sits near \SI{410}{\kelvin}, far below the point at which a silver bed sinters on high-test peroxide \cite{cervone2006htp_rockets}. The trade is that a catalyst-clean, low-stabilizer propellant grade costs more than the commodity solution, so the fill should be treated as propulsion-grade rather than the cheapest industrial stock.

If the plumbing and support hardware for this microfluidic system weigh no more than \SI{50}{\gram}, the total dry mass would sit near the baseline propane tank of \autoref{sec:cold_gas_fluid_choice} and far below a standard \SI{400}{\gram} nitrogen tank \cite{meyer_copv}. That \SI{50}{\gram} target is not yet demonstrated as a complete flight system, but it is within reach of existing hardware. A commercial catalytic hydrogen peroxide thruster from Takasago Fluidic Systems already delivers \SI{0.2}{\newton} from a \SI{22.7}{\gram} thruster and an \SI{8.0}{\gram} valve, a combined \SI{31}{\gram} that leaves margin for tankage and control within the budget \cite{takasago_h2o2_thruster}. Terrestrial microfluidic chips already run the catalytic hydrogen peroxide decomposition this design needs, with the exothermic reaction warming the microchannel as it proceeds \cite{yang2024microchannel}. For scale, a complete flight-proven MEMS cold-gas module masses about \SI{220}{\gram}, including its tank and four microthrusters \cite{nasa_soa_propulsion}. 

The compact geometry also reduces drag and makes ejection ahead of pusher contact more straightforward (see \autoref{sec:coordinator_node_dry_mass_disposal}). As the thin polymer bladder skin fragments travel through the gas plume toward the pusher, they are decelerated and ablated, likely limiting impact damage to the pusher's ablative layers. The increased complexity of microfluidics introduces the risk of catastrophic PuffSat failures. However, the system must already be resilient to PuffSat losses, given many are used on each launch.

\subsubsection{Cool Gas Generators}
Propellant can also be stored as a solid that makes gas on command. Cool gas generators (CGGs) decompose a solid charge into gases, then route them through a heat-absorbing filter so they leave cool. The charge sits unpressurized until fired, which removes both the launch hazard and the fragmentation risk of a pre-pressurized tank. This approach has nanosatellite flight heritage. The T$^3\mu$PS cold-gas system built around CGGs flew on TU Delft's Delfi-n3Xt \cite{cgg_delfi_n3xt}, where two generators were ignited in orbit before an ignition-train fault ended the test \cite{delfi_n3xt_inorbit}. It drew on earlier ESA cold gas generator work \cite{esa_cold_gas_generators}. For a PuffSat, the chemistry should be chosen so the exhaust stays biocompatible. Non-azide automotive gas generants fit well. Guanidine nitrate burned with a basic copper nitrate oxidizer produces mostly nitrogen, water, and carbon dioxide \cite{guanidine_nitrate_gas_generant}, the same biocompatible products this design favors elsewhere. These generants are mass-produced for automotive airbags. Worldwide motor-vehicle production reached roughly 93 million units in 2023 \cite{oica_production_2023}. Automotive gas generants therefore draw on a manufacturing base built for high-volume vehicle production. A phase-stabilized ammonium nitrate formulation is also attractive, since it shares chemistry with the ANFO PuffSat fill (see \autoref{sec:explosive_puffsat}) and burns by controlled deflagration rather than detonation. Two chemistries should be avoided. Classic sodium-azide inflators leave a solid sodium-silicate slag, which is the non-volatile shrapnel we work to eliminate. Aluminized solid-rocket grains leave alumina slag and a metal casing for the same reason. Even clean generants emit trace carbon monoxide and nitrogen oxides and burn hotter than a cold-gas blowdown, so the heat-absorbing filter stays necessary to protect the thin skin and microfluidics.

\subsubsection{Single-Shot Impulse Generators}
Firing many small, individually triggered charges for the divert job has a clear precedent in missile defense. The Patriot PAC-3 interceptor steers the same way for a kinetic, hit-to-kill strike. In its terminal phase it fires individual solid attitude control motors from a ring of 180 in the forebody, each a single shot, to drive its own body onto the target \cite{pac3_acm}. A PuffSat could carry a similar set of single-shot gas-generator pulses, each producing clean gas rather than the metal-loaded exhaust of the Patriot motors.

Because PuffSats are single-use and operate for only half of a single orbit, we can budget more propellant for aggressive drag and torque correction. For PuffSats, overestimating propellant needs is wise. Unused gas is not entirely wasted, since its momentum still lands on the pusher plate rather than venting into deep space. Variability in this leftover gas propulsion slightly complicates the target rocket's trajectory adjustments between impacts. Each impact also lands a little off the plate's center, so the rocket must cancel the resulting torque. Its reaction-control thrusters and a gimbaled main engine that can tilt to push off-axis handle this, both running non-toxic propellant. We expect the rocket to trim its trajectory after each pulse as well, correcting for the gap between a pulse's true impulse and the value it was planned against. Our control simulation covered the PuffSat side of the interception, not the rocket's, so this rocket-side response is a requirement we flag rather than a simulated result. The first few PuffSats to intercept the target rocket will experience the lowest altitudes (closest to \SI{200}{\kilo\meter}) before the rocket rises slightly as it accelerates towards orbit (see \autoref{sec:leo_orbit_details}). To optimize propellant usage, we can equip these initial units with slightly more propellant to compensate for their more challenging trajectory.

If control remains impractical, we can simplify by increasing the PuffSat interception altitude. If we raise interception to \SI{300}{\kilo\meter}, a reusable suborbital target rocket (see \autoref{sec:200_mile_high}) would need higher propellant mass fractions, suffer more re-entry heating, and gain less from PuffSat velocity. However, raising interception height may be an acceptable compromise if lower altitudes near \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} prove unmanageable.

\subsection{Covering A Lost PuffSat}\label{sec:lost_puffsat}
A micrometeorite big enough to defeat the Mylar bumper destroys a PuffSat outright (see \autoref{sec:explosive_puffsat}). Radiation upsets and thruster failures can do the same. The salvo needs an answer for a unit that will not arrive, and which answer applies depends on how much warning there is.

Start with what one unit is worth. The low-orbit case of \autoref{sec:mass_fraction} pushes a \SI{32}{\tonne} craft to \SI{7.784}{\kilo\meter\per\second} on \SI{25}{\tonne} of PuffSats. At \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} apiece that is a thousand units. The craft sheds no propellant while this happens, so its speed change per pulse is just the total divided by the count, about \SI{7.8}{\meter\per\second}. Losing one unit costs the mission that \SI{7.8}{\meter\per\second}. It also leaves every PuffSat behind it aimed at a craft that will now run slightly slower than the plan those aim points were computed against.

With hours of warning, the formation fixes itself. Consecutive PuffSats ride a few kilometers apart along their track, about \SI{3.6}{\kilo\meter} at a \SI{3}{\hertz} pulse rate and a \SI{10.9}{\kilo\meter\per\second} closing speed (see \autoref{sec:pusher_plate_mass_stroke}). A PuffSat's full \SI{500}{\gram} cold-gas load is worth roughly \SI{123}{\newton\second} at the pessimistic \SI{25}{\second} specific impulse of \autoref{sec:estimate_cold_gas}, or \SI{4.9}{\meter\per\second} on a \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} body. Drag cancellation and terminal aiming already claim most of that, so a slot change can spare about \SI{1}{\meter\per\second}. A shift applied that early grows as the divert speed times the time remaining, so \SI{1}{\meter\per\second} walks a PuffSat one slot over in about an hour. Hours of warning buy a reshuffle. Ten minutes do not.

The reshuffle is only worth running if something fills the vacated slot, which argues for flying spares. Ten extra PuffSats add \SI{250}{\kilo\gram} to a \SI{25}{\tonne} salvo, 1\% of the launched mass, and cover ten losses with no impulse shortfall at all.

A loss detected late falls to the craft instead. It already carries a gimbaled main engine, and it already trims its trajectory after every pulse to correct the gap between a pulse's true impulse and its planned value (see \autoref{sec:fine_control_thrusters}). A PuffSat that never arrives is that same gap at full size. The engine fires for the missing \SI{7.8}{\meter\per\second} and puts the craft back on the trajectory the remaining PuffSats are already aimed at. Restoring the rendezvous geometry is the point, more than recovering the speed. A craft a few meters per second off plan is out of position for every pulse that follows, and the error compounds.

This branch is expensive, which is why it is the fallback. At the \SI{380}{\second} vacuum specific impulse adopted in \autoref{sec:fare_comparison}, the exhaust speed is \SI{3.73}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. Covering \SI{7.8}{\meter\per\second} on \SI{32}{\tonne} then burns about \SI{67}{\kilo\gram} of methalox, nearly three times the mass of the \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} PuffSat it stands in for. The craft also carries that propellant from the ground, while a spare PuffSat rides up with the salvo. A reserve of \SI{500}{\kilo\gram}, under 2\% of the vehicle, covers seven late losses. Sizing that reserve properly needs a micrometeorite flux estimate for the descent, which we have not made.

\subsection{Off-Board Sensor And Navigation Architecture}\label{sec:sensor_architecture}
To maximize gas mass, the PuffSats carry only simple, low-mass electronics and offload tracking and computation to off-board assets. The closed-loop simulation supports an architecture that splits the navigation job by regime \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}. An apogee constellation pins each PuffSat's position through the coast. GNSS contributes only at low altitude. A tracker array on the target, helped by a co-flying tracker, drives terminal homing. Cameras on the PuffSats themselves form an optional redundancy layer. Dedicated coordinator satellites remain a fallback, not a requirement. Keeping the PuffSats this simple also suits mass production, since hundreds are needed per mission.

Course corrections are cheapest near apogee, where the PuffSat crawls and a burn of a few meters per second reshapes the rest of the orbit (see \autoref{sec:handling_space_debris}). Exploiting that lever takes position knowledge at apogee, and the multi-day coast is far above GNSS service. A small constellation of navigation satellites parked near the \SI{150000}{\kilo\meter} apogee supplies it \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}. Each member broadcasts a one-way, cryptographically signed Ka-band ranging signal, and the division of labor is deliberately lopsided. The precise clocks ride on the constellation. Kilogram-class space atomic clocks are flight-proven. Galileo's rubidium standard masses \SI{3.3}{\kilo\gram} and holds about \SI{10}{\nano\second} per day, and the GIOVE-A units ran 6.5 years in medium Earth orbit with no clock problem \cite{rochat2005galileo_clocks,esa_giove_a}. Holding time through a longer unattended gap costs more mass. NASA's Deep Space Atomic Clock, a 16-kilogram demonstration unit, held its timing error below 4 nanoseconds over 23 days \cite{burt2021dsac}. A nanosecond of timing corresponds to 30 centimeters of range. Each PuffSat carries only a sub-gram chip that receives and verifies the signed broadcasts, with no transmitter. Like a GNSS receiver, it cancels its own cheap clock's offset by listening to a fourth constellation member. The target rocket and the co-flyer carry the heavier end: a two-way transponder that echoes the constellation's signal after a delay stable to nanoseconds, letting the constellation measure round-trip range and pin the rockets' states as well.

GPS itself is not hopeless at this altitude. NASA's MMS mission navigated on GPS side-lobe signals, the weak spillover from antennas pointed at Earth, from above \SI{76000}{\kilo\meter} \cite{winternitz2017mms}, and later phases pushed fixes near \SI{150000}{\kilo\meter} \cite{winternitz2017mmsion}. But those signals are faint and unauthenticated, with poor geometry so far above the transmitters, so the architecture treats them as opportunistic rather than load-bearing. GNSS earns its place at low altitude instead, where the co-flyer can use it to anchor its geometry relative to the target to roughly \SI{2}{\meter} \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}.

Terminal homing is angular. Each PuffSat strobes an optical beacon in a known blink pattern. Three emitters reach the watt-scale peak and milliwatt average this takes, and they trade cost against beam spread and spectral width. A pulsed LED is cheapest and radiates into a full hemisphere, so it needs no pointing but throws most of its light where no tracker is looking. An array of vertical-cavity surface-emitting lasers (VCSELs), laser diodes that emit from the face of the chip rather than from its cut edge, and that are mass-produced for phone depth sensors \cite{iga2018vcsel}, costs little more and concentrates the same power into a narrow beam. It also emits over a much smaller spread of wavelengths. A Q-switched laser, one that stores energy and releases it as brief, intense pulses \cite{mcclung1962qswitch}, is tighter still on both counts and the most expensive today. The choice goes to whichever is cheapest at build time. The LEDSAT CubeSat was designed with flashing LEDs so ground telescopes could track it even in Earth's shadow \cite{santoni2018ledsat}. A narrowband filter matched to the beacon's wavelength lets each tracker reject sunlight and background, and gating each exposure to the known pulse times rejects still more.  ELROI experiments demonstrated this filter-plus-gating combination in simulated and long-range ground tests \cite{holmes2019elroi}. A narrowband filter tight enough to reject sunlight also rejects most starlight, so the reference stars cannot share the beacon's filtered channel. A dichroic splits the light behind the shared front optics, sending the beacon wavelength to a gated narrowband channel and the rest to a broadband channel that reads the star field. Both channels look through the same front optics, so the dominant distortion is common to the beacon and the stars and cancels when the two are differenced. This differential astrometry fixes the beacon's angle to a small fraction of a pixel \cite{liebe2002startracker}. Thermal drift in the tracker optics is handled the same way. Heating bends the optical figure smoothly across the field, and a smooth shared distortion is what the star-differencing cancels. Gaia gives the precedent that the residual is tractable, modeling its thermoelastic basic-angle wobble, a thousand times its accuracy target, and correcting it in software to reach microarcsecond astrometry \cite{lindegren2018gaia}. Hardware can back this up if a bench test ever demands it: a reflective narrowband front element rejects out-of-band sunlight before it heats the optics, and edge-cooled synthetic diamond windows from megawatt gyrotron practice show how to conduct away the little that is absorbed \cite{thumm2001diamond}.

The binding quantity is angular precision times range. A PuffSat's cross-track error is $\sigma_\theta R$, the tracker's angular precision $\sigma_\theta$ times the range $R$ to the PuffSat, and the simulation puts the requirement at an effective precision of about \SI{3}{\micro\radian} \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}. The floor here is not a bench calibration trusted to hold through the flight. Each exposure re-solves the bulk optical distortion against Gaia catalog stars in the same frame \cite{lindegren2018gaia}, so the largest error terms are re-derived in flight rather than remembered from the ground. What remains is a residual, the fine distortion the handful of stars in one frame cannot pin down. That residual is set by the optics and the star geometry, not by photon noise, so it is a fixed bias rather than a shaky image that steadies with more exposures. It is closer to a ruler with a slightly bent end, and averaging more readings cannot straighten a bent ruler. What works against it is geometric, and the architecture pulls both available levers. The first sharpens the angle. The committed design fuses five detectors, mounted at separate points on the target so no single impact loads them identically, each making its own star-referenced measurement through its own optics. Their residual biases are then largely independent, and fusion drives the combined grade toward a floor near \SI{1.6}{\micro\radian} \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}. If that grade proves optimistic on an airframe hammered by impacts, about a dozen cruder \SI{10}{\micro\radian} detectors recover it, their independent biases averaging as $10/\sqrt{12}$ to about \SI{2.9}{\micro\radian}, just inside the requirement. The second lever shortens $R$, and it works for only part of the flight. The co-flying tracker, the reused launch rocket, stands off the corridor by a distance of order a hundred kilometers and can host one large aperture where the target's distributed detectors cannot. It also breaks the exposure lock, the constraint that ties the star channel and the beacon channel to the same shutter. On the target both are gated to the same millisecond, so the reference star field is built from a millisecond of light. The co-flyer's star channel can instead run free for tens of milliseconds on its own detector, with a gyro carrying attitude across to each beacon pulse. Centroid precision improves as the square root of exposure time, so that alone is worth a factor of ten. The co-flyer therefore holds the measurement through the long mid-course, where its standoff really is the shorter baseline. That large aperture is expensive enough to be worth recovering, so raising the co-flyer's perigee clear of the \SI{50}{\kilo\meter} disposal altitude leaves it in the same ellipse to serve later PuffSat streams for tens of meters per second each.

Conservation of angular momentum gives $v_p r_p = v_a r_a$, so a fixed time spacing covers $r_a/r_p$ times more distance at perigee than at apogee, about 23 here. A \SI{0.3}{\meter\per\second} nudge along the radius at apogee, perpendicular to the velocity, therefore opens the co-flyer into a pair \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} apart at interception. Two vantages then leave no miss hidden along a line of sight. That nudge leaves the semi-major axis unchanged, so the periods stay matched and the pair closes back to within meters at the next apogee, where the reverse nudge merges it. A merged tracker rides home stowed inside a Starship-class heat shield rather than carrying one of its own. Entry from this ellipse is near \SI{10.9}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, lunar-return class rather than low-orbit class. Leaving the pair split instead keeps both vantages for the next PuffSat formation.

It hands the measurement back two to three seconds before impact. Two things force that. The target-side array looks straight down the approach corridor, so its blind direction, the line along which it cannot see displacement, carries only arrival timing rather than lateral miss. Both miss components stay observable at every instant. An off-board vantage has some real miss hidden along its line of sight instead. The second reason is $R$ itself. The target's range to a PuffSat falls to zero at impact while the co-flyer's standoff holds roughly fixed, so the target's product $\sigma_\theta R$ reaches \SI{1.6}{\centi\meter} at \SI{10}{\kilo\meter} even at the coarser angular grade above. The crossover of those two products, not sensor quality, sets the handover.

This also explains why the impact-gated millisecond exposure costs so little. Gating exists only while impacts are landing, and by then $R$ is short enough that the coarse angle still gives centimeters. A tenfold degradation of the target mount would still deliver \SI{10}{\centi\meter} with half a second in hand.

Impacts do not blind this array for long. Each strike briefly saturates the rearward view, but at a pulse rate of a few hertz there is a quiet window between impacts, and the millisecond exposures are gated into it \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}. The plate itself hides the flash's point of origin from detectors mounted forward of it. The mechanical blow matters less than it first appears, because a shock that shifts the whole tracker moves the beacon and the reference stars together, and that common motion cancels in the same-frame differencing above. The one effect it cannot cancel is smear within a single exposure, so the binding constraint is a rate limit, not a displacement limit. The mount's angular rate during the millisecond exposure must stay small enough that the beacon and stars do not blur past the centroiding tolerance. Star sensors keep centroiding through rates of a few degrees per second \cite{zhang2012blurredstar}, though that tolerance is quoted at an accuracy class far coarser than the floor here, so it cannot carry the bound alone. What closes the gap is that near-uniform smear costs precision, not bias. A trail swept at constant rate centroids at the mid-exposure attitude, and the beacon pulse is timed to that same midpoint through the gating above, so the first-order shift differences away just as displacement does. What would bias the measurement is rate that changes within the millisecond, ringing at hundreds of hertz, and that is the band a shock isolator attenuates most. The bar is therefore the residual low-frequency rate the isolator leaves, not a fully still mount. A shock isolator working across the quiet window delivers that, and it need only damp the ringing within the window, not hold a fixed pointing between hits. The M712 Copperhead provides a closer precedent. GAO reports that its laser seeker homes on reflected laser energy, that its guidance section contains optical components, and that cannon launch subjects the projectile to nearly \num{9000} times gravity \cite{gao1985_copperhead}. Copperhead does not qualify the microradian tracker proposed here for repeated shocks, but it shows that cannon launch does not by itself rule out optical terminal guidance. The reference stars are re-acquired in every exposure, so no stored star map has to survive the hit.

A layer of cameras on the PuffSats themselves adds an independent measurement, not just a spare. A few-gram camera pointed backward images the follower's beacon against dark sky, never staring into the impact flash ahead, and the short range between neighbors makes even a crude camera precise. Each unit's blink pattern identifies it. Pointed forward at the target's beacon, the same camera lets a PuffSat run its own terminal guidance, with the flash rejected by gating the exposure to the beacon's pulse times. That bearing comes through different optics from a different vantage than the target-side array, so its error is largely independent, and fusing the two sharpens the estimate. The vibration is milder here than on the target mount. A PuffSat is the projectile, striking the plate once at the end, so its camera never works through the repeated impact shocks the target absorbs. What remains is its own microthruster jitter and residual body rate, and a short exposure freezes that. Gating the exposure to the pulsed beacon shortens it to the pulse itself, so the beacon holds a sub-pixel centroid even while the body turns a few degrees per second, the same effect star trackers use to stay accurate under high slew \cite{wei2014exposure}.

Freezing the frame is only half the measurement. A sharp centroid fixes the beacon's bearing in the camera's own frame, and turning that into a line of sight in inertial space needs the camera's pointing at the instant of exposure. The target-side tracker reads that pointing from the catalog stars in the same frame. A PuffSat sees only the beacon against dark sky, so it carries a gram-class MEMS gyro and reconstructs the inertial line-of-sight rate from its own measured body rate, the standard strapdown-seeker method \cite{sun2015los}. The binding error becomes the gyro's drift over the few-second terminal phase, not the blur. Redundancy sits on top of this. Carrying three cameras lets a two-of-three vote discard a unit whose calibration has silently failed, the fault-containment logic of the triple-voting flight computers in \autoref{sec:leo_orbit_details}. The vote alone is not a precision gain, since cameras from one production batch share distortion. Three few-gram cameras fit inside the \SI{250}{\gram} dry-mass budget. The apogee constellation and ordinary GNSS can each serve as an optional cross-check on this angular measurement, overlapping near the fifteen-minute mark where Earth occults the constellation and GNSS geometry turns good. None of this layer is simulated, and the target-side array remains the load-bearing path.

The suite closes with the sacrificial lead PuffSat of \autoref{sec:formation_challenges_current_missions}, which measures the block's shared offset in flight. If any of this falls short, coordinator satellites remain available: a small number of better-equipped satellites flying alongside the formation, each tracking the PuffSats and relaying adjustment commands while accounting for communication and actuator latencies. As shown in \autoref{fig:coordinator-nodes}, these heavier nodes do not impact the target rocket. The closed-loop simulation finds the low-orbit case closes without them \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}, so they are held in reserve rather than depended on.

\begin{figure}[htpb]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{images/Coordinator Nodes.png}
    \caption{Coordinator nodes (non-impacting) handle measurement and computation.  PuffSat solids detach or pass through the pusher plate's aperture before impact.}
    \label{fig:coordinator-nodes}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Steering PuffSats With High-Powered Lasers}
The guidance schemes above all put the actuator on the PuffSat itself, a microthruster that fires on command. The actuation could instead sit off-board, steering each PuffSat from a distance. A coordinator node aims a kilowatt-class laser at the PuffSat, and the beam's radiation pressure nudges its trajectory. The same beam could also induce temporary thermal or photochromic changes in the PuffSat's skin, a light-triggered shift in its geometry or albedo. If such a change persists briefly, the altered sunlight pressure on the skin adds to the beam's own push and magnifies the trajectory change. Steering from a distance lets the PuffSat shed its microthrusters and electronics. It then costs less and carries fewer non-volatile solids, the leftover hardware that does not gasify and so threatens the pusher plate in a collision. The cost moves to the coordinator node. The beam exerts only a weak force on the PuffSat, and producing it demands a kilowatt-class power supply on the node.

\subsection{Dry Mass Disposal}\label{sec:coordinator_node_dry_mass_disposal}

Inspired by the smallest PocketQube pico-satellite designs \cite{pocketqube_mass_reference}, each PuffSat carries no more than \SI{250}{\gram} of dry mass. This dry mass includes sensors, power systems, microthrusters, and low-bandwidth communication links to the off-board assets of \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture}. Two items dominate it. The propellant tank runs about \SI{88}{\gram} and the battery capacity that keeps it warm about \SI{48}{\gram} (see \autoref{sec:cold_gas_fluid_choice}). Roughly \SI{50}{\gram} of valves and plumbing, three few-gram cameras, and a gram-class gyro bring the identified total near \SI{200}{\gram}. The remaining \SI{50}{\gram} covers structure, the radio, and the energy the electronics draw across the multi-day coast. That last entry is not sized anywhere in this paper, and it is the one most likely to push the budget upward. If typical PuffSats carry more than \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} of fuel and propulsion mass, non-volatile solids constitute less than 1\% of their total mass. To prevent this dry mass from impacting the rocket, we propose several disposal strategies:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Discard Before Impact:} The dry mass components could launch slightly away from the rocket just before impact. If explosives are used to gasify the PuffSat (see \autoref{sec:explosive_puffsat}), shaped explosive sections can direct and accelerate the non-volatile solids radially outward at high speed. The shaped explosives ensure the non-volatile solids outrun the expanding gas envelope and clear the target radius prior to impact. The release must preserve the solids' forward escape margin while clearing the plate. Functioning packages then make the prograde disposal burn of \autoref{sec:near_term_disposal}. \label{item:discard_before_impact}
    \item \textbf{Active Elimination:} Equipment on the target rocket could obliterate the dry mass via electric discharges, directed energy beams, or sprays of material that hit the incoming PuffSat solids before pusher plate impact.
    \item \textbf{Passive Routing:} The dry mass components could pass harmlessly through a small aperture in the pusher plate.
    \item \textbf{Spinning Solids Connected by Tension:} If the PuffSat is spin-stabilized, its dry mass can be divided into two equal-mass electronics packages connected by a bundle of redundant, ultra-thin conductive tethers of metallized high-tensile fiber (see \autoref{sec:fiber_selection}). Coordinated microthrusters on each package actively control the tether orientation and the PuffSat's center-of-mass trajectory, keeping both packages outside the projected radius of the pusher plate during terminal approach. \autoref{sec:spinning_tension_detail} works through the balance, severing, and heritage details. \label{item:spinning_solids_tension}
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{The Spinning Tethered Pair In Detail}\label{sec:spinning_tension_detail}

The two packages need not be identical. To trim cost, one can be a passive counterweight, or a thruster slaved to its partner with no navigation or sensing of its own. That variant forfeits the two-package sensing baseline, trading estimation precision for a cheaper build. Unequal fuel use over the flight shifts the pair's balance, so the packages sweep unequal circles. Reeling the tether slightly on one side holds the mass-times-radius products equal and keeps the center of mass at the midpoint; the lighter package can also vent a little propellant to match.

Those thrusters are canted so their plumes point away from the tether and never play directly across it. A symmetric canted pair still produces net thrust along the intended axis at a small cosine penalty, the standard way spacecraft keep exhaust off solar arrays and other sensitive surfaces \cite{dettleff_plume}. The tether length also separates the two packages by meters, which lets one carry the thrusters and the other the optical sensors; the exhaust dissipates long before reaching the far end. The sensing package carries a small disposal thruster of its own, which fires prograde after detachment and clearance of the rocket. Each package reserves the \SI{10}{\meter\per\second} disposal allowance of \autoref{sec:near_term_disposal}. The spinning tethered pair has flight heritage. Gemini XI flew tethered to its Agena target vehicle in 1966 and generated measurable centrifugal gravity by slowly rotating the pair \cite{gemini11_tether}.

Thrust bends the bundle into a shallow V, since the two half-tethers must drag the center body along, which leaves the beacon off the line the plate is aimed at. An accelerometer beside the beacon recovers that angle from the direction of the tether's pull, so the correction rides along with the burn and no thrustless coast is needed before impact, unlike the \SI{45}{\second} settling wait VISORS takes after each firing \cite{guffanti2023autonomous}. In sunlight the package's solar panel fixes the spin rate that reading is referenced against, since its output rises and falls once per rotation, which tracks the small rate changes each firing leaves behind. In darkness a crude star field does the same job, since the spin trails stars across the frame and trail length alone gives the rate. That is a far lower bar than the star-referenced pointing of \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture}, which needs a catalog match and an absolute attitude solution.

The tether bundle itself cannot be left intact. It spans the gap between the packages, so an uncut bundle would sweep across the target craft's full width even though the end packages clear it. Severing the tethers is not enough either, since a cut line trailing from a departing package could flail back inside the plate radius.

Just before impact, an electric pulse sent through the conductive coating fires a mild detonating fuse, a thin cord cored with the high explosive PETN, that runs the bundle's length. The reaction is a detonation, a shock-driven front traveling near \SI{7}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so it crosses the \SI{25}{\meter} bundle in about \SI{3.6}{\milli\second}, effectively all at once. A slower deflagrating pyrotechnic, burning at meters per second, would consume the bundle over tenths of a second and leave one end intact long after the other fired. The same detonation vaporizes the tethers and triggers atomization of the remaining PuffSat mass. Mild detonating fuse and flexible linear shaped charge have long flight heritage in stage separation and range-safety destruct systems \cite{bement_pyro_manual}.

A live PETN cord running the length of an exposed bundle carries its own risk. Shock pressures of about a gigapascal are enough to set off a sensitive explosive like PETN \cite{souers_thresholds}, and a hypervelocity grain striking the core delivers far more, so a chance hit could fire the charge early. Sheathing the cord in a thin inflatable bumper blunts this. The bumper breaks an incoming grain into a debris cloud before it reaches the core, the same Whipple principle the pusher plate uses \cite{whipple_shield}, so a strike is far likelier to sever the fibers than to detonate the cord. A premature sever away from the target is harmless, since a stray cut only threatens the plate during terminal approach. The sheath adds a little drag area, a modest price. The pulse only triggers; the stored chemical energy does the vaporizing, which keeps the capacitor within the \SI{250}{\gram} dry-mass budget.

If a live PETN cord proves too costly or hazardous to fly on every unit, an inert variant keeps the trigger pulse but drops the explosive. The thread is sheathed instead in a thin gas tube a few centimeters across, holding nitrogen at a few percent of an atmosphere, which cannot be set off early by a grain strike, the failure mode that forces the PETN cord's bumper. At worst a hit vents the tube and cuts the fiber, harmless away from the target. The tube doubles as a Whipple bumper in transit \cite{whipple_shield}, and at the plate its gas and the atomization plume shred the bare fiber and ablate the pieces, the same fate the polymer bladder skin meets (see \autoref{sec:fine_control_thrusters}), so no coherent line survives to rake the plate. The gas does not slow the fiber, whose momentum per unit frontal area is far too high for so thin a cushion; it breaks the line into fragments rather than stopping it. The price is the detonation's near-simultaneous cut. A gas sheath parts the fiber only where the plate meets it, so a length can trail briefly from a departing package before the plume takes it, a load the ablative plate must absorb. Spreading ablative oil thicker over that small impact area reinforces it.

After the flyby, packages on the baseline disposal trajectory continue into the atmosphere without another burn (see \autoref{sec:handling_space_debris}). Splitting the electronics into two packages also lengthens the sensing baseline, which sharpens relative position and attitude estimation. The plate remains the backstop for this option. The sail is the craft's widest element, so a package that strays inward strikes the sail face rather than the vehicle behind it.

All of these impact mitigation techniques would occasionally fail. In this case, the pusher plate itself is a last line of defense. A heat-tolerant Kevlar-backed pusher plate (see \autoref{sec:lightweight_pusher_plates}) structurally resembles Whipple shielding \cite{whipple_shield}. The pusher plate would pulverize the non-volatile solids and prevent catastrophic damage to crewed compartments or high-value satellites. The target rocket would then ideally steer out of the PuffSat stream and abort. If it carries people, it survives reentry with single-use but reliable ablative heat shields. The vehicle might also carry swappable, redundant pusher plates to allow mission continuation after a single component failure. Even with redundancy, we retain the capability for reentry in a worst-case multiple-failure scenario.

\subsubsection{Splitting One PuffSat Into A Tethered Ring}\label{sec:tethered_ring}

The pair above divides the dry mass. The same spin-and-tether machinery can divide the water. Instead of one \SI{100}{\kilo\gram} bag, a PuffSat can fly as eleven smaller bags on Kevlar spokes, ten of about \SI{9}{\kilo\gram} spaced evenly around a ring with one more on the axis. This is an enhancement rather than a requirement. The restitution factor $f$ and the capture fraction $\eta_{\mathrm{capture}}$ of \autoref{sec:mass_fraction} were both computed for a single cloud, so the ring is a variant still to be swept, not the baseline the rest of this paper describes.

Shape is the first gain. The impact simulation finds the plate couples best when the arriving gas is close to a flat disk \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. A single bag has one knob for reaching that shape, the time between atomization and impact, and it shapes the cloud by letting it expand. Expansion is also what carries gas past the plate rim, which is the loss $\eta_{\mathrm{capture}}$ measures. Formation geometry is a second knob. Starting from a ring already near the target shape reaches a flat disk with less expansion, and less expansion spills less. The target is a particular width rather than the narrowest one. The best survivable configuration in the geometry sweep places the footprint at about half the plate radius, where peak facesheet pressure reaches \SI{342}{\mega\pascal} against a \SI{400}{\mega\pascal} baseline limit at \SI{16}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. Concentrating the same mass onto \num{0.3} of the plate radius buys about \num{0.01} more $f$ and takes the peak to \SI{1470}{\mega\pascal}, which destroys the plate. The ring's job is to place the footprint, not to shrink it, and footprint is the axis the delivered impulse is most sensitive to. The hub bag earns its place here. Closer azimuthal spacing lets neighboring puffs merge sooner, but filling the center of a ring still takes radial expansion of order the ring radius, however many bags sit on it.

Subdividing is nearly free in mass. Skin area grows as the cube root of the bag count, so eleven bags carry about \SI{2.3}{\square\meter} of film against \SI{1.05}{\square\meter} for one sphere (see \autoref{sec:spherical_water_thermal}). Each shell goes from about \SI{20}{\gram} to \SI{44}{\gram}, which leaves the water fraction alone.

Each bag also has to be held against turning on its own. Circling the axis and spinning about one's own center are separate motions, and a spoke compels only the first. One ending at a single point pulls through the bag's center, which is no leverage to twist with, so the bag keeps whatever spin it was deployed with. Spin the assembly up after the spokes are taut and the bags never start turning at all. They would circle the axis while holding fixed against the stars, parking one bumper hemisphere in permanent sun near \SI{150}{\celsius} and leaving the other permanently dark. That undoes the light-and-shade cycling the thermal balance of \autoref{sec:spherical_water_thermal} rests on.

Ending each spoke in a bridle of three lines to separated points on the shell restores the leverage. Turning the bag would have to lengthen at least one line, and the spin that flings it outward keeps all three taut. Two lines would leave free the axis running through both attachment points, so three is the minimum. The bag then turns once per lap and holds one face to the hub, the geometry the Moon keeps toward Earth. It is locked to the hub rather than to the Sun, so its own field stays fixed in its frame while the Sun still sweeps past once per rotation. A sphere under a central force is torque-free, so the bridle carries spin-up and the re-point only, never a steady load. The re-point asks about \SI{0.1}{\milli\newton\meter} of it, against \SI{18}{\newton} of spoke tension.

Redundancy is the second gain. A dud initiator costs the whole pulse, and so does a micrometeorite through both shells. Subdividing turns either into a shortfall. More bags mean more skin to puncture, but the cost of a puncture falls faster than the exposure rises. Skin area grows as $N^{1/3}$ while the loss per hit falls as $1/N$, so the expected shortfall falls as $N^{-2/3}$. Eleven bags cut it about fivefold.

A lost bag does more than shrink the pulse. It moves the pulse off axis, and the imbalance is the missing bag's own moment about the plate axis. For \SI{9}{\kilo\gram} arriving at \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second} on a \SI{2}{\meter} ring radius that is about \SI{2e5}{\newton\meter\second} of angular impulse, delivered in one event that nothing on the vehicle absorbs. An off-center pulse at \SI{90}{\percent} strength is worse for the target than no pulse at all, since a missing pulse is recoverable by firing another PuffSat.

The answer is to vent the opposite bag. Venting rather than leaving it unfired is the point, because an unatomized bag arrives as a coherent \SI{9}{\kilo\gram} slug of water, the impact this whole architecture exists to prevent. With an even count in the ring, fully venting the antipode cancels the imbalance exactly, and it is also the cheapest cancellation available. An odd ring has no antipode, and the best substitute is partially venting the two bags flanking it, at a cost of \num{1.24} bag masses instead of one. So the ring holds an even count. A failure then costs two bags rather than one, a shortfall of $2/N$ of the ring mass, or \SI{20}{\percent} at ten. The hub is the safe bag to lose, since it sits on the axis and its loss costs shape rather than balance.

Whether the vent arrives in time depends on the failure. A puncture or a dead thruster shows up minutes to hours ahead, which is ample. A dud initiator shows up only when the charge is commanded, and that is the hard case. It survives on lead time atomization already needs. Bags that must expand a meter or two at cloud speeds of tens of meters per second have to fire tens of milliseconds before impact. Vented vapor leaving near \SI{500}{\meter\per\second} clears \SI{20}{\meter} in \SI{40}{\milli\second}, enough to miss a \SI{5}{\meter} plate. All initiators fire together, and any that failed are answered by venting the antipode a few milliseconds later. Should the atomization lead time prove to be microseconds rather than milliseconds, the vent covers only the slow failures and the dud stays uncovered.

What the ring adds is a timing requirement on the trigger. At \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, \SI{1}{\milli\second} of scatter in atomization times smears the disk \SI{11}{\meter} along the flight direction. The mild detonating fuse above crosses \SI{25}{\meter} in \SI{3.6}{\milli\second}, simultaneous enough to sever a bundle but enough to smear a ring over \SI{40}{\meter}. A ring wants an initiator at every bag fired by a common electrical pulse, which crosses the same distance in about \SI{170}{\nano\second}.

The rest it inherits. Guidance stays a single interception, because the bags are rigidly tethered and the thrusters steer one center of mass. A spinning ring also holds its plane in inertial space, which is most of what squaring the disk to the closing velocity asks for. What it does not settle is which inertial direction. Stirring wants the spin axis perpendicular to the Sun-line (\autoref{sec:spherical_water_thermal}) while the disk wants it along the closing velocity, and mission geometry decides whether those agree. When they do not, the ring flies the thermal attitude through the coast and re-points late. Even a full \SI{90}{\degree} costs about \SI{63}{\newton\second}, an equivalent \SI{0.63}{\meter\per\second} on the \SI{100}{\kilo\gram} assembly. That is about half the \SI{500}{\gram} propellant load at the pessimistic specific impulse of \autoref{sec:estimate_cold_gas}, and it takes under sixteen minutes of firing (\autoref{sec:spin_reorientation}). The spokes reach the plate as bare fiber, and a \SI{0.2}{\milli\meter} Kevlar strand struck broadside is about \SI{0.3}{\kilo\gram\per\square\meter} against roughly \SI{400}{\kilo\gram\per\square\meter} of water, so the plume shreds and ablates it as it does the severed bundle. A vented bag leaves an empty shell of tens of grams, which joins the dry mass of \autoref{sec:coordinator_node_dry_mass_disposal} rather than posing a new problem.

Severing that bag's spoke on its own is unnecessary. A vented bag has no moment left to cancel, so a cutter would remove a residual the vent has already made negligible, and would do it slowly. A severed bag carries no centripetal force and departs tangentially, its radial clearance growing at first as $\tfrac{1}{2}\omega^{2}Rt^{2}$, about \SI{2}{\meter\per\second\squared} for a \SI{2}{\meter} ring at \SI{1}{\radian\per\second}. Clearing \SI{3}{\meter} that way takes \SI{1.7}{\second} against the vent's \SI{40}{\milli\second}, and the trigger pulse destroys the whole tether system at atomization in any case. The vent also covers the case that would otherwise want a cutter. A bag leaking at an unknown rate has an unknown mass, so no antipodal vent is the right size. Venting the damaged bag to empty first restores a known state, and the antipode then answers it exactly.

\subsection{A Rough Sketch Of The Software Guidance For PuffSat Interception}\label{sec:neural_navigation}
To ensure a precise interception of the next PuffSat, the target spacecraft's rocket-based reaction control system must rapidly adjust its trajectory. It must do this within fractions of a second after each impact, correcting for the difference between the expected and actual momentum transfer from the previous PuffSat. A prerequisite for these hypervelocity PuffSat rendezvous is accurately capturing the position and velocity of the PuffSats. For this, a standard Unscented Kalman Filter (UKF) \cite{wan2000unscented} offers robust state estimation when dynamics are well-modeled and noise is Gaussian. The large formation of PuffSats provides a dense measurement stream, further improving filter performance. 

PuffSat interceptions, however, may occur near low periapsis in denser atmospheric layers where drag, gravitational anomalies, sensor dropouts, and outliers challenge UKF's assumptions and accuracy. When these effects exceed UKF tolerance, state estimation could be improved with hybrid approaches that augment the UKF with neural networks that learn residuals on real and simulated orbital data. For example, methods like KalmanNet \cite{revach_2022_kalmannet,revach2022unsupervised} have shown robustness to unmodeled disturbances in simulations. Neural inference also scales better as the number of estimated states grows.  The UKF's covariance update costs roughly the cube of the state dimension, while a trained network's forward pass grows far more slowly. As a result, neural estimators may support more frequent updates in a large PuffSat formation. 

Interpretable, data-efficient techniques like model predictive control (MPC) can replan trajectories to correct for momentum prediction errors after PuffSat impacts. Neural networks can warm-start conventional tools with good initial guesses to reduce solve times \cite{guffanti2024transformerstrajectoryoptimizationapplication}, especially when there are many real-time constraints \cite{briden_constraint}. However, the control law itself could remain grounded in well-understood optimization and stability guarantees. 

Since conservative approaches appear feasible, we've prioritized them over faster, more opaque methods, such as parallel deep learning ensembles optimized for inference speed. That said, these black-box strategies merit consideration. Our PuffSat system must already tolerate frequent hardware failures such as lost PuffSats (see \autoref{sec:lost_puffsat}), so an occasional control error from a black-box method is no worse than the anomalies we already plan for. Reinforcement learning even shows promise for developing fast trajectory recovery algorithms after such anomalies, where standard control techniques struggle \cite{zavoli2021reinforcement}.

The binding lateral requirement is only to land on the \SI{5}{\meter} plate, a tolerance near \SI{2}{\meter}.  Tighter centering is a refinement, not a prerequisite. Gas generation unfolds over a finite duration and has less precision along the thrust axis. Centering the impact to roughly \SI{10}{\centi\meter}, which the beacon metrology is sized to reach, makes the plume impinge symmetrically and preserves the vehicle's inertial stability. The target rocket's reaction control system must still cancel the off-center torque from the occasional PuffSat that lands well off center, the price of the relaxed lateral tolerance.

The terminal endgame is forgiving on control authority. The PuffSat's thrusters can pull it back onto the plate from a miss of roughly \SI{475}{\meter} \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}, the catch radius the block argument above relies on. That distance is fixed by how far constant thrust moves a mass in the time remaining, the familiar $\tfrac{1}{2}a t^2$, not by how well the PuffSat is tracked.

As the PuffSat closes on the plate, the time remaining shrinks and the reachable sideways distance shrinks with it, so the correctable region is a funnel that narrows toward the plate. The closer the PuffSat gets, the less room it has to swerve, and the size of that last swerve is set by its thrusters, not its eyesight. The PuffSat reaches the mouth of this funnel with a lateral spread near \SI{224}{\meter}, one standard deviation of the upstream error budget. The \SI{475}{\meter} catch radius is a little over twice that, so the PuffSat enters with roughly a factor of two to spare on authority \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}. That margin is conservative. Most of the spread is along-track, and the cross-track part the funnel has to null is nearer \SI{33}{\meter} \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}. What sets the real limit is therefore not control but knowledge, how well the PuffSat knows its sideways position in the final seconds. More thrust or a later burn cannot buy knowledge the tracker never had. Supplying that knowledge, through the fused tracker array and the co-flyer, is the job of the sensor architecture in \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture}.

\subsection{Handling Space Debris} \label{sec:handling_space_debris}
\subsubsection{Earth Escape After Periapsis Delivery}\label{sec:near_term_disposal}
We recommend Earth escape for near-term Earth-delivery PuffSats. Stage the formation on a long ellipse, then pay for an inward injection near its apogee. The resulting hyperbola places the \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} encounter at periapsis, where the arriving velocity is horizontal. This removes the disposal-driven downward inflow and its forward-thrust penalty (\autoref{sec:pusher_plate_down_kick}). We prefer paying for the distant injection to spending pulse momentum on oblique arrivals and routinely returning dry hardware to the atmosphere. This is an architectural choice; a full mission cost comparison has not been computed.

Take an initial ellipse with \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} perigee altitude and apogee at a geocentric radius of \SI{1000000}{\kilo\meter}. Slow motion near apogee retains the opportunity to adjust formation timing and spacing before injection. An approximately \SI{0.95}{\kilo\meter\per\second} burn, almost directly inward, supplies a vacuum periapsis speed \SI{5}{\meter\per\second} above local escape. The Earth-only coast from injection to encounter takes about \SI{8.4}{\day}. This burn belongs to the delivery propulsion budget, separate from the PuffSat's cold-gas fine-control allowance. \autoref{sec:staging_escape_geometry} derives the injection and escape margins.

Retain bound staging while investigating an argon solar-electric carrier for delivery. At an assumed \SI{2000}{\second} specific impulse, the reference \SI{0.95}{\kilo\meter\per\second} costs \SI{4.7}{\percent} of initial maneuvering mass as propellant, before charging the power system. The trajectory must distribute this thrust over time. At \SI{50}{\percent} thruster efficiency and \SI{10}{\watt\per\kilogram} of total maneuvering mass, accumulating that impulse takes about \SI{22}{\day}. Allowing parabolic escape before a powered return does not itself save impulse. At the same million-kilometre radius, reversing the outward motion onto the specified arrival costs about \SI{1.84}{\kilo\meter\per\second} in an instantaneous Earth-only comparison.

Deployment need not coincide with apogee. Coasting inward on the same long ellipse to \SI{150000}{\kilo\meter} altitude reduces the instantaneous injection to about \SI{212}{\meter\per\second}, leaving \SI{13.5}{\hour} to encounter. This differs from making that altitude the staging apogee. The shorter lead time limits both delivery thrust and scheduling adjustments; \autoref{sec:staging_escape_geometry} gives the comparison.

After detachment and clearance of the rocket, each functioning dry-mass package uses its adjustment thrusters for a reserved \SI{10}{\meter\per\second} prograde burn, directed along its Earth-relative velocity. Executed near periapsis, it raises the local escape margin to approximately \SI{15}{\meter\per\second} before losses. The corresponding hyperbolic excess speed rises from about \SI{332}{} to \SI{575}{\meter\per\second}. The reserve accelerates the detached package, not the delivered payload. Both packages need independent burn capability after tether severance if both are to receive this margin.

Atmospheric drag and separation consume that margin. The inbound drag acts on the intact PuffSat; the outbound drag acts on hardware with a different mass relative to its projected area. For an uncontrolled \SI{250}{\gram} remnant exposing \SI{100}{\square\centi\meter}, the reference atmosphere gives about \SI{0.12}{\meter\per\second} of outbound drag after release at periapsis. Raising the density at \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} to \SI{1e-9}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} gives about \SI{0.43}{\meter\per\second}.
