A plate is a material surface that takes the jet at its face. For a reflected jet of speed $v$, the energy it must survive per newton-second of impulse is $v/4$. Asking \SI{100}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of it rather than \SI{40}{\kilo\meter\per\second} therefore asks it to absorb \SI{25}{\kilo\joule} per newton-second rather than \SI{10}{\kilo\joule}. Standoff does not lift that ceiling, because it dilutes the heat and the impulse together. This is where Orion's plate stopped, at \SIrange{2000}{6000}{\second} of specific impulse, or \SIrange{20}{60}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{projorion}. Mini-Mag Orion reaches \SI{155}{\kilo\meter\per\second} from a comparable pulse by putting a field where the plate would be \cite{ewig2003minimag, lenard2007minimag}. \autoref{sec:dv_effective} needs \SI{100}{\kilo\meter\per\second} or more. The plate is the cheaper device and the slower one, and the near-Sun step is the place this architecture cannot afford slow.

This argument is about the kinetic pulse alone. \autoref{sec:epstein_drives} keeps Orion's shaped charges for the fusion target, where the source is meant to radiate and no coil sits beside it.

\subsection{Solid Projectiles Rather Than Gas Generating PuffSats}\label{sec:solid_PuffSats}
Given the extreme velocities at periapsis, we switch from low-density PuffSat-generated gases to solid projectiles. Despite the harsh conditions, the corona's low perturbation environment (see \autoref{sec:periapsis_challenges}) enables high precision. Like Proba-3, we target millimeter-scale precision for head-on hits on small solids. At these speeds, impact vaporizes and ionizes the solids, so the interaction must be modeled as a radiating plasma \cite{zeldovich_raizer}. Project Orion shows that opacity and radiation transport matter in pusher interactions \cite{balcomb1970nuclear}, but its nuclear-pulse geometry does not establish the optical depth or momentum coupling here. Those quantities depend on composition, areal density, geometry, and nonequilibrium radiation transport. The iron dopant discussed below is intended to increase optical depth, but a dedicated radiation-hydrodynamic calculation must determine the required fraction. Until then, low radiative loss and near-elastic coupling are design targets rather than established results.

We'd make these solid projectiles primarily from cheap low atomic number (low Z) materials like the carbon materials and ceramics that dominated the Parker Solar Probe's thermal protection system \cite{parker_thermal_protection}.   A small amount of cheap higher Z materials like iron would be included to ensure opacity to the blackbody X-rays produced.  
All the projectile's mass contributes to propulsion, irrespective of its composition.  As shown in \autoref{fig:parker_cross_section}, the projectiles can contain electronics, heat shielding, and some liquid that passively or actively cools the skin through a centrifugally driven convection cycle.    When close to the Sun, they can operate on batteries or compact heat engines, like MEMS-scale Stirling engines \cite{mems_stirling_engine}. These alternatives are good options where intense near-Sun sunlight can degrade traditional photovoltaics.

One possible low-cost projectile architecture might use graphite as both a structural backbone and thermal conductor, channeling heat toward shadowed radiators.  If the graphite can be cheaply made into insulating carbon foams, these foams will also reduce heat flux towards electronics.  The foams don't need to be optimized for low density like the Parker Solar Probe since all the projectile mass contributes to energy on impact. A bonding layer joins a thin ceramic skin designed to reflect incident solar flux. Ceramic or ablative heat shields protect electronics and propulsion systems housed in permanently shadowed zones. Given the low raw material costs of graphite and ceramics, large-scale manufacturing could drive per-kilogram projectile costs down to just a few dollars.

\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{images/Parker Projectile Cross Section.png}
    \caption{This cross-section shows a projectile with a highly reflective, heat-tolerant skin that is cooled by a centrifugally driven fluid. Heat is radiated away through a pipe that extends into the projectile's shadow, or at least to areas that receive sunlight obliquely. Batteries and electronics provide power.}
    \label{fig:parker_cross_section}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Navigation Challenges Near Periapsis}\label{sec:periapsis_challenges}
Close solar periapsis poses several challenges. Sensors using visible light must filter solar illumination, and instruments must withstand heat and solar wind. Relativistic effects are predictable but must enter the trajectory calculations, and the high collision speeds demand extremely accurate navigation. The pulsed propulsion chamber may ablate slightly on each pulse, and the projectiles must be miniaturized to at most a few kilograms to contain the immense energy released at these velocities. Solar-luminosity variability is another navigation input. Average radiation pressure can be included in the trajectory model, while variations from sunspots and other magnetic phenomena must be forecast or estimated in flight. Neural networks have been studied for predicting total and spectral solar irradiance from photospheric images \cite{muralikrishna2022solar}.

Despite the extreme environment, solar approaches to within the outer corona experience relatively mild total perturbations. The outer corona is tenuous \cite{corona_density}, far below the residual-atmosphere densities that drag low- and very-low Earth orbit satellites \cite{leo_orbit_perturbation_simulation}. Combined with the predictable gravitational environment near solar periapsis, this leaves the unpredictable share of drag and gravitational variation smaller in the outer corona than in low-Earth orbit.
  
Accelerating rockets gently over minutes would require a large time gap between successive PuffSats. That gap is set by orbital phasing and stays fixed around the orbit. The PuffSats sit farthest apart at periapsis, where they move fastest, and bunch together at larger radii, where they move slower. These two effects cancel, so the arrival cadence at the rocket is the same everywhere. Because periapsis speeds are so high, even a large and hard-to-maintain spacing yields only a brief gap between arrivals. Left at that spacing, the Earth-crossing impacts would land in too short a window for the minutes-long push a crewed vehicle needs. That cadence is not the binding constraint, though. We spread the stream during the long return coast to Earth, not at periapsis. Deploying the PuffSats about \SI{0.5}{\astronomicalunit} out and giving each a small self-steering $\Delta V$ makes them arrive over a five-minute window, about $\pm 2.5$ minutes. The furthest unit needs only about \SI{45}{\meter\per\second}, its \SI{22500}{\kilo\meter} half-window divided by the \SI{5e5}{\second} coast at \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, well within a microthruster's reach. The target's acceleration is then $a = \Delta V / T$, the delivered speed over the window length $T$. An \SI{8}{\kilo\meter\per\second} climb to low Earth orbit spread over the \SI{300}{\second} window is about 2.7 g, and an \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second} eccentric insertion about 3.7 g. Widening the window lowers g directly. Passengers held to the 2 g cap in the benign chest-to-back $Gx$ direction of \autoref{sec:pregnant_women} need the window widened to about $\pm 3.5$ minutes, bought with slightly more $\Delta V$ or a deployment farther out. Because the stream supplies the velocity change from outside, the payload carries little or no propellant, and a reusable ground launcher can give it the low-speed initial nudge. Orbital speed itself still comes from the PuffSat impacts, not from that launcher.   

A gentler staged path stays available for the most delicate payloads. Rather than push astronauts directly, we can use two sets of propulsion PuffSats. First, our Earth-crossing PuffSats from the Sun push an unmanned payload into an eccentric orbit around Earth. Then that eccentric payload deploys PuffSats to push the high-value cargo, people or satellites, into its final orbit just as we discussed in \autoref{sec:starship_safelaunch}. The staging roughly doubles the propellant, a small penalty at the \$80 per kilogram materials cost of \autoref{sec:methalox_rebuttal}.

\subsection{Initial Periapsis Orbits With Jupiter Gravity Assists} \label{sec:jupiter_gravity_initial}
A final issue is placing the initial payload in a retrograde low periapsis solar orbit.  The best approach for this is likely using a gravity assist from Jupiter, which was the original plan for the Parker Solar Probe mission \cite{mccomas2005solar}. 

To operate in deep space without relying on large solar arrays, the spacecraft may draw power from fuel cells or batteries. However, electrochemical systems are difficult to optimize under extreme cold. To mitigate thermal constraints, exothermic fuels can provide localized heating.  Alternatively, power can be generated via miniaturized chemically driven heat engines such as MEMS-scale gas turbines \cite{mems_gas_turbine}. If these energy sources use explosive reactants or yield volatile reactant products, these compounds can later be repurposed as PuffSat propellants. They then contribute to momentum transfer on impact. Near Jupiter, the PuffSats' volatiles would freeze and shield the spacecraft from radiation. The internal power source and shielding infrastructure would permit a close Jupiter flyby that maximizes our gravitational slingshot.

\subsubsection{A Meter Of Water Stops Neutrons, A Meter Of Argon Does Not}\label{sec:jovian_neutron_shield}
That shield has one gap worth naming. Jupiter's belts trap protons past \SI{50}{\mega\electronvolt}, and the field near the planet could in principle hold protons above \SI{100}{\giga\electronvolt} \cite{roussos2021jovian_belts}. A proton that energetic does not merely ionize the material it crosses. It breaks a nucleus apart and sprays neutrons, a reaction called spallation. The hard tail is thin, since measured Jovian proton spectra stop below about \SI{80}{\mega\electronvolt} and the modeled intensity falls steeply above \SIrange[range-phrase={--},range-units=single]{50}{100}{\mega\electronvolt} inward of Io \cite{nenon2018jovian_protons, roussos2021jovian_belts}. Electrons carry far more of the flux, and they make neutrons by a second route. A stopping electron radiates bremsstrahlung, the photons a charge emits as it decelerates in a nucleus's electric field. A photon above a nucleus's neutron separation energy, the work needed to pull one neutron out, knocks a neutron loose. That threshold is \SI{15.66}{\mega\electronvolt} for oxygen and \SI{9.87}{\mega\electronvolt} for argon-40, and Jupiter's inner-belt electrons run past \SI{50}{\mega\electronvolt} \cite{roussos2021jovian_belts}. Both channels are small against the ionizing dose. Neither is zero, and no magnetic field steers a neutron.

Water handles them on the strength of its hydrogen. A neutron gives up the most energy when it hits a nucleus of its own mass. The bookkeeping quantity is $\xi$, the average drop in the logarithm of the neutron's energy per collision. It is 0.92 for water, 0.12 for oxygen alone, and 0.049 for argon \cite{lamarsh2018nuclear}. Falling from \SI{2}{\mega\electronvolt} to a thermal \SI{0.025}{\electronvolt} is a drop of $\ln(2\times10^{6}/0.025) = 18.2$ in that logarithm, so it takes $18.2/0.92 \approx 20$ collisions in water against $18.2/0.049 \approx 370$ in argon. Oxygen on its own would need 150. The hydrogen is doing the work.

A meter of argon does not make up the difference by being thick. At \SI{1.4}{\gram\per\cubic\centi\meter} and a scattering cross-section near \SI{2.5}{\barn}, the mean free path is \SI{19}{\centi\meter}. A 370-step random walk of that stride wanders $\sqrt{370}\times19 = \SI{3.6}{\meter}$ from where it started before the neutron is slow, and one meter is a third of that. Seeding the argon with boron does not rescue it either, because boron catches neutrons rather than slowing them. Boron-10's capture cross-section is \SI{3840}{\barn} at thermal energy and falls as $1/v$ with neutron speed, which leaves it at \SI{0.61}{\barn} at \SI{1}{\mega\electronvolt}. Against argon's \SI{2.5}{\barn} of scattering, a fast neutron bounces off argon four times for every chance boron gets to take it, and each bounce costs it almost nothing. Argon does knock down the hard end of the spectrum, since argon-40's first excited state sits at \SI{1.46}{\mega\electronvolt} and inelastic scattering degrades anything above it quickly. What survives is a \SIrange{0.1}{1}{\mega\electronvolt} population that diffuses through the remaining meter almost intact.

In water the boron is close to free and worth taking, for a reason that is about photons rather than neutrons. Ordinary water already captures its own thermal neutrons on hydrogen. That reaction, $^{1}\mathrm{H}(n,\gamma)^{2}\mathrm{H}$, emits a \SI{2.22}{\mega\electronvolt} gamma ray, which is penetrating, and it emits it deep in the shield where little water is left to absorb it. Boron-10 captures instead as $^{10}\mathrm{B}(n,\alpha)^{7}\mathrm{Li}$, putting \SI{2.3}{\mega\electronvolt} into an alpha particle and a lithium nucleus that both stop within microns, and emitting only a \SI{478}{\kilo\electronvolt} gamma \SI{94}{\percent} of the time. Boron also shortens the distance a thermal neutron wanders before capture from a few centimeters to about one, which moves the emission outward where the remaining water still attenuates it. The dose of boron is small. Matching hydrogen's thermal capture rate needs a macroscopic cross-section $\Sigma_{\mathrm{B}} = \Sigma_{\mathrm{H}} = \SI{0.022}{\per\centi\meter}$, and natural boron at \SI{764}{\barn} supplies that at \SI{0.52}{\gram\per\liter}. Ten times that concentration makes boron the dominant capture path at \SI{5.2}{\gram\per\liter}, which is \SI{6.7}{\gram\per\liter} of boron carbide, or \SI{0.5}{\percent} of the shield's mass.

Use the carbide powder rather than dissolved boric acid. Ice rejects solutes as it grows, so boric acid stirred into water that later freezes segregates into brine pockets and leaves the bulk of the shield unborated. Boric acid's solubility also falls to about \SI{27}{\gram\per\liter} at \SI{0}{\celsius}, just under the concentration wanted. Boron carbide is insoluble to begin with, so freezing does nothing to it, and at half a percent of shield mass it is invisible to the plume the PuffSat later becomes.

An argon cycle that wants the same protection buys it with a liner instead of a dosing. Roughly \SIrange{20}{30}{\centi\meter} of borated polyethylene inside the argon supplies the hydrogen the argon lacks. Polyethylene is \SI{14}{\percent} hydrogen by mass, and the attenuation length for a degraded fast spectrum in it is around \SI{10}{\centi\meter}, so \SI{30}{\centi\meter} is a factor of roughly 20. That liner is \SIrange{19}{29}{\gram\per\square\centi\meter} against argon's \SI{140}{\gram\per\square\centi\meter}, about a fifth more shield mass. Medium-Z argon outside stops the electrons and much of the bremsstrahlung they throw off. The hydrogenous liner inside slows what neutrons get through, so its boron can capture them without the \SI{2.22}{\mega\electronvolt} exit photon. That ordering is not the best one for photons. Shielding trades for Jupiter missions put low-Z outside and high-Z inside, so the low-Z layer stops electrons while making less bremsstrahlung and the high-Z layer absorbs what does get made \cite{cherng2007jovian_shielding}. A liner buried under the propellant has that order reversed. The penalty is small. The argon's own \SI{140}{\gram\per\square\centi\meter} already absorbs most of the bremsstrahlung it generates before the liner is reached, and the liner is carried for neutrons rather than for photons.

Two caveats sit outside this arithmetic. The flyby is near-equatorial, because the bend that places the dive is a bend in Jupiter's orbital plane and Jupiter's obliquity is only \ang{3.1}. Juno flies a polar orbit at a similar \SI{4000}{\kilo\meter} perijove precisely to pass under the equatorial belts \cite{bolton2017juno}. That option is not open to a trajectory whose whole purpose is the in-plane turn. The dose the electronics actually see is therefore set by electrons and their bremsstrahlung, not by the neutrons priced here. The second caveat is that a full dose budget for the pass has not been computed. What this subsubsection settles is only which shield material can take the neutron job and what the boron costs.


These alternatives are not the only option. Large solar arrays have already powered craft at Jupiter. NASA's Juno spacecraft reached Jupiter on three \SI{9}{\meter} arrays and has operated there on about \SI{500}{\watt} since 2016, the first spacecraft to run on sunlight at Jupiter's distance \cite{bolton2017juno, juno_solar_record}. Concentrating optics, mirrors or lenses that gather sunlight from a wide aperture onto a small cell, can shrink that array or warm the electronics directly, much as we propose for the Ceres power depots in \autoref{sec:ceresly_good}.

Optionally we can push more mass into the initial orbit by using a Jupiter gravity assist to place propulsion PuffSats into a retrograde Hohmann trajectory back to Earth. We can add a burn at Jupiter to use its Oberth effect.   They would cross Earth at around \SI{69}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, which could push a larger terrestrial payload onto  prograde and retrograde colliding trajectories at low solar periapsis.

\subsection{Jupiter-Only Exponential Launch Growth}\label{sec:jupiter_only_growth}
A simpler way to bootstrap an exponential launch cycle is to use the retrograde Hohmann trajectory from Jupiter described in \autoref{sec:jupiter_gravity_initial} but without close solar approaches.   With an unaugmented pusher plate, PuffSats returning from Jupiter can push about 8 times their weight into a highly eccentric orbit around Earth.   A small conventional burn can return this payload to Jupiter and restart the cycle.     The growth is slow compared to other proposals but may provide sufficient launch capacity to supply the satellite market within a few decades.  A trajectory search in the companion calculations repository, scored on how quickly the launched mass doubles, found the best cycle is also the simplest. A direct burn from Earth with an unpowered Jupiter flyby (no burn at Jupiter) beats every gravity-assist chain through the inner planets. Its Earth-phased return closes at about \SI{60}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and doubles the launched mass every 4.0 years once the small apoapsis burn that turns the retrograde push into a prograde departure is charged \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Catching the returning PuffSats head-on in a pulsed propulsion chamber instead, the prograde--retrograde trick of \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}, cuts that chemical charge to 6\% of the departing mass and shortens the doubling to about 3.0 years \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Growing faster than that asks the departing batch to split at Jupiter. The \textbf{growth wave} arrives early and pushes the next payload into its parking orbit, and the \textbf{departure wave} arrives one parking orbit later to supply the projectiles that launch it. Pulling the growth wave that far ahead of the departure wave costs a \textbf{separation burn}. An earlier model treated the planets as moving on circles in one plane, and in that model the split had to be bought with a burn at Jupiter. Rescoring the chain on real ephemerides retires that charge. A real ephemeris lets the transfer choose when it meets Jupiter, a second steering knob the circular model does not have. Eight of the eleven flown cycles then buy their ten-day head start for under \SI{1}{\meter\per\second}, a chain mean of \SI{0.179}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The other three are expensive, and \autoref{sec:split_tail} works out why the chain pays them rather than dodging them. The chain then doubles the launched mass every 1.74 years when the nozzle delivers 60\% of its ideal impulse, and every 1.45 years at 80\% \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. That efficiency is the parameter everything else rides on, and \autoref{sec:mass_interest} prices what it is worth. \autoref{tab:doubling_ladder} sets that pair beside every other doubling time this subsection quotes, because they are not all measurements of the same thing.

\subsubsection{The Expensive Splits Are Worth Paying, And Low Thrust Cannot Pay Them}\label{sec:split_tail}
The separation burn is cheap on eight cycles and not on the other three, and there is nothing in between. The eight pay under \SI{0.3}{\meter\per\second}. The three pay \SI{398.5}{\meter\per\second}, \SI{556.0}{\meter\per\second} and \SI{1011.3}{\meter\per\second}, departing in February 2042, February 2030 and September 2036. The flyby is the reason rather than the schedule. All three have their growth wave already at a perijove radius of \num{1.056} Jupiter radii, which is exactly the \SI{4000}{\kilo\meter} altitude floor, and it still cannot turn far enough. What is left over is an angle rather than a speed mismatch, so on two of the three no change of flyby helps at all. Adding the departure wave's deep-space maneuver, meaning a burn made in mid-coast rather than at a planet, the worst cycle's whole correction is \SI{1029.8}{\meter\per\second}, which costs 26\% of the wave as methalox \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

Skipping those cycles is the obvious move, and it loses. The rule the chain flies takes the two-synodic return whenever the departure wave's deep-space maneuver stays under \SI{50}{\meter\per\second}, and falls back to three synodic periods otherwise. A stricter rule that also refuses a two-synodic return when the growth wave would then face an expensive separation does remove the tail, holding every correction in the chain under \SI{1.51}{\meter\per\second}. It doubles the launched mass every \SI{1.863}{\year} against \SI{1.737}{\year}, so avoiding the tail costs 7.3\% of the clock. Two things do that. Each fallback spends \SI{1.092}{\year} of extra clock, so the stricter rule fits ten cycles into the same \SI{28.39}{\year} where the flown rule fits eleven, and a cycle is worth about \num{2.9} times the mass. And three-synodic returns arrive colder, at \SIrange{56.53}{57.43}{\kilo\meter\per\second} against \SIrange{61.83}{65.13}{\kilo\meter\per\second} (\autoref{sec:two_leg_nozzle}), so the departure nozzle weakens as well \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

Buying the corrections with something other than methalox is the other way out. Argon solar-electric propulsion at \SI{2000}{\second} of specific impulse has an exhaust speed of \SI{19.61}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, \num{5.26} times methalox's \SI{3.73}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. On the propellant ledger alone that turns the worst cycle's bill from 26\% of the wave into about 6\%.

The thrust is what fails. A low-thrust stage of mass $m$ drawing $P$ watts at thruster efficiency $\eta$ accelerates at $a = 2\eta P/(m v_e)$, so at $\eta = 0.5$ the acceleration is just specific power divided by exhaust speed. One watt per kilogram of wave therefore buys \SI{5.1e-5}{\meter\per\second\squared} at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}. Integrating what an array of a given size can actually deliver along the real transfer legs, the worst cycle's \SI{1011}{\meter\per\second} of separation needs \SI{8.02e-5}{\meter\per\second\squared} there, which is \SI{1.57}{\kilo\watt} for every tonne of wave. On the \SI{500}{\tonne} reference wave that is \SI{0.79}{\mega\watt} of array, \SI{40}{\newton} of thrust near Earth and \SI{1.5}{\newton} at Jupiter, where the inverse square has taken 96\% of the output. A flight-class \SI{12.5}{\kilo\watt} Hall thruster makes about \SI{0.64}{\newton}, so this is on the order of 60 of them \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Building a smaller wave does not rescue it. Buying a fixed \(\Delta V\) in a fixed time takes power in proportion to the mass being pushed, and array mass follows power, so the array is the same fraction of a \SI{5}{\tonne} wave as of a \SI{500}{\tonne} one. At \SI{15}{\kilo\gram\per\kilo\watt} of array, power conditioning and thrusters, that fraction is 2.4\%.

Which cadence rule wins turns on the propellant, and that is the part worth carrying. \autoref{tab:cadence_propellant} prices all three both ways. On methalox the rule that never falls back is the worst by a wide margin, because its thirteen cycles carry corrections up to \SI{5.04}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and only a fifth of the wave survives one. On argon it is the best. The \SI{50}{\meter\per\second} fallback threshold exists only because methalox cannot afford a kilometer-per-second correction, so changing the currency turns the fallback from insurance into lost clock. Charging the array as mass takes most of that back. The never-fall-back rule needs \SI{7.7}{\mega\watt}, which is 15 to 31\% of the wave depending on array specific mass, and by \SI{15}{\kilo\gram\per\kilo\watt} it has lost the lead again.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{The three cadence rules priced on their correction burns \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The 2S and 3S columns count how many of each chain's returns close in two and in three Earth--Jupiter synodic periods, all three chains spanning the same \SI{28.39}{\year}. Doubling is years to double the launched mass, at a nozzle recovery of 0.6 and a growth-push elasticity of 0.8. The last column charges the growth wave's array as mass at \SI{15}{\kilo\gram\per\kilo\watt} and still leaves the departure wave's array free. Reproduce with \texttt{make sep-split}.}
    \label{tab:cadence_propellant}
    \begin{tabular}{|l|r|r|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Cadence rule} & \textbf{2S} & \textbf{3S} & \textbf{Methalox} & \textbf{Argon} & \textbf{Argon + array} \\\hline
        Flown (fall back on the departure wave) & 7 & 4 & 1.737 & 1.690 & 1.719 \\\hline
        Stricter (fall back on either wave) & 4 & 6 & 1.863 & 1.863 & 1.863 \\\hline
        Never fall back & 13 & 0 & 2.270 & 1.501 & 1.859 \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

So the chain flies the impulsive methalox correction and keeps the cadence rule it has, absorbing the three expensive cycles rather than avoiding them. Charged for its own array, argon is worth 1.1\% in doubling time, and even that figure charges only the growth wave's array. The departure wave's array is free everywhere in the table, so 1.1\% is an upper bound on a margin already too thin to buy hardware with. The obstacle is the array's power rather than its mass. Under 3\% of a wave is an affordable array. A \SI{0.79}{\mega\watt} solar array at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, built and destroyed with the wave every \SI{2.18}{\year}, is a manufacturing claim rather than a trajectory one, and nothing here settles it. If that power level proves reachable, the electric option is worth reopening.

Two things this calculation does not do. It compares a budget of thrust-seconds against an impulsive burn rather than solving the low-thrust trajectory, and a push spread over a year does not in general move the endpoint as far as one kick placed well. A finite-thrust optimization could shift the requirement either way, and it is the one piece of work that could overturn the verdict. Separately, no ledger charges the difference between what the two waves' departures require. Both leave Earth at the same instant but need different periapsis speeds, and that gap runs \SIrange{224}{559}{\meter\per\second} at a twenty-day split and about half that at the ten days flown here. It is not carried propellant, since the departure is the head-on nozzle, so it lands as extra slug on half the batch \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

Simpler is worth an argument, because the hard part of the near-Sun cycle is not the Sun. Parker showed that a shield survives a close pass, and \autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal} sizes one for a deeper dive. The hard part is the heat the collision itself makes. Closing speed at 4 solar radii is \SI{618}{\kilo\meter\per\second} against this cycle's \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so watering the plume down the same way (\autoref{sec:watering_it_down}) still leaves it at least ten times hotter than the \SI{15000}{\kelvin} worked there. The extra ionization helps the field grip the plasma, but blackbody flux goes as $T^4$, so the optical flash the liner and the coils have to survive rises by some four orders of magnitude. Near the Sun the retrograde projectiles must also shrink toward the hundred-gram end of the \SIrange{0.1}{2.5}{\kilo\gram} band of \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}, or the nozzle runs to hundreds of tonnes, and a hundred-gram body is the hardest place to put a heat shield. The \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} PuffSat coming back from Jupiter carries no shield at all. None of these look unsolvable. They are just harder than their Jupiter counterparts.

We can also combine this Jupiter launch cycle with reusable rockets or the lunar ice based cycle discussed in \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}.  By increasing the number of initial PuffSat launches toward Jupiter, we can more rapidly scale up our launch throughput. Once throughput is up, Earth-based payloads can ride the returning PuffSats to orbit.

Waves arrive 2.18 or 3.28 years apart, so most of the fleet is idle most of the time, and the idle inventory has to be parked. Empty space near Earth will not hold it. Two million kilometers out, the solar tide pulls a body away from Earth at $3n^2 d$, where $n$ is the Sun's angular rate of \SI{1.99e-7}{\radian\per\second} and $d$ is the distance from Earth. That is \SI{0.238}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared}. Earth pulls back with $\mu/d^2$, only \SI{0.100}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared}. Holding station against the \SI{0.138}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared} difference costs \SI{4.4}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of solar electric propulsion every year, which no mass fraction supports. The Earth--Moon libration points are the places where those two pulls already cancel. Station-keeping there corrects a slow drift away from an equilibrium instead of carrying a load, and a halo orbit about Earth--Moon L1 or L2 holds for 5 to \SI{15}{\meter\per\second} a year. Those points also sit \SI{326000}{\kilo\meter} and \SI{449000}{\kilo\meter} from Earth, far outside the outer Van Allen belt, which fades out near \SI{60000}{\kilo\meter}. The alternative is to leave the fleet on the \SI{150000}{\kilo\meter} ellipse of \autoref{sec:puffsat_control_simulation}. Its period is 2.68 days, so a parked body crosses the belts 594 times waiting out a two-synodic gap and 894 times waiting out a three-synodic one, spending 15\% of the wait inside \SI{60000}{\kilo\meter}. A libration park pays two crossings per cycle, one out and one back.

Leaving that park is cheap, and the Moon does most of the work. A body falling from Earth--Moon L2 to a \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} perigee arrives at \SI{10.93}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, only \SI{80}{\meter\per\second} short of the \SI{11.01}{\kilo\meter\per\second} escape speed at that altitude. The fall alone therefore delivers it just under escape, the regime \autoref{sec:handling_space_debris} wants, and small thrusters finish the job. Inclination is the knob a customer cares about, and a lunar flyby supplies it rather than the tank. A body coasting out to lunar distance on a near-escape ellipse moves about \SI{187}{\meter\per\second}, nearly straight outward. The Moon crosses its path at \SI{1022}{\meter\per\second}, so the two meet at \SI{1.04}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. A flyby turns that relative vector through an angle $\delta$ given by $\sin(\delta/2) = 1/(1 + r_p v_\infty^2 / \mu_{\mathrm{Moon}})$, which at a \SI{100}{\kilo\meter} flyby altitude is \ang{93}. Turning a \SI{1.04}{\kilo\meter\per\second} vector through \ang{93} is worth \SI{1.5}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of propulsive plane change, and the Moon does it for nothing. Solar electric engines then trim what the flyby cannot place exactly.

The same arithmetic runs backward and brings the expensive hardware home. A body crossing perigee at escape speed is \SI{93}{\meter\per\second} of retrograde burn away from an ellipse whose apogee reaches the Moon. That burn has to be spent at perigee, where the craft is moving fastest and a given impulse buys the most energy. Spent late instead, it is nearly useless. A body already \SI{1}{\mega\meter} out carrying even \SI{472}{\meter\per\second} of hyperbolic excess speed, the speed it would still have infinitely far from Earth, is on a twenty-year loop back. Spent at perigee, the burn caps the apogee at lunar distance, a second flyby bends the arrival into the halo, and solar electric engines close the insertion. This is not a paper maneuver. NASA's two ARTEMIS spacecraft reached Earth--Moon L1 and L2 libration orbits by this route, moving out through the Sun--Earth weak stability region and back on lunar gravity assists, on a fuel budget that could not have bought the transfer directly \cite{folta2012artemis}. That is a way home for the parts worth recovering. The co-flyer of \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture} carries a large aperture and a transponder stable to nanoseconds, and its binding quantity is an angular precision near \SI{1.6}{\micro\radian}. Radiation dose attacks exactly that, raising detector dark current and leaving hot pixels in the star field the co-flyer centroids against. Recovering it to a libration park between waves avoids 592 of the 594 belt crossings. This is an option for the slow buildup cycle, where the hardware idles for years between waves. The high-cadence launch business of \autoref{sec:starship_safelaunch} cannot take it, because its co-flyer is wanted at perigee on every stream and never idles long enough to be worth moving. There the dose is a cost to be shielded against rather than avoided.

\subsubsection{The Flown Cadence Already Locks, And Padding Buys Heat Rather Than Time}\label{sec:synodic_lock}
A cycle that repeats has to hand its successor the geometry it was handed itself. What decides that is the \textbf{departure phase}, meaning where Earth and Jupiter stand relative to each other when the payload leaves. It is measured as a fraction of the \SI{1.0920}{\year} synodic period, so phase 0 and phase 1 are the same picture. When flight plus coast sums to a whole number of synodic periods, the cycle comes back to its own departure phase and repeats with no steering. Call that a \textbf{synodic lock}. A cycle closing at \num{2.09} synodic periods instead hands the next payload a phase nine hundredths of a period worse. The one after that is worse again, until the chain has drifted into departures nothing can afford.

That drift is a real failure mode, and this chain does not have it. All eleven flown cycles close on an exact synodic multiple, seven at \SI{2.1841}{\year} and four at \SI{3.2761}{\year}, with a departure-phase drift under \num{1e-4} of a synodic period per repetition \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The adaptive cadence rule of \autoref{sec:split_tail} builds every return on an exact multiple to begin with, so the chain is locked by construction. There is no remainder left to pad away.

That also settles what the four three-synodic returns are, and they are not the chain paying down a phasing debt. They are the perijove floor. On those cycles the growth wave is already at \num{1.056} Jupiter radii and still cannot turn far enough, so the cycle takes the longer clock instead. Audited against real Earth--Jupiter geometry over two hundred years, only 45 of 91 two-synodic windows clear the \SI{4000}{\kilo\meter} floor \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. No padding scheme reaches that, because the obstacle is how hard Jupiter can pull rather than where Earth is standing.

A circular coplanar model of the two planets does produce a two-synodic lock, and it is a trajectory this paper already has rather than a new one. It leaves at departure phase \num{0.8082} and flies \num{1.95} synodic periods, parking the remaining \SI{20.06}{\day}. It arrives at \SI{63.35}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and departs on a burn of \SI{8.613}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The flown two-synodic cycles arrive between \SIrange[range-phrase={ and }]{61.83}{65.13}{\kilo\meter\per\second} after burns of \SIrange{6.84}{7.17}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so the arrival lands inside that family and the burn sits about 20\% above its dearest member. That gap is what the circular model costs. It cannot choose when the transfer meets Jupiter, which is the second steering knob real ephemerides supply and the same one that makes the split above nearly free. Two different models, so this is a family resemblance rather than an identity.

The park has a floor, and the floor is structural rather than a tolerance. Every cycle already ends with one full period of the bound near-escape orbit the returning wave pushes the payload into. The push lands at one periapsis and the departure burn lights at the next, which is the prograde--retrograde trick of \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}. Parking lengthens that orbit and cannot shorten it, so no lock parks for less than the \SI{20}{\day} cycle orbit. A nine-day park scores a doubling 3.5\% faster and is not a trajectory \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

A single-cycle optimum can still fail once a chain has to keep flying it, so the locks were run through a chain search rather than argued. The search ranges over every repeating policy on the departure-phase graph, with the park free to take any length. At each exhaust speed the best long-run rate is a single cycle that loops back to its own phase \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. A one-cycle loop is a synodic lock, so the single-cycle answer survives the lookahead here.

What padding does buy is a hotter arrival on an unchanged clock. Fly the outbound leg shorter and faster than the window allows, then hold the payload in the cycle orbit for the remainder. Call that \textbf{fly-and-park}, which is not the idle-fleet parking above. That one stores hardware at a libration point between cycles; this one lengthens a single cycle's coast. A hotter arrival pushes harder, and an exchange rate says what the heat may cost. Against a \SI{53.5}{\kilo\meter\per\second} baseline, arriving at \SI{68}{\kilo\meter\per\second} instead is worth \SI{0.99}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of extra departure burn on methalox and \SI{3.13}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at \SI{1200}{\second} of specific impulse. The maneuver costs a median \SI{1.04}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so methalox misses by about 5\% while a stream-driven departure clears it three times over \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Count the departure phases that offer an unpadded three-synodic cycle, and padding wins at 1 of 11 on methalox, 16 of 30 at \SI{1200}{\second} and 25 of 30 at \SI{2214}{\second}. The best gain is \num{1.27} in returned mass.

The same sweep answers a scheduling question the cadence rule does not. A \textbf{usable phase} is a departure phase from which some closing cycle actually grows the payload. The usable set widens with exhaust speed, from 13 of 73 sampled phases on methalox to all 73 above \SI{1900}{\second}. What a fraction cannot say is whether those phases form one window or several, and that is the difference between a launch campaign that waits once and one that waits repeatedly. They form a single contiguous arc at every exhaust speed tested. It widens about the \textbf{sweet phase}, the departure phase where reaching Jupiter is cheapest, which sits at \num{0.726}. There the cheapest available burn is \SI{4.41}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, against \SI{38.49}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at the worst phase, a factor of \num{8.7} that comes only from where Jupiter is standing \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Measured cyclically around the circle, the window runs \SI{71}{\day} of the \SI{399}{\day} synodic period on methalox and \SI{240}{\day} at \SI{1200}{\second}. So the launch-cadence result is one window \num{3.4} times wider, not a scattering of them.

Locking at two synodic periods rather than three is a second operating point and not an upgrade. It doubles about \num{1.4} times faster, and it pays for that in launch window. At \SI{2214}{\second} a two-synodic lock exists at 26 of 73 phases where a three-synodic one exists at all 73, and the asymmetry survives a full change of exhaust speed. The fast option at \SI{2214}{\second} is still narrower than the slow option at \SI{1200}{\second}, which reaches 44 of 73. On methalox nothing pads to two synodic periods at all, and three synodic periods is reachable from 12 phases of 73 to double in \SI{3.641}{\year} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The chain search declines the two-synodic point there for a plain reason. Its \SI{8.613}{\kilo\meter\per\second} departure burn on a \SI{3.727}{\kilo\meter\per\second} exhaust delivers 9.9\% of the vehicle. So the choice between the two locks is one an operator makes with both columns in view, and the window is not a convenience to be traded away quietly. It is the reason the architecture does not need every departure to leave at once.

\autoref{tab:doubling_ladder} collects every doubling time this subsection quotes, because they come off four different calculations and the last four rows are not comparable with the rest. Those four score one locking cycle at its best departure phase on the circular model, and they charge nothing for delivering the projectile stream. The two-wave chain rows fly eleven cycles on real ephemerides and pay for that stream explicitly, splitting 20 to 29\% of each batch onto the nozzle bend, mass that mints no payload \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The lock rows are therefore optimistic against the chain rows by an amount nobody has computed, in a direction that is known. The fastest figure in the table is \SI{0.873}{\year}. The fastest figure defensible today is \SI{1.450}{\year}, because it is the only one flown on real geometry with its projectiles paid for.

Those efficiencies need reading carefully, because this paper carries three different parameters that all take values near 0.6 and 0.8. The chain rows sweep the \textbf{nozzle impulse recovery}, the fraction of its ideal impulse the nozzle actually delivers, which derates the whole push from outside the momentum ledger. The elasticity $f$ of \autoref{eq:PuffSat_ratio} is a second parameter, held at \num{0.8} on every row of the table including the lock rows. The jet energy efficiency inside the square root of \autoref{eq:ve_general} is a third, the one \autoref{sec:depth_cost} sweeps, and the mapping between it and the recovery has not been worked. Where this subsection says the nozzle delivers 60\% or 80\%, it means the recovery.

\subsubsection{Mass Interest Is Paid In Kilograms, And The Early Buyer Wins}\label{sec:mass_interest}
Someone has to pay for the first few cycles, and here is a way to ask. An investor does not buy a launch slot. They buy a mass interest in the fleet, an ownership stake denominated in kilograms rather than dollars, and those kilograms stay in the loop and multiply. The word carries its two ordinary meanings at once. An interest is a fractional claim on an asset, the way an oil well is owned in fractional shares, and interest is what a holding earns by being left alone. The operator finances the first Starship loads of PuffSats toward Jupiter against those subscriptions, and the growing fleet in transit is the collateral. A bank account pays a few percent a year. A kilogram in this fleet is worth about half again what it was worth last year. A kilogram returning from Jupiter pushes about eight times its own weight into a high Earth orbit, and that is where the growth comes from. The hardware this funds is not exotic in scale. The outbound leg's pulse is small enough that its magnetic nozzle masses tens of tonnes rather than hundreds. The seed vehicle is therefore a ship a single Starship can loft (\autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}).

What sets the rate of return is how well that nozzle works. A PuffSat arrives head-on at about \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and vaporizes a slug of cheap matter carried aboard the departing craft. A magnetic field then turns the fireball into a jet out the back. The share of the ideal collimated impulse that the real device delivers is the jet efficiency $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ of \autoref{sec:jet_efficiency}, and it is the product of two factors known to very different standards. The chemical factor $\eta_{\mathrm{chem}}$ is fixed by \autoref{eq:eta_chem}, because the plume freezes before it returns the energy that pulled the slug's water apart. It runs from 0.78 to 0.91 across the rows below that deliver any growth at all. The geometric factor $\eta_{\mathrm{geom}}$ collects plume divergence, exhaust-speed spread, radiative escape, and mass the field fails to grip. The table sweeps this combined factor. \autoref{sec:jet_efficiency} gives modeled expansion efficiencies and distinguishes them from the full impulse factor used here. The fudge factor $f$ of \autoref{sec:mass_fraction} is a third quantity again, and it says how elastically gas rebounds off a solid plate.

One nozzle could in principle fly both legs of this cycle, rather than a pusher plate on the Earth boost and a nozzle on the Jupiter departure. \autoref{sec:two_leg_nozzle} prices that option and finds it the better one over most of the range. \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} settles first what the slug that either device vaporizes should be made of.

\autoref{tab:mass_interest_growth} scores the whole chain against $\eta_{\mathrm{geom}}$ and $f$ together. Each entry is the multiple by which the mass launched off Earth grows across the eleven cycles the chain actually flies, from November 2026 to April 2055. Both parameters matter and neither should be quoted without the other.

The flown jet efficiency is $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = \eta_{\mathrm{chem}}\eta_{\mathrm{geom}}$. The chemical factor is not swept; \autoref{eq:eta_chem} fixes it between 0.78 and 0.91 across the rows that grow, falling as the solver raises the slug ratio.         0.30 & 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 \\\hline

        0.50 & \num{2.6e-4} & \num{1.1e-3} & \num{3.8e-3} & 0.010 \\\hline
        0.60 & 1.5 & 6.7 & 22 & 61 \\\hline
        0.70 & 108 & 486 & \num{1664} & \num{4674} \\\hline
        0.80 & \num{1320} & \num{6148} & \num{2.17e4} & \num{6.29e4} \\\hline
        0.90 & \num{6787} & \num{3.26e4} & \num{1.19e5} & \num{3.55e5} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Read the last column first. At $f = 0.8$ a nozzle whose geometry returns 60\% of what the chemistry leaves grows the fleet 15.6\% a year, and one returning 90\% grows it 56.9\% a year. Anyone who has compared a 3\% savings account against a 6\% one over thirty years knows that tripling the rate does not triple the money. Here it is \num{5827} times the mass, 61 against \num{3.55e5}. Three tenths of a point of nozzle geometry is worth nearly four orders of magnitude of delivered payload. No financing structure does that. Only the hardware does.

The top of the table is why this is an investment rather than a gift, and the two zero rows are the sharpest part of it. Forward thrust vanishes at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = \sqrt{m_{rp}}$ (\autoref{sec:jet_efficiency}), which on this head-on leg is $1/\sqrt{1+k}$. At $\eta_{\mathrm{geom}} = 0.25$ and $0.30$ the product $\eta_{\mathrm{chem}}\eta_{\mathrm{geom}}$ sits under that floor, so the nozzle pushes the craft backward and the chain returns nothing whatever. Diluting does not buy a way out. Carrying more slug lowers the floor as $1/\sqrt{1+k}$, but \autoref{eq:eta_chem} takes $\eta_{\mathrm{chem}}$ down faster, and no slug ratio anywhere in the ignition window of \autoref{sec:two_leg_nozzle} closes the gap on the coldest cycle. The best any ratio manages at $\eta_{\mathrm{geom}} = 0.30$ still falls \num{0.0069} short. The rows below that do run forward and still lose mass, spending more to reach Jupiter than they bring home. You can be underwater on a mass interest, and the top half of this table shows what that looks like.

None of this can be settled on a test range, which is the reason early cycles are worth buying. The energy is not the obstacle. Seventy kilograms moving at \SI{70}{\kilo\meter\per\second} carries \SI{171}{\giga\joule}, about 41 tonnes of TNT, a large but ordinary test. Delivering that energy is the obstacle. Reaching the speed inside a launcher of length $L$ demands an acceleration of $v^2/2L$. Even a \SI{10}{\kilo\meter} barrel subjects the projectile to \num{25000}~g. A guided round carrying a slug does not survive that. The air is a second obstacle. At \SI{70}{\kilo\meter\per\second} sea-level air presses on the nose at roughly \SI{6}{\giga\pascal}. The entire flight path therefore has to be evacuated. That means the Straw Way of \autoref{sec:straw_way_to_heaven}, infrastructure this cycle is supposed to pay for rather than presuppose. Shrinking the pulse to something a laboratory can make does not rescue the test either. \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} shows that a smaller fireball cools and radiates faster, so a scaled-down shot returns a geometry that does not carry over to flight. The sub-scale literature makes the point better than we can. One group has spent two decades firing lasers into magnetic nozzles and setting the results beside their own codes, at energies below a joule where every initial condition is known. The comparisons disagree by factors of three to seven, and they disagree in both directions. Their solenoid returned \SIlist{17.0;32.1;45.9;73.3}{\micro\newton\second} across four field strengths against a simulated \SIlist{1.01;4.77;7.68;10.7}{\micro\newton\second}, a gap their own text calls about sevenfold \cite{inatomi2023quantitative}. Their axial cage ran the other way, with the code above the bench by three to seven \cite{schilling2024subscale}. A third study measured the rupture threshold five times stricter than the analytic value \cite{kawashima2016structure}. A fourth found momentum efficiency leaving the power law its own two exponents predict, rising as the \num{1.2} power of laser energy against an initial momentum going as the \num{0.85} \cite{ogawa2026characterization}. Two pendulum methods on one nozzle do not agree with each other \cite{koba2025measurement}. Nobody can yet predict a magnetic nozzle's impulse to better than a factor of several on a bench, with the target in front of them. Carrying that across the \num{1.3e11} between a half-joule shot and our \SI{62.9}{\giga\joule} pulse is not a calculation. The only instrument that measures the flight value of $\eta_{\mathrm{geom}}$ is a flight.

Being early therefore beats being rich. The chain flies eleven cycles in 28.4 years, spaced 2.18 and 3.28 years apart, because that is how long Jupiter takes to return to a usable position \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Each cycle returns one measurement of $\eta_{\mathrm{geom}}$ and permits one redesign of the nozzle, so eleven redesigns is the entire budget. A competitor with ten times the capital still waits 2.18 years for their next data point. A rival starting five years late is two redesigns behind and stays two redesigns behind. That is the part of this business that money cannot buy.

One condition sits under every number in the table. The parked payload splits into the craft that departs and the slug that craft will vaporize, and both go back to Jupiter. A kilogram taken as delivered cargo is therefore a kilogram not reinvested, and the fleet grows at its own rate less whatever holders withdraw. The multiples above assume nobody withdraws. A working business compounds first and harvests later, so the valuable claim is the one with the most cycles still ahead of it. That is the sense in which the early buyer wins.

\subsubsection{Watering It Down, Literally}\label{sec:watering_it_down}
A magnetic nozzle does not run cold. Two things heat it. The first is the current the plume itself carries. A hot plasma pushes the magnetic field out of its own interior, and it does that by running a sheet of current around its edge. That current runs through a plasma of finite conductivity, so it dissipates, and the field slips through the plume rather than gripping it perfectly. The field leaks out about eight times more slowly than the plume expands, which is good enough to steer with and far from lossless. The pulsed field also drives currents in the coils themselves, and a superconducting coil has to have that heat pumped back out at cryogenic temperature. The second source is light. A plume opaque enough to trap its own radiation glows like a blackbody, and at \SI{15000}{\kelvin} a surface radiates \SI{2.87}{\giga\watt\per\square\meter}. Intercepting even a small share of that is a large thermal load on a small ship. Radiators sized for it would weigh more than the nozzle.

That last penalty is worse than it sounds, and it has a cheap fix. A superconductor carries current with no resistance right up until some small patch warms past its critical temperature. The patch turns resistive, the current heats it further, and the normal region spreads. Engineers call that runaway a quench, and it ends the pulse and possibly the coil. Heat is expensive to remove down there as well. Pumping a joule out at \SI{77}{\kelvin} against a \SI{300}{\kelvin} sink costs \SI{2.9}{\joule} at the Carnot limit and roughly twenty joules with a real cryocooler. The fix is to keep the induced current out of the superconductor entirely. A separate shell of ordinary copper, mounted between the plume and the coil, is a lower-impedance path for a rapidly changing field than the coil behind it, so the current flows in the copper instead. The copper has resistance and warms, but it warms at whatever temperature it is mounted at and radiates from there rather than from inside the cryostat. Pulsed fusion propulsion designs already work this way. Romanelli, Mignone and Cervone give each ring of their multi-coil chamber a superconducting coil that supplies the seed field and a separate conducting thrust coil that carries the current the expanding plasma induces \cite{romanelli2017pulsed_fusion_nozzle}. The superconductor sets the field up and the copper takes the hit.

A second layer of defense sits inside the conductor itself. REBCO tape is plated with copper as a stabilizer, a parallel path that carries the current and absorbs heat if a patch does go normal. It matters more in these tapes than in older superconductors, because a normal zone spreads slowly in REBCO and is correspondingly hard to catch by watching for a voltage. Marchevsky reports that raising the copper cross-section from \SI{0.16}{\square\milli\meter} to \SI{0.40}{\square\milli\meter} halved the hot-spot temperature in current-dump tests on insert coils \cite{marchevsky2021quench}. That copper carries no supercurrent, so more of it means less current per square millimeter of conductor and a weaker field for the same coil mass. The shell outside the coil costs nothing in current density, which is why it belongs first in the order of defenses and the stabilizer last.

That order of defenses handles the field leak. The flash needs a different answer from the one \autoref{sec:lightweight_pusher_plates} gives the pusher plate. That section sets its sprayed film by opacity rather than by ablated mass, because a micron of carbon-loaded liquid only has to stop light before it reaches metal. A nozzle liner is judged the other way round. It faces a plume at \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} against the plate's \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, which is seventeen times the energy per unit mass, and no film thin enough to spray lasts through it. The nozzle carries a solid liner instead, of pyrolytic graphite, a layered carbon grown by cracking hydrocarbon gas onto a hot surface. It conducts along its sheets nearly as well as diamond and holds to \SI{3900}{\kelvin}, where aluminum melts at \SI{933}{\kelvin}. Nothing survives the flash untouched. A surface in radiative balance with the peak flux would sit near \SI{23500}{\kelvin}, and the highest-melting solid known gives out near \SI{4300}{\kelvin}. So the liner protects itself by boiling away, and about \SI{4.9}{\kilogram} of carbon leaves with the exhaust each pulse, which is 2.3\% of the slug. Where the flash is weaker the liner only glows and loses nothing. The oxygen problem \autoref{sec:lightweight_pusher_plates} raises for phenolic ablators applies here as well. The carbide coatings it names are the right family for the part of the wall that is not being consumed. The liner does not touch the shell behind it. Aluminum is there to take the induced current and graphite to take the flash, and neither job needs the two in contact, so the liner stands off on pads of insulating carbon foam with a vacuum gap over the rest. A gap is not insulation on its own. Two surfaces facing each other across vacuum still trade $\sigma T^4$, and between bare surfaces the shell settles at the liner's own temperature. That runs near \SI{2600}{\kelvin} at the hot pulse, well past the \SI{933}{\kelvin} at which aluminum melts. What the gap needs is low emissivity rather than distance. Polishing both faces is enough at the loads this section books. If the plume turns out to radiate most of its share before it clears the exit, floating shields go in as well. They cannot be the aluminized polymer that flies on spacecraft. The first shield would sit near \SI{2900}{\kelvin}. Refractory foil is what that temperature allows, tantalum melting at \SI{3290}{\kelvin} or tungsten at \SI{3695}{\kelvin}. Blocking the back face makes the liner shed inward instead, which raises it to about \SI{3100}{\kelvin} and leaves \SI{800}{\kelvin} of margin against its own limit. It is laid as lapped tiles rather than as one continuous skin, so a crack stops at a tile edge and the zone the flash actually consumes can be replaced without discarding the rest. Ablation recoil presses each tile back onto its pads, which is the direction a brittle material wants, since graphite carries several times as much in compression as in tension. \autoref{sec:argon_slug_thermo} works the radiation that crosses the gap.

The shell behind that liner should be aluminum rather than copper, which is not the obvious pick. Copper conducts better and melts \SI{425}{\kelvin} higher. Neither is what decides it. A shell of a given mass shields the same amount whether it is thin and wide or thick and narrow, because the radius and the thickness cancel out, and what is left is conductivity divided by density. Aluminum wins that ratio two to one, and it holds 2.3 times as much heat per kilogram per degree. At equal mass a copper shell takes in twice the energy with less than half the capacity to hold it, so it reaches its higher melting point about three times sooner. The alloy matters for the same reason. Nearly pure 1100 rather than structural 6061, since the shell is backed along its whole area and carries no bending load, and 6061 would give up 38\% of the margin.

Sending the slug through that shell raises a problem the metal choice does not solve. The slug is not plain water. The potassium carbonate seed makes it alkaline at about pH 11.7, and aluminum keeps its protective oxide film only up to about pH 8.5. Above that the metal dissolves and gives off hydrogen. So the seed goes in downstream. Clean water passes through the shell, and a concentrated carbonate stream joins it afterward on the way to the bag. That stream is 3.4\% of the flow and costs almost none of the cooling. It has to meet the liquid rather than the steam, because dissolved salt does not evaporate. When the slug flashes to vapor the steam comes off pure and the potassium stays behind in the droplets, which is the same fact \autoref{tab:seed_window} already rests on. Behind that, the channel walls carry a thick grown oxide, which holds against clean water though it would not have held against the carbonate. One operating rule follows. The shell has no path to space of its own, so the pulse rate is tied to the flow, and an eight tonne shell with the coolant stopped reaches melting in about \SI{340}{\second}. The time available grows as the square of the shell mass, which is most of why the shell is eight tonnes and not four. The heat is not wasted. At two pulses per second the preheat is roughly \SI{30}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} of slug. On the Jupiter leg that pays about 5\% of the \SI{0.65}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} owed to warm and melt \SI{122}{\kelvin} ice. On the Earth leg the slug is already liquid, so the same heat raises the vapor fraction of \autoref{tab:bag_state} from \num{0.18} to about \num{0.19}.

The slug can absorb that heat instead, and this is the reason it is water. Write $k$ for the slug ratio, the kilograms of carried matter vaporized for every kilogram of arriving PuffSat. The Jupiter cycle's \SI{213}{\kilogram} slug and \SI{25}{\kilogram} projectile give $k = 8.52$, and this paper writes $8.5$ throughout as the round figure. Taking water from \SI{300}{\kelvin} to steam at \SI{1000}{\kelvin} absorbs \SI{3.88}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, against \SI{1.16}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} for liquid nitrogen and \SI{1.38}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} for dry ice. Most of that is the phase change. Boiling a kilogram of water costs \SI{2.26}{\mega\joule} on its own, eleven times what boiling a kilogram of nitrogen costs, and that one number is most of why water wins. At $k = 4$ the full figure soaks up 1.5\% of the pulse energy, where nitrogen manages 0.45\%. Read those as a ceiling rather than as an operating point. \autoref{tab:bag_state} shows the flown bag stopping well short of them, and explains why. Nothing is thrown away in the process. Heat poured into the slug before the collision is heat the collision no longer has to supply, so the energy needed to turn the slug into a conducting plasma falls from \SI{85.1}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} to \SI{81.3}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}. The waste heat is not vented, it is spent on the propellant's own ignition. Water also sits still for the trip. A passive body runs near \SI{278}{\kelvin} at Earth and \SI{122}{\kelvin} at Jupiter, and ice is stable across that whole range. Nitrogen boils at \SI{77}{\kelvin} and would need active refrigeration at both ends of a cycle lasting two to three years. Carbon dioxide reaches about \SI{40}{\bar} of vapor pressure at Earth distance and would need a pressure vessel. This is the opposite of the choice made for the PuffSat itself, which is atomized into droplets rather than boiled (\autoref{sec:icy_puffsat}), and the reason for the difference is simply that the PuffSat has no waste heat to spend while the ship has heat it must get rid of.

Where the ship is changes the accounting, and the cycle's two burns sit at opposite ends of it. The Jupiter departure fires where a passive body runs \SI{122}{\kelvin}, so its slug is not cool water waiting to boil. It is ice a hundred and fifty kelvin below freezing, and nothing can be vented until it is melted. Warming that ice to \SI{273}{\kelvin} takes \SI{0.24}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} on the measured heat capacity of ice~Ih, melting it takes \SI{0.33}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, and bringing the liquid up to room temperature takes another \SI{0.08}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}. The total is \SI{0.65}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}. Only the first two of those three gate melting, which \autoref{sec:argon_slug_thermo} returns to, and the ship owes it every pulse whether or not the nozzle runs hot. The Earth boost fires at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, where the same slug sits near \SI{278}{\kelvin} as liquid water and owes almost none of that bill. One burn gets a free \SI{0.65}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} of heat sink from the cold it was stored at. The other does not, and pays for the difference in bag film.

Water alone will not steer. A magnetic field grips a conductor, and steam at a few thousand kelvin is not one. The fix is an alkali seed, a trace of an easily ionized metal that supplies free electrons at temperatures where nothing else will \cite{kerrebrock1964nonequilibrium}. Open-cycle magnetohydrodynamic power plants seed their combustion gas the same way and for the same reason \cite{rosa1968mhd, messerle1995mhd}. We assume potassium carbonate at 1.77\% of the slug by mass, which delivers 1\% potassium. The carbonate is what makes it practical, and it is cheaper and easier to handle than potassium metal, which catches fire on contact with water. Getting 1\% potassium takes \SI{1.80}{\gram} of carbonate per \SI{100}{\milli\liter} of slug, against the \SI{110}{\gram} that will dissolve \cite{crc_handbook}, so the solution sits at about a sixtieth of saturation and nothing precipitates at any temperature the slug sees. Solubility is not what narrows the field. Potassium chloride would be cheaper and dissolve as readily, but it would put chlorine into the plume, and chlorine destroys ozone catalytically \cite{molina1974ozone}. This paper already declines to put lead in the atmosphere (\autoref{sec:explosive_puffsat}), and chlorine is refused on the same grounds. Potassium sulfate is ruled out the same way rather than on solubility, since \SI{12}{\gram} per \SI{100}{\milli\liter} is still five times what the seed needs. Sulfur in the plume is the objection. Carrying the carbonate rather than the bare metal costs \SI{0.36}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} of extra dissociation, which is 0.42\% of the ignition budget. Potassium is also the right alkali rather than merely an affordable one. Cesium ionizes more easily but weighs 3.4 times as much, so above about \SI{3800}{\kelvin} a kilogram of potassium supplies more electrons than a kilogram of cesium, having already ionized most of what it carries.

How much conductivity that seed actually buys depends on what it is mixed into, and the classic numbers are for a gas that is not water. Kerrebrock's worked example is potassium in argon. Conductivity is $\sigma = n_e e^2 / (m_e \nu)$, where $n_e$ is the free-electron density and $\nu$ is the rate at which one electron collides with the neutral gas it drifts through. Argon is a bare atom with no dipole, and electrons at these energies largely slip past it. Water is a bent molecule with a permanent dipole, so it scatters the same electrons much harder, and every collision is a step the electron does not take along the field. Marzouk computes the equilibrium conductivity of the combustion products themselves, at one atmosphere with a 1\% seed mole fraction \cite{marzouk2024conductivity}. Potassium in the pure steam left by burning hydrogen in oxygen gives \SI{0.35}{\siemens\per\meter} at \SI{2000}{\kelvin} and \SI{40.5}{\siemens\per\meter} at \SI{3000}{\kelvin}. The same seed in air-fired hydrogen, where nitrogen dilutes the steam, gives \SI{64.6}{\siemens\per\meter} at \SI{3000}{\kelvin}, so the steam case runs 37\% lower at equal temperature and seed. Both of those gases carry water, so that pair prices dilution rather than the whole distance from argon. The distance from argon is larger, and the slug sits at the far end of it. This is a tax rather than a wall. Conductivity climbs by a factor of 116 across that thousand kelvin, so buying back a factor of 1.6 costs about \SI{100}{\kelvin}. Density moves it the other way. At a fixed seed fraction Saha gives $n_e \propto \sqrt{n}$ while the collision rate rises as $n$, so $\sigma$ falls as $1/\sqrt{n}$. One atmosphere of steam at \SI{3000}{\kelvin} is \SI{0.073}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}, and the plume is denser than that when the collision happens and thinner than it once it has expanded, so the correction changes sign during the pulse.

What the estimate leaves out points the same way the carrier gas does. It counts the seed as free potassium atoms, and a water-rich gas gives potassium somewhere else to sit. Aspitarte and colleagues fired an excimer laser into a potassium-seeded oxy-fuel jet and found the fluorescence signature of two-photon ionization of potassium hydroxide, which puts KOH in a real seeded flow rather than only in a chemistry model \cite{aspitarte2025photoionization}. Potassium bound as hydroxide is potassium not standing in the Saha balance as an atom. How much of \autoref{tab:seed_window} either effect costs is not something we can settle here. Both push the $Rm$ column down and the \SI{3300}{\kelvin} floor derived below up, and pinning the size of either belongs with the radiation-hydrodynamic calculation already owed in \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}.

The seed buys something beyond conductivity, and it decides how much of the collision energy is usable. Breaking water into its atoms takes \SI{917}{\kilo\joule\per\mole}, or \SI{50.9}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} \cite{crc_handbook}, about 60\% of the whole ignition bill, and that energy sits in broken bonds rather than in motion. It comes back only if the atoms find each other again as the plume cools and expands, and the honest answer is that most of it does not. The rate is not the problem. The reaction needs three bodies to meet, so its rate climbs steeply with density, and at \SI{1}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} the recombination time really is a hundredth of a microsecond against an expansion lasting hundreds. The problem is where that rate is evaluated. At \SI{1}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} the plume is still fully atomized and has nothing to give back yet. It has something to return only once it has cooled enough to want to re-form, and by then it has expanded to about \SI{0.02}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} and is past the nozzle lip, where the time it has left drops by a factor of eight in one step.

Following the expansion rather than sampling it puts the crossing where three-body recombination can no longer keep up at \SIrange{0.011}{0.024}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}, with \SIrange{90}{100}{\percent} of the bond energy still held \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. The plume leaves chemically frozen. The loan defaults, and what it strands is between \SI{19}{\percent} of the dissipated budget at the \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} pulse and \SI{47}{\percent} at the \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} end of the growth push. That is not a new energy term to add. It is one of the five contributions to $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ that \autoref{sec:jet_efficiency} already names, and \autoref{eq:eta_chem} is what prices it, so charging it twice would be double-counting. What the seed still buys is conductivity, and that part is unaffected: potassium keeps supplying electrons long after the water would have re-formed, which is what \autoref{tab:seed_window} rests on.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Conductivity and chemistry of a seeded water plume as it cools, at 1\% potassium by mass \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. The magnetic Reynolds number is $Rm = \mu_0 \sigma v L$, and it measures how strongly the field grips the flow. Both $\sigma$ and $Rm$ are printed, because $Rm$ alone cannot be checked. The conductivity is a Saha solve for the water \cite{hansen_kawaler_trimble_stellar_interiors} blended with the potassium seed, and at the \SIrange{2000}{3000}{\kelvin} end where the cliff sits it rests on the seeded-plasma measurements of \cite{kerrebrock1964nonequilibrium,rosa1968mhd,messerle1995mhd}. At the hot end, where the water is itself ionized and Coulomb collisions dominate, it reduces to the fully ionized limit \cite{spitzer1962fullyionized}. The velocity and the flux-tube size enter only as their product, and the solved expansion gives $vL = \SI{7.4e4}{\square\meter\per\second}$, the middle of a \SIrange{5.5e4}{9.7e4}{\square\meter\per\second} band. The field holds the plume at all as long as $Rm$ stays above about 1, but the share of stored field energy that leaks into the plume each expansion is about $1/Rm$, and the slug can only absorb that heat while $Rm$ stays above roughly 20. Reproduce with \texttt{make plume-state}.}
    \label{tab:seed_window}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|r|l|}\hline
        \textbf{Temperature} & \textbf{Potassium ionized} & \textbf{$\sigma$ [\si{\siemens\per\meter}]} & \textbf{$Rm$} & \textbf{State of the water} \\\hline
        \SI{2000}{\kelvin} & 0.02\% & 1.05 & 0.1 & recombined, field lets go \\\hline
        \SI{3000}{\kelvin} & 2.0\% & 63 & 5.9 & recombined \\\hline
        \SI{4000}{\kelvin} & 18.5\% & 286 & 26.6 & recombined \\\hline
        \SI{5000}{\kelvin} & 56.3\% & 492 & 45.8 & mostly recombined \\\hline
        \SI{6000}{\kelvin} & 85.6\% & 600 & 55.8 & dissociating \\\hline
        \SI{15000}{\kelvin} & 98.8\% & \num{6987} & 650 & fully dissociated \\\hline
