\autoref{tab:seed_window} shows the window this opens, and without meaning to it also prices the field leak that \autoref{tab:bag_state} charges the slug for. Those are the same number, and the paper spent a while treating them as two. Magnetic field soaks into a conductor over the diffusion time $\tau_d = \mu_0 \sigma L^2$, and the plume expands across its own size in about $L/v$. Divide one by the other and the length appears once, leaving $\mu_0 \sigma v L$, which is the definition of $Rm$. So the share of the field's stored energy that leaks into the plume during one expansion is about $1/Rm$, and the $Rm$ column of \autoref{tab:seed_window} is a leak schedule written backwards.

Written that way the identity is checkable, and the two tables now pass the check. \autoref{tab:bag_state} charges the coldest leg \SI{2.54}{\percent}, which is $Rm = 39$, and that falls between the \SI{4000}{\kelvin} and \SI{5000}{\kelvin} rows of \autoref{tab:seed_window}, where a plume cooling through the window spends most of its time. The hottest leg's \SI{0.11}{\percent} is $Rm = 909$, above the last tabulated row, which is right for a plume that starts at \SI{26500}{\kelvin} and is more than half ionized. Reading the leak off a single row was the older mistake. The plume sweeps the whole column as it cools, so what the slug is charged is the residence-weighted average over that path rather than the value at any one temperature.

The same column also fixes where the field stops working at all. At the stated $vL$, $Rm$ falls to 1 near \SI{2450}{\kelvin}, and below that the field diffuses out of the plume faster than the plume expands, so there is nothing left to steer. That crossing is solved on the continuous conductivity rather than read between the rows above \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. The rows sample every thousand kelvin while $\sigma$ climbs sixtyfold between the first two, so interpolating them puts the cliff about \SI{120}{\kelvin} too high. Little rests on the middle of the $vL$ band being exactly right. Across the whole \SIrange{5.5e4}{9.7e4}{\square\meter\per\second} span the crossing moves only from \SI{2524}{\kelvin} to \SI{2386}{\kelvin}. That cliff is not what binds the design. The leak limit above it sits at \SI{3800}{\kelvin}, some thirteen hundred kelvin earlier, and the slug runs out of capacity to absorb the leaked heat long before the field loses its grip.

Read that way the window is narrower than the ionization alone suggests. At \SI{4000}{\kelvin}, $1/Rm$ is 3.8\%, which the slug absorbs without difficulty. At \SI{3000}{\kelvin} it is 17\%, and at the stored energies of \autoref{tab:bag_sizing} that would ask the slug to take up about four times its own mass as vapor. It cannot, and what fails at that point is not the bag but the confinement. Setting the leak equal to what fully vaporizes the slug puts the usable floor near \SI{3800}{\kelvin}. Potassium does condense out near \SI{2500}{\kelvin}, but the leak binds about thirteen hundred kelvin before condensation gets the chance. Nothing in the recombination argument is lost, since \autoref{tab:seed_window} already has the water recombined by \SI{4000}{\kelvin}, which is still above the floor though by less than it used to be. More potassium will not move it either. For a trace alkali in a neutral gas Saha gives $n_e \propto \sqrt{n_K}$, so $Rm$ climbs only as the square root of the seed fraction, and lifting $Rm$ at \SI{3000}{\kelvin} to its \SI{4000}{\kelvin} value would take twenty times the potassium, which is a fifth of the slug. Sodium would work in the same role but holds the cold end about \SI{430}{\kelvin} less well, so matching 1\% potassium takes 2.5\% sodium at \SI{5000}{\kelvin} and 12.7\% at \SI{3000}{\kelvin}. One condition sits under all of it, and it has since been checked. Recombination freezes if the fireball is thinner than about \SI{0.01}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}, and the density a real pulse produces has now been computed rather than assumed. The freeze lands at \SIrange{0.011}{0.024}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}, so the threshold was drawn in very nearly the right place and the pulse falls on the wrong side of it. The consequence is priced above and in \autoref{eq:eta_chem}.

Venting the slug as steam into a bag before the collision buys one more thing, and it is the field strength. Spreading the same mass through more volume lowers its density and therefore its pressure, and the field only has to stand off pressure. It does not make the plume hotter. Temperature depends on energy per kilogram, which the bag does not change. The field needed falls as the bag radius to the power of three halves, so the \SI{213}{\kilogram} slug a \SI{25}{\kilogram} PuffSat vaporizes at $k = 8.5$ wants \SI{21}{\tesla} at \SI{1.8}{\meter} and \SI{2.0}{\tesla} at \SI{8.7}{\meter}. The bag cannot simply be made enormous, because the same expansion that lowers the pressure raises the surface area, and the plume radiates from that surface. Loss grows as the radius squared. It also has to stay opaque, since a plume thin enough to see through radiates from its whole volume rather than only its skin.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Bag sizing for the \SI{213}{\kilogram} slug of a \SI{25}{\kilogram} PuffSat at $k = 8.5$, with the plume at \SI{15000}{\kelvin}. This is a sweep of enclosed volume, quoted as the radius of the sphere holding it, so it is independent of the shape the volume is finally poured into; \autoref{tab:axial_bag} handles shape. The field falls with size and the radiative loss rises with it. The two field columns bracket the burn. The first is the coldest pulse the fleet flies, \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} closing, where the plume sits at \SI{14700}{\kelvin} and is only \SI{5}{\percent} ionized. The second is the hottest, \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, where energy conservation and Saha together give \SI{26200}{\kelvin} and \SI{57}{\percent}. The mist column is the coldest the mist can sit and still hold together as vapor at that density, and it depends on the bag before the collision rather than on the plume after it. Its model is \autoref{tab:bag_state}'s Earth-storage vapor fraction held fixed while only the volume changes, with the saturation curve inverted at the resulting vapor density, in the Magnus form fitted by \cite{alduchov1996magnus} and checked against \cite{nist_webbook_water} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}, so the \SI{5.4}{\meter} row reproduces that table by construction. Holding the fraction is what the two outer rows strain. A bag past about \SI{7}{\meter} has gone optically thin and reabsorbs less of its own radiation, so its vapor fraction should fall rather than hold, and those rows should be read as upper bounds on temperature. The band the section settles on is inside the region where the assumption holds. The loss column holds the plume at \SI{15000}{\kelvin} for the full expansion, which is a cold-pulse figure rather than a bound on the burn. Radiative loss goes as $T^4$, and the solved temperatures run from \SI{15165}{\kelvin} at the coldest pulse to \SI{26521}{\kelvin} at the hottest \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}, so at the flown \SI{5.4}{\meter} bag the true loss runs from \SI{1.0}{\percent} to \SI{3.6}{\percent} and the column understates the hot end threefold. Three times a small number is still a small number, which is why the band below survives it. Reproduce with \texttt{make bag-state} in the companion calculation repository \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}, which is where every \texttt{make} target named in this paper lives.}

        \SI{3.5}{\meter} & \num{1.19} & \SI{7.9}{\tesla} & \SI{13.1}{\tesla} & 0.5\% & \SI{346}{\kelvin} \\\hline
        \SI{5.4}{\meter} & \num{0.32} & \SI{4.1}{\tesla} & \SI{6.8}{\tesla} & 1.2\% & \SI{316}{\kelvin} \\\hline

        \SI{17.5}{\meter} & \num{0.0095} & \SI{0.70}{\tesla} & \SI{1.16}{\tesla} & 12\% & \SI{259}{\kelvin} \\\hline

At this slug mass the two bounds trade places. Radiative loss is the surface area divided by the energy inside, and area grows more slowly than volume, so the loss weakens as the cube root of slug mass. A \SI{213}{\kilogram} slug at \SI{8.7}{\meter} gives up 3\% where a \SI{5}{\kilogram} one at the same density gives up 11\%. Opacity binds instead, and it binds as a ceiling rather than as a cliff. At the pessimistic absorption coefficient of \SI{0.5}{\square\meter\per\kilogram} the plume stops being optically thick past about \SI{7}{\meter}. A plume that has gone thin radiates from its whole volume rather than from its skin. For a gray coefficient that volume emission is $6\kappa M\sigma T^4$, fixed by the mass rather than by the volume holding it, which is \SI{1.83}{\tera\watt} here. Skin emission $4\pi R^2\sigma T^4$ climbs to the same figure at the same radius, since both conditions reduce to $R^2 = 3\kappa M/2\pi$. Loss therefore grows as the square of the radius up to seven meters and then flattens, never passing \num{2.1} times what the flown \SI{5.4}{\meter} bag gives up. Going thin caps the loss instead of running it away, so the upper end of the band is set by the sweep limit below rather than by radiative escape. The optical depth here is $\kappa \rho D$, taken across the bag's diameter rather than its radius, which is the chord a photon crosses on its way out through the center. The distinction is not cosmetic. On the diameter the limit is \SI{7.13}{\meter} and the flown \SI{5.4}{\meter} bag sits at $\tau = 1.74$, comfortably thick \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}; measured on the radius instead the limit would be \SI{5.04}{\meter} and that same bag would already be thin at $\tau = 0.87$. The usable band is therefore roughly three and a half to seven meters, needing three to eight tesla at the coldest pulse and five to thirteen at the hottest. A third limit caps it independently, and it is the one that ends up reshaping the bag. The arriving PuffSat has to sweep the whole slug, and once the bag is much wider than the incoming projectile the impactor punches a column through it and couples only to the mass in that column. Numbers make that worse than it sounds. Areal density, the mass per unit area lying along a path, is the quantity that decides whether one body stops another. A \SI{5.4}{\meter} sphere holding \SI{213}{\kilogram} runs at \SI{0.32}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}, so crossing it presents only \SI{3.5}{\kilogram\per\square\meter}, while a \SI{5}{\centi\meter} rod of ice massing \SI{25}{\kilogram} is \SI{3183}{\kilogram\per\square\meter}. The rod outweighs what it meets by a factor of nine hundred. Newtonian drag over the whole crossing is $\rho v A \ell$, about \SI{1550}{\kilogram\meter\per\second} against the \num{1.4e6} the rod carries. It surrenders a tenth of a percent of its momentum, leaves the far side intact, and has swept \SI{27}{\gram} of the \SI{213}{\kilogram}. That is a needle through fog. \autoref{sec:needle_through_fog} is what to do about it.

One thing the bag does not buy is a lighter nozzle, and the reason is one line of gas law. The field has to stand off the plume, so the magnetic pressure $B^2/2\mu_0$ equals the plume's pressure $P$. The energy stored in a field of that strength filling a volume $V$ is $B^2V/2\mu_0$, which is the same product $PV$. The ideal gas law then finishes it, because $PV = nR_gT$, and neither the number of moles nor the temperature has any idea how big the bag is. Dissociated water is three particles per \SI{18}{\gram}, so \SI{213}{\kilogram} of slug is \SI{35500}{\mole}, and at \SI{15000}{\kelvin} that is \SI{4.43}{\giga\joule}. The stored field energy is that number at every row of \autoref{tab:bag_sizing}, fixed before the bag's size is chosen at all. On the mass rule of \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} the coils weigh the same. What a bigger bag buys is a weaker peak field, and since the mechanical load on a coil grows as the square of the field, that is worth having on its own.

That \SI{4.43}{\giga\joule} assumes the plume is dissociated but not ionized, which is all \SI{15000}{\kelvin} buys. The collision delivers more than that. Dissipated energy per kilogram of merged blob is $\tfrac{1}{2}kw^2/(1+k)^2$, which at \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and $k = 8.5$ is \SI{265}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, against the \SI{85.1}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} that vaporizing, dissociating and heating to \SI{15000}{\kelvin} costs. The leftover \SI{180}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} does not mostly go into temperature. Stripping one electron from each of water's three atoms takes \SI{13.6}{\electronvolt} apiece, or \SI{219}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} for the molecule, so ionization absorbs the excess the way boiling absorbs heat from a pot without warming it \cite{zeldovich_raizer}. Dividing \num{180} by \num{219} would suggest the plume ends up four fifths ionized, and that is wrong, because the gas does not get to choose its ionization fraction independently of its temperature. Saha's equation fixes the fraction once temperature and density are given, and it is stingy at these densities \cite{hansen_kawaler_trimble_stellar_interiors}. At \SI{15000}{\kelvin} and \SI{0.32}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} it allows only \SI{5.9}{\percent}. The plume cannot pour \SI{180}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} into an ionization channel that narrow, so the temperature has to climb until the channel opens. Solving energy conservation and Saha together is a two-line root find, and at \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} it lands at \SI{26200}{\kelvin} and \SI{57}{\percent} ionized, splitting the budget as \SI{86}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} of thermal energy against \SI{125}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} of stripped electrons.

That state moves across the burn, which turns out to rescue the original assumption at the end that matters. The craft accelerates into the oncoming wave, so closing speed falls from \SI{56.53}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} on the slowest cycle, and the plume cools with it: \SI{22400}{\kelvin} at \SI{37}{\percent} ionized when the closing speed is \SI{65}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, \SI{19400}{\kelvin} at \SI{22}{\percent} at \SI{56.53}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, and \SI{14700}{\kelvin} at \SI{5.3}{\percent} at \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The last of those is the coldest pulse the fleet has to fly, and at that point \SI{15000}{\kelvin} with negligible ionization is not an assumption at all. It is the answer.

What ionization changes is the particle count, because every atom that loses an electron becomes two particles, and pressure counts particles rather than mass. Water goes from three particles per molecule to $3(1+f)$. Multiplying that by the temperature ratio gives a pressure \num{2.75} times the assumed value at the hottest pulse and \num{1.03} times it at the coldest, so the field runs from \num{1.02} to \num{1.66} times \autoref{tab:bag_sizing}'s original column and the stored energy from \SIrange{4.6}{12.2}{\giga\joule}. The table therefore brackets the burn rather than quoting a point. Pinning it properly, including the second ionization of oxygen that this treatment ignores, needs the radiation-hydrodynamic calculation this section keeps owing.

The bag is not free either, and its price is a short piece of arithmetic worth doing in full. Take a spherical film of thickness $t$ holding pressure $P$ at radius $R$. Cut it in half. The pressure pushing the two halves apart acts over the circle $\pi R^2$, while the film resisting it has cross-section $2\pi R t$, so the stress is $\sigma = PR/2t$ and the thickness needed is $PR/2\sigma$. Multiply that by the surface area $4\pi R^2$ and by the film's density $\rho_f$ to get the film's mass, then use the ideal gas law to write $P$ in terms of the mass of vapor inside, which is $x$ times the slug. Every factor of $R$ cancels:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bag_film_mass}
\frac{m_{\mathrm{bag}}}{m_{\mathrm{slug}}} = F\,\frac{x\,\rho_f R_g T}{M \sigma} \approx 2.6\times10^{-4}\, F\, x\, T
\end{equation}
where $x$ is the fraction of the slug carried as vapor, $T$ is its temperature in kelvin, and $F$ is a pure number set by the bag's shape alone. The numerical form is for the polyethylene fiber chosen below, worked at half its quoted strength to allow for weave and seams. At the sphere's $F = 3/2$ the bag masses about four hundredths of a percent of the slug for every kelvin of steam it holds. The radius does not appear, which makes the film the second quantity in this section a bigger bag leaves alone, the stored field energy being the first.

The shape factor earns its own line because the design below stops using a sphere. A sphere gives $F = 3/2$. A cylinder of the same radius is worse, and the half-cut argument shows why. Slice a cylinder of length $L$ lengthwise and the pressure acts over the rectangle $2RL$, while only two strips of film of combined cross-section $2tL$ hold it together, so $\sigma = PR/t$. That is twice the sphere's stress at the same thickness, and it gives $F = 2$. A capsule, meaning a cylindrical barrel of length $L$ capped at both ends with hemispheres of radius $r$, sits between the two:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bag_shape_factor}
F = \frac{2L + 2r}{L + \tfrac{4}{3}r}
\end{equation}
which runs from $3/2$ when $L$ is zero to $2$ for a long tube. The entire penalty for abandoning the sphere is therefore bounded at $4/3$, and by an aspect ratio of two most of it is already paid. Standard shell formulas reach the same stresses by a longer route \cite{young2011roark}.

\autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} prices the \SI{1000}{\kelvin} steam of the heat-sink argument and finds it unaffordable. A bag of \SI{1000}{\kelvin} steam masses about 40 percent of the slug inside it, and the collision destroys it every pulse. Everything above the latent heat is sensible heat of hot vapor, and sensible heat is exactly what pressurizes the bag, so the heat-sink capacity and the film mass are the same knob turned twice. The bag has to run cold.

What the film is made of sets that coefficient, and the property that matters is specific strength, the tensile strength divided by the density. Toyobo's comparison of high-performance fibers puts high-strength polyethylene at \SI{3.5}{\giga\pascal} and \SI{0.97}{\gram\per\cubic\centi\meter}, para-aramid at \SI{2.8}{\giga\pascal} and \SI{1.45}{\gram\per\cubic\centi\meter}, PBO at \SI{5.8}{\giga\pascal} and \SI{1.54}{\gram\per\cubic\centi\meter}, and ordinary polyester at \SI{1.1}{\giga\pascal} and \SI{1.38}{\gram\per\cubic\centi\meter} \cite{toyobo_zylon}. Per kilogram carried, polyethylene comes within 4 percent of PBO, beats para-aramid by 1.9 times, and beats high-tenacity polyester by 4.5. Oriented films of the same polymers run lower still than their fibers, so a film-only bag is the expensive way to build this one.

Polyester is the obvious cheap answer and it loses anyway, for a reason that recurs whenever this paper picks a material. A \SI{213}{\kilogram} slug wants \SI{4.9}{\kilogram} of polyethylene fiber against \SI{21.6}{\kilogram} of polyester, and every one of those kilograms was lifted off Earth and is destroyed in the pulse. Delivering the seventeen extra kilograms costs about \$85{,}000 at the near-term fare of \autoref{sec:fare_comparison}. The feedstock difference between two commodity fibers is a hundred dollars or so. Those are two to three orders of magnitude apart, so no plausible price for either fiber decides this and the mass decides it instead. Read as slug lost per cycle rather than returned, 10.2 percent against 2.3 percent costs roughly 60 percent of the fleet over the chain's eleven cycles. Cheap by the kilogram is not cheap here. Polyethylene fiber is in any case a commodity rather than a specialty item, made at scale for rope, ballistic fabric and fishing line.

Polyethylene is normally disqualified for the one reason that stops applying here. It melts near \SI{423}{\kelvin} \cite{toyobo_zylon}, which is why it belongs nowhere warm, and a \SI{1000}{\kelvin} bag would have destroyed it before the collision got the chance. The mist runs at \SI{316}{\kelvin} on the Earth boost and at \SI{279}{\kelvin} on the Jupiter departure. The same bag design keeps \SI{107}{\kelvin} of margin in the harder of the two cases. Its second standard objection is creep under sustained load, and this bag is loaded for seconds and then vaporized. Sanborn and colleagues measured Dyneema SK76 single fibers from \SI{e-3}{\per\second} to \SI{e3}{\per\second} and found failure strength rising with strain rate to a plateau near \SI{1}{\per\second} \cite{sanborn2014dyneema}, so loading this bag fast is the favorable case rather than the risky one.

Plume chemistry settles the choice. Polyethylene is carbon and hydrogen and nothing else, so a vaporized bag puts no halogen, no sulfur and no metal into the exhaust. That matches decisions the paper has already made. Chlorine is refused for the seed above, lead for the primaries in \autoref{sec:explosive_puffsat}, and unhalogenated polyethylene is already the pick for the airlock pipe of \autoref{sec:vacuum_tube_details} and for the bulk of the pusher-plate slab in \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}. A metallized barrier film would break that rule for nothing, because the bag holds at most \SI{8.7}{\kilo\pascal} for the seconds between venting and impact, and under \SI{1}{\kilo\pascal} if the slug was stored cold, and a plain polyethylene liner leaks slowly enough at that pressure. Weave and barrier can then be the same polymer, welded rather than glued, which is also the practical answer to polyethylene's poor adhesive bonding.

Cold is easier here than the number \SI{373}{\kelvin} suggests, because \SI{373}{\kelvin} is not a property of water. It is the temperature at which water's vapor pressure reaches one atmosphere, and there is no atmosphere. What sets the floor instead is the bag's own density. Vapor condenses back to fog once its pressure passes the saturation pressure at its temperature, so the more room a given mass is given, the colder it can stay a gas. The last column of \autoref{tab:bag_sizing} follows that curve down. A bigger bag is a colder bag. Containment runs the other way, and the section should not pretend otherwise. On Earth a bag of steam sitting at ambient pressure carries no load at all, because the air outside pushes back just as hard. In vacuum the film carries the whole difference.

How much of the slug turns to vapor is not a choice. The ship has no radiator, so every joule of waste heat has to go into the water, and once the ice has melted the water answers by boiling. Divide what is left after melting by the latent heat and the vapor fraction falls out. The saturation curve then fixes the temperature, the temperature fixes the pressure, and \autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} fixes the film. \autoref{tab:bag_state} runs that chain for the flown column's \SI{660}{\cubic\meter}.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{The bag's state is derived, not chosen. Everything below the double rule is an output. Per kilogram of slug, for the \SI{213}{\kilogram} slug in the \SI{660}{\cubic\meter} the column encloses. The waste heat is held the same across both columns so that only the storage temperature varies. The field leak is computed from the solved cooling history rather than assumed \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}, and is quoted at the \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} cold end of the growth push, which is the leg that leaks most. The first film row is the pressure vessel alone, which is what \autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} sizes. The row below it takes the greater of that and the handling floor of \autoref{tab:axial_bag}, which is what a bag costs once it has to survive being packed and deployed. The Jupiter column's warming row is solved rather than assumed. Only warming the ice and fusing it gate melting; the liquid warming that follows competes with boiling for the same surplus, so it is charged at the mist temperature the column reaches rather than at room temperature. Recomputed at \SI{660}{\cubic\meter} with \texttt{simulations/bag\_geometry.py}, using the companion calculation at revision \texttt{b4ddcf0}.}
    \label{tab:bag_state}
    \begin{tabular}{|l|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Per \si{\kilogram} of slug} & \textbf{Jupiter, \SI{122}{\kelvin}} & \textbf{Earth, \SI{278}{\kelvin}} \\\hline
        Blackbody light intercepted (1.2\% radiated, a tenth of the sky) & \SI{0.10}{\mega\joule} & \SI{0.10}{\mega\joule} \\\hline
        Field leaking through the plume (2.54\% of \SI{21.5}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}) & \SI{0.55}{\mega\joule} & \SI{0.55}{\mega\joule} \\\hline
        \textbf{Waste heat to be absorbed} & \textbf{\SI{0.65}{\mega\joule}} & \textbf{\SI{0.65}{\mega\joule}} \\\hline
        Warming and melting the slug up to liquid & \SI{-0.59}{\mega\joule} & \SI{-0.21}{\mega\joule} \\\hline
        Left over to boil water & \SI{0.05}{\mega\joule} & \SI{0.44}{\mega\joule} \\\hline\hline
        Vapor fraction $x$ & \num{0.022} & \num{0.18} \\\hline
        Temperature, from the saturation curve & \SI{279}{\kelvin} & \SI{316}{\kelvin} \\\hline
        Pressure & \SI{0.91}{\kilo\pascal} & \SI{8.7}{\kilo\pascal} \\\hline
        Bag film as a pressure vessel, from \autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} & 0.24\%, \SI{0.51}{\kilogram} & 2.3\%, \SI{4.8}{\kilogram} \\\hline
        Bag film once the handling floor of \autoref{tab:axial_bag} is applied & \SIrange{2.0}{8.4}{\kilogram} & \SIrange{4.8}{8.4}{\kilogram} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

The two columns differ only in the temperature the slug was stored at, and that difference now decides whether there is a pressure vessel at all rather than how heavy one is. Cold storage is doing real work. Ice at \SI{122}{\kelvin} takes \SI{0.59}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} of the waste heat into warming and melting, and only the \SI{0.05}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} left over turns into pressure. The fix for the warm end is machinery this paper already flies. A detachable multilayer sunshade of the kind described in \autoref{sec:lox_puffsat} takes a shaded body at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} well below freezing, so a slug parked behind one before the Earth boost arrives at the burn frozen and recovers the whole \SI{0.65}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} ladder. It is not fast. Rejecting \SI{139}{\mega\joule} from a \SI{213}{\kilogram} slug through a couple of square meters at \SI{150}{\kelvin} takes about a month, which suits mass that is already parked between delivery and use and does not suit mass on a short turnaround. Shading the slug is therefore a scheduling choice rather than a hardware one, and what it is worth is the pressure vessel rather than the whole bag.

The cold that helps the slug is a hazard everywhere else on the ship, which has to hold two temperatures at once. Electronics stop working well before \SI{122}{\kelvin}. The coils want to be colder than that, not warmer. Both sit on the same vehicle, and the answer is to zone it. Multilayer insulation, a stack of thin reflective sheets separated by vacuum, blocks radiative heat flow well enough that a warm bus and a cold magnet can share a structure. What the warm side then needs is power.

Sunlight at Jupiter is weak rather than absent. Flux falls as the inverse square of distance from the Sun, so at \SI{5.2}{\astronomicalunit} it is about a twenty-seventh of Earth's \SI{1361}{\watt\per\square\meter}, near \SI{50}{\watt\per\square\meter}. That is enough, and it has been flown rather than argued. Juno reached Jupiter on solar power with no radioisotope generator aboard, using three nine-meter arrays carrying \num{18698} cells that make about \SI{14}{\kilo\watt} at Earth and about \SI{500}{\watt} at Jupiter \cite{bolton2017juno, jpl_juno_solar_record}. Galileo, the only earlier Jupiter orbiter, carried radioisotope generators instead. Two more solar-powered Jupiter missions, JUICE and Europa Clipper, are in cruise, so the approach is being repeated rather than left as a one-off.

Concentrators buy the same power from less array. A lens or a mirror gathers sunlight over a wide aperture and delivers it onto a small cell, so the expensive part scales with the concentrated area rather than the collecting area. Deep Space 1 flew line-focus silicone Fresnel lenses at about eight times concentration and ran both the spacecraft and its ion engine on them for the mission's thirty-eight months \cite{stella2000scarlet}. Mirrors are the other option, and the non-imaging kind is worth preferring here. A concentrator that forms no image accepts light across a wider range of incidence angles than one focusing a sharp image, so it tolerates looser Sun-pointing. A ship whose attitude is disturbed by pulses has reason to want that.

Power at Jupiter also buys cryogenics more cheaply than the same power would at Earth, and the reason is the radiator on the warm end. A refrigerator's minimum work depends on the gap it pumps heat across, as the ratio of that gap to the cold temperature. Lifting a joule from the \SI{77}{\kelvin} of liquid nitrogen to a \SI{300}{\kelvin} radiator costs \SI{2.9}{\joule} at the Carnot limit. At Jupiter a shaded radiator settles near \SI{150}{\kelvin} instead, and the same joule costs \SI{0.95}{\joule}, three times less. The distance that makes the electronics need a heater is what makes the magnet affordable to keep cold, and multilayer insulation is what lets one ship have both.

Two features of that table deserve more than a row each. The first is that the field leak is no longer the least settled number in it. It is an output. The leaked share is $1/Rm$, the reciprocal of the magnetic Reynolds number of \autoref{tab:seed_window}, and a cooling plume sweeps that whole window rather than sitting on one row of it. The leak is therefore an integral over the cooling history, and the impact simulation now supplies that history \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. Weighting $1/Rm$ by how long the plume lingers at each temperature gives \SI{0.11}{\percent} at the \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} pulse and \SI{2.54}{\percent} at the \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} cold end of the growth push. The flown \SI{4.4}{\percent} sits above that entire range. The cold leg leaks twenty times harder than the hot one because it spends its life in the \SIrange{3000}{6000}{\kelvin} window where $Rm$ falls toward unity. The hot leg is still more than half ionized and barely leaks at all, since water supplies about \num{1.7} electrons per molecule at \SI{26000}{\kelvin} against the \num{0.005} that fully ionized \SI{1}{\percent} potassium contributes. The seed only becomes the conductor once the water has recombined. Leakage also falls as the square of the bag radius through the magnetic diffusion time, so the same growth that makes the bag colder makes it tighter magnetically.

That reorders the two heat sources, and the ordering is not the same at both ends of the burn. At the cold leg the leak delivers \SI{0.55}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} against \SI{0.10}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} of intercepted light, a ratio of five to one rather than the nine to one a flat \SI{4.4}{\percent} implied. On the three hot legs the leak falls to \SIrange[range-phrase={--},range-units=single]{0.06}{0.10}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} and light becomes the larger term. The nozzle's heat problem is resistive at the cold end of the burn and radiative at the hot end, which matters because the two are fixed by different things and a design sized for one does not cover the other.

The consequence reaches past the row. From Jupiter's \SI{122}{\kelvin} storage the waste heat is \SI{0.65}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} while warming and melting the ice takes \SI{0.57}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, so the slug finishes melting with about \SI{0.08}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} to spare. Warming the liquid to room temperature is what the remaining \SI{0.08}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} would have bought, and it goes into vapor instead. That surplus boils a few percent of the flow, and the bag stands off a pressure of order \SI{1}{\kilo\pascal} against the Earth leg's \SI{8.7}{\kilo\pascal}. \autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} therefore returns a fraction of a kilogram rather than zero, which is still far under the handling floor, so what sizes the cold bag is a containment membrane held to loads this paper does not compute. Every film mass quoted in this section is therefore the Earth-storage case, where the same leg boils to $x = 0.18$ and does need a pressure vessel. That is the number a designer should size to, because a slug that misses its shading window arrives warm and the bag has to be aboard already. The margin behind it is thinner than cold storage makes it look. Melting completes at a leak of \SI{2.19}{\percent} and the cold leg runs at \SI{2.54}{\percent}, so it is already past that line rather than short of it, while the three hot legs clear theirs five to seven and a half times over. The leak model has moved once, and it moved in the direction that costs.

The second is that a two-phase mist regulates itself, which no single-phase coolant can. Liquid and vapor sitting together are locked to the saturation curve, so extra heat has nowhere to go except into boiling more water. The temperature does not move. If the nozzle runs hotter than designed, the bag answers with a larger vapor fraction at the same \SI{316}{\kelvin} and only the pressure creeps up. A tank of gas would simply get hotter and push harder on a film sized for something cooler.

The mist settles one more thing, and it is the seed. Potassium carbonate does not evaporate. It has no vapor pressure worth the name below the roughly \SI{1170}{\kelvin} at which it decomposes, so boiling the slug dry would strand the seed in the tank and send clean steam to the collision. Venting a mist leaves two thirds to nine tenths of the slug as liquid droplets, and each droplet carries its dissolved carbonate along. The concentration rises by a tenth to a half, which changes nothing. The claim that the seed needs no feed system of its own therefore survives, but the reason it survives is the mist rather than the solubility.

Argon is the alternative to all of this, and where it wins is set by how fast the impactor is coming. A bare atom has no bonds to break, so the \SI{50.9}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} goes away and so does the toll \autoref{eq:eta_chem} charges for it. That toll is a fixed quantity of energy set against a budget growing as the square of the closing speed, so it bites hardest where the collision is slowest. At the \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} cold end of the growth push and $k = 8.52$, $\eta_{\mathrm{chem}}$ is \num{0.730}. At \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} it is \num{0.910}. At the \SIrange{400}{600}{\kilo\meter\per\second} closing speed of a periapsis pulse it is above \num{0.997}, which is to say water's chemistry has stopped costing anything. The field leak points the same way. \autoref{tab:bag_state}'s cold leg leaks \SI{2.54}{\percent} against the hot leg's \SI{0.11}{\percent}, because a plume that has recombined conducts only through its seed and spends its life near the bottom of \autoref{tab:seed_window}. Argon has no recombination to wait for. It is also the gas Kerrebrock measured potassium in \cite{kerrebrock1964nonequilibrium}, and that is the larger of the two gains. Water scatters slow electrons through a permanent dipole rather than an induced one, so it scatters roughly fifty times harder per molecule than argon, and a kilogram of it supplies \num{6.7} times as many particles to scatter off. Those multiply, and they turn a minority of the mass into nearly all of the collision rate. Leaving the \SI{62.5}{\kilogram} of ice projectile and plug in place would make water \SI{21}{\percent} of the blob by mass and \SI{92}{\percent} of its electron collision rate, so an argon slug alone recovers about an eighth of what argon can deliver (\autoref{tab:argon_scatter}). The whole blob therefore goes over. The plug becomes frozen slug argon, since it is deployed from the ship rather than flown in, and the projectile carries a five-layer sunshade of the kind JWST flies \cite{jwst_sunshield}. Argon dissolves nothing, so the seed rides as a dry carbonate powder frozen into the snow rather than in solution, which is also what retires the corrosion problem above, since the only fluid reaching the aluminum is then clean argon. Both bodies at \SI{1623}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} hold the matched-density condition of \autoref{sec:needle_through_fog} at exactly one, the value ice on ice gives. What still scatters is what the ship itself sheds. The polyethylene bag and the ablating graphite liner are \SI{32}{\percent} of the remaining collision rate between them and the projectile's electronics are under a thousandth of it, but none of that carries a dipole, so it costs a factor of one and a half where the ice cost a factor of eight. A kilogram of argon is \SI{25}{\mole} against dissociated water's \SI{166}{\mole}, so the same energy has a sixth as many particles to share it among and the plume runs hotter and further ionized. That energy is not stranded the way the bonds are, since ionized gas is what the field grips. Argon's chemistry advantage matters most toward the slow end of the overtake burn. \autoref{sec:jet_efficiency} separates the chemical limit from the crossover set by a particular nozzle.

What argon gives up is the heat sponge, and the bill is smaller than it looks. \autoref{sec:argon_slug_thermo} also finds the sponge somewhere other than the propellant, in the ice the ship is carrying as payload. Two loads reach hardware. The booked \SI{4.9}{\kilogram} of carbon ablation per pulse provides about \SI{585}{\mega\watt} of sublimation capacity at two pulses per second. The earlier \SI{43}{\mega\watt} flash estimate is not a solved argon liner load. \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} gives the water reference emission; an argon load cannot be assigned from that calculation. The shell's induced current is \SI{13.4}{\mega\watt}, which over \SI{426}{\kilogram\per\second} of argon is \SI{31.5}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram}. Store the argon solid at its \SI{83.8}{\kelvin} triple point and that lands within \SI{7}{\percent} of the \SI{29.5}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} it takes to melt \cite{crc_handbook}, and the channel has only to stay above the triple point's \SI{69}{\kilo\pascal}, below which argon skips the liquid and sublimes at \SI{193}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} instead. The coolant then enters solid and leaves as saturated liquid rather than as two-phase flow through channels backed by an eight tonne shell. Vaporizing the whole flow absorbs \SI{69}{\mega\watt} against that \SI{13.4}{\mega\watt}, so the coolant runs short only if the load rises fivefold. Past that the split is adjustable, and the induced current is the half that adjusts. A pickup winding can route part of it into a refractory resistor instead of leaving all of it in the shell, which puts the heat somewhere allowed to run hot. Graphite at \SI{1200}{\kelvin} sheds \SI{118}{\kilo\watt\per\square\meter}, so the whole \SI{13.4}{\mega\watt} leaves through \SI{114}{\square\meter}, and at \SI{2000}{\kelvin} through \SI{15}{\square\meter}. That energy is dissipated either way, so relocating it costs no thrust. The argon then takes only what is left. \autoref{sec:argon_slug_thermo} works these numbers. The bag survives but stops being a pressure vessel. Argon cannot be a mist at the bag's \SI{0.32}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}, because saturated argon vapor at its triple point is \SI{3.95}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} against water's \num{0.0048}, twelve times too dense to sit over liquid there. Sprayed argon flashes instead, freezes, and settles near \SI{60}{\kelvin} as \SI{22}{\percent} vapor over solid snow at \SI{0.88}{\kilo\pascal}, against the \SI{8.7}{\kilo\pascal} of the steam bag. \autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} then returns \SI{0.5}{\kilogram}, well under the handling floor of \autoref{tab:axial_bag}, so what is left is a membrane. The film gets easier with it. Polyethylene's \SI{423}{\kelvin} melting point clears the steam bag by \SI{107}{\kelvin} and this one by \SI{363}{\kelvin}. The objection that nearly disqualified it above stops applying. One cost no design move removes. Ice is stable across the whole \SIrange{122}{278}{\kelvin} the cycle sees, while argon needs a cold tank at both ends and a sunshade on every projectile, each of which is destroyed on arrival. The mission value of argon's chemistry advantage depends on how many overtake pulses occur at those lower closing speeds. The companion mission calculation has not yet supplied that fraction. Near the Sun, water's dissociation penalty is small and its heat capacity remains useful. Dissipated energy grows as the square of the closing speed while the momentum it yields grows as the first power, so the heat to dispose of per newton-second rises in proportion to that speed, and the leg that has to dispose of the most is the leg that can least afford to carry a cryogen.

Between those two ends sits a compromise, and it is the heat that decides how far along it the design lands. Nothing requires the slug to be one substance. Water carries the heat and argon carries everything else, so a slug split between them buys as much argon as the heat budget leaves room for. The shape is easy, because water needs a bag and argon does not. One bag of mist with a sprayed argon cone around it is simpler than two bags. What the split buys is not proportional across both of argon's gains, and the difference deserves saying out loud. Bond energy is a mass average, so \autoref{tab:argon_water_split} falls smoothly from \num{0.948} at no water to \num{0.735} at all water. Conductivity does not behave that way. The collision rate a drifting electron sees is a sum over everything it can hit, so the worst scatterer sets it. A water molecule scatters roughly fifty times harder than an argon atom, and a kilogram of water supplies \num{6.7} times as many particles to scatter off. Water's scattering therefore equals argon's at \SI{0.3}{\percent} of the slug by mass. Half and half is twice as conducting as water and a hundred and seventy times less conducting than argon. The chemistry comes in proportion and the conductivity does not come at all. One geometric caveat could change that, and it is worth flagging rather than claiming. The field grips the plume through a current sheet at the plume's outer surface, so what matters is the conductivity there rather than the average through the volume. Argon sprayed as a sheath around a water core puts the good conductor where the field is. Whether that arrangement survives the mixing of a fireball belongs with the radiation-hydrodynamic calculation this section keeps owing.

The same argument may carry to the near-Sun cycle, on one side of it. The prograde three quarters of the split in \autoref{sec:jupiter_gravity_initial} carry their reaction mass aboard a shielded rocket, so water is admissible there and brings the same replacement for radiators to a chamber running far hotter than this one. The retrograde quarter cannot use it. Those projectiles are released to fly their own Sun-grazing orbit with no shield of their own, which is why \autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats} builds them from carbon and ceramic instead. Water would be gone long before periapsis. We have not priced a near-Sun slug and coolant budget, so this is an observation rather than a result.

\subsubsection{A Needle Through Fog}\label{sec:needle_through_fog}
There are two ways to make a projectile couple to a slug it currently ignores. Widen the projectile until it covers the bag, or narrow the bag until it matches the projectile. The first is the obvious one and it loses, for a reason that has nothing to do with mass.

A head-on nozzle is a chamber with a hole in the end the projectile came through. Roughly half the collision's thermal energy initially travels toward that end (\autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}). The field must redirect the resulting flow. In this collisional plume, escape through the entrance is a fluid-flow problem governed by the aperture and the pressure distribution (\autoref{sec:jet_efficiency}).

Neutral atoms exchange momentum with ions through collisions, so the un-ionized fraction is not a separate ballistic loss fraction. A small entrance limits the physical opening through which the coupled gas can escape, though its area alone does not determine the loss. Spreading \SI{25}{\kilogram} of impactor across a \SI{3}{\meter} bore opens the whole end of the chamber. Keeping the impactor compact leaves an opening about \SI{0.15}{\meter} across into a bore of \SI{28}{\square\meter}, which is \SI{0.25}{\percent} open.

The simplest entrance leaves the small port open and budgets the escaping gas. For a mixed chamber, the useful area comparison is with the nozzle's sonic throat, where the flow reaches the sound speed, rather than its expanded exit. If that leakage costs too much impulse, a continuously rotating disk with an aperture could provide a timed, leaky closure, as in \autoref{sec:vacuum_airlock}. An empty flight tube between the disk and the first impact gives it both the projectile's inward flight time and the gas front's return time to close. For illustration, a \SI{1}{\meter} projectile arriving at \SI{50}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, with impact \SI{10}{\meter} beyond the disk and a returning front at \SI{20}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, leaves \SI{0.68}{\milli\second} after tail clearance. Covering a \SI{0.15}{\meter} port then requires an edge speed near \SI{220}{\meter\per\second}. The actual front speed sets the timing margin. A longer rod delays tail clearance, and the disk must withstand the pulse pressure and remain closed until discharge.

\autoref{tab:two_leg_growth} prices what that is worth. Moving $e_2$ from 0.6 to 0.8 multiplies the fleet by fourteen over eleven cycles. A second argument points the same way and costs nothing extra. A compact arrival might be expected to couple poorly, sweeping a narrow column and leaving most of the slug behind, but the snowplow front widens itself as it goes, for the reason given next, so a \SI{0.15}{\meter} arrival still ends up sweeping most of the bag and delivers $k = 7.09$. That is close to the slug ratio the growth chain would choose for itself, while a full-bore arrival delivers 8.52 and overshoots it, returning \SI{92}{\percent} of the achievable growth against \SI{99.7}{\percent} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. No amount of bag film or slug water competes with either effect, so the projectile stays compact and the bag has to change shape.

That the front widens at all is worth deriving, because the needle-through-fog arithmetic above assumed it does not. Treat the front as rigid and a \SI{0.15}{\meter} rod sweeps a \SI{0.15}{\meter} column the whole way, delivering $k = 0.02$ and coupling to almost nothing. A real front cannot stay that narrow. The freshly shocked layer at its nose takes $v^2/2$ of specific energy, which at \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} is about \SI{1040}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}. Inverting that through the same dissociation and ionization ladder used above puts the layer near \SI{94600}{\kelvin}, where the sound speed is \SI{21.1}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. A layer that hot vents sideways at its own sound speed while the front advances at \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, which is a half-angle of \SI{24.9}{\degree}, very nearly half the closing speed.

That is fast enough to change the problem. Spreading at that angle, a \SI{0.15}{\meter} arrival reaches the wall of a \SI{3}{\meter} bore after about \SI{6}{\meter} of a \SI{23.8}{\meter} column, so it runs the remaining three quarters of the crossing at full bore and ends up sweeping $k = 7.1$ rather than 0.02. The needle problem is real at the start of the crossing and solves itself thereafter. Two checks bound the estimate. Venting the shocked layer sideways until its pressure balances the cold cloud's ram pressure gives a spreading speed \SIrange{1.6}{1.9}{} times the sound speed, so taking the sound speed is the conservative choice; and the shock compression ratio, which is the weakest input, moves the answer by only \SI{9}{\percent} across a range from two to sixteen. The compact projectile the paper wants for the aperture therefore pays no coupling penalty for being compact.

Narrowing the bag is cheaper than it looks, because of the invariance found above. The standoff volume is set by $PV = nR_gT$ and does not care about shape, so pouring the same \SI{660}{\cubic\meter} into a long tube instead of a ball gives a bore of
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bore_from_length}
r = \sqrt{\frac{V}{\pi \ell}}
\end{equation}
for a column of length $\ell$. Bore falls as the inverse square root of length. What it costs is conductor. A solenoid of length $\ell$ making field $B$ needs $NI = B\ell/\mu_0$ ampere-turns, and each turn is $2\pi r$ of tape, so the tape mass runs as $B r \ell$, the field times the area of the magnetic wall being built \cite{rebco_tapes}. Substituting \autoref{eq:bore_from_length} makes that $B\sqrt{V\ell/\pi}$, which grows as the square root of length. \textbf{Bore and conductor therefore trade inversely, one for one. Halving the bore doubles the tape.} Structure mass does not enter, because the virial floor of \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} tracks contained energy, and contained energy is the $nR_gT$ that shape leaves alone.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Pouring the same \SI{660}{\cubic\meter} of standoff volume into columns of different length. Bore uses the cylindrical volume approximation of \autoref{eq:bore_from_length}; the film columns use the capsule model. Bore falls as $\ell^{-1/2}$ and conductor rises as $\ell^{1/2}$, both normalized to the sphere of the same volume. $F$ is the shape factor of \autoref{eq:bag_shape_factor}. The pressure column follows \autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} at \autoref{tab:bag_state}'s Earth-storage vapor state, which is the case that has to hold pressure, and from cold Jupiter storage the slug boils about an eighth as much water into a colder mist, so that column runs \SIrange{0.51}{0.67}{\kilogram} across the same lengths and never leaves the handling floor. That is a tenth of the Earth column. The handling column is what a membrane of the same area costs at a gauge between \SI{6}{\micro\meter} and \SI{25}{\micro\meter}, since a bag standing off no pressure at all still has to be fabricated, packed, deployed and inflated. A real bag pays the greater of the two \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The two do not scale alike. A capsule's membrane area is exactly $2\pi r \ell$, because the hemispherical caps put back what shortening the cylinder removed, so with the volume fixed the area goes as $\sqrt{\ell}$ and never saturates while $F$ saturates at 2. Recomputed at \SI{660}{\cubic\meter} with \texttt{simulations/bag\_geometry.py}, using the companion calculation at revision \texttt{b4ddcf0}.}
    \label{tab:axial_bag}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|r|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Column length} & \textbf{Bore radius} & \textbf{Conductor} & \textbf{$F$} & \textbf{Area} & \textbf{Pressure} & \textbf{Handling} \\\hline
        \SI{10.8}{\meter} (sphere) & \SI{5.40}{\meter} & 1.00 & 1.50 & \SI{367}{\square\meter} & \SI{4.8}{\kilogram} & \SIrange{2.0}{8.4}{\kilogram} \\\hline
        \SI{16}{\meter} & \SI{3.62}{\meter} & 0.99 & 1.82 & \SI{364}{\square\meter} & \SI{5.9}{\kilogram} & \SIrange{2.0}{8.4}{\kilogram} \\\hline
        \SI{23.78}{\meter} & \SI{2.972}{\meter} & 1.21 & 1.91 & \SI{444}{\square\meter} & \SI{6.2}{\kilogram} & \SIrange{2.5}{10.2}{\kilogram} \\\hline
        \SI{32}{\meter} & \SI{2.56}{\meter} & 1.41 & 1.94 & \SI{515}{\square\meter} & \SI{6.3}{\kilogram} & \SIrange{2.8}{11.8}{\kilogram} \\\hline
        \SI{50}{\meter} & \SI{2.05}{\meter} & 1.76 & 1.97 & \SI{644}{\square\meter} & \SI{6.4}{\kilogram} & \SIrange{3.6}{14.8}{\kilogram} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Nothing in \autoref{tab:axial_bag} picks a row, so something outside the physics has to. We let the launch envelope decide. A \SI{5.4}{\meter} bore means coils about \SI{11}{\meter} across, which has to be assembled in orbit. A \SI{3}{\meter} bore means coils about \SI{7}{\meter} across at the chamber, inside an \SI{8}{\meter} fairing. The magnet still does not fly up in one piece, because the winding flares downstream to about \SI{10.3}{\meter} across at the exit (\autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}), so it ships in sections and is joined once. What the narrow bore buys is a magnet that is fairing-sized along most of its length and oversized only at the exit, against a sphere that is oversized everywhere. Length is the cheap dimension for a rocket to carry and diameter is the expensive one, and \autoref{sec:mass_interest} already sells the seed vehicle as a ship one Starship can loft. At the adopted \SI{660}{\cubic\meter} volume and aspect ratio $\ell/(2r)=4$, the column is \SI{23.78}{\meter} long with a \SI{2.972}{\meter} radius. Its cross-section is \SI{27.76}{\square\meter} and its mean slug density is \SI{0.323}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}. The conductor increases by a fifth and the pressure film by \SI{1.3}{\kilogram} relative to the equal-volume sphere.

The film is the part worth being explicit about, because it is the number a reader expects to be large. It is not. The shape factor moves the bag from \SI{4.8}{\kilogram} to \SI{6.2}{\kilogram}, which is \SI{2.9}{\percent} of the slug. Those are the Earth-storage figures of \autoref{tab:bag_state}. That is under a third of what the polyester bag rejected earlier would have cost, and it buys a bore small enough to launch. The plug described next takes it further down. Working as a heat sink it removes enough of the boiling load to bring the pressure film to \SI{4.5}{\kilogram}. That is under the \SI{5.2}{\kilogram} that \SI{12.7}{\micro\meter} of polyethylene weighs over the column's \SI{444}{\square\meter}, itself inside the \SIrange{2.5}{10.2}{\kilogram} handling range \autoref{tab:axial_bag} prints for that row \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The flown bag is handling-governed at both ends of the burn. Cold storage does it on the Jupiter leg and the plug does it on the Earth one.

Launched cold from Jupiter the slug finishes melting and boils a few percent of itself, so \autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} returns a fraction of a kilogram. A fraction of a kilogram is not the mass of a bag. What is left is a handling floor, the gauge a membrane needs in order to be fabricated, packed, deployed and inflated rather than in order to hold anything in. Echo flew orbital balloons on films in this class \cite{nasa_echo_ii_1966}, and the gauge is not settled closely enough here to print one number. Over the \SI{444}{\square\meter} of the \SI{23.78}{\meter} column, \SIrange{6}{25}{\micro\meter} of polyethylene is \SIrange{2.5}{10.2}{\kilogram}, or \SIrange{1.2}{4.8}{\percent} of the slug \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The bag pays the larger of the two models, so cold storage removes the pressure vessel rather than the bag.

The two models also scale differently, and the difference points the same way the launch envelope does. A pressure vessel's film does not depend on bore at all and rises only through $F$, which gains 8\% between \SI{16}{\meter} and \SI{50}{\meter}. A membrane pays for area instead, and area goes as $\sqrt{\ell}$, which gains \SI{77}{\percent} over that same span. Stretching the bag into a longer column therefore costs film faster than the shape factor alone suggests, which is one more reason to stop at \SI{23.8}{\meter}. The slug itself does not grow at all. The ratio $k$ comes from the ignition window and the launch budget, not from the bag's shape.

A narrow bag alone still does not solve the needle problem, because a rod crossing \SI{23.78}{\meter} of \SI{0.323}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} mist meets \SI{7.7}{\kilogram\per\square\meter} and is still nine hundred times denser than that. What couples the two is a solid plug of ice at the end of the column where the projectile enters. Now the projectile hits something of its own density, and the governing rule is the hydrodynamic penetration law: a projectile striking a target of comparable density erodes over a depth of about its own length times $\sqrt{\rho_p/\rho_t}$ \cite{birkhoff1948lined}. Ice on ice makes that square root equal to one, so the plug has to be at least as thick as the projectile is long. A \SI{25}{\kilogram} ice rod \SI{0.1}{\meter} in radius is \SI{0.87}{\meter} long, so a plug of the same width and half the mass is only \SI{0.43}{\meter} thick and lets a residual jet through into the mist. We size the plug at \SI{37.5}{\kilogram}, half again the projectile's mass, and let the extra go into width, since the crater runs wider than the rod.

The plug is better than free twice over. It adds nothing to the film itself, because \autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} counts only the vapor fraction $x$ and condensed water exerts no pressure on the bag. And it works as a heat sink on the way to being a target. The plug has to stay condensed, not frozen, so it can absorb the full \SI{0.65}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} of warming and melting from \SI{122}{\kelvin} without becoming vapor. Over \SI{37.5}{\kilogram} that is \SI{24.5}{\mega\joule} of the pulse's \SI{138}{\mega\joule} waste-heat bill removed before the boiling step ever sees it, a sixth of the whole bill. From cold storage the plug is what keeps the bag dry rather than what lightens it. The bare slug melts through by \SI{0.076}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, and the plug absorbs more than that, so the column boils nothing at all. The figures below are therefore the Earth-storage case, the one every film mass in this section is quoted at. The vapor mass falls to \SI{28.9}{\kilogram} from the \SI{39.3}{\kilogram} it would reach without the plug, and the saturation curve brings the mist down from \SI{316}{\kelvin} to \SI{310}{\kelvin} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Polyethylene keeps \SI{113}{\kelvin} of margin below its melting point.

Ice is also the right material for the projectile, and the argument is chemistry rather than cost. Making the projectile out of the same substance as the slug leaves the merged blob a single material, so \autoref{tab:seed_window} and the \SI{85.1}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} ignition budget describe all \SI{238}{\kilogram} instead of \SI{213}{\kilogram} plus a contaminant. Polyethylene is the obvious alternative and it breaks the recombination loan that \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} depends on. \SI{25}{\kilogram} of polyethylene carries \SI{21.4}{\kilogram} of carbon, which is \SI{1.78}{\kilo\mole}, and carbon scavenges oxygen into carbon monoxide. The C--O bond is the strongest in diatomic chemistry at \SI{11.1}{\electronvolt} \cite{crc_handbook}, so that oxygen does not come back on a hundred-microsecond expansion. Fifteen percent of the slug's oxygen never re-forms water, and since the loan is \SI{50.9}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} out of an \SI{85.1}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} budget, that costs about nine percent of the ignition energy for nothing. Ice's own weakness is the cruise rather than the impact. A bare ice body near \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} does not sit at the \SI{278}{\kelvin} a rock would. It cools until the latent heat carried off by sublimation, plus what it radiates, balances the sunlight it absorbs. Setting \SI{340}{\watt\per\square\meter} of averaged absorbed flux against $\sigma T^4$ plus $L$ times the sublimation rate puts that balance near \SI{194}{\kelvin}, where ice's vapor pressure is \SI{0.069}{\pascal} \cite{marti1993ice} and the free-evaporation rate, from the kinetic-theory flux $P\sqrt{M/2\pi R T}$ that a surface loses into vacuum \cite{knudsen1915verdampfung}, is \SI{7.9}{\kilogram\per\square\meter} per day. Sublimation is what holds the temperature down. At that balance the latent heat carried off is \SI{259}{\watt\per\square\meter} against only \SI{81}{\watt\per\square\meter} radiated, so the evaporating term is three times the radiating one. The \SI{25}{\kilogram} rod is the \SI{0.87}{\meter} cylinder above, which has \SI{0.61}{\square\meter} of surface and therefore carries \SI{41}{\kilogram\per\square\meter}. It would be gone in about five days \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Behind the detachable multilayer sunshade already flown in \autoref{sec:lox_puffsat} the projectile sits near \SI{150}{\kelvin}, where the same arithmetic gives \SI{0.58}{\kilogram\per\square\meter} across a two-year cruise, about \SI{1.4}{\percent} of the rod. Not free, but affordable. The shade is not optional and the reason is the steepness of the vapor-pressure curve. Dropping the surface from \SI{194}{\kelvin} to \SI{150}{\kelvin} cuts the evaporation rate by four orders of magnitude. Guidance hardware and structure still want a polymer, so the projectile is a hybrid and the question is the ratio rather than the choice.

One consequence of the plug reaches past the bag and into the magnet. Concentrating the collision at one end means the fireball sweeps the mist as a snowplow rather than merging with all of it at once, and a snowplow's pressure falls as it goes, because it is spreading a fixed amount of dissipated energy through a growing volume. Working \autoref{eq:bore_from_length}'s geometry against the swept mass gave the original bag-referenced field requirement near \SI{20}{\tesla} a meter in, \SI{12}{\tesla} at \SI{3}{\meter}, \SI{9}{\tesla} at \SI{6}{\meter}, and \SI{5}{\tesla} at the exit. We hold the graded profile downstream of wall contact and flatten the upstream field into a shelf.

The field must keep plasma off the graphite liner, rather than inside the vaporized bag's original boundary. In the companion snowplow model, expanding beyond the bag adds no swept mass because the clearance gap is empty. Measuring contact against a constant \SI{3.50}{\meter} wall radius gives a maximum field demand of \SI{10.95}{\tesla} across the tested closing speeds at the faster spreading bracket \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. The flared liner lies at or outside that radius, making this contact estimate conservative within the prescribed model. We therefore adopt an \SI{11}{\tesla} peak. The sound-speed spreading case gives \SI{8.25}{\tesla}, but does not cover the faster bracket. Taking the sound speed understates swept mass in the coupling estimate, but also delays wall contact and understates the required peak field. The model caps swept area at the bag boundary; it does not resolve how lateral expansion into the clearance gap changes the flow.

We retain the \SI{5}{\tesla} exit pending evaluation of a profile graded against the liner along its full length. That exit field was also derived from bag-referenced standoff pressure, rather than an independent collision requirement. The existing \SI{12}{\tesla} expansion and winding calculations remain reference results, and their mass estimates remain the budget carried here. They have not been rerun at \SI{11}{\tesla}.

For the earlier \SI{12}{\tesla} reference cap, the original first-order estimate puts field energy at \SI{88}{\percent} and tape at \SI{96}{\percent} of the uncapped values. That reference cap also improves the modeled expansion efficiency. Reducing the chamber field from \SI{20}{\tesla} to \SI{12}{\tesla}, with the exit held at \SI{5}{\tesla}, reduces the expansion area ratio from 4.0 to 2.4. More thermal motion remains at the exit, but the denser plume has a lower Alfv\'en speed. It reaches the model's release condition sooner and suffers less downstream divergence. Without an extension, the net gain in $\eta_{\mathrm{exp}}$ is \numrange{0.045}{0.155} across the two closing speeds and both expansion branches \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. \autoref{sec:jet_efficiency} defines this expansion factor and distinguishes it from the full impulse efficiency.

Commercial high-temperature superconducting magnets are already built to about \SI{20}{\tesla} at \SI{20}{\kelvin} \cite{creely2020sparc}. At an \SI{11}{\tesla} peak that working point is a comfort rather than a requirement. The field must be graded in space and static in time. \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} keeps induced current out of the superconductor on purpose, so a coil field that deliberately changes during a pulse would break the quench design the same section argues for.

\subsubsection{A Water-Injected Pusher Plate for the Overtake Leg}
\label{sec:water_injected_overtake}

Our preferred candidate for the overtake leg is a water-injected pusher plate. The craft sprays carried water into the incoming PuffSat flow near its rear-facing plate. Collision heat turns the added water into expanding exhaust, whose pressure pushes the plate. This is bulk reaction mass, separate from a thin protective coating. Once an unaugmented pusher plate is available, the added hardware is a water tank and injection system. Mechanical pressure coupling removes the magnetic nozzle's need for superconducting coils and a conducting plume.

The short pulse also changes the thermal constraint. The gas need not remain below the sustained chamber-temperature limit that favors light gases such as hydrogen in a material nozzle. Orion provides a precedent for transferring impulse from hot plasma through brief exposure and an ablative facing \cite{projorion}. Pulse heat deposition and peak stress still bound the plate design. The water loading can therefore be chosen for impulse and carried-mass economy without imposing that chamber-temperature ceiling.

Mixing one kilogram of incoming material with $k$ stationary kilograms reduces the mixture's bulk drift to $w/(1+k)$, where $w$ is closing speed. Its bulk momentum remains unchanged, but that identity omits the pressure driven by collision heat. A plate receiving the hot mixture can accelerate the added water into backward exhaust. For projectile mass $m$, define $\beta=J/(mw)$, where $J$ is the complete impulse delivered to the craft. Energy and momentum conservation give the same ideal ceiling for material and magnetic redirection,
\begin{equation}
    \beta_{\max}=1+\sqrt{1+k}.
    \label{eq:water_plate_ceiling}
\end{equation}
This ceiling assumes cold stationary water, negligible craft recoil, and complete conversion of incoming kinetic energy into backward exhaust at a single speed \cite{Katz_water_injected_plate_2026}. It does not assign a flat plate the collimation efficiency of a magnetic nozzle.

For a representative overtake that accelerates the craft through \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, closing speed falls from \SI{68}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{57}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. With constant $k$ and $\beta$, integrating the impulse while consuming carried water gives
\begin{equation}
    \frac{M_f}{m_{\mathrm{in}}}
    =\frac{k}{(68/57)^{k/\beta}-1},
    \label{eq:water_plate_delivery}
\end{equation}
where $m_{\mathrm{in}}$ is total incoming PuffSat mass and $M_f$ is the mass remaining after the push. The ideal ceiling gives \num{19.37} at $k=9$ and \num{19.80} at $k=10$. Exceeding fifteen requires $\beta>3.379$ and $\beta>3.454$, respectively. These are performance requirements for insertion into the approximately \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second} elliptical parking orbit under the impulsive insertion approximation. The delivered mass includes the craft and its plate, excludes consumed water, and makes no allowance for ascent losses or launcher overhead. The two loadings are candidate operating points, not a computed optimum for the sprayed plate.

Water's chemistry need not impose the frozen dissociation loss found on the magnetic nozzle's expansion history. Argon avoids molecular dissociation altogether. Water instead can recover some of that energy as its hydrogen and oxygen recombine during cooling. The companion \texttt{puffsat\_impact\_simulation} study finds that this return overlaps appreciable wall loading \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026,Katz_water_injected_plate_2026}. At \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and $k=8.5$, its finite-rate neutral-chemistry calculation returns about \SIrange{1.3}{1.4}{\giga\joule} by \SI{193}{\micro\second} and \SI{1.7}{\giga\joule} by \SI{250}{\micro\second}. These are about \SIrange{23}{25}{\percent} and \SI{31}{\percent} of the molecular energy still stored at \SI{153.5}{\micro\second}. Nominal reaction rates nearly reproduce the equilibrium return on this history; the remaining store is principally a consequence of the temperature history.

The reference flow also delivers \SI{2.63}{\mega\newton\second} of wall impulse by \SI{1}{\milli\second}, against \SI{1.92}{\mega\newton\second} without added water. Peak wall pressure rises to \SI{5.79}{\giga\pascal}. This calculation uses stratified water against a planar adiabatic wall. It establishes a pressure-driven gain, but does not determine the performance of a finite plate with local spray mixing, edge escape and wall losses. The chemistry calculation follows prescribed parcel density histories rather than feeding its reaction rates back into the flow. The reported energy return therefore cannot yet be converted into a predicted mass-delivery ratio above fifteen.

\subsubsection{Comparing the Overtake Devices}\label{sec:two_leg_nozzle}
The existing chain calculations below compare magnetic nozzles with unaugmented overtake plates, which carry no added reaction water.
The cycle collides twice, and the two collisions face opposite ways. On the Jupiter departure the PuffSat arrives head-on, so its momentum points backward. A plate reverses that momentum and therefore brakes the craft. Forward thrust instead requires exhaust carrying more backward momentum than the projectile brought in. At fixed collision energy, expelling added slug mass allows that larger momentum, which is why this head-on burn needs a slug and a nozzle. The Earth boost is an overtake. The returning PuffSat catches the craft from behind, its momentum already points where the craft is going, and a plate need only turn it around. Write $w$ for the closing speed, meaning how fast the two approach each other, and $k$ for the slug ratio introduced in \autoref{sec:watering_it_down}. An ideal nozzle in an overtake delivers $1+\sqrt{1+k}$ times the arriving momentum, against $\sqrt{1+k}-1$ head-on. Set $k$ to zero in the overtake and the first expression gives exactly 2, a perfect elastic bounce, which is what a plate does. That coincidence is arithmetic and not physics. A magnetic field steers a conductor, and a slug that vanishes dissipates nothing in the merge, so there is no plasma to steer. A plate is a genuinely different device rather than a nozzle run empty.

An efficiency shortfall changes delivered impulse differently on the two legs. For projectile mass $m$, closing speed $w$, and slug ratio $k$, the paper's impulse model gives $I/(mw)=\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}\sqrt{1+k}+1$ on the overtake and $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}\sqrt{1+k}-1$ head-on. At fixed $k=8.52$, reducing $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ from 0.775 to 0.679 is a \SI{12.4}{\percent} efficiency decrease. Overtake impulse falls from $3.391mw$ to $3.095mw$, or \SI{8.7}{\percent}. Head-on impulse falls from $1.391mw$ to $1.095mw$, or \SI{21.3}{\percent}. These are sensitivity examples using the full ship-frame jet efficiency, not assignments of the modeled expansion factor to that efficiency.

In the magnetic-nozzle chemical model, reducing slug mass raises the chemical efficiency, but can reduce impulse per projectile. Holding other loss factors fixed, the chemical model's gross exhaust momentum peaks at $k=9.20$ for a \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} closing speed. The reference $k=8.52$ lies within \SI{0.23}{\percent} of that maximum; reducing it to 5.2 lowers gross exhaust momentum by \SI{7.8}{\percent}. A higher efficiency ratio therefore need not mean more thrust.

The mission calculation instead maximizes compounded fleet growth across the eleven flown cycles, subject to ignition and launch-return constraints \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The two-nozzle calculation chooses a separate slug ratio for each leg and holds each fixed across the cycles. It does not optimize a pulse-by-pulse loading schedule.

The magnetic nozzle also imposes an ignition constraint on $k$. Only the dissipated share of the collision heats the blob, and that share is $k/(1+k)$ spread over $1+k$ kilograms, so the energy per kilogram peaks at $k=1$ and falls away on both sides. Too much slug spreads a fixed energy too thin. Too little dissipates almost nothing and leaves the plume cold. The admissible slug ratios are therefore a closed interval rather than everything below a maximum, and $k=0$ sits outside it, which is the second reason the plate is not the small-slug limit of a nozzle. The interval has to hold at the coldest moment of the burn, not the hottest. The push accelerates the craft from rest to \SI{10.95}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so the craft climbs toward the oncoming wave and the closing speed falls as the burn proceeds. The last pulse is the coldest one. One nozzle loading flies all eleven cycles, so the fleet must take the intersection over every cycle, and the slowest cycle decides.

The cadence is what makes some cycles slow. A round trip squeezed into two Earth-Jupiter synodic periods needs a more energetic transfer, which costs more to depart on and comes home faster. Three synodic periods relax the schedule at both ends. Seven of the eleven flown cycles take the shorter route and four take the longer, and the two groups do not overlap in speed. The two-synodic returns close between \SIrange[range-phrase={ and }]{61.83}{65.13}{\kilo\meter\per\second} after departure burns of \SIrange{6.84}{7.17}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The three-synodic returns close between \SIrange[range-phrase={ and }]{56.53}{57.43}{\kilo\meter\per\second} after burns of only \SIrange{5.32}{5.54}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The slowest of them starts its push at \SI{56.53}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and ends it at \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, where the plume will light only for $k$ between 0.098 and 10.21. The best two-synodic cycle would allow 15.32 on its own. The cheap-to-leave cycles are the ones that cost the fleet a third of its slug ratio.

Plasma temperature turns out not to be what binds. The slug has to be launched from Earth, and both legs draw on the same launched kilograms. Charging two thirds of liftoff as the launcher's own propellant and a quarter of the remainder as its dry mass leaves a quarter of liftoff arriving at the intercept point, and we require the loop to return at least a fifteenth of liftoff to be worth flying against a reusable rocket. Across the sixty-four combinations swept, that budget is what stops $k$ in fifty-six of them, while the ignition window binds in one. A temperature of \SI{15000}{\kelvin} is a low bar for a \SI{46}{\kilo\meter\per\second} impact. This charge lands on the pusher plate too, which had never been asked to pay it, and it moves the top two rows of \autoref{tab:mass_interest_growth} without touching anything below them.

These recoveries act on the impulse left after the incoming momentum is debited, so unlike the jet efficiency of \autoref{eq:ve_general} they carry no thrust floor and no entry here reverses.
