\autoref{tab:equivalent_plate} expresses each two-nozzle result as the unaugmented plate restitution needed to match it. At $e_1=e_2=0.6$, two nozzles return \num{6.63e4}, against \num{7827} for an unaugmented plate at $f=0.6$ with the same departure recovery. At 0.8 the returns are \num{2.83e7} and \num{7.83e5}, a factor of 36. At 0.25, the nozzle's launch budget forces $k$ toward the bottom of its ignition window, and the two-nozzle return falls below the plate chain's. These comparisons precede the magnetic-nozzle chemistry charge.

The unaugmented plate's restitution is supported by simulations in the overtake speed range. A hydrodynamic sweep across \SIrange{45}{63}{\kilo\meter\per\second} returns $f=\numrange{0.817}{0.820}$ \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. We retain $f=0.8$ for that plate. The $2fmw$ impulse law describes this unaugmented reference; the water-injected plate of \autoref{sec:water_injected_overtake} also receives pressure impulse from the heated reaction mass.

Charging the magnetic-nozzle chemistry through \autoref{eq:eta_chem} narrows the advantage over the unaugmented plate. At $\eta_{\mathrm{geom}}=0.9$, two nozzles return \num{9.52e6}, against \num{4.24e5} for the chain with an unaugmented plate at $f=0.818$. At $\eta_{\mathrm{geom}}=0.8$, the corresponding returns are \num{7.72e5} and \num{7.49e4}, a factor of about 10 \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Taking $f=0.800$ instead gives a plate-chain return of \num{3.55e5} in the first comparison, widening that ratio to about 27. Restitution $f$ and nozzle recovery $e_1$ have different physical meanings and ceilings, so equal numerical values do not establish equal device performance.

The chemistry charge belongs to the modeled magnetic-nozzle expansion. That flow freezes chemically at \SIrange{0.010}{0.024}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}, with 90 to 100\% of its dissociation store still held \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. \autoref{tab:mass_interest_growth} includes the loss through \autoref{eq:eta_chem}; \autoref{tab:two_leg_growth} and \autoref{tab:equivalent_plate} do not. At $e_1=e_2=0.8$, the uncharged two-nozzle return is about 37 times the chemistry-charged result. The water-injected plate follows a different compression and cooling history, as described in \autoref{sec:water_injected_overtake}.

The overtake geometry places the incoming PuffSat and the departing exhaust at the same end of the craft. Each arrival must therefore be timed to avoid interference from the preceding pulse's plume. This constraint applies to both overtake devices. The head-on nozzle instead receives the projectile at the front and expels the exhaust at the back.

\paragraph{The two-nozzle alternative.} If magnetic nozzles are used on both legs, the two encounters require different flow geometries. We call the strong-field end the chamber and the downstream opening the exit. In the expansion model, the throat is the sonic station, where the flow reaches the sound speed, at the chamber. Thrust points prograde on both legs, so the exhaust leaves retrograde on both legs, so the exit faces the same way twice. On the departure the projectile enters the far end through a \SI{0.15}{\meter} aperture and the plume leaves through the exit, making a tube open at both ends. On the growth push the projectile enters through the exit itself, and whatever comes back out leaves the same way, making a cup closed at the ship end. The first device expands a flow already going the right way. The second reverses one going the wrong way. Those are different jobs for the same field, and \autoref{eq:reflection_baseline} prices the difference. The drift enters with opposite signs. A nozzle doing nothing returns $+\sqrt{f_d}$ head-on, because the drift already points out the back, and $-\sqrt{f_d}$ on the overtake, because it does not. At the $k = 8.5$ the cycle flies, $\sqrt{f_d} = 0.324$ against a baseline of $0.529$, so the departure nozzle supplies $0.205$ and the overtake nozzle $0.853$. The overtake does four times the work to reach the same number. The baseline itself is the same on both legs, since $|{-V} + u\cos\theta|$ is distributed like $|V + u\cos\theta|$. What differs is the floor it is measured against.

They are not, though, different field profiles. Both want a strong field at the ship end and a weak one at the exit, because on both legs the collision products end up at the ship end and the exhaust has to leave from the other. That is a useful coincidence, and it means the ship carries one bore with one gradient rather than two magnets pointing opposite ways.

The growth push must reverse the merged plume as a fluid. The collisionless mirror picture, in which each particle's angle to the field determines whether it turns back, does not apply here \cite{chen2016plasma,Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. The field acts as a wall where it can support the fluid pressure. The wall holds if $B^2/2\mu_0$ exceeds what the plume pushes with, which is its static pressure plus its ram pressure $\rho v^2$, the extra push a moving fluid delivers by being stopped. Their ratio is $Mv^2/(\gamma-1)E$, and it decides where the plug can sit. Take $\gamma = 1.2$ rather than the $5/3$ of a monatomic gas, because dissociation and ionization are absorbing energy that a monatomic gas would put into translation. Both cases below are worked at a \SI{56}{\kilo\meter\per\second} closing speed \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Put the plug at the ship end, where the projectile would reach it cleanly, and the merged \SI{62.5}{\kilogram} arrives at \SI{22.4}{\kilo\meter\per\second} with all \SI{23.5}{\giga\joule} of its dissipated energy intact. The ratio is 6.7, the wall sees \SI{1.26}{\giga\pascal}, and it would take \SI{56}{\tesla}. Put the plug at the exit end instead and the fireball snowplows the whole \SI{23.8}{\meter} of mist before it meets the wall, arriving as \SI{238}{\kilogram} at \SI{5.9}{\kilo\meter\per\second} spread through \SI{660}{\cubic\meter}. The ratio falls to 1.17, the wall sees \SI{23}{\mega\pascal}, and \SI{7.6}{\tesla} is enough.

That comparison is not the one intuition offers, and the reason is worth having. Sweeping the whole column dissipates \emph{more} energy, \SI{35.1}{\giga\joule} against \SI{23.5}{\giga\joule}, because a projectile that stops more mass leaves less of its kinetic energy behind as drift. Both cases carry the same \SI{1400}{\kilogram\kilo\meter\per\second} of momentum, so the heavier blob drifts at \SI{5.9}{\kilo\meter\per\second} where the lighter one drifts at \SI{22.4}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The ram term goes as $v^2$ and collapses by a factor of 14, while the static term rises only in step with the extra dissipated energy and is then spread through 23 times the volume. The harder collision is the cheaper one to hold. The plug therefore sits at the projectile's entrance on both legs, which is the far end on the departure and the exit end on the growth push.

What does leak is not the loss cone but Rayleigh--Taylor instability, the growth of fingers when a dense fluid is supported against acceleration by a light one \cite{chandrasekhar1961hydrodynamic}. Decelerating a plume with a field is exactly that case, since the field has no mass at all. The arithmetic is unusually clean. A ripple of wavelength $\lambda$ grows at $\gamma = \sqrt{kg}$ with $k = 2\pi/\lambda$, the stopping time is $v/a$, and writing the deceleration as $a = v^2/2d$ over a stopping distance $d$ makes the speed cancel:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rt_efolds}
\gamma t = \sqrt{2kd} = \sqrt{\frac{4\pi d}{\lambda}}
\end{equation}
A \SI{30}{\centi\meter} ripple grows \num{11} e-folds over a \SI{3}{\meter} stop and \num{4.6} over a \SI{0.5}{\meter} one. Stopping the plume gently is worse than stopping it hard, which is the reverse of the mechanical intuition. The established fix is minimum-B geometry, a field arranged to grow in every direction away from the plasma, which stabilizes these modes at the cost of giving up axial symmetry \cite{chen2016plasma}. Whether that cost is worth paying here waits on the same radiation-hydrodynamic calculation as everything else in this section.

Carrying both topologies does not mean carrying two magnets. The field has to be static during a pulse, because \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} keeps induced current out of the superconductor on purpose and a coil that deliberately swings its own field would defeat that. Static during a pulse is not static forever. The two burns of a cycle are separated by an interplanetary transfer, so recharging a persistent magnet over a day of cruise costs nothing measured against a pulse. Build one bore with independently fed coils, run the graded profile on the departure, and for the growth push put the current at the ship end and leave the rest near zero. That is a single-ended wall in the same hardware, with no moving parts.

Physically reshaping a magnet between legs is the option that sounds attractive and loses, and it loses on the structure rather than on the conductor. The virial floor of \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} runs from \SIrange{0.4}{1.2}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, and the upper end is not a material property. It is bought by winding the structure under pre-compression, so that the assembly starts out squeezed and has more room to stretch before the tape reaches its strain limit. Pre-stress is created at assembly and destroyed by taking the coil apart. A re-formable magnet therefore comes back at the plain-build \SI{0.4}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, tripling the dominant mass term. Even granting that both legs need equally heavy magnets, two fixed ones cost $2M$ against a reshapable one's $3M$, before any allowance for joints, mechanisms, and qualifying a magnet that comes apart. Superconducting tape is bendable while it is being wound and cannot stretch past about \SI{0.4}{\percent} afterwards, which is the same limit that sets the floor in the first place \cite{rebco_tapes}.

\subsubsection{Inner Planet Assist Alternatives}\label{sec:inner_planet_returns}
Spacecraft launches whose dates can follow a planetary return window have another option. Send the loaded PuffSats through an initial Earth periapsis, burn there toward a Venus or Mars encounter, and deliver the payload on a later Earth return. This trades months of inventory transit for a faster incoming stream. The \emph{200 Mile High Club} of \autoref{sec:200_mile_high} instead needs formations ready for scheduled departures, so its ordinary delivery route remains the long-ellipse injection of \autoref{sec:near_term_disposal}.

The small burn is available near Earth, where the Oberth effect converts a velocity increment into more orbital energy. It is not a \SI{300}{\meter\per\second} burn at the distant staging apogee. In a circular, coplanar transfer estimate, reaching Venus requires Earth-relative departure excess $u \approx \SI{2.50}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$; Mars requires about \SI{2.94}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. From an Earth periapsis speed $v_0$ at radius $r_i$, the prograde injection is \cite{vallado_astrodynamics}
\begin{equation}
\Delta v_{\mathrm{inj}} = \sqrt{\frac{2\mu_\oplus}{r_i}+u^2}-v_0.
\end{equation}
At \SI{200}{\kilo\meter}, starting \SI{5}{\meter\per\second} above local escape gives about \SI{274}{\meter\per\second} for Venus and \SI{382}{\meter\per\second} for Mars. Thus a roughly \SI{300}{\meter\per\second} burn can supply a Venus transfer if the outgoing direction and planetary alignment fit. These increments accelerate the loaded PuffSat and are additional to supplying its first Earth passage. The corresponding minimum-energy outbound transfers take about 146 and 259 days; the return adds another leg.

A planetary flyby can exchange heliocentric energy and angular momentum with the planet, increasing the Earth-relative speed of the returning stream. That extra speed can improve the impulse delivered per arriving PuffSat mass enough to justify the injection. For an illustrative Earth-return excess of \SI{8}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, the vacuum encounter speed at \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} would be
\begin{equation}
v_{\mathrm{enc}} = \sqrt{\frac{2\mu_\oplus}{r_i}+v_{\infty,\mathrm{return}}^2}
\approx \SI{13.61}{\kilo\meter\per\second}.
\end{equation}
That is about \SI{2.60}{\kilo\meter\per\second} faster than the near-escape delivery stream. For a collinear overtake, it adds the same amount to closing speed at a fixed rocket speed. This is a useful return-speed target, not a demonstrated \SI{300}{\meter\per\second}-to-\SI{8}{\kilo\meter\per\second} exchange. The flyby turn limit and Earth-return phasing determine the achievable excess, encounter direction, and any further burns. A high-speed crossing of Earth's orbital radius alone is not an Earth rendezvous. Those constraints decide whether the gain survives the propulsion mass and waiting time.

Inner solar system trajectories can also use solar electric propulsion, which allows gradual
$\Delta V$ accumulation over long arcs in strong sunlight. These low-thrust arcs can be paired with high-thrust burns near perihelion or during flybys, which increase orbital eccentricity and aphelion, enhancing Earth-relative velocities for PuffSat pulsed propulsion.
 
Combining inner-planet and Jupiter assists raises throughput by giving more favorable launch windows.

\subsubsection{Electric and Solar Sails}\label{sec:esails}
An electric sail deflects solar wind protons with long, positively charged tethers, producing propellantless thrust \cite{janhunen2004esail}. Its thrust falls off with solar distance as $r^{-7/6}$, more slowly than the $r^{-2}$ of photon pressure, and its thrust vector can tilt roughly \SI{30}{\degree} from the anti-Sun line \cite{janhunen_sandroos2007_esail}. Reference designs target a characteristic acceleration, the thrust per unit spacecraft mass at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, near \SI{1}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared} \cite{mengali2008esail}.

We simulated a dive-and-boost profile under this thrust model \cite{katz_esail_sim_2026}. The sail departs Earth's orbit at zero hyperbolic excess, sheds angular momentum until its perihelion drops to \SI{0.5}{\astronomicalunit}, coasts down, and then thrusts along its velocity vector back out to \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}. At \SI{1}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared} it returns after 300 days and crosses Earth's orbit at \SI{19}{\kilo\meter\per\second} relative to Earth. Diving to \SI{0.35}{\astronomicalunit} yields \SI{23}{\kilo\meter\per\second} in 1.4 years. Reaching \SI{49}{\kilo\meter\per\second} in 8 months requires \SI{5}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared}, far beyond current designs. Low thrust cannot harvest the Oberth effect. An impulsive burn at perihelion converts the well depth into orbital energy, while a gentle push adds only what it can deposit during the brief perihelion pass, so the dive helps mainly by moving the sail to where its thrust is stronger.

The simulation surfaced two constraints on flying such dives. At \SI{1}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared} the thrust is a sixth of solar gravity at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, so thrusting continuously against the direction of motion spirals the orbit down while keeping it nearly circular, and the sail comes home with almost no Earth-relative speed. The sail must instead brake only along an arc near aphelion, which pins the aphelion at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} while the perihelion drops. A planar flip to a retrograde orbit also fails, since the path passes through zero angular momentum, a solar impact trajectory. Retrograde injection therefore stays with the Jupiter assist of \autoref{sec:jupiter_gravity_initial}.

These modeled sail speeds exceed the illustrative \SI{8}{\kilo\meter\per\second} return excess considered above, without a planetary encounter window or propellant, and the sail can dive again on a roughly yearly cadence. Two limits temper the appeal. The thrust inherits the solar wind's variability \cite{janhunen_sandroos2007_esail}, so sails deliver coarse trajectories, and terminal precision stays with ballistic coasts plus the fine-control thrusters of \autoref{sec:fine_control_thrusters}. The quoted acceleration also assumes a bare sail. A sail hauling several times its own mass in PuffSats slows to \SIrange[range-phrase={--},range-units=single]{0.2}{0.3}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared} and round trips of 2 to 3 years, so electric sails fit best as swarms of small, self-delivering momentum carriers rather than freighters.

Solar sails could fly the same profile on photon pressure \cite{mcinnes1999solar_sailing}, but their mass budget is harsher. Sunlight at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} exerts at most \SI{9.1}{\micro\newton\per\square\meter} on a perfect reflector, closer to \SI{8}{\micro\newton\per\square\meter} for a real aluminized film, so a sail system must stay under about \SI{8}{\gram\per\square\meter} of areal density to reach \SI{1}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared} with nothing attached. Pushing a \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} PuffSat at that acceleration takes \SIrange{5000}{8400}{\square\meter} of sail, a square 70 to 90 meters on a side massing \SIrange{15}{42}{\kilo\gram} \cite{katz_esail_sim_2026}, and that assumes \SIrange{3}{5}{\gram\per\square\meter}. LightSail 2 flew \SI{32}{\square\meter} at \SI{5}{\kilo\gram} total, about \SI{160}{\gram\per\square\meter} \cite{spencer2021lightsail2}, and IKAROS flew \SI{196}{\square\meter} at \SI{315}{\kilo\gram} \cite{tsuda2011ikaros}. The flight record is also thin and mixed. Cosmos 1 was lost to a launch failure \cite{cosmos1_2005_loss}, and NEA Scout deployed from Artemis I but was never contacted \cite{neascout2022lost}. The two electric sail tether experiments to reach orbit, ESTCube-1 and Aalto-1, both failed to deploy their tethers \cite{slavinskis2015estcube1, praks2021aalto1}, so no electric sail has yet produced measured thrust in space. Even so, a single sail returning a kilogram-scale impactor at \SI{19}{\kilo\meter\per\second} would rehearse PuffSat momentum exchange end to end at small cost, which earns the concept a place in early flight tests.

\subsubsection{Apoapsis-Raise Return With Onboard Burns}\label{sec:apoapsis_raise_loop}
The gentlest member of this family needs no third body at all. Instead of borrowing speed from a planet or the solar wind, the craft raises its aphelion with onboard burns and lets the fall back to Earth supply the closing speed. A craft leaving Earth at escape speed spends \SI{1.2}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of methalox at \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} to lift its aphelion to \SI{2.26}{\astronomicalunit}, where an argon solar-electric burn sheds \SI{4}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of tangential speed. Sunlight at \SI{2.26}{\astronomicalunit} is about a fifth of its \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} strength, so lightweight mirrors concentrating roughly five suns onto the cells, the same optics proposed for the Ceres power depots (\autoref{sec:ceresly_good}), restore roughly \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} intensity at the cell. The power plant is the least mature part of this loop. Shedding \SI{4}{\kilo\meter\per\second} over 90 days takes \SI{0.51}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared}. At an argon exhaust speed of \SI{21}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, the \SI{2200}{\second} specific impulse implied by the mass budget below, that needs about \SI{9}{\watt} of electrical power for every kilogram of departing craft, or \SI{14}{\watt\per\kilo\gram} if the burn is squeezed into 60 days. NASA's Psyche carries \SI{21}{\kilo\watt} of array at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} on \SI{2747}{\kilo\gram}, about \SI{7.6}{\watt\per\kilo\gram}, and fires one xenon thruster at a time for \SI{240}{\milli\newton}, an acceleration of \SI{0.09}{\milli\meter\per\second\squared} \cite{psyche_press_kit}. This loop asks for about a fifth more power per kilogram than Psyche carries, delivered where sunlight is a fifth as strong, with the concentrator counted in the mass. Perovskites suit such an array, a thin-film photovoltaic reaching \SI{44}{\watt\per\gram} at \SI{20.1}{\percent} efficiency in flexible form \cite{hailegnaw2024flexible}, though that is a cell-level figure and a deployed array stays limited by its structure rather than its cells. Concentration does cost one advantage of the distance. Five suns at \SI{2.26}{\astronomicalunit} put roughly \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} intensity back on the cell, so it runs warmer than an unconcentrated array, and perovskite ageing is thermally activated \cite{mavlonov2024thermal}. Trading the mirrors for five times the area would buy a colder, slower-ageing cell at the cost of the structure to hold it. Either design requires qualification under vacuum and AM0, the unfiltered solar spectrum above the atmosphere, conditions that degrade tested perovskite architectures \cite{costa2025perovskite}. A finite-thrust check confirms that a 60--90 day burn centered on apoapsis reproduces the ideal impulse to under 1\% \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The craft then falls back and meets Earth \SI{1.69}{\year} after departure, closing at about \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, just over twice the local escape speed at the \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} interception altitude. The aphelion is the free knob that closes the phasing, the same trick the resonant solar dive of \autoref{sec:earth_reintercept} uses.

Unlike the flybys and sails above, these burns are carried propellant. They total \SI{5.2}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and cost 41\% of the departing mass. The collision then pushes 2.6 times the arriving mass to escape speed (\autoref{tab:mass_scenarios}), so the fleet multiplies by 1.55 each cycle and reaches a millionfold in about 54 years, three times the 17 of the phased solar-dive loop (\autoref{sec:earth_reintercept}). What the slow loop buys is nearness. It needs no solar dive, no gravity assist, no boost node off Earth, and no engine beyond flown types, so it is a candidate first flight of the re-intercept cycle while the network is young. The engines are flown hardware; the lightweight concentrated array that drives them is not. It also leans hardest of any row in \autoref{tab:mass_scenarios} on the fudge factor. Multiplying the 0.59 dry fraction into \autoref{eq:PuffSat_ratio} gives growth of $1.93f$ per cycle, so the cascade stops growing below $f \approx 0.52$. The trajectory numbers stand on their own; the doubling economics inherit the uncertainty in $f$ at a collision energy roughly $1/40$ of the regime the fudge factor was framed around (\autoref{sec:PuffSat_ratio_approximation}).

\subsubsection{Inner Planets Combined With Lunar ISRU}
An object launched from the Moon towards Earth can reach Venus or Mars with modest additional maneuvers.  If its rockets fire at a \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} perigee, only \SIrange{300}{600}{\meter\per\second} $\Delta V$ beyond lunar escape is needed \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Gravity assists, coupled with small burns or solar-electric propulsion, could then return these payloads to the Moon on high-speed flybys.

If these payloads are PuffSats filled with lunar volatiles, they could launch several times their own mass from the Moon. This option requires mastering lunar ISRU and will likely need some initial fuel supplies from Earth. However, it offers faster launch capacity growth than PuffSat cycles that use suborbital rockets launched from Earth. This approach also avoids the need to master the prograde/retrograde chamber collisions discussed in \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}.

Near-Earth asteroids and comets are other possible sources of volatiles for PuffSats, but they have serious disadvantages. Mining in low gravity with limited available energy is difficult.  The logistics of resupply and navigating materials into Earth flyby trajectories are also challenging. Their recoverable volatile inventories depend on size, composition, accessibility, and mining method. The Moon's proximity and available oxygen make it the baseline initial target for PuffSat ISRU.

\subsection{Jupiter Aims, The Sun Multiplies}\label{sec:jovian_dive_cycle}
This paper now has two growth cycles at opposite extremes, and nothing in between. The Jupiter-only cycle of \autoref{sec:jupiter_only_growth} never goes near the Sun. Its PuffSats cross Earth at about \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and it doubles the launched mass every \SI{1.74}{\year} at a nozzle impulse recovery of 0.6 \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The direct dive of \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket} goes to 4 solar radii, crosses Earth near \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, and pays for its phasing with an Earth boost of about \SI{37.5}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, which is \SI{39.11}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at the \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} interception altitude (\autoref{sec:earth_reintercept}). One cycle is gentle and slow. The other is violent and fast. This section fills the gap between them with a family of cycles that share one architecture and differ only in how deep the dive goes.

The move is to reuse a tool the paper already has. \autoref{sec:jupiter_gravity_initial} uses a Jupiter gravity assist to place the \emph{first} low-periapsis orbit, which was the original Parker Solar Probe mission design \cite{mccomas2005solar}. Let Jupiter place the dive on \emph{every} cycle instead. The payload departs Earth for Jupiter, an unpowered flyby drops its perihelion to whatever depth we choose, it takes the Oberth boost there, and it climbs back out across \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} where Earth is waiting. Call it the \textbf{Jovian dive cycle}.

The payoff is what the Earth-side collision no longer has to buy. In the direct dive, the collision at Earth must inject the payload onto a solar-dive orbit and phase it, which is the \SI{39.11}{\kilo\meter\per\second} above. Here it only has to buy a Jupiter transfer, and a modest one. The cycle closes at a departure hyperbolic excess speed of \SI{10.54}{\kilo\meter\per\second} for the deep version, barely above the \SI{8.79}{\kilo\meter\per\second} a minimum-energy Hohmann transfer to Jupiter needs, which is \SI{15.24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Substituting \num{15.24} for \num{39.11} is the whole prize. The rocket equation is exponential in that number, so a smaller push spends far fewer PuffSats per kilogram delivered.

\subsubsection{The Clock Is Set By Jupiter, And Only One Setting Works}\label{sec:three_synodic}
The cycle has to repeat, so the next payload must find the same geometry the last one found. That forces the loop to take a whole number of Earth--Jupiter synodic periods, the \SI{1.0920}{\year} between successive alignments of the two planets \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Three works, and it is the only multiple that does.

The test is the bend. An unpowered flyby turns the arriving velocity vector by an angle set by how close the trajectory passes Jupiter, and the closer approach the paper allows is a \SI{4000}{\kilo\meter} perijove altitude. A faster arrival bends less, because the planet has less time to pull. One synodic period demands \SI{160.45}{\degree} of turning and Jupiter can supply \SI{59.07}{\degree}, short by \SI{101.38}{\degree}. Two demands \SI{121.52}{\degree} against \SI{114.68}{\degree} available, short by \SI{6.84}{\degree}. Three demands only \SI{74.73}{\degree} against \SI{133.53}{\degree}, a margin of \SI{58.80}{\degree}. Four synodic periods is \SI{4.3682}{\year}, and the longest a zero-revolution direct transfer to Jupiter can fly is \SI{3.661}{\year}, so there is no trajectory to put in the slot \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

Three synodic periods is therefore bracketed on both sides. Two cannot bend far enough and four cannot fly slowly enough. The cycle is \SI{3.2761}{\year} long, and it stays \SI{3.2761}{\year} long at every dive depth, because the clock is set by Jupiter rather than by the Sun \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. That fixed clock is what makes the next section a trade rather than a menu.

The near miss at two synodic periods is worth one line, because the obvious fix does not work. A burn at closest approach to Jupiter is the natural second knob, and sweeping it from \SIrange{0}{5}{\kilo\meter\per\second} never closes the gap. The deficit bottoms out at \SI{6.14}{\degree} because the clock binds. Braking lengthens the dive, so holding the cycle time forces a hotter outbound leg, which raises the bend demand straight back. Closing two synodic periods takes about \SI{2}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of carried propellant spent away from the flyby, and at \SI{380}{\second} of methalox specific impulse that is roughly 42\% of the departing mass \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

\subsubsection{Two Pulses, Five Days Apart}\label{sec:split_push}
The Earth-side push has to start from the pad, and the payload arrives at the \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} burn point on a ballistic lob carrying only \SI{3.60}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. Reaching the \SI{15.24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} the departure needs is therefore an \SI{11.64}{\kilo\meter\per\second} climb, and the shallow version of the cycle needs \SI{13.06}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. That is a large push to buy with collisions, and how it is flown matters more than its size.

The reason is the arrival angle. \autoref{eq:ve_general} charges the incoming projectile's own momentum against the thrust, and the sign of that term depends on where the projectile comes from. Write $\theta$ for the angle between the direction the craft is thrusting and the direction the stream arrives from. Overtaking the craft from behind is $\theta = 0$, and the projectile's momentum adds to the push. Meeting it head-on is $\theta = \SI{180}{\degree}$, and the momentum subtracts. Collecting the whole impulse law into a single factor $\beta$, the useful impulse per kilogram of arriving projectile, gives \num{3.39} at $\theta = 0$ against \num{1.67} at the \SI{124.8}{\degree} the shallow cycle's departure aim forces, with a slug ratio $k = 8.5$ of onboard reaction mass per kilogram of projectile \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The same collision is worth twice as much from behind.

The departure cannot choose its angle. It has to leave along the aim the three-synodic closure demands, and the returning stream does not point that way. What it can do is split the push in two and fly only the smaller half at the bad angle. Call that the \textbf{two-wave departure}, to keep it distinct from the \textbf{split dive} of \autoref{sec:split_dive}, which splits a heliocentric burn across months rather than an Earth-orbit one across days.

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Orient a parking orbit along the push axis and take an \textbf{overtaking push} from the lob up to it, at $\theta = 0$ and $\beta = 3.39$.
    \item Coast to apoapsis and rotate the orbit there, where the craft is moving slowly and turning is cheap.
    \item Fall back to a periapsis now aimed for departure, and fly the short canted leg at $\beta = 1.67$ \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.
\end{enumerate}

The parking orbit does most of the work, and it does not have to be a large one. Periapsis speed on an ellipse is dominated by the $2\mu/r_p$ term, so even a six-hour ellipse reaches \SI{9.87}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, which is 90\% of the \SI{11.01}{\kilo\meter\per\second} escape speed at that altitude. A five-day ellipse reaches \SI{10.86}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The overtaking push therefore covers \SI{7.27}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of the shallow cycle's \SI{13.06}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at the good angle, and the canted leg covers the remaining \SI{5.79}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

Five days is a saturation point rather than a tuned one. The rotation at apoapsis costs $2v_a\sin(\phi/2)$ for a turn of $\phi$, where $v_a$ is the apoapsis speed, and $v_a$ falls as the orbit lengthens. Stretching the parking orbit from five days to forty drops that cost from \SI{0.527}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{0.130}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and buys only 6\% more growth, while sliding Earth \SI{39.43}{\degree} around its orbit instead of \SI{4.93}{\degree}. Shortening it below about half a day reverses the whole argument, because the re-aim then costs more than the good angle saved \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Two waves of PuffSats about five days apart is the reference, and the second wave's trajectory differs from the first's by very little.

That rotation is the one impulse in this cycle that is not a PuffSat collision. No stream is present at apoapsis, so it burns carried methalox, costing 11\% of the vehicle on the deep cycle and 13\% on the shallow one \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The Jupiter-only cycle already pays a chemical apoapsis reversal of the same kind at about 6\% (\autoref{sec:jupiter_only_growth}), so this extends a charge the architecture already carries rather than introducing a new one.

\subsubsection{Perihelion Is A Dial}\label{sec:depth_dial}
Because the clock is fixed by Jupiter, the dive depth is a free parameter, and every quantity that matters moves smoothly along it. \autoref{tab:dive_depth_dial} runs the ladder.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{The Jovian dive cycle against perihelion depth, at a fixed \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} climb-out so the variation is all at the solar node \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Perihelion speed is $\sqrt{2\mu_\odot/r_p}$ and the opposing streams meet at twice it. Solar flux and equilibrium temperature are the isothermal values for a sphere, before any shield geometry. Node exhaust speed is the effective exhaust velocity the periapsis collision delivers by \autoref{eq:ve_general}, and node survival is the mass fraction left after the boost is bought at that exhaust speed. The last column is the impactor mass that delivers the same energy per pulse as a \SI{100}{\gram} body at 4 solar radii, since pulse energy scales as the square of closing speed and coil mass scales with the energy the field must contain (\autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}). Reproduce with \texttt{make dive-depth}.}
    \label{tab:dive_depth_dial}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|r|r|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{$r_p$ ($R_\odot$)} & \textbf{$v_p$} & \textbf{Closing} & \textbf{Flux} & \textbf{$T_{\mathrm{eq}}$} & \textbf{Node $v_e$} & \textbf{Survival} & \textbf{Impactor} \\
         & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & (\si{\mega\watt\per\square\meter}) & (\si{\kelvin}) & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & & (\si{\gram}) \\\hline
        4 & 308.3 & 616.6 & 3.93 & 2041 & 68.1 & 0.870 & 100 \\\hline
        6 & 251.5 & 503.0 & 1.75 & 1666 & 55.5 & 0.812 & 150 \\\hline
        8 & 217.6 & 435.2 & 0.98 & 1443 & 48.1 & 0.759 & 201 \\\hline
        12 & 177.4 & 354.8 & 0.44 & 1178 & 39.2 & 0.664 & 302 \\\hline
        16 & 153.3 & 306.6 & 0.25 & 1020 & 33.9 & 0.582 & 404 \\\hline
        24 & 124.8 & 249.6 & 0.11 & 833 & 27.6 & 0.452 & 610 \\\hline
        32 & 107.7 & 215.3 & 0.061 & 722 & 23.8 & 0.354 & 820 \\\hline
        40 & 96.0 & 192.0 & 0.039 & 645 & 21.2 & 0.279 & 1031 \\\hline
        48 & 87.3 & 174.6 & 0.027 & 589 & 19.3 & 0.222 & 1247 \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Read the two ends against each other. Backing the dive out from 4 solar radii to 32 divides the solar flux by 64 and the equilibrium temperature by 2.83, taking it from \SI{2041}{\kelvin} to \SI{722}{\kelvin}. \SI{2041}{\kelvin} is above the melting point of every structural metal, and that is the equilibrium figure for a sphere before any concentration. \SI{722}{\kelvin} is an ordinary hot-radiator problem. The opposing streams meet at \SI{215}{\kilo\meter\per\second} rather than \SI{617}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so the energy thermalised per kilogram of merged blob falls from \SI{5934}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} to \SI{724}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}, a factor of 8.2. Rendezvous timing loosens by the same 2.87 that the closing speed falls by, so \SI{100}{\meter} of along-track miss is \SI{464}{\micro\second} of relative timing error rather than \SI{162}{\micro\second} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Every row of \autoref{tab:dive_depth_dial} assumes the two streams meet head-on, which is the arrival that charges the most incoming momentum against thrust and also the one that needs the expensive retrograde placement. A near-radial plunger meets the payload at \SI{135}{\degree} instead (\autoref{sec:arrival_angle}), which halves the collision heat at every slug ratio and is worth about a factor of two in depth on that term alone. \autoref{sec:opposing_stream_depth} prices what it gives up in exchange.

That factor of 8.2 in pulse energy is the one that bears on hardware the paper has already sized. \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} shows coil mass scaling with the energy the field must contain, and it concludes that near-Sun projectiles have to shrink toward the \SI{100}{\gram} end of the \SIrange{0.1}{2.5}{\kilo\gram} band or the nozzle runs to hundreds of tonnes. It also notes that a \SI{100}{\gram} body is the hardest place to put a heat shield, and that the heavy end of the band is the thermally comfortable one. At 32 solar radii the same nozzle catches an \SI{820}{\gram} impactor \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The miniaturization requirement that made the deep cycle depend on roughly another decade of progress in gram-scale spacecraft relaxes to something close to flown hardware.

The Earth end relaxes with it. The returning stream arrives at \SI{85.07}{\kilo\meter\per\second} rather than \SI{157.82}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, which is within a third of the \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} the Jupiter-only cycle already collides at. That cycle's departure nozzle is priced in \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} at \SIrange{8.0}{15.4}{\tonne} for the cold pulse and \SIrange{17.3}{37.6}{\tonne} for the hot one, at a slug ratio of 8.5, which is the same slug ratio the shallow cycle runs. The Earth-side magnet the shallow Jovian dive cycle needs is the magnet the paper has already sized, carrying about 1.3 times the energy per kilogram of impactor \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

\subsubsection{What The Dial Costs}\label{sec:depth_cost}
None of that thermal relief is free, and \autoref{tab:jovian_dive_cycles} prices it end to end.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{The two reference Jovian dive cycles, with the Earth departure charged from the ballistic lob and split into two pulses five days apart \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Both are three-synodic closures of \SI{3.2761}{\year}. The mass fractions $f_1$ and $f_2$ are what survives the overtaking push and the canted departure leg. The re-aim fraction $f_r$ is what survives the apoapsis rotation on methalox at \SI{380}{\second} of specific impulse, \num{0.893} here and \num{0.868} on the shallow cycle, and the chain to the transfer is the product of the three. Node survival is rounded down from the impulse-law derivation, which returns \num{0.5977} at 4 solar radii and \num{0.3537} at 32. Growth is kilograms returned per kilogram of impactor spent. The last two rows are the launch ledger of \autoref{sec:two_leg_nozzle}. A quarter of the mass on the pad reaches the intercept point, so the return per pad kilogram is that quarter times the chain times node survival, against a committed floor of a fifteenth. Reproduce with \texttt{make dive-depth}.}
    \label{tab:jovian_dive_cycles}
    \begin{tabular}{|l|r|r|}\hline
        & \textbf{4 $R_\odot$, $k = 30$} & \textbf{32 $R_\odot$, $k = 8.5$} \\\hline
        Earth departure excess (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & 10.54 & 12.50 \\\hline
        Speed needed at \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & 15.24 & 16.66 \\\hline
        Jupiter arrival excess (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & 12.17 & 10.95 \\\hline
        Bend margin, unpowered (\si{\degree}) & $+58.80$ & $+54.48$ \\\hline
        Perihelion boost (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & 35.04 & 24.71 \\\hline
        Stream speed at Earth (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & 157.82 & 85.07 \\\hline
        Departure cant (\si{\degree}) & 89.9 & 124.8 \\\hline
        Burn, lob to departure (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & 11.64 & 13.06 \\\hline
        Overtaking leg $f_1$ & 0.762 & 0.791 \\\hline
        Apoapsis re-aim (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & 0.420 & 0.527 \\\hline
        Departure leg $f_2$ & 0.817 & 0.720 \\\hline
        Chain to the transfer, $f_1 f_r f_2$ & 0.556 & 0.494 \\\hline
        Node survival & 1/2 & 1/3 \\\hline
        \textbf{Growth per cycle} & \textbf{23.0} & \textbf{3.49} \\\hline
        \textbf{Doubling} (\si{\year}) & \textbf{0.724} & \textbf{1.816} \\\hline
        Millionfold (\si{\year}) & 14.4 & 36.2 \\\hline
        Returned per pad kilogram & 0.0695 & 0.0412 \\\hline
        \textbf{Versus the committed 1/15} & \textbf{1.04} & \textbf{0.62} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Per-cycle growth falls by a factor of 6.6 and the doubling time rises by only 2.5, because the \SI{3.2761}{\year} clock does not move. A 64 times cooler node, an 8.2 times gentler collision, and rendezvous timing loosened by 2.87 cost a factor of two and a half in doubling time \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

Three separate mechanisms make the deep node win, and they compound. The first is the Oberth effect. Buying a fixed escape surplus costs less impulse the faster the periapsis where it is bought, so the deep node asks for an 11.4\% rise in perihelion speed where the shallow one asks for 23.0\%. The second is that the node's own effective exhaust velocity scales with the opposing streams' closing speed, which is twice the perihelion speed, giving \SI{68.1}{\kilo\meter\per\second} against \SI{23.8}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The third is the departure cant. At 4 solar radii the aim the closure demands lands at \SI{89.9}{\degree}, where $\cos\theta = 0.002$ and the arriving momentum neither helps nor hurts. At 32 it lands at \SI{124.8}{\degree}, where $\cos\theta = -0.571$ and the momentum term is actively subtracting. All three favor the node that is hardest to build \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

The launch ledger has now been re-scored against the two-wave departure, and it separates the two ends of the dial. Every cycle in this paper is charged for its own ground launch (\autoref{sec:two_leg_nozzle}). Two thirds of liftoff is the launcher's own propellant and a quarter of what remains is its dry mass, so a quarter of the mass standing on the pad reaches the \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} intercept point, and the loop has to send back a fifteenth of liftoff to be worth flying against a reusable rocket. That check was first run on this cycle with the payload appearing at the burn point already at escape speed, which is not where a \SI{3.60}{\kilo\meter\per\second} lob leaves it. Charging the departure from the lob instead costs 32.0\% of the pad return at 4 solar radii and 31.3\% at 32. The deep cycle returns \num{0.0695} kilograms per pad kilogram and clears the floor by 4\%. The shallow cycle returns \num{0.0412} and misses it by 38\% \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

The shortfall is not a loading that can be tuned away. Pad return peaks in the slug ratio $k$ and falls off on both sides of the peak, and at 32 solar radii the best any $k$ manages is \num{0.761} of the floor, at $k = 1.93$. Above the peak the reason is direct. Every extra kilogram of slug has to be lifted off the same pad and accelerated by the same collisions, so the chain to the transfer falls from \num{0.61} at $k = 2$ to \num{0.23} at $k = 47$. At 4 solar radii the floor is cleared from $k = 0.68$ to \num{34.97}, so the $k = 30$ of \autoref{tab:jovian_dive_cycles} sits inside the window with 4\% to spare, and the same cycle flown at $k = 8.5$ returns \num{0.0884} per pad kilogram, \num{1.33} times the floor, at a doubling time of \SI{0.835}{\year} rather than \SI{0.724}{\year} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The deep cycle has a dial to trade the thin margin against. The shallow one has none.

The two-wave departure also added a leg that had not been checked for conduction. The overtaking pulse is taken at $\theta = 0$, so the craft's own speed subtracts from the closing speed, and the collision gets gentler the faster the craft is already going. That leg therefore ends colder than the canted leg ever gets. On the shallow cycle it finishes at a closing speed of \SI{74.21}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, against \SI{91.71}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at the coldest instant of the canted leg. The slug ratio cap described at the end of \autoref{sec:jovian_dive_open} asks the plume to still be conducting once the expansion is finished, and asks it to clear that bill by a factor of 1.5 rather than to sit on it. At \SI{91.71}{\kilo\meter\per\second} the cap admits $k$ between \num{2.34} and \num{8.95}, so the reference \num{8.5} scrapes in. At \SI{74.21}{\kilo\meter\per\second} it admits no slug ratio at all \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The shallow cycle fails twice over, and the two failures are independent. Its first pulse does not stay coupled to the field, and its loop does not pay for its launch.

Neither failure is tuned away by the one knob the trade leaves free, because the two constraints pull opposite ways on it. That knob is the climb-out excess, the heliocentric speed the boosted payload keeps once it has escaped the Sun, \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} on the shallow cycle and \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} on the deep one. Boosting less at perihelion spends less of the vehicle at the node, so the pad return rises. A slower climb-out also arrives at Earth more slowly, and the plume needs that closing speed to stay conducting. At a \SI{40}{\kilo\meter\per\second} climb-out, and at the same $k = 8.5$, the shallow cycle returns \num{0.0753} per pad kilogram, \num{1.13} times the floor, and doubles in \SI{1.363}{\year}, beating its own reference on both counts. Its overtaking pulse then closes at \SI{47.07}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, and below \SI{79.56}{\kilo\meter\per\second} the cap admits nothing at any slug ratio. Every climb-out that pays is barred and every climb-out that is admissible fails the floor \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

That \SI{79.56}{\kilo\meter\per\second} threshold belongs to the two-wave departure rather than to the dive, so it is the same number at every depth. The overtaking pulse ends at the stream speed at Earth less the \SI{10.86}{\kilo\meter\per\second} the parking orbit is already carrying, so the stream has to arrive at \SI{90.42}{\kilo\meter\per\second} or faster before any slug ratio conducts. The \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} climb-out of \autoref{tab:dive_depth_dial} does not reach that at any depth in the table. At 4 solar radii it arrives at \SI{89.77}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and at 32 solar radii at \SI{85.07}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so the overtaking plume stops conducting at both ends. Nothing in that table's own columns moves, since none of them touch the Earth departure, but the departure has to be flown from a faster climb-out than the one the table holds fixed, and a faster climb-out is bought at the node.

The dial therefore has a shallow end, and the two constraints set it together. At any depth the best climb-out to fly is near the slowest one that still conducts, because the pad return rises as the boost falls. It is not the slowest one exactly. The window of slug ratios whose plume survives the expansion narrows as the climb-out drops, and at the threshold it pinches shut close to $k = 4$, while the pad return peaks below $k = 2.3$. So the optimum sits a few tenths of a kilometer per second above the climb-out whose overtaking pulse closes at \SI{79.56}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. Walking that point inward along the depth dial, the committed fifteenth is lost at about 23 solar radii. At 24 the best admissible cycle returns \num{0.0635} per pad kilogram, \num{0.95} of the floor. At 23 it returns \num{0.0664}, which is \num{0.997}. At 22 it returns \num{0.0695}, the same figure the deep cycle manages, and clears by 4\%. Almost all of that fall is the solar node rather than the Earth departure, as the survival column of \autoref{tab:dive_depth_dial} shows on its own. The cycle at the crossing doubles in \SI{1.746}{\year}, within half a percent of the Jupiter-only chain's \SI{1.74}{\year}. The depth at which the dive stops paying for its launch and the depth at which it stops matching the chain it has to beat are the same depth, to the precision either is known to. Nothing in the derivation connects them \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Reproduce the crossing and the frontier it sits on with \texttt{python -m src.solar\_dive\_depth\_trade -{}-pad-frontier}, which \texttt{make dive-depth} does not run on its own.

Set against the cycles the paper already has, the 32 solar radii reference is not faster. It doubles in \SI{1.816}{\year} against the Jupiter-only chain's \SI{1.74}{\year}. Both cycles charge the Earth departure the same way, as two waves flown from a parking orbit in low Earth orbit, though this one starts from a \SI{3.60}{\kilo\meter\per\second} ballistic lob where the Jupiter chain starts from rest. The nozzle efficiencies are not the same parameter and should not be read as one. The Jupiter chain is swept on the recovery fraction, which multiplies the whole impulse, while these cycles are scored on the jet energy efficiency inside the square root of \autoref{eq:ve_general}, and the mapping between the two has not been worked \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. So the two rates are close enough to compare and too loosely matched to rank. What the shallow end of this family buys for that matched rate is a solar node and a dial, and the launch ledger says where the usable part of that dial starts. Not at 32 solar radii, which does not pay for its own launch, but at about 23. Every step inward from there is bought with thermal engineering and paid back in growth, and the deep end reaches \SI{0.724}{\year}, which neither of the paper's existing cycles approaches. The case for flying a shallow version first is that it reaches the deep version without a redesign, and the shallowest version that case can start from is the 23 solar radii dive rather than the 32. Backing the dive out from 4 to 23 solar radii still divides the solar flux by 33 and the equilibrium temperature by 2.4, so most of the thermal relief is still bought. What is not bought is the coolest end of the dial, which was the end that made the first flight the easy one.

\subsubsection{What This Does Not Settle}\label{sec:jovian_dive_open}
Three things are open, and the first is architecture rather than detail.

The opposing stream does not ride this cycle. Reaching a low perihelion from Jupiter means cancelling most of Jupiter's \SI{13.06}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of orbital motion, and an unpowered flyby rotates the arrival velocity without rescaling it. That sets a floor on the arrival speed, and the floors for the two directions spread apart as the dive gets shallower. A prograde dive needs \SI{11.96}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at 4 solar radii and \SI{9.98}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at 32; a retrograde one needs \SI{14.16}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and \SI{16.14}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. Neither cycle's own arrival reaches its retrograde floor, arriving with \SI{12.17}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and \SI{10.95}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The opposing stream therefore needs its own departure energy and its own schedule. It is not expensive, because projectiles are one-way and expendable, so their trajectory has to exist and be timed rather than close on a synodic clock. A tangential Earth departure at \SI{11.83}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of excess arrives with the \SI{16.14}{\kilo\meter\per\second} the retrograde placement needs, which is below the payload's own \SI{12.50}{\kilo\meter\per\second} departure. What the split costs is a second schedule, not a second energy budget \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Meeting the payload in longitude and in time, rather than merely arriving at the right depth, costs a further \SIrange{13}{38}{\meter\per\second}. Excess above the placement floor sweeps the perihelion-longitude gap through a full turn over roughly \SI{50}{\meter\per\second}, so a root always exists and the solutions are discrete in the same way the three-synodic closure is. The stream leaves Earth 6.7 days before the payload at 4 solar radii and 40.5 days after it at 32 \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The Jupiter-only chain of \autoref{sec:jupiter_only_growth} pays none of this, for a structural reason rather than a lucky one. Its retrograde return is flown only from the Jovian bend to the \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} crossing, so its perihelion is never reached. A chain with no dive node has no second arrival to place.

The remaining two are model scope. The closure treats Earth and Jupiter as moving on circles in one plane. The \SI{54}{\degree} of bend margin should absorb the variation that real ephemerides introduce, since the same margin on the Jupiter-only cycle survived a two-hundred-year audit, but that audit has not been run here \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. And the growth figures are upper bounds by about a percent. The opposing impactors consumed at the solar node, 1.34\% of the vehicle at 4 solar radii and 2.15\% at 32, are not charged against the growth ledger, and neither is the impulse that puts them there. Charged, the placement costs this cycle \numrange{1.0014}{1.0075} times its doubling time across the depth dial and \SIrange{0.45}{2.00}{\percent} of its pad return, flipping no verdict in \autoref{tab:jovian_dive_cycles} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. \autoref{sec:opposing_stream_depth} works that bill out and says which architectures pay the large version of it.

One caveat runs the other way and is worth stating, because it makes the numbers above conservative. The slug ratios here are capped by requiring the plume to still conduct when the expansion is finished, rather than merely to ignite when the blob merges. A magnetic nozzle works by letting the plume expand, and expansion cools it, so a blob that lights and then falls out of conduction decouples from the field partway down. That cap is computed for a water plume required to hold \SI{15000}{\kelvin} at the nozzle exit \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Both inputs are pessimistic for this architecture. \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} puts the temperature at which the field loses its grip at \SI{2450}{\kelvin} and the binding leak limit at \SI{3800}{\kelvin}, and the \SI{15000}{\kelvin} figure in that section is the plume's own state at the coldest Jupiter-cycle pulse rather than a requirement on it. The slug is also argon rather than water (\autoref{sec:argon_slug_thermo}), which pays no atomization toll at all. Argon's ionization is not a frozen loss either. Three-body recombination, in which a free electron carries off the energy released as an ion captures another electron, runs at a rate proportional to $n_e^2$ with a coefficient scaling as $T_e^{-4.5}$. At the \SI{0.32}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} of \autoref{tab:bag_sizing} and a few electron volts that is nanoseconds against an expansion of order \SI{100}{\micro\second}, and it speeds up as the plume cools. The energy comes back inside the nozzle.

That pessimism is now load-bearing, because \autoref{sec:depth_cost} rules a depth out with it, so it is worth pricing rather than asserting. Read the plume's outlet state three ways and score each reading at the climb-out that pays best while still conducting. At 32 solar radii and the factor of 1.5, requiring \SI{15000}{\kelvin} leaves the shallow node at \num{0.67} of the committed fifteenth and \SI{6000}{\kelvin} leaves it at \num{0.85}, while charging only the frozen chemistry lifts it to \num{1.01}, which is a boundary rather than a clearance. Dropping the factor to 1, which means flying exactly on the floor the factor exists to keep off, lifts \SI{15000}{\kelvin} to \num{0.97}, \SI{6000}{\kelvin} to \num{1.16} and frozen chemistry to \num{1.31}. So the plume physics decides whether the shallow node is admissible. It does not decide whether the node is worth flying, because every corner of the bracket that lets it pay leaves it doubling in \SIrange{1.81}{2.07}{\year}, slower than the Jupiter-only chain's \SI{1.74}{\year} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Those six cells come off the same scan as the crossing in \autoref{sec:depth_cost}. What would settle the bracket is the magnetic Reynolds number at the nozzle exit, and that has not been computed.

\subsection{Half Now, Half At Aphelion}\label{sec:split_dive}
The cycle above retires the Earth-side dive boost by letting Jupiter place the perihelion. A cheaper move is available without leaving the inner solar system, and it is worth pricing, because the direct dive of \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket} pays for everything at Earth. Its single-impulse resonant dive folds the phasing and the injection into one \SI{37.53}{\kilo\meter\per\second} heliocentric boost, of which \SI{28.10}{\kilo\meter\per\second} is charged from a 20-day parking orbit whose periapsis already carries \SI{10.95}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. That is the largest single number in the cycle. Reaching a 4-solar-radii perihelion from \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} is a 54:1 radius ratio. That is far past the roughly 15.6:1 where a bi-elliptic transfer always beats a direct one. So the obvious question is whether to raise aphelion first and buy the injection at the far end, where the vehicle is barely moving.

Two things have to be said before that question is asked. The first is that the paper's own trajectory is easy to mis-describe. The single-impulse resonant dive is \emph{already} a raise-then-drop. Its closing aphelion is \SI{1.9259}{\astronomicalunit}, and its \SI{37.53}{\kilo\meter\per\second} boost decomposes into \SI{28.80}{\kilo\meter\per\second} pointed radially outward plus \SI{24.07}{\kilo\meter\per\second} retrograde \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Pushing outward first is what it already does, which is why its arrival back at Earth lands a favourable \SIrange{31}{38}{\degree} off that outward push axis. What is new below is \emph{splitting} the impulse across two nodes, not the outbound leg.

The second is that \SI{37.53}{\kilo\meter\per\second} buys one of the two arrivals the dive node needs, not both. A pulse at perihelion requires the payload and an opposing stream to meet there, and placing that opposing stream is its own Earth-side bill. \autoref{tab:mass_scenarios} prices it once, at \SI{40.17}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of speed at the \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} interception altitude for a retrograde placement, but it prices it against the unphased Jupiter arrival and never carries it along the depth dial. \autoref{sec:opposing_stream_depth} does, and that leg turns out to move the opposite way from the payload's.

Call the family the \textbf{split dive} and parametrize it by the \textbf{outbound perihelion} $q$, the perihelion of the ellipse the Earth boost puts the vehicle on. At $q$ = 4 solar radii that ellipse \emph{is} the dive ellipse, the far burn is zero, and the loop reduces to the paper's own dive, same \SI{1.9259}{\astronomicalunit} aphelion and same \SI{0.8925}{\year} clock. At the other end, $q$ near \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, the Earth boost does nothing but raise the aphelion, and a burn out at that aphelion buys the whole dive. Every row in between splits the bill. On every row but the first the vehicle never reaches $q$, because the far burn drops it to 4 solar radii before it gets there.

The impulse ledger is not close. Injecting onto the 4-solar-radii dive costs \SI{24.09}{\kilo\meter\per\second} as one tangential burn at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, \SI{16.94}{\kilo\meter\per\second} through a \SI{3}{\astronomicalunit} aphelion, and \SI{12.34}{\kilo\meter\per\second} in the limit of an infinite one, which is $(\sqrt{2}-1)$ times Earth's orbital speed \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Charged from the parking orbit the Earth side collapses further. Departing \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} for a \SI{3}{\astronomicalunit} aphelion needs \SI{6.694}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of hyperbolic excess, which is \SI{12.88}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at the \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} interception altitude against the parking orbit's \SI{10.95}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. That is a \SI{1.93}{\kilo\meter\per\second} burn where the paper's dive spends \SI{28.10}{\kilo\meter\per\second}.

What the split costs is clock, and the reason is where \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} sits on the outbound ellipse. The paper's ellipse crosses \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} at a true anomaly of \SI{169.09}{\degree}, eleven degrees short of aphelion, so its outbound leg costs \SI{0.385}{\year} out of a \SI{0.8925}{\year} loop. A small prograde push puts \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} at the outbound ellipse's own perihelion, and the outbound leg becomes a half orbit. Reaching a comparable aphelion then takes \SI{0.823}{\year}, which is 2.1 times the coast, on a loop that had \SI{0.89}{\year} in it altogether \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

\subsubsection{The Cheaper Injection Buys The Pad, Not The Clock}\label{sec:split_dive_growth}
Score the family the way \autoref{sec:depth_cost} scores the Jovian cycles, at a slug ratio $k$ of 30, a jet energy efficiency $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}^2$ of 0.60, and a node survival of 0.60. Both nodes are magnetic nozzles fed by the returning stream, so the rocket equation is charged against slug at each of them and the impactor bill is the two nodes' slug divided by $k$.

Taken all the way, the split loses. A fully phased cycle through a \SI{3}{\astronomicalunit} aphelion runs \SI{2.2789}{\year} and multiplies the payload \num{20.6} times per pass, which is a doubling time of \SI{0.5223}{\year} and \num{0.875} kilograms of launched slug per delivered kilogram. The paper's dive runs \SI{0.8925}{\year} at \num{7.61} times per pass, doubling in \SI{0.3048}{\year} for \num{2.365} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The arithmetic behind that verdict has the same shape as the one in \autoref{sec:depth_cost}. Impulse enters growth logarithmically and the clock enters linearly, so 2.7 times less slug is worth 1.3 times in e-foldings against 2.55 times more clock. Normalizing the pad side by time does not reverse it. Returned kilograms per launched-slug kilogram per year run \num{0.288} for the paper's dive and \num{0.301} for the phased split, a 4.5\% edge rather than a reversal, against \num{0.852} for the Jovian cycle of \autoref{sec:jovian_dive_cycle}.

Stopping short is a different matter, and it is where the family's rate optimum sits. \autoref{tab:split_dive_curve} walks the family along the Earth re-intercept closure, which pins the aphelion for each $q$ so that the return still crosses \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} where Earth is. Doubling time is nearly flat along it while the slug bill falls monotonically.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering

The first row is the paper's own single-impulse dive, which the family contains exactly. The Earth column is the burn charged from the 20-day parking orbit, the node column is the heliocentric impulse taken at the outbound aphelion, and growth is kilograms returned per kilogram of impactor spent. The last column is the longitude gap the far node would have to close to be fed by the returning beam rather than by a dedicated delivery, and on this curve it never reaches zero. What that dedicated delivery costs is priced below, and it is what retires the interior row's claim to dominance. Reproduce with \texttt{make split-dive}.}

    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|r|r|r|r|r|}\hline

        (\si{\astronomicalunit}) & (\si{\astronomicalunit}) & (\si{\year}) & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & & (\si{\year}) & (\si{\degree}) \\\hline
        0.0186 & 1.9259 & 0.8925 & 28.101 & 0 & 7.61 & 0.3048 & 116.24 \\\hline
        0.2 & 1.9695 & 0.9696 & 18.918 & 6.210 & 9.40 & 0.3000 & 109.88 \\\hline
        0.4 & 1.9703 & 1.0278 & 13.223 & 9.425 & 10.99 & 0.2972 & 109.77 \\\hline
        \textbf{0.4918} & \textbf{1.9649} & \textbf{1.0543} & \textbf{11.052} & \textbf{10.535} & \textbf{11.72} & \textbf{0.2969} & 110.31 \\\hline
        0.6 & 1.9549 & 1.0876 & 8.689 & 11.674 & 12.64 & 0.2971 & 112.02 \\\hline

The optimum sits in the interior at $q = \SI{0.4918}{\astronomicalunit}$. Call it the \textbf{partial split}, the member of the family that stops short of the bi-elliptic end. On the two axes this ledger scores it beats the paper's dive, doubling in \SI{0.2969}{\year} against \SI{0.3048}{\year} and spending \num{1.536} kilograms of launched slug per delivered kilogram against \num{2.365} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. No impulse is added to buy that. The Earth boost was being taken anyway and the vehicle was passing through that aphelion anyway, and all that changes is that \SI{10.535}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of the \SI{28.101}{\kilo\meter\per\second} is deferred to the far end of the coast. Those slug figures are launched slug per delivered kilogram, which is not the currency \autoref{sec:two_leg_nozzle} commits this paper to. Scored on the pad instead, the split's advantage carries a depth, and \autoref{sec:self_cooling_departure} gives it.

The word to avoid here is dominating, and the price that decides it is now in. The ledger charges the impactors consumed at both nodes and nothing for delivering them to \SI{1.96}{\astronomicalunit}, which the returning beam does not reach on this member of the family. The trap is that the far node does not need mass out there. It needs mass moving at \SI{153.35}{\kilo\meter\per\second} out there, against a vehicle doing \SI{13.45}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. A Hohmann delivery arrives nearly co-moving and is worth nothing as an impactor. Buying that speed from \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} costs \SI{113.20}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of Earth departure excess against the payload's own \SI{19.12}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, and it delivers \SI{1.0}{\percent} of what is launched \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. That is the co-linear arrival, which is the cheapest one the geometry allows and therefore a bound. The perpendicular arrival the beam actually makes costs \SI{125.80}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and delivers \SI{0.6}{\percent}.

Charged, \num{1.536} kilograms of launched slug per delivered kilogram becomes \num{3.552}, against the paper's own dive at \num{2.365}. The partial split does not beat the dive it was said to dominate. The claim is withdrawn rather than held, and it is worth being clear that the delivery does not merely trim its margin. It reverses the verdict. What the reversal shows is where fast mass comes from. The returning beam carries \SI{153.0}{\kilo\meter\per\second} past the far node for nothing, because it is a climb-out from the dive and it was going that way regardless. Nothing launched from \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} matches that at a sane price, since the cheap way to make fast mass is to drop it down the Sun's well first. So the beam-fed closures of \autoref{sec:far_node_colocation} are not a convenience over the partial split. They are the only affordable source of the far node's ammunition.

The family punishes one intuition twice. The pure bi-elliptic end, $q = \SI{0.99}{\astronomicalunit}$, doubles 6\% \emph{slower} than the paper's dive despite spending less than half the slug, so a cheaper injection is not automatically a better one and the curve's own far end says so. The interior optimum then says it again, one ledger later, by winning on the axes that were scored and losing on the one that was not.

The gap that forces all of this is the last column of \autoref{tab:split_dive_curve}, which never reaches zero along this curve. Something has to put impactors at \SI{1.96}{\astronomicalunit} at the right time and at the right speed. Closing that gap, rather than paying it, is what the next subsubsection does.

\subsubsection{The Far Node Eats What Earth Missed}\label{sec:far_node_colocation}
That gap can be closed, and closing it is the structural result here. The boosted climb-out leaves 4 solar radii carrying angular momentum only $r_p v_p$, so it is very nearly a straight line outward. It is \SI{2.34}{\degree} off radial at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} and \SI{0.73}{\degree} off at \SI{3.3}{\astronomicalunit} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. A beam's \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} crossing and its \SI{3}{\astronomicalunit} crossing are therefore the same ray, \num{21.1} days apart in time and \SI{1.54}{\degree} apart in longitude. The far node needs no launch and no separate aim. It eats the share of a wave that Earth did not intercept.

Getting that for free costs geometry rather than propellant. Three conditions have to hold together.

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Earth re-intercept.} The return crosses \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} where Earth is, which is the condition \autoref{sec:earth_reintercept} already imposes.
    \item \textbf{Outer-node co-location.} The far burn happens on a ray that a beam is flying down at that moment.
    \item \textbf{Rational beam reuse.} The offset between a beam's Earth crossing and the far burn it feeds is a whole fraction $j/m$ of a cycle, so the offsets close into a repeating pattern. The denominator $m$ is the number of payload chains that have to be in flight at once.
\end{enumerate}

The first two alone never intersect. Along the whole re-intercept curve the co-location gap runs \SIrange{109.45}{121.86}{\degree} with no extra revolutions, and \SI{-55.63}{\degree} to \SI{-81.46}{\degree} with one, never crossing zero \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. That is two conditions on two knobs with no solution, which is the last column of \autoref{tab:split_dive_curve} refusing to reach zero.

The third knob is taking the far burn a few degrees past aphelion rather than at it. Out there the vehicle is slow and Earth is not, so one degree of burn-point shift moves the phasing by about \num{3.4}, and a \SI{110}{\degree} gap closes in eleven degrees of true anomaly. Three conditions on three knobs makes the solution set discrete, indexed by the revolution count and the reuse fraction, in the same way the three-synodic closure of \autoref{sec:three_synodic} is discrete. \autoref{tab:split_dive_closures} lists the ones that close.

    \centering

Each row satisfies Earth re-intercept, outer-node co-location and rational beam reuse at once, so the beam that feeds Earth also feeds the far node of a chain further round the pattern. Chains is how many payloads are in flight simultaneously, and cadence is how often a beam reaches Earth in steady state. Reproduce with \texttt{make split-dive}.}
    \label{tab:split_dive_closures}
    \begin{tabular}{|c|r|r|r|r|r|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Reuse} & \textbf{Chains} & \textbf{$q$} & \textbf{Aphelion} & \textbf{Past apo.} & \textbf{Cycle} & \textbf{Node} & \textbf{Doubling} & \textbf{Cadence} \\
        & & (\si{\astronomicalunit}) & (\si{\astronomicalunit}) & (\si{\degree}) & (\si{\year}) & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & (\si{\year}) & (\si{\year}) \\\hline
        3/5 & 5 & 0.5948 & 3.0387 & 9.830 & 2.1340 & 8.130 & 0.5696 & 0.4268 \\\hline
        \textbf{5/8} & \textbf{8} & 0.9193 & 2.9400 & 11.296 & 2.2789 & 10.256 & 0.5223 & 0.2849 \\\hline
        8/13 & 13 & 0.8157 & 2.9733 & 11.024 & 2.2211 & 9.638 & 0.5357 & 0.1709 \\\hline

The 5/8 pattern in full runs like this. Depart Earth at \SI{10.09}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of excess aimed \SI{57.2}{\degree} from prograde, which is \SI{3.926}{\kilo\meter\per\second} from the parking orbit. Coast \SI{1.482}{\year}, sweeping \SI{151.2}{\degree}. Take \SI{10.26}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at \SI{2.879}{\astronomicalunit}. Fall \SI{0.769}{\year} to 4 solar radii, take the Oberth boost, and climb out for \SI{0.028}{\year} at \SI{151.1}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to arrive where Earth is. Earth advances \SI{100.41}{\degree} while the vehicle sweeps the same \SI{100.41}{\degree} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Eight vehicles are in flight and a beam reaches Earth every \SI{0.2849}{\year}. Each beam is split 30.5\% caught at Earth against 69.5\% flying on for \num{21.1} days, to serve the far node of the chain five cadences ahead. The price of the phasing is chain count, not propellant.

\subsubsection{The Departure That Cools Itself}\label{sec:self_cooling_departure}
At 4 solar radii the split trades a 1.7 times worse clock for a 2.7 times cheaper pad, and reasonable people would decline it. That is also the only depth at which the trade is on offer. Scored on the committed fifteenth of liftoff rather than on launched slug, and with node survival derived rather than held, the split clears the floor from 4 solar radii out to \num{5.58} and nowhere beyond \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Its margin at 4 is \num{1.179} against the direct route's \num{0.657}, so the claim that the cheaper injection buys the pad is true, and true only just. Its edge over the direct route then shrinks from \num{1.80} times at 4 solar radii to \num{1.29} at \num{22.93} and \num{1.09} at 32. The architecture that buys the pad buys it only where the thermal case is worst.

That matters because \autoref{sec:depth_cost} recommends starting the dive shallow, near 23 solar radii, which was where the split's argument was supposed to live. At that depth neither architecture earns its launch. The choice there is between two failures, with the split \SI{29}{\percent} less bad than the route it is competing with. What follows is the mechanism that was thought to settle the shallow case, and then the price that says it does not.

The first guess about what a shallow dive does to the Earth push is wrong, and it is worth clearing away. Backing the dive out weakens the returning beam, but it weakens the boost that beam has to deliver by nearly as much, because a shallower perihelion is a smaller drop to buy. The resonant dive's boost falls from \SI{37.53}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at 4 solar radii to \SI{28.43}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at 32, while the stream at Earth falls from \SI{157.4}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{96.6}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. On the Earth side the shallow dive is a tax on impulse rather than a wall.

On the node side it is a good deal more expensive than this paper has been reporting, and \autoref{tab:derived_node_survival} is the correction. The ledger that walks the resonant dive down the depth dial holds node survival at \num{0.60} at every depth, and so does every row of \autoref{sec:split_dive_growth}. Node survival is the fraction of the vehicle that lives through the perihelion burn. Derive it from the boost instead, the way \autoref{tab:dive_depth_dial} derives its own, and the held value is right at 4 solar radii and wrong everywhere else. The reason is that the node's effective exhaust speed scales with the arrival speed, and that speed collapses as the dive is backed out, from \SI{68.09}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at 4 solar radii to \SI{22.38}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at 32.

    \centering
    \caption{Node survival on the single-impulse resonant dive, held at the stated \num{0.60} against the value derived from the perihelion boost \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The doubling columns are the same cycle scored both ways, and the last column is how much better the held value reports the cycle to be. \num{22.93} solar radii is the pad floor of \autoref{sec:depth_cost}. Reproduce with \texttt{make shallow-dive}.}
    \label{tab:derived_node_survival}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{$r_p$ ($R_\odot$)} & \textbf{Derived} & \textbf{Stated} & \textbf{Doubling, derived} & \textbf{Doubling, stated} & \textbf{Flattered by} \\
        & & & (\si{\year}) & (\si{\year}) & \\\hline
        4.00 & 0.5895 & 0.60 & 0.3075 & 0.3048 & 1.009 \\\hline
        22.93 & 0.2589 & 0.60 & 0.6570 & 0.3431 & 1.915 \\\hline
        32.00 & 0.1629 & 0.60 & 0.9484 & 0.3091 & 3.068 \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Read the two doubling columns against each other, because the damage is there rather than in the survival figures. Held at \num{0.60} the cycle doubles in \num{0.3048}, \num{0.3431} and \num{0.3091} years down the dial, which is non-monotone and inside \SI{13}{\percent}. That flatness is what made backing the dive out look nearly free, and it is an artefact of the constant, not a property of the cycle. Derived, the same three depths give \num{0.3075}, \num{0.6570} and \num{0.9484}, so the dial costs a factor of three across its length. The stated value stays defensible where this paper actually spends it, at 4 solar radii, where the two agree to \SI{0.9}{\percent}. What it will not survive is being carried to depth, which is exactly what a shallow-dive comparison invites. The shallow dive is still not a wall, since \SI{0.657}{\year} at the pad floor beats the Jupiter-only chain's \SI{1.74}{\year}. It is a much heavier tax than a fixed survival made it look.

What does not scale down with depth is the conduction threshold of \autoref{sec:depth_cost}, the closing speed below which the merged blob stops expanding enough to stay conducting and the nozzle has nothing to grip. At $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}^2 = 0.60$ and the same factor of 1.5 in expansion margin, that threshold is \SI{79.56}{\kilo\meter\per\second} wherever it is applied.

The Earth departure is the one burn in this architecture that cools itself. It aims \SI{31}{\degree} off the arriving stream, which is nearly along it, so the vehicle accelerates away from what is feeding it and the closing speed falls through the burn. The coldest instant is the end. A bigger burn against a slower beam pushes that instant through the floor, and \autoref{tab:split_departure_conduction} shows where.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Closing speed through the Earth departure burn, against the \SI{79.56}{\kilo\meter\per\second} conduction threshold of \autoref{sec:depth_cost} at an expansion margin of 1.5 \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The direct columns are the single-impulse resonant dive, whose whole injection is bought at Earth, and the arrow is the closing speed at the start and the end of that burn. The split columns are the phased cycle of \autoref{tab:split_dive_closures}, whose Earth node buys only \SI{3.93}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and so barely cools the stream. Its far node takes the larger burn and still holds its closing speed, because it thrusts across the beam rather than along it. Reproduce with \texttt{make split-dive}.}
    \label{tab:split_departure_conduction}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|r|r|r|r|c|}\hline
        \textbf{$r_p$ ($R_\odot$)} & \textbf{Stream} & \textbf{Direct burn} & \textbf{Direct closing} & \textbf{Split burn} & \textbf{Split closing} & \textbf{Far node} & \textbf{Conducts} \\
        & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & \\\hline
        4 & 157.4 & 28.10 & $148.3 \rightarrow 125.0$ & 3.93 & 156.1 & 153.1 & both \\\hline
        12 & 122.4 & 24.72 & $113.0 \rightarrow 91.6$ & 3.93 & 121.8 & 118.3 & both \\\hline
        16 & 114.4 & 23.46 & $105.0 \rightarrow 84.5$ & 3.93 & 114.0 & 110.7 & both \\\hline
        23 & 104.9 & 21.57 & $95.4 \rightarrow \mathbf{76.3}$ & 3.93 & 104.6 & 101.9 & split only \\\hline
        32 & 96.6 & 19.48 & $87.0 \rightarrow \mathbf{69.5}$ & 3.93 & 96.2 & 94.6 & split only \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}
