The direct single-impulse departure goes cold at 19.80 solar radii, bisected on the constraint rather than sampled near it \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. That number carries two conditions and is meaningless without both. The first is the expansion margin: 19.80 solar radii at a margin of 1.5, \num{27.40} at \num{1.25}, \num{40.72} at \num{1.0}. The second is the perihelion burn, held here at the \SI{35.98}{\kilo\meter\per\second} that tunes a 4-solar-radii dive to the paper's \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} Earth crossing. That second condition is the stronger of the two. The crossing runs 12.71 solar radii at a \SI{30}{\kilo\meter\per\second} burn and 26.04 at \SI{40}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, roughly 1.9 solar radii per \si{\kilo\meter\per\second}, because a harder boost returns a faster stream and a faster stream is what keeps the plume conducting \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The split's Earth node has no crossing anywhere between 4 and 48 solar radii at any of these settings, because its burn is \SI{3.93}{\kilo\meter\per\second} rather than \SI{21.6}{\kilo\meter\per\second}.

The far node's burn is the large one, \SI{10.26}{\kilo\meter\per\second} against the Earth node's \SI{3.93}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so the obvious worry is that it goes cold instead. It does not, and the reason is the geometry this section already charged as a cost. The beam arrives within a degree of radial and the injection thrusts tangentially, so the impact angle runs \SIrange{86}{90}{\degree}. The burn is perpendicular to what feeds it. Closing speed across the whole \SI{10.26}{\kilo\meter\per\second} moves from \SI{153.50}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{153.17}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, three tenths of a percent \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. A burn cannot run away from a stream it is not travelling along. That cant costs the far node about a fifth of its $\beta$, the useful impulse per kilogram of arriving projectile defined in \autoref{sec:split_push}, and buys it immunity to the floor that kills the direct departure.

State what this does and does not establish, because the price that would have settled it has since been paid. Within one architecture the result is clean. The Earth departure of the single-impulse dive cools itself and can be pushed through the floor, and the split's cannot, at any depth or margin in the table. It does not establish that the split is required in order to fly shallow, and the node-side cost now says the opposite. A perihelion burn of \SI{38.10}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, \num{1.059} times the tuning above, holds the direct departure conducting out to the \num{22.93} solar radii pad floor, and the cycle still grows there. It multiplies \num{2.505} per pass and doubles in \SI{0.657}{\year}, the derived figure of \autoref{tab:derived_node_survival} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The extra \SI{6}{\percent} of burn costs \SI{7.4}{\percent} of node survival and about \SI{1}{\percent} of clock, and the clock moves the helpful way, because a hotter perihelion burn climbs out faster. So the conduction crossing says the direct route cannot fly shallow \emph{at the paper's tuning}. It does not say the split is what makes a shallow dive flyable. The weaker of the two readings was the true one.

One consequence runs against the split, and it is why no depth window is claimed anywhere in this subsection. At \SI{38.10}{\kilo\meter\per\second} the conduction crossing moves out to \num{23.01} solar radii, which is past the \num{22.93} pad floor rather than short of it. There is therefore no band in which the direct route has lost its departure and still earns its launch. The window the split was once thought to open is empty at the tuning that keeps the direct route conducting \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

\subsubsection{The Other Half Of The Node}\label{sec:opposing_stream_depth}
Everything above prices the payload's arrival at the dive perihelion, which is the leg the \SI{37.53}{\kilo\meter\per\second} headline covers. The node needs a second arrival, and it behaves the opposite way under the depth dial. Placing the opposing stream retrograde gets \emph{dearer} as the dive is backed out, because a shallower perihelion keeps more of the orbit's angular momentum and reversing the sign of that momentum costs more. \autoref{tab:opposing_stream_depth} runs the two legs against each other.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{The two arrivals the dive node needs, as heliocentric impulse from \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The payload leg gets cheaper with depth and the opposing stream's retrograde placement gets dearer, and they cross near 8 solar radii. The radial plunge is the third option. Its price is flat at Earth's own orbital speed because it only has to cancel it, and that flatness is what disqualifies it (\autoref{sec:node_depth_admissibility}); the column is printed for comparison rather than as a choice. The split routes both legs through a \SI{2.94}{\astronomicalunit} aphelion. Its prograde raise costs \SI{6.60}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at every depth, and the flip column is the retrograde reversal taken out there, so the opposing stream's split cost is those two added. The saving columns are the direct cost divided by the split's on each leg. Reproduce with \texttt{make split-dive}.}
    \label{tab:opposing_stream_depth}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|r|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{$r_p$ ($R_\odot$)} & \textbf{Payload} & \textbf{Retrograde} & \textbf{Plunge} & \textbf{Split flip} & \textbf{Opp. saving} & \textbf{Payload saving} \\
        & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & & \\\hline
        4 & 24.09 & 35.48 & 29.78 & 14.32 & 1.70 & 1.41 \\\hline
        8 & 21.81 & 37.76 & 29.78 & 15.12 & 1.74 & 1.34 \\\hline
        12 & 20.10 & 39.47 & 29.78 & 15.73 & 1.77 & 1.29 \\\hline
        16 & 18.70 & 40.87 & 29.78 & 16.24 & 1.79 & 1.24 \\\hline
        23 & 16.69 & 42.88 & 29.78 & 16.98 & 1.82 & 1.16 \\\hline
        32 & 14.62 & 44.94 & 29.78 & 17.77 & 1.84 & 1.08 \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Past about 8 solar radii the opposing stream is the dominant Earth-side cost, and by 32 it is three times the payload's leg. It does not have the conduction problem of \autoref{sec:self_cooling_departure}. Aiming retrograde against a radially outward stream means never running along what feeds it. Its closing speed therefore holds or rises through the burn, \SI{99.5}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{107.3}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at 32 solar radii, and it clears the threshold at every depth \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Its problem is price, and the two failures should not be conflated.

Two things have to be said about that price before it is carried anywhere. The first is which architecture it belongs to. The \SIrange{35.48}{44.94}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of the table is the Earth-direct route, and it is the only route open to the single-impulse resonant dive and to the split dive, neither of which visits Jupiter. The Jovian dive cycle of \autoref{sec:jovian_dive_cycle} places the same stream for \SIrange{11.06}{11.83}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of Earth excess, launched tangentially and bent retrograde by an unpowered flyby, which is \numrange{0.95}{1.09} times that cycle's own departure rather than three times it. Co-locating the arrival with the payload in longitude and in time costs a further \SIrange{13}{38}{\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Quoting the larger figure without naming the architecture overstates the bill three to four times over.

The second is what the placement does to the growth numbers once it is billed, and the answer is very little. No ledger in this paper charges it, which is a real omission. Charged, it costs \numrange{1.0014}{1.0075} times the doubling time on the Jovian cycle across the depth dial and \numrange{1.0063}{1.0112} on the paper's own dive, and on the pad ledger it is \SIrange{0.45}{2.00}{\percent} of returned mass, flipping no verdict \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The reason is mass rather than impulse. At a slug ratio of 30 the node wants only \SIrange{0.17}{0.49}{\kilogram} of opposing stream per impactor kilogram of payload, so even a \SI{44.94}{\kilo\meter\per\second} placement flown through a nozzle that delivers a fifth still lands near one percent. So the two-leg asymmetry is a result about impulse and node geometry. It is not a correction to any growth figure printed here, and it should not be reported as one. The exception is the split dive, whose far node has to be fed as well as reached, and \autoref{sec:split_dive_growth} prices that separately.

The split helps this leg more than it helps the payload's, and the saving grows exactly where the payload's shrinks. The raise costs the same either way, so only the flip term differs, and the flip is what the depth dial inflates. At 32 solar radii the split places the opposing stream for \SI{24.37}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, less than the paper's own payload dive costs at 4 solar radii. The case for the split at a shallow dive therefore rests more on this leg than on the one scored first. That is a statement about placement impulse and it does not rescue the pad ledger, which \autoref{sec:self_cooling_departure} shows both architectures failing at that depth.

That leaves the plunge column, which is the cheapest direct placement on the table at every depth and is not available. A projectile falling nearly straight in has only to cancel Earth's \SI{29.78}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so it undercuts the direct retrograde by \SI{5.7}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at 4 solar radii and by \SI{15.2}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at 32, and it arrives cooler as well. \autoref{sec:node_depth_admissibility} bars it, and bars it before any of that trade applies. It would have lost the trade in any case. A plunge reaches the payload at \SI{135}{\degree} rather than head-on, and at the slug ratio of 30 that the departure hands the node it keeps \num{0.757} of the head-on effective exhaust speed \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. \autoref{sec:arrival_angle} works that toll out across the slug ratio and prices the collision heat it would have bought back.

\subsubsection{Nothing May Pass Inside The Node}\label{sec:node_depth_admissibility}
The plunge column of \autoref{tab:opposing_stream_depth} is barred by a rule this paper should have been enforcing all along. Every trajectory in the architecture must have a perihelion no lower than the dive node's depth. That binds the payload, the opposing stream, and any projectile stream feeding any node. Nothing in the system may pass inside the node. Call it \textbf{node-depth admissibility}.

A plunge fails it by construction. A projectile carrying no angular momentum has a perihelion of zero, so it does not skim the Sun, it enters it. It crosses the node radius on the way down and keeps going. Three consequences follow, and each one on its own is disqualifying.

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{The unconsumed share hits the Sun.} A stream is never perfectly consumed. \autoref{sec:lost_puffsat} is about exactly that, and the depth dial's own rendezvous timing tolerance is an along-track miss budget rather than a guarantee. A plunger architecture therefore puts a steady fraction of its projectiles on a Sun-impacting trajectory, cycle after cycle.
    \item \textbf{It contradicts the reason for the depth.} A shallow dive is chosen to escape the thermal load. Backing the payload out to 23 solar radii while aiming the ammunition at the Sun's center has not escaped anything. It has moved the exposure onto the half of the collision nobody was scoring.
    \item \textbf{The depth stops meaning one thing.} Under the rule, ``the dive is at 23 solar radii'' is a statement about the closest approach of everything in the system. Without it, the same phrase describes one of the two arrivals and says nothing about the other.
\end{enumerate}

The tell is already printed in \autoref{tab:opposing_stream_depth}, in the one column that does not move. The plunge costs \SI{29.78}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at every depth on the dial, because cancelling Earth's orbital motion is the whole of the maneuver. A depth-independent price is the giveaway that the trajectory is not aiming at a depth at all. Compare the payload column, which falls from \SI{24.09}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{14.62}{\kilo\meter\per\second} across the same rows because it is.

The obvious repair does not work, and the reason closes the argument. Force the plunger to bottom out at the node rather than pass through it, and it has to carry the tangential speed a perihelion there requires, \SI{15.16}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at 32 solar radii for a \SI{14.62}{\kilo\meter\per\second} burn \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. That is the payload's own prograde injection, to the digit. The repaired plunger arrives alongside the payload instead of across it, and there is no collision left to price. Zero angular momentum is the plunger's defining property, so ``plunger'' and ``bottoms out at the target depth'' are mutually exclusive.

Retrograde placement is therefore not the dearer of two options that both work. It is the only admissible way to get a head-on arrival, and that promotes the bi-elliptic route of \autoref{tab:opposing_stream_depth} from convenient to load-bearing. Injected at one far node, the payload and the opposing stream fly the same ellipse in opposite senses. The payload cuts its tangential speed to drop perihelion and the stream reverses its own, so both legs share a semi-major axis and both perihelia are the same point. They arrive together at \SI{180}{\degree} with no residual to tune away, and neither leg ever goes closer to the Sun than the node. At the \SI{1.9649}{\astronomicalunit} node the payload's cut falls from \SI{14.541}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{9.478}{\kilo\meter\per\second} between 4 and 32 solar radii while the opposing flip rises from \SI{20.362}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{25.425}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The rule is satisfied there by construction, which is a better guarantee than a rule anyone has to remember.

That last point is worth being blunt about, because the plunge is the cheapest-looking of the three placements at every depth in the table. A rule left to judgement is a rule left to lose. The companion repository now enforces admissibility rather than stating it, flagging every placement sense and refusing to turn an inadmissible one into a trajectory \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

\subsubsection{What The Split Does Not Settle}\label{sec:split_dive_open}
The model is circular and coplanar, like the closure of \autoref{sec:jovian_dive_closure}. Outer-node co-location is a longitude match, and it has had no real-ephemeris audit. Neither has the Jovian route for placing the opposing stream, which is a longitude and epoch match in the same circular coplanar model.

Node survival is held at \num{0.60} rather than derived in every table of \autoref{sec:split_dive_growth}. That is defensible only because all of them are scored at a 4-solar-radii dive, where \autoref{tab:derived_node_survival} puts the held and derived values \SI{0.9}{\percent} apart. Nothing in this subsection carries that constant down the depth dial, and nothing should.

The conduction threshold is imported. It was built for the overtaking leg of \autoref{sec:split_push}, not re-derived for a departure plume. It carries no slug-ratio dependence, so the transfer is defensible, but it is a transfer. The two architectures have now been scored head to head at a shallow depth, and no depth window survives the scoring, which is why none is claimed here.

The perihelion burn is held at \SI{35.98}{\kilo\meter\per\second} across the whole depth dial, which is the 4-solar-radii tuning, while \autoref{sec:depth_cost} uses the climb-out excess as the free knob instead. Those are different sweeps through the same two knobs, so the depth rows here are not the depth rows there even where the depth matches, and the conduction crossing is the quantity that moves most under the swap. What a larger perihelion burn costs at the solar node is the missing price, and it is what would settle whether the direct architecture can buy its way out to 23 solar radii. The dive depth and that burn are also fixed at 4 solar radii and \SI{35.98}{\kilo\meter\per\second} in the phasing tables, and either is a knob that would change which reuse fractions are reachable. The reachable band at the present settings is roughly \numrange{0.61}{0.63}, which is why 1/2 and 2/3 return no root.

Four things stay open, and none of them is the one this subsection used to list first. Whether any ledger absorbs the opposing stream's placement has now been traced, and none does, which is why \autoref{sec:opposing_stream_depth} states the omission with its size rather than leaving it hanging.

The partial split's pad ledger is deliberately not reported. Now that its far node is priced the arithmetic could be run, but the launched-mass verdict already settles that architecture and a pad figure would only restate it. The far-node delivery itself is priced as a direct high-speed launch from \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}. A feeder that dives first and is caught on its own climb-out would be cheaper, but that is a second solar-dive chain rather than a delivery, and nobody has costed one. \autoref{sec:node_depth_admissibility} closes the cheap route to an off-head-on arrival without pricing the route that survives it, which is an inclined placement, and a plane change on a placement leg appears nowhere in this paper. And the split dive has been compared with the direct dive and with the Jovian cycle one pair at a time rather than on a single ledger with all three.

\subsection{The Minimum Nozzle Sets The Minimum Rocket}\label{sec:minimum_nozzle}
Everything above assumes the collision plasma can be turned into forward thrust, and near the Sun that means a nozzle. This is the main limitation of the direct-launch architecture. A nozzle sized to redirect a given per-pulse energy does not get lighter just because the rocket carrying it is small, so its mass sets a floor on how large the smallest worthwhile rocket has to be. Three ways lead past that floor. The rocket can be large enough that the nozzle is a small fraction of its mass. The impactors can be light enough that the nozzle needed to catch them is small to begin with. Or the collision can be slow enough that there is little energy to contain.

That opening assumption has a size, and \autoref{sec:jet_efficiency} gives it a name. The jet efficiency $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ is the fraction of the ideal one-directional exhaust momentum that a real nozzle delivers. What the nozzle receives is not a uniform stream. Once the two masses merge, a quarter of the pulse energy remains as bulk motion of the fireball and three quarters becomes internal energy, random in the fireball's own rest frame. The bulk quarter already travels retrograde and needs no field, but it carries exactly the momentum the incoming projectile brought in, so by itself it cancels to zero thrust. The nozzle produces every newton-second by turning the thermal three quarters into one-sided flux. The head-on geometry helps here, for the reason given below. The expansion is a pancake with a preferred axis rather than a sphere, so the field trims a divergence instead of reversing one. What the geometry cannot avoid is that roughly half of that thermal energy starts out moving toward the ship. The field has to mirror that half, which requires the magnetic pressure at the chamber to stand off the fireball's own pressure. Plasma that leaks forward is charged twice, once as exhaust that never left and once as heat on the structure. We have not computed the field strength this needs, and it belongs with the radiation-hydrodynamic calculation of \autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats}.

The system has a pass-through floor. Forward thrust reaches zero at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = \sqrt{m_{rp}}$ and reverses below it. The floor is $0.5$ at the three-to-one mix of \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}. A pusher plate has no such cliff, since a plate at any restitution still passes some momentum forward. That asymmetry is the strongest argument for a heavily diluted mix wherever the added propellant is cheap. The twenty-four-to-one dilution of \autoref{sec:methalox_rebuttal} puts the floor at $0.2$ and takes most of its margin there.

\autoref{sec:jet_efficiency} works the published anchors for $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$. Every one of them bounds the parameter from above rather than measuring it, and together they cap a solenoid between \num{0.65} and \num{0.91} \cite{ahedo2010magnetic,hyde1983laserfusion_rocket}. Measured numbers do exist, though for a different quantity on a different device. They are momentum efficiencies off half-joule laser shots, and the two pendulum methods used to obtain them disagree with each other \cite{koba2025measurement}. The pessimistic requirement of \autoref{sec:methalox_rebuttal} is about $0.89$, which sits inside that band but near its top. The anchors therefore frame the question rather than settle it, and they identify $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ as the first nozzle parameter to compute.

One published criterion argues that the sizing rule this section is about to use is too generous. It deserves stating, because a reader who knows the pulsed nozzle literature will apply it to our numbers whether we do or not. Nikitin and Ponomarenko define $\kappa$, the ratio of an exploding plasma cloud's kinetic energy to the dipole field energy integrated outside the sphere the cloud is born on, and report a threshold separating two behaviors \cite{nikitin1993retardation}. Below the threshold the field decelerates the cloud in every direction, which they call quasi-capture. Above it the cloud tears through the field and leaks, which they call rupture. For a cloud born on the axis of the field the critical value is $\kappa_c = 0.4$, meaning the field must hold two and a half times the plasma's energy, and Hyde's design ran at $0.2$ \cite{vchivkov2003thrust,hyde1983laserfusion_rocket}.

Our sizing does not satisfy that, and the shortfall is structural rather than a parameter we could retune. Standoff sets the field's energy density $B^2/2\mu_0$ equal to the plume's pressure $p$, so the field energy inside the bore is $pV$. A monatomic gas holds internal energy $pV/(\gamma-1) = 1.5\,pV$. Divide one by the other and the volume cancels along with everything else, leaving $1.5$ for every pulse this paper flies. Field energy stored outside the bore is the only thing that can lower it. Integrating $B^2/2\mu_0$ over all space for the graded column of \autoref{eq:bore_from_length}, modelled as a current sheet at the \SI{3.0}{\meter} bore, puts \SIrange{23}{52}{\percent} more energy outside the bore than a bore-only count credits, which brings the ratio down to between \num{0.99} and \num{1.22} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. That is still two and a half to three times $\kappa_c$. If the criterion transfers, the field energy has to rise by that factor. The virial floor below rises with it, and the hottest pulse's magnet goes from tens of tonnes to between \SI{37}{\tonne} and \SI{112}{\tonne} of structure. That would undo the amortization argument this section reaches at its end.

The one experiment that checked $\kappa_c$ found it stricter. Kawashima and colleagues fired \SI{6.0}{\joule} into a \SI{500}{\micro\meter} polystyrene sphere \SI{13}{\milli\meter} from a 96-turn coil and saw the plume slow only above \SI{0.67}{\tesla}, where $\kappa$ was \num{0.077} \cite{kawashima2016structure}. They call $\kappa_c = 0.4$ an overestimate for their apparatus, and they name their own geometry as the reason. Their plasma left the target on the laser side alone instead of expanding in every direction. Read against \num{0.077} our ratio is thirteen to sixteen times over rather than two and a half. That is one shot on one bench, and its authors are the ones flagging the geometry, but the number is in print.

We do not think either number transfers, and the reason sits in what $\kappa$ divides by. Its denominator is the field energy in the region beyond where the plasma is born. For a cloud born at a point that region is unambiguous, since the cloud's own volume is negligible against it. Our plume arrives at bore diameter, so it is not. Counting the whole bore downstream of the collision along with the field outside gives the \numrange{0.99}{1.22} above. Counting only the field outside the plume's own volume gives \numrange{2.9}{6.5}. The criterion does not say which reading is meant, because in the geometry it was built for the two coincide, and here they differ by a factor of three to five. What the paragraph above computes is a bore-based analogue of $\kappa$ rather than $\kappa$ itself, and it is the flattering end of the range. Rupture is a bubble bursting. The threshold was set on a compact plasma ball, born at a point, expanding in every direction until its pressure tears the field open. Nothing in our pulse does that. The bag delivers the plume already at bore diameter, so nothing has to punch its way out to nozzle scale. The field is graded to match the falling pressure at every station rather than set once and overrun. The collision also leaves a drift along the axis, though \autoref{eq:reflection_baseline} shows that drift is small at the slug ratios flown and it is the least of the three. A criterion for whether a growing sphere escapes a fixed field is not a criterion for whether a directed column follows a shaped one. The honest form of this is a conditional. Our sizing is defensible if the plume is a directed expansion. If it behaves like a bomb the magnet is three times too light against the analytic threshold, and over ten times against the measured one. The impact simulation of \autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats} is what decides which, and it is the same calculation the radiative budget already waits on.

At the highest near-Sun collision energies, a magnetic nozzle, a shaped magnetic field that redirects the ionized plasma with no wall in contact, may beat a solid absorber that has to survive each impact \cite{ahedo2010magnetic}. The Mini-Mag Orion study sized a nozzle of this kind for pulsed nuclear propulsion, an array of superconducting coils massing about \SI{200}{\tonne} that handles \SI{340}{\giga\joule} per pulse at an effective exhaust velocity near \SI{155}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{ewig2003minimag, lenard2007minimag}. That per-pulse energy is close to what a \SI{2.5}{\kilo\gram} retrograde impactor delivers on a 4 solar radii dive (see \autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal}), and the coil mass scales roughly with the energy the field must contain. Two features of our architecture cut that contained energy below the Mini-Mag baseline. Our plasma comes from an aimed head-on collision rather than a roughly isotropic fission burst, so it carries a net drift that the burst does not. That drift is not collimation. The diluted fireball moves at about \SI{155}{\kilo\meter\per\second} against an internal sound speed near \SI{200}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, which is Mach \num{0.8}, and the head-on stagnation region squirts radially into the field. The plume is a slow explosion rather than a jet. What the drift buys is a field's share near one half. That share is the fraction of pulse energy the field has to catch and turn rather than let past the open back. Mini-Mag's isotropic burst has a share near one. The defensible band runs from \numrange{0.3}{1}, and the masses quoted below take the half. A hybrid nozzle can go further and let a sacrificial low-Z absorber take the bulk axial momentum, with the field standing only the hottest core off that surface. Even trimmed, a nozzle at this scale earns its mass only on a large vehicle. The prograde rocket it pushes must carry hundreds of tonnes of payload, better still more than a thousand, so the nozzle amortizes against the delivered mass rather than dominating it. Magnetic redirection at these energies is therefore large-scale infrastructure with matching payoff, not the engine of a small craft.

A second route to a lighter nozzle shrinks the impactor rather than the coils. Periapsis speed is fixed by the orbit and not by the projectile, so the per-pulse energy $\tfrac{1}{2}mv^2$ falls in direct proportion to impactor mass, and the coil mass falls with it. Trimming a \SI{2.5}{\kilo\gram} projectile to \SI{100}{\gram} is a factor of 25 in energy. That would carry a \SI{200}{\tonne} nozzle into the single-digit tonnes, above the cooling-system and minimum-coil-size floors that eventually halt the scaling. Gram-scale spacecraft are already flight-proven \cite{tavares2019kicksat2}. Cornell's Sprite flew a radio, processor, solar cells, and sensors on a \SI{3.5}{\centi\meter} board massing about \SI{4}{\gram} \cite{manchester2013kicksat}, and gram-scale wafer craft carrying cameras and two-way optical links are a studied design target \cite{lubin2016roadmap}. A \SI{100}{\gram} sunward projectile is then dominated by its heat shield, not its electronics, which is why the 4 solar radii thermal design of \autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal} already tucks gram-scale guidance hardware into the shadow of its cone. These masses are optimistic and assume roughly another decade of miniaturization, but they extrapolate flown hardware rather than inventing it. If they hold, the nozzle no longer needs a thousand-tonne vehicle to amortize against. That is the larger of the two feasibility gains, and the one that opens the architecture to far smaller ships.

The second route carries a physics caveat, not just the hardware floors above. A lighter pulse is not simply a scaled-down heavy one. The plasma from a \SI{100}{\gram} hit is smaller and cools faster. More radiation can escape before the nozzle turns the remaining energy into thrust, lowering the jet efficiency $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ of \autoref{sec:jet_efficiency}. The opacity estimate behind that assumption was sized for the denser, slower low-orbit collision. Applying it to a gram-scale near-Sun pulse is an extrapolation for the independent impact-simulation cross-check among our next steps. Until that check is done, the practical impactor is better read as a band, plausibly \SIrange{0.1}{2.5}{\kilo\gram}, than as one mass. Radiative loss sets its lower edge.

A third route sidesteps both the coil mass and that caveat, because it is lighter by speed rather than by impactor mass. The Jupiter-only cycle of \autoref{sec:jupiter_only_growth} closes at about \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} rather than \SI{618}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. Only the reduced mass of a collision is dissipated, and for a \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} PuffSat merging with the \SI{213}{\kilo\gram} slug of \autoref{sec:needle_through_fog} that is $\mu = (25)(213)/238 = \SI{22.4}{\kilo\gram}$. The pulse then carries $\tfrac{1}{2}\mu w^2 = \SI{62.9}{\giga\joule}$, against \SI{477}{\giga\joule} for the near-Sun case, a factor of \num{7.6}. Two rules then disagree about what magnet that needs, and the disagreement is instructive rather than merely inconvenient. Scaling the Mini-Mag coil mass linearly with pulse energy, the rule used above, gives \SI{37}{\tonne}. Sizing the magnet by what it actually has to hold gives less. The field only stands off the plume's pressure, and pressure comes from translation alone, so the energy locked in dissociation and ionization is contained by nothing. That is most of the pulse. The stored field energy is the $nR_gT$ of \autoref{sec:watering_it_down}, \SI{4.4}{\giga\joule} at the coldest pulse of the burn and \SI{12.2}{\giga\joule} at the hottest, and the virial floor of the paragraphs above turns that into \SIrange[range-phrase={--},range-units=single]{3.7}{11}{\tonne} and \SIrange[range-phrase={--},range-units=single]{10}{30}{\tonne} of structure respectively. Structure is not the whole magnet. The conductor that carries the current has a mass of its own, and adding it gives \SIrange{8.0}{15.4}{\tonne} at the cold pulse and \SIrange{17.3}{37.6}{\tonne} at the hot one \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

We take the virial figures, because the linear rule charges the magnet for energy that never pushes on it, and we quote the whole band rather than an end of it, because the burn genuinely runs across it. Call it tens of tonnes. That is 8\% to 38\% of a \SI{100}{\tonne} craft, so the amortization floor falls from a thousand-tonne ship to one a single Starship can loft, but the nozzle is a serious fraction of that ship rather than a rounding error on it.

The conductor is worth its own paragraph, because it decides the floor and it turns on a number this paper has not so far given. Ampere's law fixes the ampere-turns at $NI = B\ell/\mu_0$, each turn wraps $2\pi r$ of tape, and thin-substrate REBCO runs about \SI{1.5}{\gram\per\meter}. Substituting \autoref{eq:bore_from_length} makes the conductor mass run as $B\sqrt{V\ell/\pi}$, which is the right scaling on the wrong radius. \autoref{eq:bore_from_length} sizes the bag, not the magnet. The winding stands outside the bag and then widens, because the flux tube that converts the plume spreads as it converts, and a tube whose field drops fourfold has twice the radius. In the retained \SI{12}{\tesla} reference calculation, following the plume's bounding tube takes the winding from \SI{3.50}{\meter} at the chamber to \SI{5.17}{\meter} at the exit, a mean half-angle of about \SI{4}{\degree}. Against a straight winding at the bag's radius carrying the uncapped profile, the flare and the \SI{12}{\tesla} cap together cost \SI{18}{\percent} more tape. Nothing in that expression is free except the current each tape carries, and the mass is exactly inversely proportional to it. We take \SI{500}{\ampere}, which is what commercial REBCO delivers at magnet operating conditions rather than at \SI{77}{\kelvin} in self-field \cite{rebco_tapes}, and that gives \SI{5.2}{\tonne} of tape at the cold pulse and \SI{8.5}{\tonne} at the hot one. The defensible band runs from \SI{300}{\ampere} for a pessimistic winding to \SI{1000}{\ampere} for a well-cooled high-field one, which moves the cold-pulse conductor between \SI{8.6}{\tonne} and \SI{2.6}{\tonne}. That factor of 3.3 is a larger lever on the total than the structure's pre-compression band, and it deserves the attention the pre-compression gets. The result worth carrying is that the optimistic end of the structure range is not reachable. At \SI{500}{\ampere} the conductor alone outweighs the \SI{3.7}{\tonne} floor, so no amount of clever winding gets the magnet under about \SI{9.4}{\tonne}. The radiative caveat of the previous paragraph works in this case's favor instead of against it, since a slower collision makes a cooler fireball that radiates less. The plasma is still well ionized. Spread over carbon atoms, \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} delivers about \SI{350}{\electronvolt} per atom. That is some thirty times carbon's \SI{11.3}{\electronvolt} first ionization energy. The weakly ionized \SI{8}{\kilo\meter\per\second} gas of a low-orbit insertion delivers only about \SI{4}{\electronvolt} and falls short of it. A magnetic nozzle is therefore legitimate on the outbound leg, and cheap enough that the growth cycle can afford one early.

A different answer drops the nozzle entirely and reverts to Orion's flat plate. The ship would release a slab of cheap propellant, mostly polyethylene with a thin iron backing, into the projectile's path behind the craft. The incoming projectile overflies the ship, so this option is for uncrewed vehicles only, the same firing-line logic as the front-mounted sail of \autoref{sec:lightweight_pusher_plates}. On impact the backing acts as a tamper, an inert layer whose inertia biases the fireball's expansion away from itself and toward the plate. That is the collimating work Orion's case and channel filler performed when converting bomb x-rays into directed propellant \cite{balcomb1970nuclear}; here the kinetic energy arrives already directed along the collision axis, so the backing only sharpens an asymmetry the head-on geometry provides for free. Momentum conservation says the bias grows with the backing's share of the slab's mass \cite{gurney1943fragments} and with a concave backing shape, and no hydrocode has yet quantified the split, so we flag a possibility rather than a design. The plasma reaches the plate in the same velocity class as Orion's own propellant hit its plate, so the ablation budget starts from Orion's, managed with the coatings already described. A plate redirects the fireball without containing it, escaping the coil-mass scaling above, and pays for that by catching only the forward wing of the expansion.

Whichever option is chosen, the near-Sun thermal problem belongs to the pulse and not to the Sun. Sunlight at 4 solar radii carries \SI{3.9}{\mega\watt\per\square\meter} (\autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal}), so a nozzle and structure a few tens of meters across present a few hundred square meters and take in on the order of a gigawatt, and that is the bound where every surface absorbs everything. The impacts run far above it. A \SI{1000}{\tonne} ship taking the \SI{36}{\kilo\meter\per\second} periapsis boost at \SI{155}{\kilo\meter\per\second} effective exhaust velocity spends about \SI{255}{\tonne} of collision mass by \autoref{eq:external_reaction_mass}, a quarter of it arriving retrograde at \SI{618}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. That is roughly \SI{1e16}{\joule} of collision energy. Delivered over a periapsis burn of order \SI{1000}{\second} it averages about \SI{10}{\tera\watt}, and stretching the burn across the whole deep passage still leaves terawatts. Sunlight is then three to four orders of magnitude down, and a nozzle that misdirects even a tenth of a percent of its pulse energy into hardware swamps it. The small projectile's thermal design is a solar problem, worked in \autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal}. The ship's is not.

The magnetic nozzle does not help with the part that matters here. A field steers charged particles but is transparent to photons. Radiation supplies no thrust and lands on whatever hardware is in view. Two fractions set the load. They are the radiated share of pulse energy and the share of the sky filled by coils and structure. Their product multiplies the \SI{10}{\tera\watt} above. A few thousand square meters of structure at \SI{1500}{\kelvin} sheds about a gigawatt while the burn runs. Any excess has to enter a heat sink. The product therefore has to sit near \num{e-4} for the nozzle to stay a passive structure. With coils filling of order a tenth of the sky, radiative escape must remain below about a tenth of a percent. Every term in that estimate belongs to the near-Sun burn.

For the Jupiter pulses, the companion radiation calculation integrates each parcel's emission during its nozzle transit. It gives \SIrange{0.99}{2.65}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} on the frozen branch and \SIrange{3.55}{12.03}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} on the equilibrium branch \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. For \SI{238}{\kilogram} per pulse at two pulses per second, these correspond to \SIrange[range-phrase={--},range-units=single]{0.47}{1.26}{\giga\watt} and \SIrange[range-phrase={--},range-units=single]{1.69}{5.73}{\giga\watt} emitted inside the nozzle. These energies already exclude downstream emission; multiplying them by an in-nozzle radiation fraction again would count that exclusion twice.

These are water-plume reference calculations using the original 4:1 expansion area ratio, without radiative cooling fed back into the flow. They have not been rerun for the adopted nozzle. Emitted power is not absorbed liner power. The calculation therefore does not by itself determine liner temperature or shield count. At the largest emitted load, the booked \SI{585}{\mega\watt} sublimation capacity could remove all intercepted radiation only if the absorbed share were below about \SI{10}{\percent}.

The largest case occurs at a \SI{56.53}{\kilo\meter\per\second} closing speed on the equilibrium branch. It emits \SI{2.86}{\giga\joule} inside the nozzle per pulse. The dissipated collision energy at that speed is \SI{35.75}{\giga\joule}, so the radiated share is \SI{8.0}{\percent}. Using the \SI{62.9}{\giga\joule} pulse at \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} as its denominator would understate that share. The earlier cylindrical-wall estimate of a five-percent passive-radiation limit is not a thermal qualification of the flared liner. The absorbed distribution and paths for heat to leave the liner set that budget.

Radiation also reduces useful exhaust momentum. Gas speed $v_g$ goes as the square root of retained energy, so losing 5\% of the pulse to radiation costs about 2.5\% of $v_g$. Effective exhaust velocity is what remains after debiting the incoming retrograde momentum (\autoref{eq:ve_general}). The same loss costs about 5\% of $v_e$ at the three-to-one mix and more near the pass-through floor.

Two knobs act on that product, and both point toward the large end of this architecture. Absorbed power falls in proportion to the window the boost is spread over. Stretching a \SI{1000}{\second} burn to \SI{10000}{\second} spreads it across roughly 4 to 6 solar radii, where collision speed has dropped about 18\%, so a tenfold thermal gain costs on the order of 10\% of averaged exhaust velocity. That is a good trade for a vehicle that is heat-limited rather than fuel-limited. Impactor mass is the second knob. A kilogram-class projectile makes a larger and denser fireball than the \SI{100}{\gram} case above, and holds its opacity longer while the momentum transfers. The heavy end of the \SIrange{0.1}{2.5}{\kilo\gram} band is therefore the thermally comfortable one, which suits the large ships a nozzle at this scale already needs.

That is a hard problem, and it is the easy version of it. Adding fusion to the pulse (\autoref{sec:epstein_drives}) changes the kind of load, not just the size. Deuterium--tritium puts 80\% of its \SI{17.6}{\mega\electronvolt} into a \SI{14.1}{\mega\electronvolt} neutron \cite{orth2003vista}. A magnetic field does not steer neutrons, and the low-Z opacity that stops our collision's soft radiation (\autoref{sec:radiative_differences}) does not stop them either. Their mean free path in lithium is \SI{0.15}{\meter}, so the VISTA fusion vehicle stacked about a meter of lithium behind a meter of lithium hydride to keep its superconducting coils cold \cite{orth2003vista}. That shield covers under 4\% of the solid angle at the target and still masses about \SI{505}{\tonne} against a \SI{216}{\tonne} coil, with about \SI{7}{\giga\watt} to reject through its own radiators \cite{orth2003vista}. Target x-rays add a second charge. They stop in the shield's inner face rather than heating its bulk, and Orth found the resulting ablation to be one of the strongest constraints on the magnet's size and so on the whole vehicle \cite{orth2003vista}. A kinetic pulse pays neither bill. Its waste heat lands on surfaces that can be low-Z and sacrificial, and none of it arrives as neutrons. Fusion multiplies pulse energy, but even a modest multiplier can cost more shield and radiator mass than the added thrust returns, unless the fuel is the aneutronic mixture of \autoref{sec:epstein_drives}.

\subsection{Moneyball Meets Methalox}\label{sec:methalox_rebuttal}\cite{moneyball_film}
This is the most speculative economics in the paper. The chain below multiplies uncertain projectile costs against a nozzle efficiency we have not confirmed. That efficiency is the jet efficiency $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ of \autoref{sec:jet_efficiency}. It is not the pusher-plate restitution factor $f$ of \autoref{sec:mass_fraction}. The independent impact simulation among our next steps has yet to check its near-Sun value. The chain is worked at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 1$ throughout, and the paragraph after it prices the shortfall. Read it as an order-of-magnitude rebuttal, not a cost model. So we anchor on the worst case rather than the best. Suppose a retrograde projectile costs \$3200 per kilogram once delivered onto its 4 solar radii trajectory. That price is dominated by the projectile itself, which must survive the dive and steer to millimeter accuracy. It also assumes an early generation before repeated cycles drive projectile costs down. If the architecture beats chemical launch even there, it beats it.

\autoref{sec:fare_comparison} left the near-term fare stuck near \$5000 and named the reason. The expense is lifting hundreds of tonnes of PuffSats from Earth, not the propulsion. A skeptic sharpens this into one question. How can a heat-tolerant, precisely-steered projectile ever undersell a tank of methalox? The answer is that the costly projectile is only a small, fast seed of the reaction mass, not the bulk of it. Each collision hands its momentum to far more cheap mass, so its price per delivered kilogram falls by the dilution ratio, step after step down the cascade.

Start with the \$3200 projectile. At periapsis it collides head-on with cheap prograde propellant carried three parts to its one (\autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}). The merged plasma leaves at an effective exhaust velocity near \SI{155}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. This is the loss-free value $v_p/2$ at the \SI{309}{\kilo\meter\per\second} periapsis speed of a 4 solar radii dive. Diluting one costly part into four drops that reaction mass to about \$800 per kilogram. A \SI{36}{\kilo\meter\per\second} periapsis boost then spends the reaction mass to throw cheap payload across Earth's orbit at \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The retrograde quarter arrives externally, so \autoref{eq:external_reaction_mass} gives a total reaction-mass ratio of about $0.255$. About a quarter kilogram of reaction mass therefore launches each kilogram delivered, putting the crossing mass near \$200 per kilogram.

At Earth the same dilution repeats. The fast mass merges its kinetic energy into about twenty-four times its own mass of cheap onboard water or plastic. Spreading the energy over twenty-five parts caps the gas speed at $v_g = \sqrt{150^2/25} = \SI{30}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$. That is not the useful figure. This collision is head-on like the one at periapsis. The arriving mass therefore debits \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} spread across twenty-five parts, or \SI{6}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. By \autoref{eq:ve_general}, the effective exhaust velocity is $30\,\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} - 6$. Its loss-free ceiling is \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, not $v_g$. We assume the \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} collision produces plasma sufficiently ionized for the magnetic nozzle of \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}. Unlike the weakly ionized \SI{8}{\kilo\meter\per\second} gas of a low-orbit insertion, this collision uses a magnetic nozzle rather than a material plate. Diluting the \$200 crossing mass across those twenty-five parts leaves reaction mass at about \$8 per kilogram. At \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} exhaust against an \SI{8}{\kilo\meter\per\second} climb, \autoref{eq:external_reaction_mass} gives a total reaction-mass ratio near $0.393$. Each kilogram of reaction mass therefore delivers about \SI{2.5}{\kilo\gram} to orbit. Spreading its \$8 cost over the mass it lifts leaves about \$3.20 per kilogram in low Earth orbit.

Even at the pessimistic anchor, that is roughly \$3.20 per kilogram to orbit. The methalox for a Starship ascent runs about \$10 per kilogram. Its roughly \$900{,}000 propellant load \cite{spacex_starship_cost} over the \SI{100}{\tonne} it lifts to low Earth orbit \cite{gunter_starship} comes to \$9 per kilogram, which we round up to \$10. We use the conservative \SI{100}{\tonne} payload carried through the rest of the paper, rather than the \SI{150}{\tonne} that \cite{gunter_starship} also lists. At the PuffSat rate a \SI{300}{\kilo\gram} passenger allocation is near \$1000 of fuel one way, against roughly \$3000 of methalox. That allowance triples the \SI{100}{\kilo\gram} of \autoref{sec:fare_comparison}, because fuel at \$3.20 per kilogram no longer rations cabin mass. Two honest corrections leave the conclusion standing. Launching the return-trajectory vehicles as well roughly doubles the bill to about \$6.40 per kilogram, still under methalox. We assume the larger prograde ship's magnetic nozzle, heat shield, and basic structure survive the pass and that PuffSats in Earth orbit decelerate and recover it, allowing its build cost to spread over many trips.

A third correction does not leave it standing unconditionally. Both collisions above run at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 1$. Their effective exhaust velocities fall steeply with this parameter because each carries a fixed incoming-momentum debit. At $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.9$, the periapsis step drops from \SI{155}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{124}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The Earth step drops from \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{21}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The doubled bill then reaches about \$9.60 per kilogram, close to methalox. Break-even occurs near $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.89$. At $0.8$ the bill is near \$16 per kilogram, and this version of the rebuttal fails. The \$3200 anchor therefore carries a requirement rather than an estimate. No calculation in this paper supplies the required jet efficiency. The value also sits near the top of the $0.79$ to $0.91$ divergence-only ceiling from the cited nozzle model (\autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}). The scale case of the next paragraph is far less exposed because its fleet cost is roughly 290 times lower. It holds down to about $0.7$. At the three-to-one periapsis mix, forward thrust reaches zero at $0.5$ and reverses below it (\autoref{sec:jet_efficiency}).

The cost of the guided units on the 4 solar radii retrograde trajectory drops sharply as manufacturing scale and projectile mass grow. At aviation scale, a projectile is mostly carbon foam and a thin ceramic skin costing a few tens of dollars per kilogram. A small high-Z cap provides opacity, and gram-scale electronics provide guidance. Together these put unit cost near \$80 per kilogram. Unlike the Parker heat shield, the skin needs to provide only minimal structural support to a much smaller craft, so it can be made thinner and cheaper. At the scales used for global energy production (\autoref{sec:world_set_free}), larger retrograde projectiles are packed with cheap bulk material behind the heat shield (\autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats}). A large power-generation ship blends the two costs. Its magnetic nozzle earns its mass only on a vehicle carrying hundreds of tonnes or more (\autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}), so most of its retrograde mass can be cheap bulk. Packing about ninety percent bulk with ten percent guided units puts the guided units at \$8 per kilogram of fleet mass. The bulk is not free. At the few dollars per kilogram that \autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats} projects for graphite and ceramic at scale, it adds about \$3, for a fleet cost near \$11 per kilogram. That \$11 per kilogram of retrograde mass then dilutes about sixteenfold on the way to Earth. Mixing three-to-one with cheap prograde propellant drops it fourfold. The reaction-mass ratio of $0.255$ spreads that mixture over nearly four times its mass of payload, dropping the cost again to about \$0.70 per kilogram. At \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} each kilogram carries $\tfrac{1}{2}v^2 \approx \SI{3.1}{\mega\watt\hour}$, so that \$0.70 per kilogram delivers raw energy at about \$0.22 per megawatt-hour. This stays below one dollar per megawatt-hour even after an inefficient conversion to electricity (\autoref{sec:strawway_economics}).

Even priced at the worst-case projectile, then, fuel to orbit competes with chemical launch. At scale the generation cost sits well under a dollar per megawatt-hour, below any current wholesale electricity source, once the fixed costs of the Straw Way, ocean heat sink, and power-generation equipment are set aside.

\section{One Hiroshima Per Second. The World Set Free}\label{sec:world_set_free}
In \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}, we propelled PuffSats to \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} using solar gravity and pulsed propulsion. A \SI{60}{\tera\watt} pulsed heat source, which is equivalent to one Hiroshima bomb per second \cite{hiroshima}, requires harnessing the kinetic energy of just \SI{5.3}{\tonne\per\second} of propulsion mass at these speeds. For context, humanity's total primary-energy consumption is approximately \SI{20}{\tera\watt} \cite{owid-energy-production-consumption}. A single one of these pulsed heat sources would produce roughly three times that, enough thermal power to cover the energy needs of all human civilization with room to spare.  This concept echoes H.G. Wells's 1913 novel \textit{The World Set Free}, where he imagined atomic bombs as a ``blazing continual explosion'' \cite{wells1914world}.  

Using kinetic projectiles to generate terrestrial power only makes sense if it achieves massive economies of scale that outperform the linear costs and land use efficiency of conventional renewables.  Let's explore how to build such an ambitious megastructure, and then explain why it's economically attractive in \autoref{sec:strawway_economics}.     

\subsection{Straw Ways To Heaven}\label{sec:straw_way_to_heaven}
The most common proposal for utilizing space-based power on Earth involves wireless power transfer from orbiting power plants to terrestrial rectenna arrays \cite{malaviya2022comprehensive}.  Unfortunately, commercial-scale power beaming remains orders of magnitude beyond today's demonstrations and would need ground rectennas several kilometers across \cite{space_beaming_power}. The required transmission infrastructure, both in space and on Earth, would be colossal.  Given these challenges, let's investigate terrestrial solutions that directly convert kinetic impact energy to electricity on Earth.

To deliver kinetic energy projectiles to Earth, the projectiles must be able to withstand atmospheric entry.  One solution is to build a vacuum tube, extending from Earth's surface to space, precisely aligned to allow incoming projectiles to pass through unimpeded.  The tube is suspended from an anchor point as depicted in \autoref{fig:straw_way_to_heaven}.   Enthusiasts have proposed numerous structures that could in principle support an anchor hoisted above Earth's atmosphere. These include Lofstrom Launch Loops \cite{lofstrom_loop}, tethered rings \cite{swan2023tethered_ring}, global orbital rings \cite{birch1982orbital_ring}, space elevators \cite{pearson1975orbital_tower}, and space fountains \cite{hyde1985space_fountain}.  For an accessible overview see also \cite{isaac_arthur_megastructures_playlist}.  However, none has advanced beyond early conceptual or paper-study stages, so all remain far from practical implementation.

\begin{figure}[htpb]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{images/Straw Way To Heaven.png}
    \caption{Vacuum tube suspended from cables allows projectiles through the atmosphere}
    \label{fig:straw_way_to_heaven}
\end{figure}


Fortunately, PuffSat pulsed propulsion offers a promising path to position an anchor point above Earth. While this technology is at a low TRL, we would have already developed it to high reliability if we were using it to direct projectiles at Earth using the techniques from \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}.  

A heavy-lift launch vehicle such as SpaceX's Super Heavy \cite{starship} delivers the anchor assembly and its attached vacuum tube to the target altitude.   We then indefinitely hover the anchor point using equal mass PuffSats that collide their gas output head-on in a pulsed propulsion chamber (described in \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}), redirecting thrust downward. The propulsion chamber exhaust can be angled slightly to provide thrust vectoring to counter atmospheric winds on the vacuum tube and support cables. From this elevated anchor point, we sequentially deploy tube segments and support cables downward until reaching the ground. A reaction control system on the anchor point adjusts the pulsed propulsion chamber so it stays precisely positioned for the next PuffSat collision.  If these PuffSats meet at $v_p=\SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$ at an average mass flow rate of \SI{1}{\tonne\per\second}, they can theoretically suspend more than \SI{1100}{\tonne}.   

In reality, energy losses would reduce thrust.   For reliable operations, we would need to lift redundant anchor points and vacuum tubes.  Together, redundancy and energy losses imply we will need additional PuffSat propulsion collision mass per second.  To keep the vacuum tube aloft, PuffSat propulsion must remain continuously active even when incoming projectiles cannot enter the tube to generate energy. Alternatively, for maintenance, the tube could be lowered at night and then re-hoisted by a heavy-lift rocket the following day.  Compared to magnetic systems like Lofstrom Launch Loops, hoisting an anchor with rockets or PuffSat pulsed propulsion is energy inefficient. That inefficiency is acceptable here. Lifting a few tons per second of propulsion PuffSat mass from Earth (as in \autoref{sec:starship_safelaunch}) or the moon (as in \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}) seems economically viable if we use our vacuum tube to cleanly produce all the energy required by humanity.

\subsection{Vacuum Tube Details}\label{sec:vacuum_tube_details}
To mitigate micrometeorite risk in the upper atmosphere, the vacuum tube incorporates sacrificial shielding or modular, swappable segments. Self-healing materials \cite{self_healing_material_survey} may repair small tube ruptures.  For example, a thin wire mesh embedded in the tube wall could detect conductivity changes caused by tears or punctures. Briefly increasing the current through these wires would cause localized heating, melting the surrounding material and heat-sealing small breaches. Redundant parallel tubes may also be deployed to ensure continuous operation despite localized damage.  

The tube's walls get thinner at higher altitudes, where less structural support is needed to maintain a vacuum.  At lower heights, ribbed or corrugated designs  keep the tube lightweight and flexible. Thin, high-strength tensile cables attached every few meters along the tube connect the tube and the anchor point.  Each attachment point includes several parallel tensile cables, ensuring structural redundancy in the event that one or more are severed by micrometeorite impacts.   Equipped with winching micro-actuators, these cables dynamically control their length to precisely tune the tube's curvature. The actuators shape the tube to counteract Coriolis, tidal, drag, and gravity-variation forces affecting the projectile's transit. The anchor point itself will rotate throughout the day to track the Sun.  Sensors along the vacuum tube measure and broadcast the tube's position and shape, allowing incoming projectiles to navigate through it accurately.

Additional sensors are placed along the cables and at the top of the tube to accurately measure any perturbations. These measurements help to precisely model the projectile's trajectory as it enters the tube. Once a projectile is inside, making adjustments with microthrusters would contaminate the vacuum and damage the walls. Instead, if the tube is metalized or partially conductive, we can steer projectiles using small magnets. These magnets generate Lorentz forces from induced currents in the walls, allowing for precise control. In its upper, near-orbital reaches the wall also meets atomic oxygen, which erodes bare polymer, and unfiltered ultraviolet. A continuous metallized or silica coating holds off both, the same technique that protects polyimide solar-array blankets in orbit \cite{ao_protection_banks}, and the metallized version doubles as the conductive layer the magnetic steering relies on.

Creating and maintaining a high vacuum in long tunnels is no simple task, but facilities like the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) prove it's feasible. Our tube, like the LHC's, uses non-evaporable getter (NEG) \cite{neg_coatings} thin-film coatings placed at intervals to chemically scavenge most atmospheric gases. These coatings, however, are ineffective against noble gases like argon. While we could embed cryopumps throughout the tube, that would add complexity and mass. Fortunately, our projectiles bury the argon, either in the tube wall or in their own skins.  A struck atom either rebounds off the projectile and lodges in the wall, or is driven into the projectile and leaves the tube with it.  Both happen at any speed.  Closing velocity only shifts the odds between them, and it shifts them toward the projectile as it rises.  At \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} most of the argon rides out on the projectiles.  Near Earth escape speed most of it ends up in the wall (\autoref{sec:death_star}).  This is the same mechanism a sputter-ion pump, a standard accelerator vacuum pump, uses to remove the noble gases that getter films cannot.  It buries them in a surface instead of binding them chemically \cite{schulz1999sputterion}.  These impacts also sputter atoms from both the walls and the projectiles. The NEG coatings scavenge sputtered volatile atoms, while non-volatile elements recondense onto the tube interior.

Burial is not a permanent trap.  In a sputter-ion pump, later impacts re-sputter buried argon and release it in cyclic pressure bursts, an effect called argon instability that sets in once the argon partial pressure passes roughly \SI{1e-6}{\milli\bar} \cite{schulz1999sputterion}.  Several features keep it in check here.  The projectiles bury argon across hundreds of thousands of square meters of wall rather than the few square centimeters of a compact pump cathode, so a given buried atom is unlikely to be struck again.  Argon is under one percent of the atmosphere, and the tube walls are built to be nearly impermeable, so its partial pressure stays well below that threshold.  In a pump the severity of these bursts is set by the ratio of cathode-atom mass to gas-ion mass \cite{vaumoron1970argon}, and a light aluminum wall sits on the unfavorable side of that ratio.  Two things offset it.  Slow projectiles leave the rebounding atoms far below the energy a pump cathode delivers, so they barely sputter the wall and disturb what is already buried even less.  Fast projectiles bury most of their argon in their own skins and carry it out, so little of it reaches the wall to be re-released.  A pressure excursion in a kilometers-long straw is far less damaging than in a small chamber, and redundant parallel tubes absorb any transient. To ensure an even better vacuum inside the tube, thin films of oxygen-specific chemical adsorbents  are applied to the top few kilometers. By limiting exospheric oxygen from building up in the tube, we make our vacuum comparable to low orbit environments.  Scheduled tube refurbishment or replacement will be necessary due to wear from micrometeorites, argon sputtering, and NEG chemical consumption. Even if we pessimistically need a replacement once per month, the costs are minor compared to the revenue from global power generation (see \autoref{sec:strawway_economics}).

Projectiles that catastrophically miss the vacuum tube would break up high in the atmosphere at their extreme velocity, rather than striking the surface intact. Even so, breakup is not automatically harmless: the much slower Chelyabinsk airburst still injured roughly 1,500 people \cite{popova2021chelyabinsk}. Siting the tubes in remote areas limits ground-level effects on populated regions, but the fragmentation altitude and blast footprint of a missed projectile would need to be modeled.   The vacuum tube itself is likely destroyed, but redundant vacuum tubes would then be moved into place.

\subsection{Tether Tapering And Countering Extreme Drag Forces}\label{sec:tether_tapering}

Incoming projectiles experience dynamic pressure approximately  $\frac{v_{\text{projectile}}^2}{v_{\text{PuffSat}}^2} = \frac{(150\,\text{km/s})^2}{(11\,\text{km/s})^2} \approx 185$ times greater than that faced by trans-lunar PuffSats, due to their much higher velocity. This amplifies drag forces far beyond those analyzed in \autoref{sec:estimate_cold_gas}.

Because these projectiles spend only \SIrange{1}{2}{\second} at lower altitudes when arriving at such extreme velocities, we may be able to counteract the resulting terminal forces by briefly firing small chemical thrusters and vectoring their thrust. Despite the rarefied air at \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} altitude, projectiles still face intense ram heating. Thin, thermally conductive skins can be cooled via convective heat transfer and latent heat absorption from phase-changing PuffSat volatiles (e.g., water vaporization).

Projectile guidance and thermal management are easier if projectiles enter the vacuum tube at \SIrange{300}{400}{\kilo\meter} altitude, rather than the \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} baseline used in \autoref{sec:formation_challenges_current_missions}. That altitude exceeds Kevlar's self-support breaking length. SK99 Dyneema's instantaneous breaking length, near \SI{430}{\kilo\meter} \cite{dyneema_sk99_datasheet}, is on paper the longest of the four, but polyethylene creeps under sustained load, which rules it out for a permanent column (see \autoref{sec:fiber_selection}). The column therefore wants PBO, whose \SI{380}{\kilo\meter} self-support length \cite{zylon_technical_info} comes with low creep. The taper of the following paragraphs then supplies the safety margin (see \autoref{sec:fiber_selection}). A continuous silica or metallized barrier holds off the atomic oxygen and ultraviolet the exposed column meets, and the dry vacuum largely suppresses PBO's hydrolysis pathway, so PBO earns its place in the coated column rather than the humid air near the surface.

Unlike the projectiles, PuffSats that provide pulsed propulsion to anchor the tube travel at only \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. Their lower velocity enables more precise navigation and impact targeting at reduced altitudes. Therefore, we can place one anchor at the lower \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} altitude and connect the upper portion of the tube to a second anchor at higher altitude. Shorter cables anchored to surface vessels can serve as guy wires to counter winds on the lower \SI{20}{\kilo\meter} of the tube. These guy wires can be actively winched to adjust tension, minimizing peak wind forces the longer anchor cables must withstand.

Despite these optimizations, our current cable designs still lack sufficient engineering safety margin. Tapered cables offer a promising solution. The mass-optimal cross-sectional area of a tapered cable at distance $x$ from the anchor scales as $A(x) = C e^{-\frac{x}{b}}$, where $b$ is the material's breaking length and $C$ is a constant (see \autoref{sec:breaking_length_derived}). 

To maintain an engineering safety factor of 3 across a cable stretched to its full breaking length, the cross-sectional area at the anchor must be roughly 20 times greater than at the point where the cable connects to the lowest portions of the tube. Fortunately, this tapering requirement aligns with our existing structural strategy. We already deploy thin control wires every few meters along the tube. By progressively merging and intertwining these wires near anchor altitudes, we achieve the required cross-sectional increase without adding appreciable mass beyond our existing budget.



\subsection{Power Plant Chamber Details}
Beneath the vacuum tube, we propose excavating a steam chamber designed to vaporize incoming projectiles. These chambers are large construction projects, engineered to withstand sudden changes in pressure and temperature.  The chamber walls would be lined with steel backed by bunker-grade reinforced concrete, while internally mounted spring-loaded metal plates would absorb shock and mitigate structural stress.  

Suppose a \SI{0.1}{\cubic\kilo\meter} steam chamber containing \SI{50}{\kilo\gram\per\cubic\meter} steam receives a \SI{600}{\tera\joule} kinetic impact.   As a simplification, let's say the steam has a constant specific heat of \SI{3.5}{\kilo\joule\per(\kilo\gram\celsius)}.  In this case the temperature change is 
$\frac{\SI{600}{\tera\joule}}{\SI{5e9}{\kilo\gram}\times\SI{3500}{\joule\per(\kilo\gram\kelvin)}} = \SI{34.2}{\celsius}$.  An impact of this magnitude every 10 seconds produces the heat to continuously provide all humanity's energy.  This steam chamber would be connected to thousands of turbines and a massive cooling loop to harness and dissipate this energy.  

Releasing large amounts of heat into the atmosphere would lead to unacceptable local temperature increases. Using a coolant loop that exchanges heat with deep ocean water will also have environmental consequences. Spreading $\SI{40}{\tera\watt}$ of heat over $\SI{10}{\cubic\kilo\meter}$ of deep ocean water would increase its temperature by $\frac{\SI{40}{\tera\watt}\cdot\SI{3600}{\second\per\hour}}{\SI{1e13}{\kilo\gram}\cdot\SI{4186}{\joule\per\kilo\gram\per\celsius}} = \SI{3.44}{\celsius\per\hour}$.  This rapid temperature change could severely damage local marine habitats.

The mid-water column is not empty. Mesopelagic fish alone may total around 10 billion tonnes, likely the bulk of all fish biomass \cite{irigoien2014mesopelagic}. The case for tolerable harm rests instead on scale. The directly heated volume is a vanishingly small fraction of the ocean, so the disturbance stays local rather than basin-wide. When compared to the widespread impacts of global anthropogenic climate change or even the wildlife displacement caused by solar farms, the environmental harm from this specific heat rejection method appears relatively modest.

The projectiles would have explosive or water interiors that disintegrate quickly on hypervelocity shock contact with the chamber's high pressure steam. Made from carbon composites and polymers, their structural exteriors would vaporize into gases at impact temperatures. Inside the steam chamber, temperatures are so high that the carbon turns into a superheated plasma. Steam also decomposes in these conditions, releasing oxygen that combusts this carbon along with any air present. Although this process forms very little carbon soot before the carbon turns to carbon dioxide, any that does form can be filtered out with charcoal screens before reaching the turbines.   The remaining gases are released or sequestered as they travel along with the steam cycling through the system.   Solid residues that precipitate on chamber walls could be cleaned off either continuously or during maintenance windows.  Continuous processes might rely on trapping solids in cheap viscous liquids like silicone fluids, organic oils, or molten salts that slowly ooze down chamber walls and drain at the bottom.  

An alternative converter skips the steam. If the chamber floor is held at vacuum, each projectile arrives as fast plasma that a magnetic channel can decelerate and tap for current directly, the way a magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) generator draws power from moving conductive gas \cite{dominguez2024mhd}. This favors many rapid, small impulses over a few large ones, since a smaller pulse is easier to contain and clears before the next arrives.

\subsubsection{Stopping The Iron Instead Of Vaporizing It}\label{sec:coil_brake}
A third converter keeps the projectile intact.  Arrivals near Earth escape speed, the slow traffic of \autoref{sec:death_star}, can be flown as solid iron rods and stopped magnetically in a shaft beneath the tube.  The shaft is a coilgun run backwards, a linear generator whose moving part is the rod itself.  A rod entering at \SI{11.2}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and stopping in \SI{5.5}{\kilo\meter} decelerates at $v^2/2L = \SI{1.14e4}{\meter\per\second\squared}$, about $1160g$, and comes to rest in \SI{0.98}{\second}.  Nothing boils and nothing sprays.  The steam chamber, the fast rotating-disk airlock of \autoref{sec:vacuum_airlock}, and the wall-cleaning problem all go away together.

The reason to want this is thermal.  A steam chamber is a heat engine, so Carnot caps it near 40\% and every delivered watt of electricity arrives with about \SI{1.5}{\watt} of heat bound for the deep ocean.  A magnetic decelerator is not a heat engine.  Its waste heat is only what the machine loses, so a 90\% efficient stop rejects \SI{0.11}{\watt} per delivered watt.  That is a factor of 14 off the ocean heating computed above.

The rod's own share of that loss has a hard ceiling.  A kilogram arriving at \SI{11.2}{\kilo\meter\per\second} carries \SI{62.7}{\mega\joule}, while heating iron from room temperature and through the melt costs only \SI{0.93}{\mega\joule\per\kilo\gram}.  Deposit more than 1.5\% of the rod's kinetic energy in the rod and it arrives liquid.  Deposit 13\% and it arrives as vapor, which is the steam chamber again by a longer road.  Induced current in the rod flows in a skin of depth $\sqrt{2\rho/\omega\mu}$, about \SI{1.5}{\milli\meter} in iron at the \SI{11}{\kilo\hertz} a \SI{1}{\meter} pole pitch sees at entry.  Loss in that skin scales as $\sqrt{\rho\mu}$ at fixed force, so a higher-resistivity iron alloy makes the problem worse.  A copper skin over the iron core is the fix, since copper's lower resistivity and unit permeability cut the surface resistance by a factor near 80.  That puts roughly 0.1\% of the energy in the rod and warms it by \SI{170}{\kelvin}, against the \SI{1200}{\kelvin} bare iron would see.

Reuse is what makes the rods worth catching, and the return leg is cheap.  Reaching \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} for PuffSat interception takes only \SI{1.95}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so the toss costs \SI{1.9}{\mega\joule\per\kilo\gram}, 3\% of what the stop just collected.  The catch happens at the bottom of Earth's gravity well and the push happens at the top, so the well is never paid for twice.  A solid rod's ballistic coefficient near \SI{5e5}{\kilo\gram\per\square\meter} makes the climb through air nearly free, costing about 1\% of exit speed to drag and ablating a fraction of a millimeter off the nose.  At \SI{1}{\kilo\tonne\per\second} the toss deserves a launcher of its own rather than a rocket.  Methalox at the \SI{3.73}{\kilo\meter\per\second} exhaust speed of \autoref{sec:fare_comparison} would burn at least \SI{690}{\tonne\per\second} of propellant to deliver \SI{1.95}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, while a ground launcher need only supply the \SI{1.9}{\tera\watt} the rods carry away, and that speed is low enough for scramjets, spin launchers, magnetic tracks, or the base of a space fountain.  What the mechanism has to do is impart the speed and let the rod coast.  Carrying the mass up instead does not scale.  Winching at \SI{100}{\meter\per\second} takes \SI{2000}{\second} for the climb, and a \SI{1}{\kilo\tonne\per\second} flow would hang two million tonnes of iron on a structure rated for \SI{1100}{\tonne}.

The lunar bombardment of \autoref{sec:death_star} is one natural traffic for this converter, and it repairs that section's worst limitation.  Material launched from the Moon and falling to Earth arrives at \SI{11.1}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, within 1\% of the escape-speed case worked above, so the shaft stops it on the same terms.  It also arrives at any hour rather than only when the straw can face the Sun.  Iron for the rods is a co-product of the lunar oxygen that \autoref{sec:lunar_mining} already wants.  Hydrogen reduction of ilmenite, the iron--titanium oxide that runs to a fifth of some mare soils by volume, goes as $\mathrm{FeTiO_3 + H_2 \rightarrow Fe + TiO_2 + H_2O}$, and the water is then electrolyzed for its oxygen.  Every kilogram of oxygen leaves \SI{3.5}{\kilo\gram} of metallic iron behind.  Molten regolith electrolysis yields the same pair, oxygen at the anode and molten metal at the cathode.

Where the iron comes from decides what the traffic is worth.  A rod launched from the lunar surface onto an Earth-fall trajectory costs about \SI{2.6}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, or \SI{3.4}{\mega\joule\per\kilo\gram}, and delivers \SI{61.6}{\mega\joule\per\kilo\gram} at the bottom of the shaft.  Earth's gravity multiplies the launch investment eighteenfold, and in the $\Delta V$ that actually sets PuffSat mass (\autoref{sec:mass_fraction}) it is a factor of four.  An Earth-sourced rod tossed back up for another pass cannot do this.  Whatever speed it leaves \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} with is the speed it comes back with, so the PuffSats must supply the whole \SI{60.8}{\mega\joule\per\kilo\gram} of the climb to recover the \SI{62.7}{\mega\joule\per\kilo\gram} of the fall.  A reusable Earth rod is a bucket rather than a source.  It turns PuffSat kinetic energy into electricity efficiently and gains nothing from the well.  That still makes it the way to start, because Earth iron needs no lunar refinery, and the standing inventory is set by throughput times cycle time rather than by annual flow.  A one-day cycle at \SI{1}{\kilo\tonne\per\second} keeps \SI{86}{\mega\tonne} of iron in circulation, about 6\% of a year of world pig iron production.

Mixing the two is better than either.  The coils grip only the iron, so a reusable shell can carry a one-way lunar payload, and the eighteenfold multiplication then applies to the payload while the shell merely breaks even.  This also answers where the iron goes.  Rods flown one-way and pure would land \SI{31}{\giga\tonne} of iron on Earth each year, roughly 24 times world pig iron production, with nowhere obvious to put it.  Loose regolith arriving intact and cold is instead exactly what the carbonate sequestration of \autoref{sec:death_star} consumes, and the plant has process heat to spare for warming it.  The shell's wall carries the payload's inertia through the stop.  A \SI{13}{\centi\meter} rod \SI{10}{\meter} long with \SI{5}{\milli\meter} walls is 52\% regolith by mass at a bulk density of \SI{1500}{\kilo\gram\per\cubic\meter}, and decelerating that at $1160g$ puts \SI{990}{\mega\pascal} in the wall, which asks for maraging steel rather than mild steel.  Doubling the wall to \SI{1}{\centi\meter} drops the load to \SI{430}{\mega\pascal} and the payload share to 32\%.  Wall thickness is the knob that trades tonnage delivered against the alloy the rod is made of.

The near-Sun traffic can feed this converter as well, and it does not need a deeper shaft to do it.  A projectile returning at \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} does not have to enter the tube to power it.  Spent at the \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} interception altitude instead, it boosts iron onto an eccentric Earth orbit, and the iron falls back down the straw at \SI{11.1}{\kilo\meter\per\second} like everything else the shaft stops.  Closing speed is what makes the exchange so lopsided.  Diluted twenty-four to one as in \autoref{sec:methalox_rebuttal} the collision runs at $v_e = \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$, and \autoref{eq:external_reaction_mass} then lifts about \SI{44}{\kilo\gram} of rod to \SI{10.9}{\kilo\meter\per\second} for every kilogram of fast mass arriving.  At the $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.89$ of that section's pessimistic anchor it is \SI{36}{\kilo\gram}, and a pusher plate at $f = 0.8$ manages \SI{21}{\kilo\gram} by \autoref{eq:PuffSat_ratio}.
