Take the first figure.  Those rods carry \SI{2.7}{\giga\joule} to the bottom of the shaft per kilogram of fast mass, or \SI{2.4}{\giga\joule} of electricity after the 10\% stopping loss.

Diving that same kilogram into the steam chamber does better, by a factor of 1.9.  It deposits \SI{11.25}{\giga\joule} of heat and Carnot returns about \SI{4.5}{\giga\joule} of it.  The rod route pays that penalty in fast mass to buy two things the straw cannot give.  Waste heat falls from \SI{1.5}{\watt} per delivered watt to \SI{0.11}{\watt}, so \SI{55}{\tera\watt} of electricity sends \SI{6}{\tera\watt} into the ocean instead of \SI{83}{\tera\watt}.  The eccentric orbit is also a buffer.  The boost happens when the fast traffic arrives and the fall happens whenever the rods are phased to fall, so the daytime restriction of \autoref{sec:death_star} goes away without waiting on a lunar refinery.

The energy that misses the rods still has to go somewhere.  At the \SI{1}{\kilo\tonne\per\second} rod flow of \autoref{sec:death_star}, \SI{22.8}{\tonne\per\second} of fast mass arrives carrying \SI{257}{\tera\watt}, and only \SI{60}{\tera\watt} of that ends up as rod velocity.  The other \SI{197}{\tera\watt} leaves as plasma.  Most of it should leave for good, since a \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} exhaust off a rod moving at a few \si{\kilo\meter\per\second} clears the \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second} escape speed at \SI{200}{\kilo\meter}.  The bound remainder does not have to be large to matter.  Ten percent staying behind is \SI{20}{\tera\watt} into the upper atmosphere, three times what the shaft itself rejects.  That fraction comes out of the plume's velocity distribution, so it belongs with the nozzle calculation of \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}.

Two things keep this speculative.  The rod diameter the shaft can grip is $8\sigma_m L / \rho v^2$, and at \SI{5.5}{\kilo\meter} with a gap field near iron's \SI{2.1}{\tesla} saturation that is only \SI{13}{\centi\meter}, so a \SI{1}{\kilo\tonne\per\second} flow fills most of the tube's swept volume with iron.  Extraction is also uniform along the shaft at $\dot m a$, which is \SI{11}{\giga\watt} per meter with tens of gigawatts of switched peak power per stage across \SI{5.5}{\kilo\meter} of them.  Steam avoids that by feeding thousands of ordinary turbogenerators.  The shaft is also confined to slow arrivals.  At \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} the same stop wants a shaft a thousand kilometers deep, which is why the fast traffic enters through the boost above rather than through the tube.

\subsection{Vacuum Exchange And Airlock Details}\label{sec:vacuum_airlock}
An airlock separates the steam chamber from the rest of the tube as shown in \autoref{fig:power_plant_vacuum_exchange}.   A door with a closing speed of \SI{300}{\meter\per\second} could close a \SI{30}{\centi\meter} diameter aperture in \SI{1}{\milli\second}.  Even a \SI{5}{\meter} long airlock is long enough to prevent steam molecules from traversing the airlock in this short time.  

\begin{figure}[htpb]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.5\linewidth]{images/Power Plant Cooling and Generators.png}
    \caption{Diagram of the power plant and vacuum airlock system.  New airlock segments are rotated in between impacts.}
    \label{fig:power_plant_vacuum_exchange}
\end{figure}

Designing an airtight door that can open and close at the necessary speeds is beyond current technology. However, a system of rapidly rotating disks with strategically placed apertures can create a quick-closing, leaky seal.

This system works like this:

\begin{itemize}
\item Two or more pressure-resistant disks with identical radii of perhaps \SI{10}{\meter} are closely mounted on an axial shaft.

\item These disks spin at slightly different speeds, and each has a \SI{30}{\centi\meter} aperture near their outer edge.

\item We add mass along the radial line of each disk's aperture to keep the rotating disks balanced.

\item When the apertures don't overlap, they block the vacuum tube and prevent steam from entering.

\item The apertures align every few seconds over the airlock opening.   This gives us time to close a second airtight door over the airlock opening.

\item The disk apertures may align more frequently than projectile arrivals.  Since projectiles might only arrive every \SI{10}{\second}, it's tricky to design fast spinning disks that only overlap at this low frequency.  As long as the secondary door can close between alignments, and the alignments are synchronized with projectile arrivals, the airlock system meets its operational requirements.
\end{itemize}

This rotating disk system offloads handling the pressure differential between steam and vacuum.  That simplifies the design for the separate, high-performance airtight door that seals the vacuum completely. The airtight door is still fast, closing on the order of a few hundred milliseconds.  Before this door closes, steam leaks through the clearance between the disks and the airlock.  This clearance is likely at least a millimeter since we must handle vibrations.  To further mitigate steam ingress, chemical desiccants are embedded in the disk housing and airlock walls. Thin films of desiccants are also used along the first kilometer or so of the vacuum tube.  The disk housing and airlock walls might also be cooled to freeze or at least slow steam molecules.

At the bottom of the airlock, the projectile releases enormous heat on contact with steam. Asteroid air-burst simulations suggest most of this energy is directed in the projectile's direction of travel, away from the airlock and the closest chamber walls \cite{tunguska_downwards}. If still needed to handle the high initial heat, sacrificial plastic segments can form the bottom part of the airlock pipe. These plastics should be strong at low temperatures but vaporize easily to avoid creating shrapnel. Their brief exposure to steam before projectile impact can be buffered by a coat of ice, which forestalls heating. To prevent the production of highly toxic gases when they decompose, the plastic should not be halogenated. Cheap polyethylene pipes may satisfy all these requirements.  The pipe can be ribbed for strength and corrugated to stop buckling under the steam's pressure.  These techniques reduce the pipe mass needed to withstand the steam's compressive load.  A thicker plastic plug at the bottom of the pipe could drastically quicken projectile disintegration, especially if the projectile primarily contains materials like water or explosives. The projectile's enormous kinetic energy would pulverize it on plug impact. The resulting intense heat and shock wave breaks any solid debris into smaller shards that rapidly vaporize in the steam far from chamber walls.  An added benefit of these sacrificial segments is that they lengthen the time the airlock door has to seal before steam can travel far enough to enter the vacuum tube.

A new airlock segment is swapped in between kinetic projectile impacts, providing time to restore the high vacuum offline before its next use. Sacrificial bottom sections are also replaced and reattached while the main airlock segment is out of rotation.

\subsection{With Economies Of Scale, Our Ambitions Can Scale Too}\label{sec:strawway_economics}

While a global power plant requires a massive initial capital investment, its costs scale favorably with output. The cost of suspending a large vacuum tube depends very little on the plant's generating capacity. The cost of deep-sea cooling loops would also be amortized across the entire planetary-scale facility.  A multi-terawatt installation could achieve per-watt cost reductions through bulk procurement of thousands of identical turbine and generator units. 

Mass production reduces the cost of projectile electronics and microthrusters. The cost of these components per projectile also remains nearly the same as individual projectile size increases.  Ten seconds of planetary-scale power corresponds to $\SI{20}{\tera\watt} \times \SI{10}{\second} \approx \SI{5.56e7}{\kilo\watt\hour} = \$555,556$ at 1 cent per kilowatt-hour.  This energy revenue would likely dwarf the material costs of the projectiles themselves, especially if their interiors are mostly water or explosives. Such cheap-bulk interiors push the production cost under \$1 per megawatt-hour (\autoref{sec:methalox_rebuttal}), a tenth of the one-cent-per-kilowatt-hour selling price used above.      

Power from our plant could be globally distributed by supertanker-sized batteries that connect to urban power grids at coastal ports.  While the cost of utility-scale batteries is progressively declining, it remains a hurdle. Current battery designs prioritize efficient energy storage and release. However, if energy efficiency becomes less critical, we could design cheaper low-performance batteries. Given that our power source exhibits strong cost efficiencies, we could simply increase the power output to compensate for any energy losses incurred by using these more affordable batteries for distribution. 

Fossil fuels remain entrenched as the dominant energy source, even as cleaner alternatives like renewables gain traction. High-temperature heat from low-cost projectile impacts offers a novel route for synthesizing hydrocarbon fuels directly from water and carbon dioxide.

One promising pathway is the cerium oxide cycle \cite{CeriaThermoCycles}, where cerium oxide is reduced at high temperatures and low pressures. Operating at low pressures simplifies vacuum chamber and airlock design (see \autoref{sec:vacuum_tube_details}). The reduced cerium oxide then reacts with carbon dioxide to produce carbon monoxide. The water-gas-shift reaction converts part of this carbon monoxide and steam into carbon dioxide and hydrogen, setting the synthesis-gas ratio. Fischer--Tropsch synthesis then converts the resulting synthesis gas into hydrocarbons \cite{water_gas_shift,fischer_tropsch}.

An alternative approach, adapted from chemical looping in blast furnaces \cite{iron_chemical_looping}, uses iron to reduce carbon dioxide to carbon monoxide. Part of the carbon monoxide stream can react with steam through the water-gas-shift process to produce hydrogen and adjust the gas ratio. Fischer--Tropsch synthesis then converts the resulting mixture into hydrocarbons \cite{water_gas_shift,fischer_tropsch}. The resulting iron oxide may be thermally decomposed to regenerate iron, forming a closed-loop cycle. Iron, being denser than iron oxide, naturally separates under gravity, much like the stratification of slag and molten iron during steel production. Incoming projectiles can be designed to gasify just prior to impact, allowing energy deposition without direct mechanical disruption. At velocities around \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, low-density gas atoms penetrate a few nanometers into the lava, transferring heat through shallow kinetic interactions. This reduces shock propagation and ejecta formation. To further enhance coupling, finely divided iron oxide powder can be injected into the vacuum chamber, presenting a much larger effective surface area to the incoming hot gas. The powder absorbs and redistributes the energy before it settles back into the molten pool. The chamber walls remain protected from peak loads by the intervening lava, which is both a volumetric heat sink and a radiative buffer, damping transient spikes.

Both cerium and iron oxides decompose only under very low pressures, requiring aggressive removal of oxygen from the reaction environment. An alternative approach is to use water-splitting cycles, such as the sulfur–iodine or copper–chlorine processes, to generate hydrogen \cite{sulfur_iodine_chlorine}. Hydrogen, together with carbon dioxide, forms an ideal feedstock for hydrocarbon synthesis. Although these water-splitting cycles run cooler than direct thermolysis, the sulfur--iodine acid-decomposition step still operates near \SI{850}{\celsius} and exposes reactor walls to hot sulfuric acid. SiC-based ceramics, including silicon carbide matrix composites, are an established candidate liner material for this stage, combining corrosion resistance with the thermal-shock and fracture tolerance needed under pulsed operation.  These liners could coat a shock-absorbing nickel superalloy chamber, which is externally heated by a molten bath of lava or liquid iron.  A common superalloy such as Inconel 718 retains useful strength only to about \SI{700}{\celsius} \cite{inconel_718_strong}, below this step's temperature, so a higher-temperature alloy or active wall cooling would be required, and the liner and chamber would need compatibility and fatigue testing under repeated acid decomposition and pulsed shock.  Projectile impact energy keeps the lava molten, indirectly providing the steady high temperatures that are optimal for the reaction chamber. This configuration also shields the ceramics from direct impact shocks.

By bypassing costly steam turbines and simplifying vacuum tube maintenance, this direct-heating approach could enable viable hydrocarbon synthesis before large-scale electricity generation becomes cost-effective.

Though the power plant and distribution infrastructure would cost billions to construct and operate, a planetary-scale plant would make trillions annually.  Turns out, if \textit{Superman} wants to be as rich as \textit{Lex Luthor}, he should get limitless energy not from the light of a yellow sun, but from its gravity \cite{superman2025}.

\subsection{Comparison To Project PACER}
Project PACER was a 1970s Los Alamos concept to generate power by extracting steam from nuclear bombs detonated in a salt cavern \cite{pacer_1975}.   Later improvements protected the cavern walls with an artificial waterfall of tritium breeding liquid salts \cite{pacer_revisited}.  

Unlike PACER, our proposal does not produce radioactive waste or encourage the mass production of nuclear weapons.   Should a liquid wall be needed to protect the chamber, it can be made from cheap materials like water.  Tritium breeding and extraction are not required.   Our design eliminates the need for a biological shield, which simplifies the direct coupling of steam to turbines. This direct steam cycle enables the rapid heat exchange with the environment necessary to build terawatt-scale power plants.

\subsection{To Solve Climate Change, Make The Earth A \textit{Death Star}}\label{sec:death_star}
A \textit{Death Star} \cite{death_star} is a giant sphere with a narrow 2-meter opening where you can shoot something down to create an explosion inside the main reactor.   Building a \textit{Death Star} from scratch  would be a huge undertaking.   Fortunately, if we build the Straw Way To Heaven described in \autoref{sec:straw_way_to_heaven}, we're living on one.  Although our Straw Way generator produces clean energy from incoming projectiles, its power production is restricted to daytime hours when the straw can face the sun.   Also, a limitless clean energy source would only slow climate change.   Industrial carbon emissions from steel, cement, agriculture, and other processes would still heat the planet \cite{steel_and_cement}.   

\subsubsection{That's No Moon .  Oh Wait, It's Literally Our Moon}\cite{kenobi1977moon}
Projectiles made from lunar materials could enter a terrestrial Straw Way from high angles of attack around the clock.  To generate \SI{60}{\tera\watt} of heat from lunar bombardment, we'd need an astonishing \SI{1}{\kilo\tonne\per\second} of lunar mass to pass through the terrestrial vacuum tube, since lunar material arrives far slower than the \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} solar projectiles and so carries far less kinetic energy per kilogram. Such a vast quantity is daunting, but the momentum for launching off the moon could come from kinetic projectiles inbound from the Sun (as described in \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}). Beyond providing thrust, these projectiles aimed at the Moon could vaporize lunar regolith. The resulting thermal energy could be harnessed for electricity generation or to power chemical and industrial processes directly. This lunar power source could support large-scale mining and production of the oxygen gas used in PuffSat pulsed propulsion  (as discussed in \autoref{sec:lunar_mining}).  If those projectiles are flown as iron rods rather than loose regolith, the magnetic converter of \autoref{sec:coil_brake} can stop them intact and skip the steam cycle altogether.

Slower arrivals do not cost the tube its vacuum. The argon pumping of \autoref{sec:vacuum_tube_details} works by burying stray argon atoms in a solid surface, and how fast the projectile is moving decides which surface is likelier to catch them. Argon is heavier than carbon or aluminum, so a light nose rarely sends it backward. A thin iron skin, being heavier than argon, does. A projectile arriving near Earth escape speed, about \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, meets residual argon gently. The argon tends to bounce off that skin rather than bury itself in it, then lodge about a nanometer into the tube wall. The \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} solar projectiles favor the other outcome, driving most of the argon they strike into their own noses to leave the tube with them. Neither outcome excludes the other, and both pull argon out of the vacuum, so the traffic that generates power also does the pumping. What the mechanism needs is a floor rather than a ceiling. Anything falling from more than a few thousand kilometers of altitude arrives fast enough. That covers lunar material, payloads returning from Ceres, and the payloads that either the Jupiter-only cycle of \autoref{sec:jupiter_only_growth} or the near-Sun returns of \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket} boost into eccentric Earth orbits.

Helium is the marginal case. It is light, so a slow projectile leaves it with far less energy than argon, too little to knock an aluminum atom out of place. Helium is also small enough to slip between those atoms without moving any of them, which is how helium loads into tungsten walls in fusion devices at comparable energies, at impact energies too low to sputter the surface at all \cite{baldwin2008helium,kajita2009tungsten}. What fraction stays put is not something we can settle here. The gap costs little either way. Helium is a few parts per million of air against argon's nine thousand, and the metallized wall that holds off atomic oxygen also slows helium permeation. Whatever collects is cleared on the refurbishment cycle already planned in \autoref{sec:vacuum_tube_details}.

Lunar regolith is primarily carbon-depleted rock \cite{mckay1991lunar}. After the regolith-derived impactors heat steam to drive a global-scale generator, the process yields a waste column of hot regolith and scalding water. If this waste is channeled through thermally insulated pipes into deep ocean water, the intense hydrostatic pressure compresses the regolith. Its large surface area and elevated temperature accelerate chemical reactions that convert silicates into carbonates, pulling dissolved carbon dioxide from seawater \cite{seifritz1990silicates}. Deep ocean pressure further shifts chemical equilibrium toward carbonate formation \cite{kelemen2008peridotite}. With an incoming regolith flow of $\SI{1}{\kilo\tonne\per\second} \approx \SI{31}{\giga\tonne\per\year}$, we could mineralize billions of tons of dissolved carbon dioxide annually. This would enhance the ocean's capacity to absorb atmospheric carbon dioxide, making lunar mineral sequestration a viable strategy to offset most of the world's industrial carbon emissions.



\subsection{Fusion Propulsion And Epstein Drives}\label{sec:epstein_drives}
The fast projectiles from \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket} could propel a spacecraft around the inner solar system at relatively high speeds.   As our technology advances, we'd want to travel on brachistochrone trajectories under constant Earth gravity acceleration between the planets as envisioned in \textit{The Expanse} \cite{Corey2012Drive}.  Evaporative cooling, paired with high-reflectivity coatings inspired by NASA's yttrium-oxide ``solar white'' work for cryo-tank thermal control \cite{nasa_solar_white_y2o3}, could in principle enable spacecraft to approach the Sun to 2 solar radii. That's closer than the Parker Solar Probe's 9.86 solar radii \cite{nasa2018parker_heat}.  At this close solar periapsis, prograde and retrograde projectile collision velocities would exceed \SI{869}{\kilo\meter\per\second} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025} in the reference frame of a fusion pulsed propulsion chamber on the target rocket.  

A 1979 Los Alamos Impact Fusion workshop concluded speeds of just \SI{200}{\kilo\meter\per\second} might be sufficient for precompressed gram sized fusion fuel projectiles to ignite \cite{impactfusion1979}.   Larger projectiles would likely be easier to confine long enough for high fusion energy gain.  Head-on thermalization alone still falls short of ignition. At \SI{869}{\kilo\meter\per\second} closing speed, each deuterium--tritium ion carries about \SI{2.4}{\kilo\electronvolt} of kinetic energy, which thermalizes near \SI{1}{\kilo\electronvolt} once electrons take their share. An igniting hotspot, the small region whose fusion output heats the fuel around it, needs more than \SI{5}{\kilo\electronvolt} \cite{murakami2005impact}. The gap is a factor of about 2.5 in velocity, and it must be closed only in that small region, not in the bulk fuel. Energy is not the constraint. Each kilogram of colliding mass carries about \SI{94}{\giga\joule}, while an igniter deposits only tens of kilojoules \cite{murakami2005impact}. The design problem is concentration in space and time, not supply.

Inertial confinement fusion long ago learned to separate the two jobs. Assemble the fuel cold and dense, then heat the hotspot fast. Both halves now have impact-driven precedents. First Light Fusion reported fusion neutrons in 2022, independently reviewed by the UKAEA, by firing a projectile at \SI{6.5}{\kilo\meter\per\second} into a shaped target \cite{firstlight2022fusion}. The target's internal geometry converted the planar impact into a convergent implosion and drove the target material past \SI{70}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. Murakami and Nagatomo's impact ignition scheme supplies the spark. A small, dense fragment striking precompressed fuel at about \SI{1000}{\kilo\meter\per\second} converts its kinetic energy into a hotspot above \SI{5}{\kilo\electronvolt} \cite{murakami2005impact}. Osaka experiments later accelerated deuterated foils to \SI{600}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and measured a hundredfold neutron yield increase when the collision was timed to peak compression \cite{azechi2009impact}. Laser ablation strains to push igniters to such speeds. Orbital mechanics hands us \SI{869}{\kilo\meter\per\second} before the target does any work at all.

A single passive target can encode the whole sequence, with a tamper of tungsten or another dense metal surrounding a few hundred grams of fusion fuel. Layers graded from heavy to light launch a ramped compression wave into the fuel rather than one strong shock. A single shock compresses an ideal monatomic gas at most fourfold and sets a high adiabat, the entropy floor that resists further squeezing, while a ramp keeps the fuel cold so the same energy buys far more density \cite{zeldovich_raizer}. A tungsten anvil sits at the stagnation point. A recessed central fuel column, delayed by its own geometry as in First Light's targets, arrives at full closing speed only after the ramp has finished. Fuel stagnating against the anvil's high shock impedance (density times shock speed, which sets how velocity partitions at an interface) is re-shocked by reflection, reaching roughly twice the temperature of a fuel-on-fuel collision at the same speed. The tungsten itself must stay out of the fuel. Bremsstrahlung losses grow with the square of atomic number, so high-Z mix is the classic hotspot killer, and a thin low-Z liner such as carbon buffers the interface while preserving the reflected shock. The liner does not weaken the anvil because reflection strength is set by what stands behind the interface. The first reflection off the carbon is soft, but the shock reverberates between fuel and tungsten inside the thin layer, and after a few round trips the pressure at the fuel face converges to what a bare tungsten wall would deliver \cite{zeldovich_raizer}. A liner much thinner than the fuel column keeps those round trips short compared to the stagnation time, so the fuel reaches nearly the full re-shock temperature without touching a tungsten atom. The anvil's own heating is incidental. Shocked tungsten carries the most energy per atom and glows hardest in X-rays, and the carbon screens the fuel from that glow as well. Key elements of the anvil-on-fuel architecture already run in the laboratory, though driven by lasers rather than impact. Los Alamos double shell capsules for the National Ignition Facility drive a tungsten or molybdenum inner shell directly onto deuterium--tritium fuel, shock heating it toward volume ignition near \SI{3}{\kilo\electronvolt} \cite{montgomery2018doubleshell}. Later designs grade beryllium into the tungsten to tame instability growth and mix at that same metal--fuel interface \cite{stark2024gradedshells}, the same low-Z buffering proposed here. The surrounding tamper still provides inertial confinement and acts as a radiation case, keeping shock-generated X-rays off the cryogenic fuel during compression (see \autoref{sec:radiative_differences}). The design keeps the staging lesson of thermonuclear weapons, compress first and ignite second \cite{doe_hbomb_history}, but the spark plug is kinetic rather than fissile. Pulsed fusion propulsion would likely still employ the shaped charges Project Orion explored to redirect energy and thrust.

The same scheme scales to the shallower dives this paper plans. Closing speeds run about \SI{394}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at the Parker Solar Probe's 9.86 solar radii, \SI{618}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at 4 solar radii (see \autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal}), and \SI{869}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at 2 solar radii. Against the roughly \SI{1000}{\kilo\meter\per\second} igniter threshold, the graded stack must amplify the central column's speed by factors of about 2.5, 1.6, and 1.15. Shock breakout from a heavy layer into a lighter one can about double the particle velocity, so one stage of grading covers the two deeper dives. Parker depth needs compounded stages whose fast layers grow thin and rarefied, costing the igniter the density impact ignition wants, so ignition there is marginal rather than excluded. The architecture degrades gracefully either way. A fizzle still delivers the full kinetic pulse, so fusion multiplies pulse energy rather than gating the propulsion.

The multiplier is not free, and its price is thermal. A deuterium--tritium pulse sends 80\% of its yield out as \SI{14.1}{\mega\electronvolt} neutrons that no magnetic nozzle can turn and no low-Z liner can stop, so the coils behind it need meter-thick shielding with radiators of its own \cite{orth2003vista}. \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} works the comparison and finds that a purely kinetic pulse of the same energy costs neither the shield nor its heat rejection. Fusion has to buy back that mass in added thrust before it is worth carrying, which is the practical argument for the aneutronic fuel below.

A second bill comes due for the same reason, and it is one the shielding argument tends to hide. Every pulsed nuclear engine has to light its own pulses. Something has to compress the next target, and at one pulse per second that machine wants megajoules on a one-second turnaround. Solar panels and heat engines are too heavy at that duty, so these designs take the energy back out of the exhaust instead, with a flux compression generator. The plasma expands into a magnetic field and shoves the field ahead of itself. That changing field drives current into a capacitor bank, the way a bicycle dynamo drives a lamp. The trouble is that the same coils have to be the nozzle. VISTA and the HOPE study both split the magnet, with superconducting coils to hold the field and separate conducting coils to take the induced current \cite{orth2003vista,schilling2022nozzle}. Schilling's point design masses \SI{35}{\tonne} to bank \SI{1.2}{\mega\joule} \cite{schilling2022nozzle}. That is the bill a driven pulse pays. Ours is not driven. The energy arrives already in the projectile, paid for by the orbit, so nothing aboard has to be charged between shots. \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} builds the same two-part magnet and puts it to the opposite use. It holds the field in superconductor and dumps the induced current into a copper shell, so nothing has to be banked. The onboard power the architecture actually needs is what keeps the cryostats cold, which is kilowatts drawn steadily across a years-long cruise rather than megajoules delivered in a second. Solar panels and a battery are the right shape for that, and they are the shape a pulsed nuclear engine cannot use.

With mostly aneutronic fusion fuel, projectiles could reach velocities on the order of thousands of kilometers per second.  Aneutronic fusion directly converts energy into charged particle kinetic energy, bypassing the mass penalty of neutron shielding.  A seed of deuterium-tritium could ignite a larger deuterium and helium-3 mixture to achieve aneutronic fusion rocket speeds.   As the spacecraft accelerates, it can also reach speeds where it triggers fusion by crashing into slower moving fusion fuel targets.   If carefully planned, these chains of fast-moving projectiles could create interplanetary highways, allowing people to travel between planets under comfortable artificial Earth gravity in time frames comparable to ocean liners crossing the Atlantic.  These highways adapt Kare's interstellar fusion runway (see \autoref{sec:pellet_prior_art}) to interplanetary trips \cite{kare1997buzzbomb, greason2020runway}.  

\subsection{The Sixth Question For Climate Change}\label{sec:sith_question}
Bill Gates' excellent book, \textit{How to Avoid a Climate Disaster} \cite{gates2021avoid}, details five practical questions \cite{breakthroughenergy_2021_five} any effective climate solution must address. Our Straw Way To Heaven (see \autoref{sec:straw_way_to_heaven}) addresses Gates' questions better than other alternatives. It zeroes carbon emissions, reverses cement emissions, and supplies indefinite global power at marginal costs that decline with output.

Yet I propose a sixth question that could rally public support for a particular climate solution. Beyond just reducing our electric bills and helping the environment, how will this breakthrough improve daily life? An investment on the scale required to solve climate change should grant human society new abilities, such as hypervelocity transport and affordable space travel. PuffSat pulsed propulsion can achieve all of these things.  

\section{When We Get Greedy, We'll Go to Phoebe}\label{sec:greedy_phoebe}
In \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket} we discussed using Sun-grazing Oberth maneuvers to accelerate projectiles to \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}. These high velocities enable the large $\Delta V$ required to place the next suborbital rocket into prograde and retrograde colliding orbits near the Sun.  However, as detailed in \autoref{sec:periapsis_challenges}, such close solar approaches introduce severe engineering and operational challenges, increasing projectile costs compared to less extreme solar encounters.  Suborbital rockets used to launch from Earth might also produce unacceptable levels of pollution in the upper atmosphere.

Orbits with higher apoapsis require less $\Delta V$ to shed orbital angular momentum and lower their periapsis. For example, transferring to a solar impact trajectory from Saturn requires only about \SI{10}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, compared to roughly \SI{30}{\kilo\meter\per\second} from Earth \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. This makes Saturn a more efficient departure point for reaching the inner solar system or the Sun.

Saturn's irregular moon Phoebe lies just \SI{6.6}{\degree} from the solar ecliptic plane (its orbital inclination of \SI{173.4}{\degree} falls \SI{6.6}{\degree} short of a perfectly retrograde \SI{180}{\degree}) \cite{phoebe}, giving it a favorable alignment for interplanetary transfers. Its high-altitude orbit around Saturn yields a low orbital velocity, enabling efficient low $\Delta V$ transfers to elliptical trajectories that match Phoebe's apoapsis and dip toward Saturn at periapsis.  From Phoebe, a prograde rocket could be launched to collide with retrograde payloads also originating from Phoebe. These collisions could take place in a pulsed propulsion chamber near Saturn, enabling highly efficient Oberth maneuvers. With careful targeting to avoid Saturn's rings, this method could launch spacecraft toward the inner solar system. Precisely aiming such collisions near Saturn is challenging, but likely easier than attempting similar maneuvers at the extreme conditions of close solar periapsis described in \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}. PuffSats launched from Saturn toward Earth could power a Straw Way generator (see \autoref{sec:straw_way_to_heaven}) or deliver suborbital rockets into orbit (see \autoref{sec:starship_safelaunch}).

These rockets could also return to Phoebe at high speeds, launching additional mass toward Saturn via pulsed propulsion. This bootstrapped launch cycle from Phoebe mirrors the lunar exponential growth loop discussed in \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel}, but benefits both from Phoebe's lower escape velocity and from Saturn's larger gravitational Oberth effect. Power for mining operations on Phoebe could come from solar energy concentrated using lenses made from Phoebe's native ice or locally produced plastic. Fission reactors transported from Earth are another possibility, as is thermal energy generated by returning impactors.  One key advantage kinetic impact energy offers is that its power doesn't decline quadratically with solar distance, unlike solar panels.

With a bulk density consistent with roughly 50\% ice by mass \cite{castillo_rogez2012_phoebe} and surface ice mapped by Cassini \cite{phoebe}, Phoebe offers nearly unlimited volatile resources to fill PuffSats. Unlike the Moon's permanently shadowed ice deposits, Phoebe's ice-rich regions receive direct sunlight. Although faint, this illumination enables solar concentrators to generate power directly adjacent to mining operations. Phoebe's abundant water could be used to wash regolith from equipment, mitigating the mechanical fouling that plagues lunar hardware. Its regolith, composed largely of frozen volatiles, is likely softer and less abrasive than the Moon's harsh dust, reducing wear on machinery. By contrast, lunar volatiles are scarce, and oxygen extraction from lunar regolith (see \autoref{sec:lunar_mining}) is highly energy-intensive.

Phoebe's ultra-low gravity makes it difficult to anchor mining infrastructure securely. However, its volatile-rich composition allows another approach: retro-rockets powered by in-situ steam or chemical propellants manufactured from Phoebe's volatiles. These thrusters press equipment against the surface and absorb the recoil of mining and launch operations, supplying the hold-down force that gravity cannot. To save energy, icy reaction mass could also be launched mechanically from Phoebe's surface without first converting it to steam. If Phoebe's gravity proves too low for certain operations, Iapetus could be a backup site. Similarly, Jupiter's moon Himalia may support analogous maneuvers if exploration reveals sizable volatile deposits.

Mining moons like Phoebe to supply PuffSat payloads is easier than extracting conventional raw materials for Earth delivery. PuffSats execute high-velocity flybys, eliminating the need for costly deceleration maneuvers or bulky reentry shielding near Earth. And unlike ice or gold, the ultra-high-energy-density pulses generated by PuffSat impacts are irreproducible. No known terrestrial technology, short of nuclear weapons, can produce such large-scale, high-density energy bursts.

\subsection{Mining Helium-3} \label{sec:mining_helium_3}
Helium-3 for aneutronic fusion may be available on the Earth's moon \cite{wittenberg1986lunar_he3}, but likely not in quantities to support large-scale fast interplanetary transport as discussed in \autoref{sec:epstein_drives}.   However, the gas giants contain vast supplies of helium-3 \cite{palaszewski2005atmospheric}.   A nuclear fission thermal rocket or scramjet could extract helium-3 and hydrogen while flying in Saturn's atmosphere and then jump a few thousand kilometers above the atmosphere.  It could then separate from a payload carrying mined helium-3, falling back to Saturn to mine more.  An accident on such a fission rocket would cause no harm, because the heavy fission byproducts would sink to Saturn's core, with no surface to contaminate. Propulsion PuffSats from Phoebe could then send the helium-3 payload into the inner solar system for use in fusion rockets.  

If fission rockets prove politically unpalatable, chemical rockets may also work.   Chemical rocket fuel powers a motor in Saturn's atmosphere for the energy to mine helium-3.  The rocket then fires its engines to hop above Saturn's atmosphere.   Propulsion PuffSats from Phoebe push the rocket into a stable orbit where it is refueled and releases its helium-3 payload.  Then new propulsion PuffSats decelerate the refueled rocket so that it falls into Saturn's atmosphere for another mining round.

\subsection{A Ceres-ly Good Alternative}\label{sec:ceresly_good}
Ceres may offer a Goldilocks environment for sourcing PuffSat materials. Pre-Dawn modeling of its low bulk density put Ceres' water-ice content at roughly 17 to 27\% by mass \cite{mccord_sotin2005ceres}, and the Dawn mission later found ice within \SI{1}{\meter} of the surface at high latitudes \cite{prettyman2017dawn_ceres_water}. Near-surface deposits may also contain nearly 20\% carbon \cite{marchi2019_ceres_carbon}, enabling in-situ production of methane and other volatiles. Nitrogen-rich phyllosilicates found on Ceres are promising for fabricating micro-explosives \cite{desanctis2015_ceres_ammoniated_phyllosilicates}. In short, Ceres contains all the essential chemistry for manufacturing icy PuffSats on-site (see \autoref{sec:icy_puffsat}).

Ceres offers adequate solar illumination, gravity sufficient for mining infrastructure, shorter transit times than Phoebe, and a low escape velocity. A solar impact trajectory from Ceres requires only about \SI{18}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, considerably less than the roughly \SI{30}{\kilo\meter\per\second} required from Earth \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. This lower $\Delta V$ supports Ceres-based launch cycles that harness the Oberth effect near the Sun with less extreme perihelion or increased payload capacity per cycle relative to Earth-based alternatives (see \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}).

Achieving centimeter-accuracy PuffSat interceptions around Ceres may prove easier than in most other prime solar system locations. In high-altitude, easily accessible orbits above Ceres, PuffSats would encounter negligible unmodeled forces. Atmospheric drag is absent, magnetic interference is small, solar illumination perturbations are modest, and deviations from modeled gravity are weak. Jupiter's influence is non-negligible but predictable. 

Rather than relying on active solar tracking in the dim sunlight around Ceres' ice-rich poles, solar power depots can be stationed in polar sun-synchronous orbits. These orbital platforms maintain continuous exposure to sunlight, bypassing the thermal cycling and power interruptions caused by Ceres' night. If we make transparent plastics like acrylic or polycarbonate from Ceres' raw material, we can produce thick concentrating optics that shield photovoltaic arrays from micrometeorites and space radiation. We favor UV-stable acrylic over polycarbonate, which yellows under ultraviolet \cite{polycarbonate_uv_aging}, and vacuum-bake the optic before flight so its outgassing does not fog the array \cite{outgassing_rp1124}. Outgassing rate also falls steeply as temperature drops, following an Arrhenius dependence \cite{outgassing_temperature_decay}, so at Ceres' orbital temperatures, more than \SI{200}{\kelvin} below the \SI{125}{\celsius} screening test, a bake-dried optic sheds volatiles far more slowly. Should plastic prove too lossy over time, a fused-silica optic drawn from the same regolith avoids both, drawing on fused silica's long use as the spacecraft window material from Apollo to the ISS \cite{fused_silica_windows}.  A reflective concentrator, an aluminized mirror instead of a transmissive lens, is a further option and avoids transmission losses altogether. This shielding extends solar panel lifespans without imported mass from Earth. Ceres' low gravity enables lofting mined carbon and water into orbit with minimal rocket propulsion. Once in orbit, these volatiles can be converted into fuel at solar-powered satellites.  This fuel maintains satellite orbits, enables return trips to Ceres, and powers mining operations on the surface.   We close the loop by mining more volatiles on Ceres and taking them back to the solar satellites for fuel conversion.

Converting those volatiles into fuel admits two chemistries. Methane and oxygen (methalox), both made from Ceres' carbon and water, are the workhorse and need far less cooling than hydrogen to store as liquids. Liquid hydrogen and oxygen are the higher-performance option, and the passive sunshade from \autoref{sec:lox_puffsat} is what makes hydrogen practical this far out. A shaded radiator equilibrates with the attenuated sunlight, so its temperature falls as the inverse square root of heliocentric distance. Anchored on the JWST sunshield's \SI{40}{\kelvin} at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} \cite{jwst_sunshield}, that is about \SI{24}{\kelvin} at Ceres near \SI{2.77}{\astronomicalunit}. Hydrogen's critical temperature, the warmest at which any pressure still holds it liquid, is \SI{33}{\kelvin} \cite{nist_webbook_hydrogen}, so a passive shade keeps hydrogen liquid only beyond about \SI{1.5}{\astronomicalunit}. At the Moon or Mercury no shade works at any pressure, while at Ceres liquid hydrogen sits at a few bar. The perihelion-lowering departure burn is made at Ceres' aphelion, with the inner leg flown ballistically or by PuffSat collision, so the hydrogen is produced and burned cold, never carried hot. That buys the higher specific impulse of hydrogen and oxygen, roughly \SI{450}{\second} against about \SI{360}{\second} for methalox \cite{sutton_rocket_propulsion}. The gain serves the local logistics rockets these operations already need: return trips, orbit maintenance, mining hold-down and retro braking, and the Saturn helium-3 miner (see \autoref{sec:mining_helium_3}). Closer to the Sun, where no shade can keep a cryogen liquid, the same stored water is electrolyzed on demand with onboard solar power. It feeds either a chemical thruster (Imperial's ICE-Cube \cite{imperial_ice_cube}) or a Hall-effect thruster (Imperial's water-electrolysis Hall thruster \cite{schwertheim2022wet_het}), the same make-it-on-demand approach as the PuffSat fine-control thrusters (see \autoref{sec:fine_control_thrusters}).

Unlike Phoebe, Ceres lacks a nearby planetary body for Oberth maneuvers. However, PuffSats returning from solar periapsis can deliver momentum for high-$\Delta V$ trajectories, either back to Earth or towards close perihelion. These returning PuffSats also offer an alternative to solar power. Their controlled impacts can be used to explosively excavate rocky overburden and transiently melt large quantities of subsurface ice, enabling easy pumping to fill new PuffSats.

\subsection{Fast Money Sound Nice?  Why Not Tap Mercury's Ice?}
Mercury harbors an estimated 20 billion to 1 trillion tons of water ice at its permanently shadowed poles \cite{lawrence2013mercury_ice}, with graphite as the dominant darkening agent across its surface at a few weight percent \cite{peplowski2016mercury_carbon}. These resources enable in-situ fabrication of icy PuffSats (see \autoref{sec:icy_puffsat}), which can be filled with harvested water. Heat-resistant PuffSat skins and structural plastics could be synthesized from graphite-derived carbon and ice, forming ISRU-enabled projectiles.

PuffSats launched from Mercury into low-perihelion orbits (see \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}) would return at extremely high velocities, ideal for PuffSat propulsion. Mercury's proximity to the Sun offers abundant solar energy for surface operations. Its short orbital period also turns launches around far faster than at Earth or destinations beyond \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} such as Phoebe or Ceres, supporting exponential scaling of launch capacity.

However, landing on Mercury remains challenging due to its deep position within the Sun's gravity well, which demands large deceleration \cite{benkhoff2021bepicolombo}. PuffSats approaching an incoming craft opposite its direction of motion could fully decelerate the craft by crashing into its pusher plate.

Key disadvantages include Mercury's harsh regolith, extreme thermal environment, and the high $\Delta V$ required to launch into retrograde solar trajectories. To overcome this high $\Delta V$ requirement, projectiles would collide at somewhat lower perihelion, gaining additional return velocity.  Higher return velocities provide greater kinetic energy for a retrograde solar injection from Mercury.

\subsection{An Atmosphere That \textit{Mars} Dreams of PuffSat Propulsion}\label{sec:mars_skyhook}
Despite Mars' abundant raw materials, its atmosphere makes it a less-than-ideal environment for sourcing the water ice needed to fill icy PuffSats (see \autoref{sec:icy_puffsat}). A system is needed to get the mined resources above the Martian atmosphere before PuffSat pulsed propulsion can push them on their way. One solution could be to use a rotating skyhook, similar to the Boeing/Tethers Unlimited HASTOL proposal \cite{skyhook_hastol}.

A skyhook with a tip speed of approximately $\SI{3.5}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$ could effectively match and cancel the orbital velocity of a low Mars orbit, enabling direct payload transfer. This tip-speed regime matches the tapered high-modulus polyethylene (HMPE) tether architecture analyzed in the HASTOL Phase II study \cite{hastol_phase_ii}, which examined an Earth skyhook using HMPE fiber \cite{dyneema_sk99_datasheet}; the Mars scaling here is our own extrapolation.  A spinning skyhook holds steady tension for years, the regime where polyethylene creeps, so the tether wants a creep-free fiber. PBO carries about \num{1.6} times Vectran's specific strength at similarly low creep \cite{zylon_technical_info}, which holds tether mass down (see \autoref{sec:fiber_selection}). A UV-opaque metallized coating is intended to shield the tip from the sunlight, atomic oxygen, and abrasive dust it meets at perigee, though such tether coatings still need orbital qualification \cite{hastol_phase_ii}, and Mars' dry environment largely suppresses PBO's hydrolysis weakness.  The skyhook itself could be reaccelerated by PuffSats returning at high speeds from solar perihelion, providing pulsed propulsion.

However, since Ceres does not require a skyhook and offers other advantages (see \autoref{sec:ceresly_good}), it appears to be a superior location for early PuffSat mining.



\section{War, Policy, And Pulsed Propulsion}
\subsection{Fusion Rockets And The Outer Space Treaty}
The Outer Space Treaty \cite{outer_space_treaty} prohibits nuclear weapons in space. The legality of pure fusion rockets like those discussed in \autoref{sec:epstein_drives} requires clarification, likely through international dialogue and new legal frameworks.  Large ``cruise ship'' vehicles traveling on brachistochrone trajectories at extreme velocities would possess enormous kinetic energies. A large cruise ship displaces on the order of \SI{1e8}{\kilo\gram} \cite{livescience_cruise_displacement}.  At \SI{500}{\kilo\meter\per\second} it would carry roughly \SI{1.3e19}{\joule}, about \SI{3000}{\mega\tonne} of TNT equivalent, some 60 times the yield of Tsar Bomba, the largest device ever detonated \cite{tsar_bomba_ahf}. As such, the deployment of these systems demands fail-safe, enforceable regulatory and safety protocols.

\subsection{Hackers Must Be Prevented From Causing Catastrophic Explosions}
As mentioned in \autoref{sec:vacuum_tube_details}, the energy projectiles for the Straw Way To Heaven discussed in \autoref{sec:straw_way_to_heaven} are intentionally designed to burn up high in the atmosphere without causing damage in case of a mishap.  However, a hacker could manipulate microthrusters on the projectiles to enter the atmosphere simultaneously rather than in sequence, leading to Tunguska-sized \cite{longo2007tunguska} multi-megaton explosions.  We can mitigate this risk with standard software security practices like using memory-safe languages and authenticated communication.

\subsection{Risk Of PuffSat Sabotage}
PuffSat pulsed propulsion might be useful for launching military assets like Golden Dome \cite{crs_golden_dome} or the Brilliant Boulders \cite{brilliant_boulders} discussed on the blog related to this paper \cite{aim2024}.   The PuffSats described in \autoref{sec:lunar_rockets_no_fuel} are likely vulnerable to destruction from high-powered terrestrial lasers by a military adversary.  Nations will need to defend their payloads from sabotage by hostile actors.

\subsection{Aviation Exclusion Zone Needed For Straw Ways}
Since the Straw Way To Heaven (see \autoref{sec:straw_way_to_heaven}) is a fixed structure in the sky, we will need regulations to ban aviation in its vicinity.   This seems feasible given the Straw Way will be placed in a remote area.   The host nation must also guard the Straw Way against sabotage.


\subsection{Overflight Of Third Countries And Strategic Stability}\label{sec:overflight_politics}
A suborbital point-to-point vehicle overflies countries between its departure and destination. Sovereignty above the Kármán line near \SI{100}{\kilo\meter} is not settled in law \cite{mcdowell2018karman}, so this overflight sits in a legal gray zone. Interception is one risk. Nations have shot down civilian airliners before, as with MH17 over Ukraine in 2014 \cite{dsb_mh17}. This violated the Chicago Convention, which prohibits the use of weapons against civil aircraft in flight \cite{chicago_3bis}. A vehicle on a ballistic arc can also be misread as a missile attack by early-warning systems.

Because the intercity vehicle reaches orbital velocity before reentry (see \autoref{sec:200_mile_high}), it can avoid a sensitive region by flying the long way around the great circle. The vehicle stays on orbit through the far arc and deorbits on the approach to the destination. This costs extra flight time rather than much additional $\Delta V$, so it is the simplest way to route around a contested region. If the long way is unacceptable, the vehicle can instead change course at a waypoint to steer around the region. That course change can come from an onboard correction burn or from a second PuffSat formation positioned along the path.

Flying most of the way around the planet at orbital velocity resembles a Fractional Orbital Bombardment System \cite{salt2_fobs}, which early-warning systems treat as a threat. This is best handled through policy rather than through routing. PuffSats are launched ahead of the flight into tracked orbits, and the vehicle departs a known airport on a published schedule. Monitoring systems then have time to tell a PuffSat-propelled craft from a surprise ground launch, given good-faith cooperation backed by shared tracking and prior notice. Pre-launch notification agreements already serve this purpose for ballistic missiles \cite{us_ussr_launch_notification}.

The real goal is a policy agreement not to extend armed conflict into civilian space transit. The norm against attacking civil aircraft \cite{chicago_3bis} should extend to suborbital and orbital passenger travel, consistent with the peaceful-use principle of the Outer Space Treaty \cite{outer_space_treaty}.

\subsection{Don't Sweat the Stratosphere (But Do Watch It)}\label{sec:ozone_policy}\cite{carlson1997smallstuff}
SpaceX's Earth-to-Earth Starship (see \autoref{sec:200_mile_high}) brakes by hitting the atmosphere at orbital speed. The bow shock, the shell of compressed air ahead of the vehicle, heats the surrounding gas past several thousand kelvin and makes nitric oxide out of ordinary nitrogen and oxygen. Together, this nitric oxide and the nitrogen dioxide it forms (\ce{NO_x}) make up one of the largest ozone-depletion terms for present-day spaceflight, on par with the chlorine from solid rocket motors \cite{ryan2022rocket}. A PuffSat suborbital plane has another route. It sheds orbital velocity against a retrograde formation of reentry PuffSats (see \autoref{sec:200_mile_high}) and enters the air slowly, with a weaker shock and far less \ce{NO_x}. Their bulk of ice, carbon, and propellant gasifies to water, nitrogen, oxygen, and carbon dioxide, largely avoiding the metal-oxide pathway flagged for reentering satellites \cite{ferreira2024ozone, barker2026megaconstellation}, though a full accounting would still include the small electronics, battery, and thruster masses each PuffSat carries. The braking is not free. The reentry PuffSats are extra mass that had to be launched, and a slow entry means much less \ce{NO_x}, not zero. Removing the dominant ozone term by design is still a real gain over aerobraking.

Water is the residual worth watching. Both the suborbital rocket burn and the Starship that lofts the PuffSats inject water vapor into the stratosphere on the way up. At the scale of replacing airline travel, that input deserves monitoring. Stratospheric water mildly depletes ozone \cite{ryan2022rocket} and adds a modest greenhouse warming \cite{solomon2010stratospheric}, but it clears in a few years, up to about a decade for the highest injections \cite{hunga2026residence}. That is far below the roughly \SIrange{50}{100}{\year} atmospheric lifetimes of the CFCs \cite{wmo2022ozone} and the centuries over which carbon dioxide persists \cite{ipcc2021ar6}. The water the PuffSats themselves release sits near \SI{200}{\kilo\meter}, well above the \SIrange{15}{35}{\kilo\meter} ozone layer. There it photodissociates into hydrogen and hydroxyl, and the light hydrogen escapes to space, so most of it never reaches the ozone below, though some may. The short residence time and the avoidable \ce{NO_x} point to one policy. Monitor the stratospheric water and \ce{NO_x} burden as suborbital traffic grows, and gate expansion to the measured impact rather than capping flights in advance.

\section{Conclusion And Future Directions}
This paper introduced PuffSat pulsed propulsion. We've argued that a PuffSat's navigation, supported by a preliminary closed-loop simulation \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026} and by advances in formation flying, could make hypersonic intercity transport practical. PuffSats also offer cheap orbital launch, a path to reversing global warming, abundant affordable power, and solar-system settlement with fusion propulsion.

Next steps: cross-check the preliminary control and impact simulations against independent tools, extend the control simulation to the rocket side of the interception, and prototype the control software as flight code. We also plan to solicit feedback on current challenges impeding these concepts and assess whether they're economically and technically surmountable.

If the basic physics of PuffSat pulsed propulsion work, we'd likely proceed with different technologies in different time frames. An aspirational ``Elon time'' \cite{isaacson2023musk,benzinga2023elontime} roadmap of when each technology could be developed is proposed in \autoref{tab:schedule_future_work}.


\begin{table}[htpb]
    \centering
    \begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{|c|L|}\hline
        \textbf{Aspirational Time Frame} & \textbf{Action} \\\hline
        Now & Prototype satellite launch and hypersonic travel using PuffSats launched by large reusable rockets\\\hline
        Now & Prototype satellite launch using gravity assists with Jupiter or the inner planets as described in \autoref{sec:jupiter_only_growth} \\\hline
        Near Term & Prototype launches using lunar volatiles or mined lunar oxygen instead of large reusable rockets \\\hline
        Mid Term & Prototype using close solar approaches or material from Phoebe/Saturn for PuffSat pulsed propulsion \\\hline
        Mid to Long Term & Prototype constructing a Straw Way To Heaven and energy infrastructure based on it \\\hline
        After the Singularity (Far Distant Future) & Prototype fusion rockets and interplanetary highways \\\hline
    \end{tabularx}
    \caption{Proposed schedule for future work}
    \label{tab:schedule_future_work}
\end{table}

Marc Andreessen once said ``software is eating the world'' \cite{andreessen_software}. If control software enables PuffSat pulsed propulsion, it will devour the solar system as well.

\appendix 

\section{PuffSat Cold-Gas Propellant: A Pessimistic Upper-Bound Estimate}\label{sec:estimate_cold_gas}

We scale GOCE to a geometrically similar \(\SI{25}{\kg}\) PuffSat composed of low-density ANFO (see \autoref{sec:explosive_puffsat}). This gives a rough, intentionally pessimistic upper bound for cold-gas propellant needs. We model GOCE as a cylinder and assume to first order that drag scales with surface area. Our conservative multiplicative assumptions follow.

\subsection*{Geometry And Geometric Scaling}
Approximate GOCE's slender drag-minimizing body \cite{goce_dragfree} as a cylinder with diameter \(D_0=\SI{1.0}{\m}\) and length \(L_0=\SI{4.0}{\meter}\). Then the radius \(r_0=\tfrac{D_0}{2}=\SI{0.5}{\meter}\), and
\[
V_0 = \pi r_0^2 L_0 = \pi\cdot(0.5)^2\cdot 4 = \pi\ \mathrm{m^3},
\qquad
S_0 = 2\pi r_0 L_0 + 2\pi r_0^2 = \tfrac{9}{2}\pi\ \mathrm{m^2}.
\]

Let the PuffSat mass be \(m=\SI{25}{\kg}\) and assume an ANFO density
\(\rho=\SI{840}{\kg\per\m^3}\). The PuffSat volume is
\[
V=\frac{m}{\rho}.
\]

If the PuffSat is geometrically similar to GOCE, the linear scale factor \(s\) satisfies
\[
V = s^3 V_0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
s = \left(\frac{V}{V_0}\right)^{1/3}.
\]
Surface area scales as \(S = s^2 S_0\), so the area ratio is
\[
\frac{S}{S_0} = s^2 = \left(\frac{m/\rho}{V_0}\right)^{2/3}.
\]

\paragraph{Numeric evaluation (ANFO, \(\rho=\SI{840}{\kg\per\m^3}\)).}
\[
\begin{aligned}
V &= \frac{25}{840}\ \mathrm{m^3} \approx 0.0297619\ \mathrm{m^3},\\[4pt]
s &= \left(\frac{0.0297619}{\pi}\right)^{1/3} \approx 0.2116,\\[4pt]
\frac{S}{S_0} &= s^2 \approx 0.0447721 \quad(\text{PuffSat area } \approx 4.48\%\ \text{of GOCE}),\\[4pt]
\text{Equivalently: } & S_0 \approx 22.335 \times S.
\end{aligned}
\]

\noindent\textbf{Remark:} geometric scaling predicts \(\sim22\times\) reduction in area. To remain conservative we \emph{assume} only a \(\times 10\) reduction in drag. Explicitly: although area scales \(\approx22\times\) smaller, we take drag to be only \(10\times\) smaller.

\subsection*{Baseline Thrust Assumption}
GOCE used up to \(\SI{20}{\milli\newton}\) for drag-free control (solar minimum) \cite{goce_dragfree}. We allow up to \(\times 10\) larger thrust during solar maximum. This gives \(\SI{200}{\milli\newton}\) as a worst-case GOCE-equivalent value. This solar maximum penalty is probably conservative, since we can time PuffSats to enter low atmosphere at night. Applying the pessimistic \(\times 10\) weaker drag for the PuffSat (above) yields a continuous required thrust of
\[
F_1 = \frac{\SI{200}{\milli\newton}}{10} = \SI{20}{\milli\newton}
\]
at the representative altitude (\(\sim\)\SI{250}{\kilo\metre}) used in this estimate. 

We further impose a short-duration surge case: an instantaneous increase in drag by a factor of \(20\) for \(t_2=\SI{30}{\s}\) between \(\SI{250}{\kilo\metre}\) and \(\SI{200}{\kilo\metre}\). This deliberately conservative bound requires
\[
F_2 = 20\times F_1 = \SI{400}{\milli\newton}
\]
for \(t_2=\SI{30}{\s}\).

\subsection*{Cold-Gas Mass Flow}
For a thruster with specific impulse \(I_{\mathrm{sp}}\) (seconds) and \(g_0=\SI{9.80665}{\m\per\s^2}\),
\[
\dot m = \frac{F}{I_{\mathrm{sp}} g_0}.
\]
We use a pessimistic \(I_{\mathrm{sp}}=\SI{25}{\s}\), about half a typical CubeSat cold-gas value \cite{tummala2017cubesat} to account for miniaturization losses. This gives
\[
\dot m_{\text{per }1\ \mathrm{mN}} = \frac{1\times 10^{-3}}{25\cdot 9.80665}
\approx 4.079\times 10^{-6}\ \mathrm{kg/s} \approx 4.079\ \mathrm{mg/s}.
\]

\subsection*{Fuel Required For The Two Intervals}
\paragraph{Continuous interval.}
Required thrust \(F_1=\SI{20}{\milli\newton}\) sustained for \(t_1=\SI{300}{\s}\):
\[
m_1 = \frac{F_1 t_1}{I_{\mathrm{sp}} g_0}
= \frac{(20\times 10^{-3})(300)}{25\cdot 9.80665}
\approx \SI{24.473}{\g}.
\]

\paragraph{Short surge interval.}
Surge thrust \(F_2=\SI{400}{\milli\newton}\) sustained for \(t_2=\SI{30}{\s}\):
\[
m_2 = \frac{F_2 t_2}{I_{\mathrm{sp}} g_0}
= \frac{(400\times 10^{-3})(30)}{25\cdot 9.80665}
\approx \SI{48.946}{\g}.
\]

\paragraph{Baseline propellant:}
\[
m_{\mathrm{base}} = m_1 + m_2 \approx \SI{73.4196}{\g}.
\]

\subsection*{Margins And Final Rounding}
Apply the following conservative margins. GOCE operated at a representative altitude of $\approx \SI{250}{\kilo\meter}$ and successfully descended to as low as $\SI{229}{\kilo\meter}$ \cite{goce_229km}. Atmospheric density rises sharply at lower altitudes. We model an instantaneous drag jump as PuffSat descends from $\SI{250}{\kilo\meter}$ to our \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} target. GOCE's designers treated $\SI{250}{\kilo\meter}$ as nominal because it offered more reliable operational conditions.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Add \(\SI{20}{\g}\) for attitude/torque control: \(m_{\mathrm{a}} = m_{\mathrm{base}} + \SI{20}{\g} \approx \SI{93.4196}{\g}.\)
  \item Double for pulsing inefficiencies and other control limitations: \(m_{\mathrm{b}} = 2 m_{\mathrm{a}} \approx \SI{186.8392}{\g}.\)
  \item Apply an additional doubling factor to account for drag at PuffSat's higher velocity, which scales as \(v^2\) (since $\frac{(\text{PuffSat's speed})^2}{(\text{GOCE's speed})^2} = \frac{\left(\SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}\right)^2}{\left(\SI{8}{\kilo\meter\per\second}\right)^2}
 \approx 2$): \(m_{\mathrm{c}} = 2 m_{\mathrm{b}} \approx \SI{373.6784}{\g}.\)

\end{enumerate}

Round up for presentation/engineering margin:
\[
\boxed{m_{\mathrm{req}} \approx \SI{374}{\g}\ \text{(computed value); rounded to }\SI{400}{\g}\ \text{for conservative margin}.}
\]

\subsection*{Notes And Explicit Assumptions}
\begin{itemize}
  \item We explicitly made two large conservative choices: (1) although geometric area scaling predicts \(\sim22\times\) lower drag, we assume only a \(10\times\) reduction (pessimistic), and (2) we include an extreme short-duration surge of \(20\times\) the baseline drag for \(\SI{30}{\s}\).
  \item The specific impulse \(I_{\mathrm{sp}}=\SI{25}{\s}\) is pessimistic. A higher \(I_{\mathrm{sp}}\) reduces propellant need proportionally.
  \item The final \(\SI{400}{\g}\) figure is a rounded engineering margin. The computed baseline with margins is \(\approx\SI{374}{\g}\).
  \item What this appendix bounds is drag-cancellation propellant, not the tank load. The closed-loop simulation finds the mission dominated by aiming rather than drag, with the anti-drag leg costing well under a gram \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}, so the bound above overshoots that leg by more than two orders of magnitude. The tank is nonetheless sized at \(\SI{500}{\g}\) in \autoref{sec:cold_gas_fluid_choice}. The extra is not drag margin. The propellant doubles as the thermal reservoir that holds its own vapor pressure up through the terminal burn, and that job sets the load.
  \item Surface area calculations did not include a propellant tank. The baseline liquefied-propane tank of \autoref{sec:cold_gas_fluid_choice} holds \SI{0.92}{\liter}, about 3\% of the PuffSat's \SI{30}{\liter} volume, so it changes the drag area negligibly. A high-pressure nitrogen tank would not be so kind. It needs \SI{1.32}{\liter} of internal volume plus a thick composite wall, a sizable fraction of the PuffSat's size, and drag would rise accordingly.
  \item The \(I_{\mathrm{sp}}=\SI{25}{\second}\) assumption stays conservative for the baselined propellant. Flown CubeSat cold-gas systems deliver about \SI{43}{\second} on butane \cite{tummala2017cubesat}, and propane's lower molar mass puts it slightly above that, so the propellant figure here carries roughly a factor of two in hand.
  \item This is a conservative back-of-the-napkin upper bound. Second-order effects are not modeled.
  \item A preliminary closed-loop control simulation confirms this bound from the other direction. Where this appendix estimates propellant from GOCE drag scaling alone, the simulation flies the full interception: corrector burns, terminal guidance, finite-thrust execution, and a dispersed environment. It still holds the total guidance propellant under 2\% of the \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} PuffSat mass, even in the worst sampled case \cite{Katz_puffsat_control_sim_2026}.
  \item \textbf{Acknowledgement:} ChatGPT extensively helped with this derivation and much of the text is copied from it verbatim \cite{chatgpt}.
\end{itemize}
% --- end ---


\section{Peroxide/Propane Bipropellant Burst Mode}\label{sec:peroxide_propane_biprop}

The burst mode of \autoref{sec:fine_control_thrusters} can burn the oxygen from the peroxide's own decomposition against a small propane charge, instead of qualifying a separate green monopropellant. This appendix sizes the mixture, the flame temperature, and the specific impulse. Thermochemical data are from the NIST Chemistry WebBook \cite{nist_webbook_propane,nist_webbook_oxygen,nist_webbook_hydrogen}. Standard enthalpies of formation used below (\si{\kilo\joule\per\mole}): \ce{H2O2}(l) \(-187.8\), \ce{H2O}(l) \(-285.8\), \ce{H2O}(g) \(-241.8\), \ce{CO2}(g) \(-393.5\), \ce{C3H8}(g) \(-104.7\).

\subsection*{Basis And Decomposition}
Take \SI{1}{\kilo\gram} of \SI{60}{\percent} solution by mass: \SI{600}{\g} (\num{17.64} mol) of \ce{H2O2} and \SI{400}{\g} (\num{22.20} mol) of water. The peroxide decomposes as
\[
\ce{H2O2(l) -> H2O(l) + 1/2 O2(g)}, \qquad \Delta H = \SI{-98.0}{\kilo\joule\per\mole}.
\]
This yields \num{8.82} mol (\SI{282}{\g}) of \ce{O2}, adds \num{17.64} mol of water for \num{39.84} mol (\SI{718}{\g}) total, and releases \(17.64\times 98.0 = \SI{1729}{\kilo\joule}\). Fully vaporizing that water would cost \(39.84\times\SI{44.0}{\kilo\joule\per\mole}=\SI{1753}{\kilo\joule}\), slightly more than is released. The \SI{60}{\percent} decomposition therefore stays two-phase near \SIrange{373}{410}{\kelvin}, which is why the catalyst needs a preheat to light propane. A dry \SI{85}{\percent} feed clears that vaporization budget and emerges near \SI{890}{\kelvin}, hot enough to autoignite propane unaided.

\subsection*{Stoichiometry}
Burning propane with the liberated oxygen,
\[
\ce{C3H8 + 5 O2 -> 3 CO2 + 4 H2O},
\]
consumes \(8.82/5 = \num{1.764}\) mol (\SI{77.8}{\g}) of propane. The oxidizer-to-fuel mass ratio is \(1000/77.8 \approx \num{12.9}\), so propane is \(77.8/1077.8 = \SI{7.2}{\percent}\) of the propellant. Combustion to gaseous water releases \(1.764\times\SI{2043}{\kilo\joule\per\mole} = \SI{3604}{\kilo\joule}\). The products are \num{5.29} mol \ce{CO2} (\SI{233}{\g}) and \(39.84+7.06 = \num{46.90}\) mol \ce{H2O} (\SI{845}{\g}), for \num{52.19} mol total and a mean molar mass
\[
\overline{M} = \frac{\SI{1077.8}{\g}}{\num{52.19}\ \mathrm{mol}} \approx \SI{20.6}{\g\per\mole}.
\]

\subsection*{Adiabatic Flame Temperature}
Balancing reactant and product enthalpies at \SI{298}{\kelvin} with all product water as vapor,
\[
\Delta H_\mathrm{rxn} = \sum_\mathrm{prod}\! n\,\Delta H_f - \sum_\mathrm{react}\! n\,\Delta H_f \approx \SI{-3.58}{\mega\joule}
\]
per kilogram of solution. This heat raises the products from \SI{298}{\kelvin}. With mean molar heat capacities over the interval near \(c_{p,\ce{CO2}}\approx\SI{53}{\joule\per\mole\per\kelvin}\) and \(c_{p,\ce{H2O(g)}}\approx\SI{43}{\joule\per\mole\per\kelvin}\),
\[
T_f \approx \SI{298}{\kelvin} + \frac{\SI{3.58e6}{\joule}}{(5.29)(53) + (46.90)(43)\ \si{\joule\per\kelvin}} \approx \SI{1880}{\kelvin}.
\]
That is the low end of the \SIrange{1900}{2200}{\kelvin} range of the HAN and ADN monopropellants \cite{green_monoprop_review}, and well below the roughly \SI{2800}{\kelvin} of a dry stoichiometric propane and oxygen flame. The excess water in the \SI{60}{\percent} solution is what buys the difference.

\subsection*{Ideal Specific Impulse}
With \(T_c \approx \SI{1880}{\kelvin}\), \(\overline{M} = \SI{20.6}{\g\per\mole}\), and a product ratio of specific heats \(\gamma \approx 1.21\), the ideal exhaust velocity for a vacuum expansion with \(p_e/p_c \approx \num{e-3}\) is
\[
v_e = \sqrt{\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma-1}\,\frac{R_u T_c}{\overline{M}}\left[1-\left(\frac{p_e}{p_c}\right)^{\!\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}}\right]} \approx \SI{2450}{\meter\per\second},
\]
so \(I_\mathrm{sp} \approx \SI{250}{\second}\) ideal. A more conservative expansion and the two-phase and boundary-layer losses of a millimeter-scale throat pull the delivered value toward \SI{230}{\second}, still comparable to the flown green monopropellants and far above a cold-gas blowdown.

\subsection*{Catalyst Mass}
At the \SI{400}{\milli\newton} surge and \(I_\mathrm{sp} \approx \SI{230}{\second}\), the total mass flow is \(F/(I_\mathrm{sp} g_0) \approx \SI{177}{\milli\gram\per\second}\), of which the peroxide is \SI{164}{\milli\gram\per\second}. Peroxide catalyst beds are sized by mass flux, with design values of order \SIrange{10}{55}{\kilo\gram\per\square\meter\per\second} \cite{jung2021catalyst_bed_scaling}. The bed frontal area is then \(\SI{1.64e-4}{\kilo\gram\per\second}\) divided by that flux, or \SIrange{3}{14}{\square\milli\meter}, a core only a few millimeters across. A washcoat on a sub-cubic-centimeter bed at ordinary noble-metal loadings carries single-digit milligrams of platinum, so at the platinum spot price the metal is a fraction of a percent of a single PuffSat's cost.


\section{Derivation Of PuffSat Mass/Rocket Mass Continuous Approximation}\label{sec:PuffSat_ratio_approximation} We want to approximate the ratio of total PuffSat mass \(m_p\) to rocket mass \(m_r\). A sequence of PuffSats, all traveling at velocity \(v_p\), pushes the rocket from initial velocity \(v_{ri}\) to final velocity \(v_{rf}\). First, assume every collision is perfectly elastic in one dimension. We model the rocket as continuously bombarded by infinitesimal PuffSats, each with mass \(dm_p\), and solve for a closed-form expression. (Note: Grok \cite{grok} helped with some of the math for this derivation and parts of this derivation are copied from Grok results directly)

The initial velocities are
\begin{itemize}
\item Mass \( m_r \): Velocity \( v_r \).
\item PuffSat: Mass \( dm_p \), velocity \( v_p \).
\end{itemize}
After the collision, let:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Mass \( m_r \): Velocity \( v_r + dv_r \).
    \item PuffSat: Velocity \( v_p' \).
\end{itemize}
\subsection{By Conservation Of Momentum} \[
m_r v_r + dm_p v_p = m_r (v_r + dv_r) + dm_p v_p'
\]
\[
m_r v_r + dm_p v_p = m_r v_r + m_r dv_r + dm_p v_p'
\]
\[
dm_p v_p = m_r dv_r + dm_p v_p'
\]
\begin{equation}
m_r dv_r = dm_p (v_p - v_p') \label{eq:momentum}
\end{equation}

\subsection[Velocity Change Of Rocket Mass]{Velocity Change Of \(m_r\)}
For an elastic collision
\begin{equation}
    v_p'\ = \frac{2m_r}{m_r+m_p}v_r + \frac{m_p-m_r}{m_r+m_p}v_p \label{eq:vb_prime_full_momentum}
\end{equation}
Since \(m_r \gg m_p\) we plug \(m_p = 0\) into \autoref{eq:vb_prime_full_momentum} and find 
\begin{equation}
v_p' = 2v_r - v_p \label{eq:vb_prime_result}
\end{equation} 

Substituting \(v_p'\) from \autoref{eq:vb_prime_result} into \autoref{eq:momentum}:
\[
m_r dv_r = dm_p (v_p - (2 v_r - v_p))
\]
\[
m_r dv_r = dm_p (v_p - 2 v_r + v_p)
\]
\[
m_r dv_r = dm_p (2 v_p - 2 v_r)
\]
\[
m_r dv_r = 2 dm_p (v_p - v_r)
\]
\begin{equation}
dv_r = \frac{2 (v_p - v_r)}{m_r} dm_p \label{eq:vchange_mr}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Integrate Over Many Collisions}
The velocity of \( m_r \) changes from \( v_{ri} \) to \( v_{rf} \) as more PuffSats collide. Integrate \autoref{eq:vchange_mr}:
\[
\int_{v_{ri}}^{v_{rf}} dv_r = \int_0^{m_p} \frac{2 (v_p - v_r)}{m_r} dm_p
\]
The left-hand side is:
\[
\int_{v_{ri}}^{v_{rf}} dv_r = v_{rf} - v_{ri}
\]
For the right-hand side, treat \( v_r \) as a function of the accumulated PuffSat mass \( m_p \). From \autoref{eq:vchange_mr}:
\begin{equation}
\frac{dv_r}{v_p - v_r} = \frac{2 dm_p}{m_r}\label{eq:dvr_velocity_relation}
\end{equation}

Since \(v_p\) is constant, \(dv_p = 0\) and \(dv_r = -d(v_p-v_r)\), so we can rewrite \autoref{eq:dvr_velocity_relation} as:
\[
-\frac{d(v_p - v_r)}{v_p - v_r} = \frac{2 dm_p}{m_r}
\]
Integrate both sides. The left side becomes
\[
\int_{v_{ri}}^{v_{rf}} \frac{dv_r}{v_p - v_r}
= \left[ -\ln|v_p - v_r| \right]_{v_{ri}}^{v_{rf}} = \ln \left| \frac{v_p - v_{ri}}{v_p - v_{rf}} \right|,
\]
and the right side gives
\[
\int_0^{m_p} \frac{2\, dm_p}{m_r} = \frac{2 m_p}{m_r}.
\]
Equating the two:
\[
\ln \left| \frac{v_p - v_{ri}}{v_p - v_{rf}} \right| = \frac{2 m_p}{m_r}
\]
Solve for \( m_p \):
\[
\left| \frac{v_p - v_{ri}}{v_p - v_{rf}} \right| = e^{2 m_p / m_r}
\]
\begin{equation}
m_p = \frac{m_r}{2} \ln \left| \frac{v_p - v_{ri}}{v_p - v_{rf}} \right| 
\end{equation}
\subsection{Interpret The Absolute Value} 
The absolute value accounts for the direction of velocities. When \( v_p > v_{ri} \) and \( v_p > v_{rf} \), both \( v_p - v_{ri} \) and \( v_p - v_{rf} \) are positive, so
\[
m_p = \frac{m_r}{2} \ln \left( \frac{v_p - v_{ri}}{v_p - v_{rf}} \right)
\]
\subsection{Compute PuffSat To Rocket Mass Ratio, With A Fudge Factor For Real World Losses}

Solve for the PuffSat-to-rocket mass ratio. Add a fudge factor $f$ in $(0,1)$ for restitution losses and imperfect volatile spread off the pusher plate. Assume $f$ is constant across collisions. In practice it varies with the relative PuffSat-rocket velocity. PuffSat sizes will in practice grow as the rocket nears $v_p$, since more mass is needed for the same acceleration.
\begin{equation}
\frac{m_r}{m_p} = \frac{2f}{\ln\left(\frac{v_p-v_{ri}}{v_p-v_{rf}}\right)}\label{eq:PuffSat_ratio}
\end{equation}

\section{Derivation Of Optimal Cable Taper}\label{sec:breaking_length_derived}

\paragraph{AI Acknowledgment}
Most of this derivation is copied directly from a solution worked out with a ChatGPT prompt \cite{chatgpt}.

\paragraph{Assumptions and notation.} 
Let $x$ denote the vertical coordinate measured downward from the top support ($x=0$ at the top, $x=l$ at the bottom). 
The material has density $\rho$ (mass per unit volume), subject to gravitational acceleration $g$. 

The ultimate tensile strength (stress limit) of the material is $\sigma$. 
Define the \emph{breaking length}
\[
b \;\equiv\; \frac{\sigma}{\rho g},
\]
i.e.\ a uniform vertical cable of length $b$ will break under its own weight. 

Let $A(x)$ be the cross-sectional area at height $x$, and $T(x)$ the internal tension at that section. 
A payload $P$ may be attached at the bottom ($x=l$). If no external load is present, set $P=0$. 

\paragraph{Equilibrium condition.} 
Static force balance gives
\[
T(x) \;=\; P + \rho g \int_x^l A(s)\,ds,
\]
where the integral accounts for the weight of the cable below $x$. 

\paragraph{Optimality condition.} 
To minimize mass for given $P$ and $l$, the stress constraint is active everywhere:
\[
T(x) \;=\; \sigma A(x).
\]

Differentiating with respect to $x$,
\[
\sigma A'(x) \;=\; T'(x).
\]
But
\[
T'(x) \;=\; -\,\rho g\, A(x),
\]
so that
\[
A'(x) \;=\; -\frac{\rho g}{\sigma}\, A(x) \;=\; -\frac{1}{b}\,A(x).
\]

\paragraph{Solution.} 
This linear ODE has the exponential solution
\[
A(x) \;=\; C\, e^{-x/b},
\]
for some constant $C$ determined by the boundary condition (e.g.\ the required cross-sectional area at the top or bottom).

\section{Deriving Effective Exhaust Velocity For Idealized 100\% Efficient Prograde/Retrograde PuffSat Collision Rocket Thrust, And The Real-Nozzle Correction}\label{sec:dv_effective}
The first subsection works the idealized case, in which the nozzle wastes nothing. The second introduces the loss parameter a real nozzle carries, and shows how the optimal mix moves with it.

\subsection{The Idealized Limit}
Suppose a retrograde PuffSat with mass $m_{rp}$ and a prograde PuffSat with mass $m_{pp}$ collide in a pulsed rocket propulsion chamber, as depicted in \autoref{tab:pulsed_combustion_illustration}. We want the maximum exhaust velocity $v_e$ for the prograde rocket, assuming 100\% engine efficiency. All the combined gas is expelled behind us, in the retrograde direction. For simplicity we say 
\begin{equation}
m_{rp} + m_{pp} = 1\label{eq:mass_is_1}
\end{equation} 
Each PuffSat has velocity $v_p$ so the retrograde PuffSat has velocity $2v_p$ in the reference frame of the prograde PuffSat. In the prograde reference frame, kinetic energy is
\begin{equation}
E = \frac{m_{rp} (2v_p)^2}{2} = 2m_{rp}v_p^2\label{eq:ke_PuffSats}
\end{equation}

Let $v_g$ be gas exhaust velocity expelled from the rocket, and assume the expelled gas mass is infinitesimal compared to the entire prograde rocket and its payload. Applying mass from \autoref{eq:mass_is_1}
 and kinetic energy from \autoref{eq:ke_PuffSats}, we have 
 \begin{equation}
 2m_{rp}v_p^2= \frac{v_g^2}{2}
 \end{equation}
 and solving for $v_g$ we get 
 \begin{equation}
 v_g = \sqrt{4v_p^2m_{rp}} = 2v_p\sqrt{m_{rp}} \label{eq:vg_result}
 \end{equation}
 Total momentum change is 
 \[(m_{rp} + m_{pp})v_g - 2v_pm_{rp} = v_g-2v_pm_{rp} = 2v_p(\sqrt{m_{rp}} - m_{rp}) \]
Differentiating and setting equal to zero gives
 \begin{equation}
 m_{rp} = \frac{1}{4}, \label{eq:max_m_rp}
 v_g = v_p,
 v_e= \frac{v_p}{2}
 \end{equation}

\subsection{Jet Efficiency And The Real Nozzle}\label{sec:jet_efficiency}
Two velocities appear above and they are not the same quantity. The gas speed $v_g$ is what the collision energy alone allows. It is the speed reached if every gram leaves at one speed along one axis. The effective exhaust velocity $v_e$ is the useful impulse per unit of total PuffSat mass spent. It is smaller because the retrograde PuffSat debits $2v_pm_{rp}$ of incoming momentum. At the optimum of \autoref{eq:max_m_rp}, the two differ by a factor of two, $v_g = v_p$ against $v_e = v_p/2$.

The step from $E$ to $v_g$ is where the idealization enters. Placing all the mass at one speed along one axis extracts the most momentum that its mass and energy allow. For real exhaust, the mass-weighted mean axial speed is smaller. We name its ratio to the loss-free gas speed the jet efficiency,
\begin{equation}
\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} \equiv \frac{\langle v_x \rangle}{v_g}, \qquad 0 < \eta_{\mathrm{jet}} \le 1,
\label{eq:eta_jet_def}
\end{equation}
where $x$ runs along the thrust axis, positive in the retrograde exhaust direction. The average covers the whole expelled mass in the rocket's frame. Cauchy--Schwarz bounds $\langle v_x\rangle$ by the actual root-mean-square speed. Energy conservation bounds that speed by $v_g$, giving the stated upper limit.
