Equality requires every gram to leave at the loss-free speed in the same direction. The square $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}^2$ is the fraction of available collision energy represented by coherent axial momentum. The single number therefore includes plume divergence, exhaust-speed spread, radiative escape, frozen ionization or dissociation energy, and mass the field fails to grip. It differs from the restitution factor $f$ of \autoref{sec:mass_fraction}. That factor describes gas rebounding off a solid pusher plate and has no referent without a plate.

One of those five contributions is now computed rather than swept, and it is large enough to separate out. Pulling a kilogram of water apart into its atoms costs \SI{50.9}{\mega\joule}. \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} finds the plume freezes chemically before it hands that energy back, so the gas expands on what is left. Start from the ideal gross jet, which puts the whole collision energy $w^2/2$ on one axis of the $1+k$ kilograms of fireball, where $w$ is the closing speed and $k$ is the slug ratio of \autoref{sec:watering_it_down}. That gives a one-axis speed of $w/\sqrt{1+k}$. Paying the bond energy $E_a$ off the top leaves $\sqrt{w^2/(1+k) - 2E_a}$, and the ratio of the two is the chemical efficiency
\begin{equation}
\eta_{\mathrm{chem}} = \sqrt{1 - \frac{2E_a(1+k)}{w^2}}, \qquad E_a = \SI{50.9}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}.
\label{eq:eta_chem}
\end{equation}
The $(1+k)$ is the part worth reading twice. Only the projectile brings kinetic energy in, while every kilogram in the fireball has bonds to break, so a wetter slug pays the toll on more mass out of the same budget. At the \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} head-on pulse and $k = 8.52$, this gives $\eta_{\mathrm{chem}} = 0.910$. At the \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} cold end of the growth push it falls to $0.730$.

Under this efficiency accounting, water chemistry alone prevents reaching the $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.775$ performance target below \SI{49.3}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at $k = 8.52$. This follows from \autoref{eq:eta_chem} with all other losses set to zero. It is a target shortfall concentrated toward the slow end of the overtake burn. The \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} departure burn has a chemical factor above the target.

Write $\eta_{\mathrm{geom}}$ for the other four contributions taken together, so that $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = \eta_{\mathrm{chem}}\eta_{\mathrm{geom}}$. The two halves are not known to the same standard. The chemical factor is computed from the impact simulation's solved expansion \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. The companion simulation now estimates divergence and residual thermal motion during expansion. Their contribution to the full impulse factor is discussed below. Where a table below sweeps an efficiency axis, it sweeps $\eta_{\mathrm{geom}}$ and charges $\eta_{\mathrm{chem}}$ from \autoref{eq:eta_chem}.

The expansion and winding results cited below retain the \SI{12}{\tesla} reference cap and the companion simulation's \SI{3.0}{\meter} chamber radius and \SI{23.8}{\meter} field length. The paper's corrected bag geometry and adopted \SI{11}{\tesla} cap have not been rerun through that simulation.

The solved expansion is strongly collisional, with particles undergoing roughly two to ten million collisions during transit \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. The plume therefore expands as a fluid guided by the magnetic field. Pressure gradients accelerate the exhaust; conservation of individual particles' magnetic moments does not describe this flow.

White's flux-compression study addresses collective magnetic pressure confinement and resistive diffusion, which remain relevant to our pulsed MHD flow \cite[pp.~11--14, 123--124]{white2026flux_compression}. Added background-gas collisions damped his observed fluting instability \cite[pp.~103--104, 113]{white2026flux_compression}; this differs from collisions within our dense plume. His magnetic-well experiments used a low background pressure, described as collisionless, and achieved repeated plasma rebound \cite[pp.~127--130]{white2026flux_compression}. Even there, he reports magnetic diffusion times roughly ten times shorter than theory predicts \cite[p.~156]{white2026flux_compression}. This identifies field leakage as a relevant loss mechanism, but supplies no measured efficiency bound for our nozzle.

For this expansion, define $\eta_{\mathrm{exp}}$ as the mean axial speed divided by the root-mean-square speed in the plume's initial rest frame. The model uses a directed flow speed $u$ and isotropic thermal motion about it, giving
\begin{equation}
\eta_{\mathrm{exp}} = \frac{\langle\cos\theta\rangle}{\sqrt{1 + 3k_BT/(\bar{m}u^2)}},
\label{eq:expansion_efficiency}
\end{equation}
where $\theta$ is the streamline angle to the thrust axis and $\bar{m}$ is the mean mass of the ions and neutral particles. The angle average is weighted by mass over paths traced through the computed winding field. For the \SI{12}{\tesla} capped profile without an extension, $\eta_{\mathrm{exp}}$ is \numrange{0.58}{0.60} at a \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} closing speed. At \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} it is \numrange{0.80}{0.86}. These ranges span the frozen and equilibrium expansion branches \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}.

The factor from residual thermal motion alone is already \numrange{0.86}{0.93} at the capped winding's exit. The main modeled loss comes from divergence beyond the last coil. The traced field is nearly axial at the exit, with $\langle\cos\theta\rangle = 0.999$, then spreads toward its return path. Flow that remains attached loses axial momentum as it follows that spread.

Pulsed inductive thrusters provide related MHD prior art. A capacitor discharge drives a coil that induces a plasma current sheet and accelerates it electromagnetically \cite{polzin2007pulsed_inductive}. Coupling weakens rapidly as the sheet separates from the coil, so release is not simply a matter of switching the field off. Our superconducting winding remains energized throughout each plasma pulse (\autoref{sec:watering_it_down}); the exhaust must detach from that sustained applied field.

The expansion factor describes the thermal jet in the plume frame. The paper's $\eta_{\mathrm{geom}}$ also includes radiative escape and mass the field fails to guide, and enters an impulse balance in the ship's frame. Applying the expansion factor to the merged plume's bulk drift requires separate accounting for its direction and any magnetic reversal. The growth tables therefore retain their sweep of $\eta_{\mathrm{geom}}$.

The nozzle design sets a higher crossover speed when expansion losses are included. Multiplying $\eta_{\mathrm{exp}}$ by water's chemical factor, the companion model reaches 0.775 at \SI{56.3}{\kilo\meter\per\second} for a \SI{10.9}{\meter} extension on the equilibrium branch \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. The roughly \SI{56}{\kilo\meter\per\second} crossover thus belongs to that modeled nozzle. The \SI{49.3}{\kilo\meter\per\second} limit comes from chemistry alone. These comparisons use the performance target; \autoref{eq:ve_general} gives the separate condition for forward thrust.

The energy is not all in one form when the nozzle receives it. Once the two PuffSats merge, the fireball's center of mass moves retrograde at $2v_pm_{rp}$. This bulk motion holds a fraction $m_{rp}$ of $E$, while the remaining fraction $1-m_{rp}$ is heat. At the three-to-one mix that is a quarter bulk and three quarters heat. The bulk part already points out the back and needs no nozzle. Its momentum equals what the retrograde PuffSat brought in, so a symmetrically expanding fireball delivers no thrust. All thrust comes from turning the thermal three quarters into one-sided flux. That is the nozzle's job.

That split also sets a reference point for $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$, and it is the number to read the pulsed nuclear literature against. Suppose the nozzle were only a perfect one-sided mirror. It reverses every particle whose axial velocity points at the ship and loses every sideways component. Write $V$ for the speed of the fireball's center of mass and $u$ for the thermal speed about it. A particle's axial velocity in the ship's frame is then $V + u\cos\theta$. For an isotropic expansion $\cos\theta$ is spread evenly over $[-1,1]$, and averaging $|V + u\cos\theta|$ over that spread gives $V$ when $V \ge u$ and $(u^2+V^2)/2u$ when $V < u$. Write $f_d$ for the share of pulse energy carried as bulk drift, so that $V = \sqrt{f_d}\,v_g$ and $u = \sqrt{1-f_d}\,v_g$. Dividing through by $v_g$ leaves
\begin{equation}
\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}^{\mathrm{refl}} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{1-f_d}} \;\; \left(f_d \le \tfrac{1}{2}\right), \qquad \sqrt{f_d} \;\; \left(f_d \ge \tfrac{1}{2}\right).
\label{eq:reflection_baseline}
\end{equation}
The two halves agree at $f_d = \tfrac{1}{2}$. Set $f_d = 0$, which is a bomb going off, and the baseline is $0.5$, because a mirror cannot recover sideways motion. Drawing the heat from a Maxwell-Boltzmann distribution instead of putting every gram at one speed gives $0.46$.

The isotropy that derivation assumes is exact for a sphere and false for the shape this paper flies. Write $\alpha = \langle v_z^2\rangle/\langle v^2\rangle$ for the axial share of the thermal motion, so that isotropy means $\alpha = 1/3$ and the drift-free baseline is $\sqrt{2\alpha/\pi}$. Expanding the merged fireball into vacuum returns $\alpha = 0.088$ for the \SI{23.8}{\meter} column against $0.360$ for the \SI{5.4}{\meter} sphere this section started from \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. Same mass, same density, same energy, and only the geometry differs, so shape is the whole of the cause. A long column is nearly an infinite cylinder. Free expansion follows the steepest pressure gradient, which is radial everywhere except near the two ends, and the ends hold little of the mass. Fifty-eight percent of it leaves within \SI{12}{\degree} of the transverse plane, and the drift-free baseline falls from $0.46$ to $0.237$. Where that came from is worth stating plainly. \autoref{sec:needle_through_fog} pours the bag into a column because length is the cheap dimension for a rocket to carry. That is a launch-fairing decision, and it quietly retired the assumption this baseline rests on.

The drift is smaller than it first appears, and \autoref{eq:reflection_baseline} is the reason it is worth working rather than asserting. Only the projectile arrives with kinetic energy, while the slug supplies mass alone, so the merged center of mass moves at $w/(1+k)$ and the drift share is $f_d = 1/(1+k)$. Dilution therefore spends it. At the three-to-one mix of \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}, $f_d = 0.25$ and the baseline is $0.577$. At the $k = 8.5$ the Jupiter cycle flies it is $0.529$, and at the $k = 24$ of \autoref{sec:methalox_rebuttal} it is $0.510$. Against a drift-free $0.5$, the collision geometry is worth about three percent where this paper actually operates, and less as the mix gets wetter. It is not the thing that separates this architecture from a bomb.

What separates them is that a real nozzle turns flow instead of merely reflecting it, converting sideways momentum into axial momentum the way the bell of a chemical rocket does. That is why \autoref{eq:reflection_baseline} is a reference point and not a ceiling. Published solenoid results for drift-free plumes report collimation ratios of \numrange{0.65}{0.85} \cite{schilling2022nozzle,hyde1983laserfusion_rocket}, which is \SIrange{130}{170}{\percent} of the $0.5$ available to them by reflection. Our own target of $0.775$ is \SI{147}{\percent} of the $0.529$ available at $k = 8.5$. This is a performance target motivated by published solenoid results. At that reference slug ratio, the head-on burn produces forward thrust above a total jet efficiency of $1/\sqrt{1+k} = 0.324$. Net fleet growth imposes a separate requirement, evaluated by the mission calculation in \autoref{tab:mass_interest_growth}.

The published efficiencies describe their respective plasma states and geometries; they do not establish a ceiling or floor for this nozzle. Our modeled plume undergoes millions of collisions during transit, so pressure-driven fluid expansion replaces the single-particle mirror picture \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. Collisions couple the neutral gas to the charged component. This supports the continuum nozzle model, but does not guarantee escape or higher efficiency. Local contractions can choke the flow, and downstream field divergence reduces axial momentum. The expansion estimates above address those losses; the growth calculation retains its separate efficiency sweep.

Two published models provide comparisons under different efficiency definitions. Ahedo and Merino's two-dimensional model reports a plume efficiency between $0.63$ and $0.83$ across a sweep of divergence rate and ion magnetization \cite{ahedo2010magnetic}. Their plume efficiency is the axial share of the downstream jet's energy, so its square root gives a divergence-only upper bound of $0.79$ to $0.91$ on $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ for their modeled flows. Their model is collisionless, electron-magnetized, current-free and low $\beta$, meaning the plasma pressure is small against the magnetic pressure. Our dense, collisional fireball lies outside that collisionless model. Schilling's three-dimensional simulation of a pulsed nuclear nozzle returns $0.34$ \cite{schilling2022nozzle}, under a definition identical to \autoref{eq:eta_jet_def}. That is a pulsed nuclear pulse rather than a steady electric one, which is nearer our case than Ahedo is. It also sits below the pass-through floor of the three-to-one mix.

Four things separate that $0.34$ from our case, and the first is the baseline above. His plasma is a static target that explodes, so $f_d = 0$ and his reflection baseline is $0.5$. He reached $68\%$ of it. Every solenoid in his own survey clears its baseline, and so does our performance target. His nozzle is the only device in the comparison that falls short of what a plain mirror would have given it. That points at his geometry rather than at pulsed nozzles in general. The other three differences are that geometry. His nozzle is a cage of 32 struts carrying axial current, so its field runs azimuthally. It is near zero inside the cage and largest at the closed apex where the struts converge. He reports that some of his plasma sits outside the nozzle at peak expansion, because the field is stronger outside the struts than inside. Plasma trapped at the apex then enters a loop. The field drives current, the current heats the plasma, and the heating raises its conductivity so the field drives more. That is a magnetic bottle rather than a diverging nozzle. His run also stops at \SI{100}{\micro\second} with performance still climbing.

It is worth being precise about which part of that we escape, because the obvious answer is the wrong one. The tempting reading is that his coupling is lossy and ours is clean, and the numbers say otherwise. He runs a hot metal plasma at \SI{e5}{\siemens\per\meter}, which puts his magnetic Reynolds number between \num{300} early in the expansion and \num{3e4} late. \autoref{tab:seed_window} puts ours between \num{39} and \num{650}. Per interaction, his field grips more cleanly than ours does. What he loses is not energy per bounce but the chance to leave, and two features of his geometry take it away. The first is that his field has gaps. Ampere's law on a loop inside a cage of 32 separate conductors encloses no net current, so the field lives at the struts and the spaces between them are open. He flew no liner or plate behind them. The second is that his strongest field sits at the closed end, so plasma driven up that gradient has nowhere to go but back out through those same gaps. Neither is true here. A solenoid's winding is continuous, so the bore is walled by field rather than fenced by it. \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} then puts a pyrolytic graphite liner and an aluminum shell behind that field. Mass the field fails to hold lands on a surface built for it, at a price already booked. The collisional flow must also pass through local variations in the winding field without choking.

A flux tube, a bundle of field lines enclosing a fixed magnetic flux, narrows where the field strengthens. Supersonic flow slows in that contraction. A deep enough narrowing can cause choking, where the flow reaches sonic speed and the passage limits the mass flow. The companion simulation compares each sampled contraction with the area change allowed by the local Mach number, the flow speed divided by sound speed \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}.

For the flared winding capped at \SI{12}{\tesla}, 18 coils is the smallest count in the tested sweep that passes this criterion. The straight winding passes at 12 coils. The tightest margin lies near the chamber's flat-field region, where the flow has only just become supersonic. These counts describe the modeled windings and sampled flow paths.

The companion calculation traces field lines through a 48-coil reference winding, with starting points distributed uniformly through the column volume \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. For a straight \SI{3.50}{\meter} winding, \SI{17}{\percent} of sampled paths cross the winding boundary with the original field profile, and \SI{14}{\percent} with the \SI{12}{\tesla} cap. None cross it in the capped, flared case. This supports retaining the flare. These calculations use the original geometry and prescribed vacuum field; they do not reconstruct the mass distribution left by the snowplow or determine the actual mass striking the liner.

His own survey of solenoidal geometries reports better collimation than his cage achieves, with Hyde's two-dimensional magnetohydrodynamic model at $0.65$ and an analytic dipole at $0.85$ \cite{hyde1983laserfusion_rocket,schilling2022nozzle}. Those are ratios of mean axial velocity to mean speed, so they bound $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ from above for the flows they describe. They do not measure the full impulse efficiency. Together with Ahedo's result, these upper bounds span \numrange{0.65}{0.91} across the published configurations. Schilling's directly comparable pulsed result describes the cage geometry. One solenoid result does state $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ itself, under our own definition, and it is worked below.

That geometry has since been built and fired. Schilling and colleagues wound 40 axial struts into a cylinder \SI{0.12}{\meter} across and \SI{0.12}{\meter} long, with \SI{1}{\kilo\ampere} through each strut for a \SI{1}{\tesla} field. They put a \SI{0.65}{\joule} laser pulse inside it \cite{schilling2024subscale}. SPFMax, the code that returned the $0.34$, placed the impulse at \SIrange{6.8}{7.3}{\micro\newton\second} with the nozzle current off and on. The charge collectors read \SIrange{1.0}{2.2}{\micro\newton\second} across the same pair. The authors attribute the gap to differences between the modelled and the built setup, and to the difficulty of reading thrust off charge collectors, so this is not yet a verdict on the cage. What it settles is the direction of the code's error on the one occasion it has been checked against hardware. It came out high, which points the cage's own figure below the $0.34$ rather than above it. That deepens the reading this section already gave, which is that the number indicts his geometry rather than pulsed nozzles in general.

The solenoid number comes from a simulation. Inatomi and colleagues ran a solenoidal nozzle from \SI{3.97}{\joule} to \SI{62}{\mega\joule}, scaling the coil as $L \propto E_p^{1/3}$, and report a momentum efficiency of \numrange{0.6}{0.7} that does not vary with plasma energy, mass, or material \cite{inatomi2023energy}. Their efficiency is axial momentum over the momentum available at $t=0$, which is \autoref{eq:eta_jet_def}. It beats Schilling's cage and falls short of our performance target. Three conditions sit on it. No experiment above a few joules stands behind it. Its high-energy hydrogen runs went unstable once the ion Larmor radius fell below the grid spacing, so the fitted law describes the runs that survived. And it was computed at a magnetic field energy five times the plasma energy, chosen because that is where the efficiency peaks.

That ratio is where this literature agrees with itself, and the paper should say so rather than wait to be told. Nagamine put the thrust maximum at five and VISTA adopted it \cite{nagamine1999analysis,orth2003vista}. Hyde's point design ran there \cite{hyde1983laserfusion_rocket}. Saito measured ion extraction ceasing to depend on field strength above \num{4.3} \cite{saito2018extraction}. Itadani, working in plasma beta rather than in energies, found deflection best at about ten times the plasma pressure \cite{itadani2018thomson}. Two of those four are measurements, so this is not three codes copying one another. Our standoff sizing puts the ratio near one. Against the place they all point, the magnet is field-poor by something like a factor of four, and the \num{0.775} performance target sits above the \numrange{0.6}{0.7} the best case returns.

Two things stand between that and a verdict, and only one of them is strong. The strong one is the difference in plasma state. Inatomi's densest case is \SI{3.0e-3}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} of gold at \SI{71.8}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, and the bench experiments ablate carbon or polystyrene to tens of electron volts. \autoref{tab:bag_sizing} puts our coldest pulse at \SI{0.32}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}, leaving at \SI{10.8}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and \SI{1.3}{\electronvolt}. That is a hundred times denser than Inatomi's gold, seven times slower, and cooler than the bench plasmas by more than an order of magnitude. It is also \SI{5}{\percent} ionized against their full ionization. An energy ratio tuned on a fully ionized plasma does not by itself establish the optimum for this dense, partially ionized flow. The neutral fraction alone does not measure how much gas the field can guide.

The weak one is the state of the field. \autoref{sec:jupiter_only_growth} works through model-against-bench comparisons in this same programme that miss by three to seven in both directions, which is reason to distrust any absolute impulse it quotes. It is not much reason to distrust the ratio. A dimensionless optimum survives calibration errors that swallow a magnitude, and two of the four sources for this one are measurements rather than codes. The concession therefore stands on its own terms. If the optimum transfers, our field is light and the magnet gets heavier by the amounts quoted above. What the record does not contain is a measurement on a plume like this one.

Release from the field sets how much downstream divergence the plume experiences. Hoyt and colleagues identify magnetic drag as a nozzle loss when the exhaust follows diverging field lines and loses axial momentum \cite{hoyt1995magnetic_nozzle}. Cassibry and Wu give the necessary release condition that the flow exceed the Alfv\'en speed, $v_A = B/\sqrt{\mu_0\rho}$ \cite{cassibry2007subalfvenic}. This is the speed of a magnetic disturbance in the plasma. At marginal pressure balance, $B^2/(2\mu_0) = p$, the ideal gas law gives
\begin{equation}
v_A = \sqrt{\frac{2R_gT}{\bar{M}}},
\label{eq:alfven_from_standoff}
\end{equation}
where $\bar{M}$ is the mean molar mass, counting all particles that contribute to the pressure. This relation applies at the local state where magnetic and gas pressures match. The field sized to contain the collision exceeds that balance during the subsequent expansion. For the \SI{12}{\tesla} capped profile, the solved flow reaches only \numrange{0.44}{0.72} times the Alfv\'en speed at the winding exit \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. It remains guided beyond the last coil, where the field spreads. That downstream loss is included in \autoref{eq:expansion_efficiency} through the traced streamline angles at the estimated release location.

Free expansion reaches the model's magnetic release condition without downstream structure. The purpose of an extension is to reduce the divergence accumulated before that release. A physical bell would have to intercept and turn the plume. Close to the winding, the flow remains strongly guided by the field. The preliminary thermal estimate places a surviving graphite bell farther outward, beyond the traced field-guided plume \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. These estimates do not establish a useful handoff from field to wall.

A magnetic extension controls the field-guided expansion directly. For the prescribed \SI{15}{\degree} flare, a \SI{10.9}{\meter} extension brings the exit flow above the Alfv\'en speed at \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} on both modeled branches. It remains below that speed at \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The corresponding expansion efficiencies $\eta_{\mathrm{exp}}$ are \numrange{0.89}{0.96} and \numrange{0.86}{0.92} \cite{Katz_puffsat_impact_sim_2026}. These results favor the magnetic extension for recovering divergence loss; they do not establish that every physical bell is ineffective.

Carrying $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ through \autoref{eq:vg_result} and the momentum balance gives
\begin{equation}
v_e = \eta_{\mathrm{jet}} v_g - 2v_pm_{rp} = 2v_p\left(\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}\sqrt{m_{rp}} - m_{rp}\right),
\label{eq:ve_general}
\end{equation}
which returns \autoref{eq:max_m_rp} at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 1$. Differentiating gives the general optimum,
\begin{equation}
m_{rp}^{\mathrm{opt}} = \frac{\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}^2}{4}, \qquad
\frac{m_{pp}}{m_{rp}} = \frac{4}{\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}^2} - 1, \qquad
v_e^{\mathrm{opt}} = \frac{\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}^2\,v_p}{2}.
\label{eq:eta_optimum}
\end{equation}
The three-to-one split is the $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} \to 1$ endpoint of a family, not a fixed number. A nozzle running at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.8$ prefers about five parts prograde to one retrograde.

The retrograde share arrives from outside the vehicle, so $v_e$ cannot enter the conventional rocket equation as though all spent mass came from onboard. Let $\mu$ be the total collision mass spent during a burn and $M_f$ the vehicle's final mass. Only the prograde share $(1-m_{rp})\mu$ is drawn from the vehicle's initial mass. For constant $m_{rp}$ and $v_e$, integration gives
\begin{equation}
\frac{\mu}{M_f} = \frac{\exp\!\left[(1-m_{rp})\Delta v/v_e\right]-1}{1-m_{rp}}.
\label{eq:external_reaction_mass}
\end{equation}
This distinction is small for the ratios used here, but it belongs in any cost or mass calculation.

\autoref{eq:ve_general} also sets a floor. Forward thrust vanishes at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = \sqrt{m_{rp}}$ and reverses below it. At three-to-one the floor is $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.5$. This is the sharpest difference between a nozzle and a pusher plate. A plate at low $f$ still delivers some forward momentum on the overtake leg. A nozzle below its floor produces net backward impulse. Dilution buys margin against the floor, which falls as $\sqrt{m_{rp}}$. The twenty-four-to-one mix of \autoref{sec:methalox_rebuttal} has a floor of only $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.2$. That step therefore tolerates a poorer nozzle than the three-to-one step.

Because the floor sits close below the three-to-one optimum, the penalty curve is lopsided. Diluting past the optimum costs little and moves away from the floor, while running richer in retrograde mass walks toward it. Holding three-to-one costs about 1\% of the thrust achievable at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.9$, about 6\% at $0.8$, and about 18\% at $0.7$. A five-to-one split stays within 4\% of the achievable optimum across that whole range. We keep three-to-one because it answers the idealized question. The mission numbers elsewhere in this paper also assume a lossy nozzle at that mix. For a design rather than a derivation, erring toward more prograde propellant is the cheaper mistake.

Two places in this paper pin $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ without naming it. The lunar cycle of \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket} assumes $v_e = \SI{3}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$ against an ideal \SI{5.5}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at $v_p = \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$. By \autoref{eq:ve_general}, this makes $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 0.77$. The cost cascade of \autoref{sec:methalox_rebuttal} needs about $0.89$ at its pessimistic anchor and about $0.7$ at scale. Both sit above the $0.5$ floor imposed by its three-to-one periapsis step. None comes from a nozzle model. They are requirements a magnetic-nozzle design has to meet, like the radiative-escape budget of \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}.

\subsection{Arrival Angle, And Why Head-On Is The Pessimistic Case}\label{sec:arrival_angle}
Nothing above needs the incoming projectile to travel exactly backwards along the rocket's velocity. It needs a large velocity difference at the meeting point. A projectile falling almost straight at the Sun, on an orbit carrying nearly no angular momentum, supplies one. It reaches the rocket's periapsis moving radially inward, \SI{90}{\degree} off the direction the rocket wants to go rather than opposed to it, and an orbit that only cancels Earth's motion is cheaper to place than one that reverses it. That plunge is barred by \autoref{sec:node_depth_admissibility}. This appendix keeps it for two reasons. The general law below prices any arrival angle and does not depend on the plunge being flyable, and the plunge's own numbers are the record of what the rule costs.

Work the general case in the rocket's frame. Let the projectile arrive with relative speed $w$ along a direction making an angle $\psi$ with the head-on axis, so $\psi = 0$ recovers the collision assumed above. Collision energy is $\tfrac{1}{2}m_{rp}w^2$ at any $\psi$, so the loss-free gas speed of \autoref{eq:vg_result} generalizes to $v_g = w\sqrt{m_{rp}}$. The incoming momentum no longer lies on the thrust axis. Its transverse part $m_{rp}w\sin\psi$ would push the rocket sideways, so the nozzle cants by an angle $\phi$ and sends that much transverse momentum out with the exhaust. Only the axial part $m_{rp}w\cos\psi$ is charged against thrust. The momentum balance then gives
\begin{equation}
v_e = w\left(\sqrt{\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}^2\,m_{rp} - m_{rp}^2\sin^2\psi}\;-\;m_{rp}\cos\psi\right),
\qquad
\sin\phi = \frac{\sqrt{m_{rp}}\,\sin\psi}{\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}},
\label{eq:ve_angle}
\end{equation}
which returns \autoref{eq:ve_general} at $\psi = 0$ and $w = 2v_p$. The cant is a rigid rotation of the whole plume rather than a new loss channel, so its price is the $\sin^2\psi$ term already written into \autoref{eq:ve_angle}. Whether a magnetic nozzle holds a \SI{22.5}{\degree} steer without spending extra $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}}$ is a design question this paper does not settle.

At a fixed closing speed the debit falls as $\cos\psi$ while the cant costs only $\sin^2\psi$ under a square root. Head-on is therefore the geometry that charges the most incoming momentum against a given collision energy. Optimizing \autoref{eq:ve_angle} over $m_{rp}$ at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 1$ gives $v_e = w/4$ at $\psi = 0$ against $v_e = 0.293\,w$ at $\psi = \SI{45}{\degree}$.

The plunge does not arrive at a fixed closing speed, and that is what decides the trade. Both bodies fall from far out to the same periapsis, so both carry speed $v_p$ there. A head-on meeting gives $w = 2v_p$. A radial arrival against a tangential rocket gives $w = \sqrt{2}\,v_p$, and in the rocket's frame that relative velocity points \SI{45}{\degree} off the head-on axis, not \SI{90}{\degree}. Substituting into \autoref{eq:ve_angle} at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 1$ and optimizing gives
\begin{equation}
m_{rp}^{\mathrm{opt}} = 1 - \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} = 0.293,
\qquad
v_e^{\mathrm{opt}} = (\sqrt{2}-1)\,v_p = 0.414\,v_p,
\qquad
\phi = \SI{22.5}{\degree}.
\label{eq:radial_optimum}
\end{equation}
The mix runs about 2.4 parts prograde to one incoming, slightly richer than the three-to-one of \autoref{eq:max_m_rp}, and the effective exhaust velocity is 83\% of the head-on figure. Half the collision energy buys 83\% of the impulse because half the debit is refunded.

That 83\% is the right number only for a node free to choose its own propellant mix, and no node in the growth cycles is. It comes from re-optimizing $m_{rp}$ at $\eta_{\mathrm{jet}} = 1$, whereas a node inherits its slug ratio $k$ from the departure that fed it. Hold $k$ fixed and the answer moves. The $\cos\psi$ debit is the impactor's own momentum arriving backwards, and a \SI{135}{\degree} arrival pays only \num{0.707} of it, so $\beta$, the useful impulse per kilogram of arriving projectile (\autoref{sec:split_push}), rises as the closing speed falls. That debit does not depend on $k$ while the useful term grows as $\sqrt{k}$. The plunge therefore keeps \num{0.960} of the head-on effective exhaust speed at $k = 1$, \num{0.864} at $k = 3$, \num{0.802} at $k = 8.5$ and \num{0.757} at $k = 30$ \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Both readings in circulation are wrong. It does not cost the full $\sqrt{2}$, and it does not cost nothing.

What it would have bought is a clean halving of the collision heat at every $k$, since thermalized energy goes as the square of the closing speed and that speed falls by a fixed factor. Read as depth, which \autoref{sec:depth_cost} shows is the expensive axis, a plunger at 4 solar radii collides as gently as a head-on node at \num{7.86} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. That is roughly a factor of two in depth bought on the collision term alone. Solar flux at the node still goes as $1/r^2$, and the arrival angle does not touch it. One framing point keeps the comparison honest. The node's own exhaust speed peaks near $k = 3$ at \SI{112.0}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and is falling by $k = 30$, where it is \SI{67.6}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The slug ratio at which the plunge looks worst is already past the node's own efficiency optimum, so a node chosen for itself rather than inherited from a departure would sit where the plunge costs \SIrange{10}{15}{\percent}. None of that saves it. \autoref{sec:node_depth_admissibility} rules the plunge out before the trade applies, and the trade itself says it would have lost at $k = 30$ anyway.

The other half of what the plunge offered was a cheaper stream, and that is what made the rule worth enforcing rather than stating. A radial fall from Earth means cancelling Earth's \SI{29.78}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of heliocentric motion. The Parker-depth retrograde ellipse of \autoref{tab:mass_scenarios} means cancelling that and adding \SI{8.82}{\kilo\meter\per\second} the other way. Scored the way that table scores its rows, with $f = 0.8$ against the \SI{69.27}{\kilo\meter\per\second} Jupiter-return stream, the plunge needs \SI{31.75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at the \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} interception altitude against \SI{40.17}{\kilo\meter\per\second} for the retrograde orbit, which raises delivered mass per PuffSat spent from $1.85$ to $2.61$. The concrete closing speeds behind the halved heat are worth recording too. At the Parker depth the plunge takes $w$ from \SI{394}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{279}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, and at 4 solar radii from \SI{618}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{437}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, which is where the sixteenfold drop in blackbody flux and the relief on \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle}'s radiative-escape budget came from. The plunge was the cheapest placement on offer and the coolest pulse on offer, and it was inadmissible the whole time.

This paper models head-on collisions throughout. Under \autoref{sec:node_depth_admissibility} that is no longer a conservative modelling choice, because in one plane head-on is the only arrival there is. An admissible stream reaches the node carrying the local near-parabolic speed to within a percent, and it carries it tangentially, because that is what a perihelion at the node depth requires. A coplanar arrival is therefore co-moving, which yields no useful closing speed, or head-on, with nothing between the two. Buying an angle in between means an inclined placement, an orbit taken out of the plane rather than one with its angular momentum removed, and the closing speed is then $2v_p\sin(\theta/2)$ for a velocity-vector separation $\theta$. \autoref{eq:ve_angle} still prices any of those arrivals. What an inclined placement costs does not appear anywhere in this paper, and it is now the only surviving route to the arrival angles the plunge used to reach cheaply.

Head-on stays the pessimistic modelling choice on the axes that carry the risk. It charges the whole incoming momentum against thrust, the largest debit any arrival angle can impose for a given collision energy, and it pays for the most expensive admissible stream orbit in \autoref{tab:mass_scenarios}. It is also the hardest pulse for the nozzle to survive.

\section{Flight-Path Angle And Incoming Downward Momentum At Interception}\label{sec:down_angle_geometry}
This appendix backs two figures from \autoref{sec:pusher_plate_down_kick}: the \SI{8.5}{\degree} flight-path angle at interception, and the growth of the incoming momentum's downward fraction from about 15\% to roughly one-half as the craft accelerates.

For the atmospheric-disposal comparison, a PuffSat rides a Keplerian ellipse with perigee radius $r_p = R_\oplus + \SI{50}{\kilo\meter}$, and a near-escape apogee, taken here as $r_a = R_\oplus + \SI{150000}{\kilo\meter}$ with $R_\oplus = \SI{6378}{\kilo\meter}$. Its semi-major axis, eccentricity, and semi-latus rectum are
\begin{equation}
a = \frac{r_p + r_a}{2}, \qquad e = \frac{r_a - r_p}{r_a + r_p}, \qquad p = a\,(1 - e^2).
\end{equation}
At the interception radius $r = R_\oplus + \SI{200}{\kilo\meter}$ the speed follows from the vis-viva equation, and the specific angular momentum $h = \sqrt{\mu p}$ is conserved, so the flight-path angle $\gamma$ below the local horizontal obeys the standard two-body relations \cite{vallado_astrodynamics}
\begin{equation}
v = \sqrt{\mu\!\left(\frac{2}{r} - \frac{1}{a}\right)}, \qquad \cos\gamma = \frac{h}{r\,v} = \frac{\sqrt{\mu p}}{r\,v},
\end{equation}
where $\mu = \SI{3.986e5}{\kilo\meter\cubed\per\second\squared}$ is the Earth's gravitational parameter. These give $v \approx \SI{10.8}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$ and $\gamma \approx \SI{8.5}{\degree}$, so $\sin\gamma \approx 0.15$. The low perigee sets the angle, not the apogee. Sweeping the apogee from \SI{30000}{\kilo\meter} to \SI{300000}{\kilo\meter} moves $\gamma$ only across \SIrange{7.9}{8.6}{\degree}.

The same interception velocity resolves into a forward component $v\cos\gamma \approx \SI{10.7}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$ and a downward component $v\sin\gamma \approx \SI{1.6}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$. The craft climbs prograde, and near orbital insertion its velocity is nearly horizontal with speed $v_c$, which builds from rest toward the circular value $v_{\mathrm{circ}} = \sqrt{\mu/r} \approx \SI{7.8}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$. The incident gas carries momentum along the relative velocity $\vec{u} = \vec{v} - \vec{v}_c$. Its forward part is the overtaking velocity $v\cos\gamma - v_c$, which shrinks as the craft speeds up, while its downward part holds fixed at $v\sin\gamma$. The downward fraction of the incoming relative momentum is then
\begin{equation}
\frac{v\sin\gamma}{\sqrt{(v\sin\gamma)^2 + (v\cos\gamma - v_c)^2}}.
\label{eq:down_fraction}
\end{equation}
At $v_c = 0$ the relative velocity is the PuffSat's own velocity, and the fraction equals $\sin\gamma \approx 15\%$. That is the floor. As $v_c$ approaches $v_{\mathrm{circ}}$ the overtaking velocity collapses to about \SI{2.9}{\kilo\meter\per\second} while the downward \SI{1.6}{\kilo\meter\per\second} holds, and \autoref{eq:down_fraction} rises to roughly one-half, near 49\%. The largest incoming angle occurs late in the boost. The delivered downward impulse depends on the plate interaction described in \autoref{sec:pusher_plate_down_kick}.

At fixed apogee, raising the disposal perigee to \SI{100}{\kilo\meter} lowers the incoming downward velocity to \SI{1.302}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, against \SI{1.595}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at \SI{50}{\kilo\meter}. The incoming angle is $\arctan[v\sin\gamma/(v\cos\gamma-v_c)]$. At $v_c = \SI{7.8}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$ it falls from \SI{29.1}{\degree} to \SI{24.1}{\degree}. Both retain a descending inflow, unlike the recommended periapsis encounter.

\subsection{Escape Injection From A Bound Staging Ellipse}\label{sec:staging_escape_geometry}
The escape route of \autoref{sec:near_term_disposal} begins on a bound ellipse with perigee radius $r_i = R_\oplus + \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} = \SI{6578}{\kilo\meter}$ and apogee radius $r_a = \SI{1000000}{\kilo\meter}$. The apogee figure is measured from Earth's centre, not its surface. With Earth's gravitational parameter $\mu = \SI{3.986e5}{\kilo\meter\cubed\per\second\squared}$, the initial apogee speed is
\begin{equation}
v_{a,0} = \sqrt{\frac{2\mu r_i}{r_a(r_a+r_i)}} \approx \SI{0.0722}{\kilo\meter\per\second}.
\end{equation}
A tangential acceleration past local escape speed makes this distant point the new perigee and cannot supply the low-altitude encounter. The injection instead supplies an inward radial velocity.

Prescribe a vacuum encounter speed $v_i = \sqrt{2\mu/r_i} + \delta$, with $\delta = \SI{0.005}{\kilo\meter\per\second}$. The squared hyperbolic excess speed is
\begin{equation}
C_3 = v_i^2 - \frac{2\mu}{r_i}
= 2\sqrt{\frac{2\mu}{r_i}}\,\delta + \delta^2
\approx \SI{0.110}{\kilo\meter\squared\per\second\squared},
\qquad v_\infty = \sqrt{C_3} \approx \SI{0.332}{\kilo\meter\per\second}.
\end{equation}
Conservation of energy and angular momentum gives the required inbound velocity at the staging radius,
\begin{equation}
v_{t,a} = \frac{r_i v_i}{r_a}, \qquad
v_{r,a} = -\sqrt{C_3 + \frac{2\mu}{r_a} - v_{t,a}^2}.
\end{equation}
These components are \SI{0.0724}{} and \SI{-0.950}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The single impulse from the original apogee is therefore
\begin{equation}
\Delta v_a = \sqrt{(v_{t,a}-v_{a,0})^2 + v_{r,a}^2}
\approx \SI{0.95}{\kilo\meter\per\second}.
\end{equation}
The initial ellipse takes about \SI{20.6}{\day} from perigee to apogee, and the injected hyperbola returns in about \SI{8.4}{\day}. At the shorter reference apogee altitude of \SI{150000}{\kilo\meter}, the same arrival margin requires \SI{2.23}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The longer ellipse reduces the injection cost by accepting a longer coast. These are Earth-only transfer figures; solar and lunar perturbations enter the actual mission targeting.

\paragraph{Deployment on the inbound leg.}
Keep the million-kilometre apogee, but let gravity accelerate the carrier inward before deployment. Write $a_0=(r_a+r_i)/2$ and $h_0=\sqrt{2\mu r_a r_i/(r_a+r_i)}$ for the staging ellipse's semimajor axis and specific angular momentum. At an inbound radius $r$, its velocity components are
\begin{equation}
v_{t,0}=\frac{h_0}{r}, \qquad
v_{r,0}=-\sqrt{\mu\left(\frac{2}{r}-\frac{1}{a_0}\right)-\frac{h_0^2}{r^2}}.
\end{equation}
The desired hyperbola has $v_t=r_i v_i/r$ and $v_r=-\sqrt{C_3+2\mu/r-v_t^2}$. The injection is the vector difference,
\begin{equation}
\Delta v(r)=\sqrt{(v_r-v_{r,0})^2+(v_t-v_{t,0})^2}.
\end{equation}
At \SI{150000}{\kilo\meter} altitude, $r=\SI{156378}{\kilo\meter}$, the radial speed changes from \SI{-2.023}{} to \SI{-2.235}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The tangential increment is only \SI{1.73}{\meter\per\second}. The total is \SI{211.5}{\meter\per\second}, about \SI{78}{\percent} below the apogee injection, with \SI{13.5}{\hour} of hyperbolic coast remaining. If \SI{150000}{\kilo\meter} denotes geocentric radius instead, the impulse is \SI{207}{\meter\per\second}. These constructions preserve the encounter's periapsis radius and speed; encounter longitude and epoch must be targeted for the selected injection point.

The smaller burn costs about \SI{1.1}{\percent} as argon at \SI{2000}{\second} specific impulse. At \SI{10}{\watt\per\kilogram} and \SI{50}{\percent} efficiency, however, accumulating \SI{211.5}{\meter\per\second} takes about \SI{4.8}{\day}. SEP must therefore begin earlier or carry more power; the instantaneous comparison is not a finite-thrust delivery solution. Waiting until periapsis would reduce the ideal burn to \SI{41}{\meter\per\second}, but would leave no coast for establishing and correcting the escaping arrival before delivery. The deployment choice trades propulsion against lead time.

Chemical thrust can supply the inbound-deployment impulse in a short burn. At the reference \SI{380}{\second} specific impulse, \SI{211.5}{\meter\per\second} consumes about \SI{5.5}{\percent} of initial maneuvering mass, compared with \SI{22.5}{\percent} for the apogee injection. At an average acceleration of \SI{1}{\meter\per\second\squared}, the burn lasts about \SI{212}{\second}. The propellant fraction excludes the engine and tank mass. If deployment precedes injection, each PuffSat needs delivery propulsion beyond its cold-gas guidance system; burning before release instead charges the maneuver to the loaded carrier.

A detached package that adds \SI{10}{\meter\per\second} prograde at periapsis raises $\delta$ to \SI{0.015}{\kilo\meter\per\second} before drag and release losses. Its $v_\infty$ becomes about \SI{0.575}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The positive specific orbital energy rises from about \SI{55}{} to \SI{165}{\kilo\joule\per\kilo\gram}. The added burn therefore triples the approximate atmospheric braking allowance, from \SI{5}{} to \SI{15}{\meter\per\second} expressed as an equivalent loss at periapsis. The reserve is carried for each departing package and is additional to its approach and attitude-control allocation.

\subsubsection{Drag On An Uncontrolled Remnant}\label{sec:remnant_drag}
Take a detached package of mass $m=\SI{0.25}{\kilogram}$ with projected area $A=\SI{0.01}{\square\meter}$, or \SI{100}{\square\centi\meter}. These are the remnant's properties, not those of the loaded PuffSat. Assume a constant effective area during the passage and drag coefficient $C_D=2.2$, giving $A/m=\SI{0.04}{\square\meter\per\kilogram}$. No drag cancellation or disposal burn is credited.

Integrate drag along the unperturbed Earth-only hyperbola with vacuum periapsis speed \SI{5}{\meter\per\second} above escape at \SI{200}{\kilo\meter}. Neglect atmospheric rotation and winds, so the atmospheric relative speed is approximated by the orbital speed $v$. For these small losses, the accumulated drag impulse per unit mass is
\begin{equation}
\Delta v_D \simeq \frac{C_D A}{2m}\int \rho[r(t)]\,v(t)^2\,dt.
\end{equation}
Use the piecewise exponential density profile in Vallado's published atmosphere table \cite{vallado_atmosexp}. Its density at \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} is \SI{2.789e-10}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}, with a local scale height of \SI{37.105}{\kilo\meter}. Integrating between the inbound and outbound \SI{1000}{\kilo\meter} crossings gives \autoref{tab:remnant_drag}. The other rows multiply the entire profile by a constant to reach the listed periapsis density. They are sensitivity cases, not occurrence probabilities or a solar-activity forecast.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Drag impulse per unit mass for an uncontrolled \SI{250}{\gram}, \SI{100}{\square\centi\meter} remnant passing a \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} periapsis near \SI{11}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The full-pass column assumes the remnant is already separate on approach. Release at periapsis exposes it only to the outbound half of the symmetric model.}
    \label{tab:remnant_drag}
    \begin{tabular}{|l|r|r|}
        \hline
        Density at \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} (\si{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}) & Full pass (\si{\meter\per\second}) & Outbound half (\si{\meter\per\second}) \\\hline
        \num{2.789e-10} (reference) & 0.24 & 0.12 \\\hline
        \num{1e-9} & 0.87 & 0.43 \\\hline
        \num{3e-9} & 2.61 & 1.30 \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

The trajectory spends about \SI{3.5}{\minute} below \SI{250}{\kilo\meter}, half of that after periapsis. A \SI{0.5}{\meter\per\second} outbound allowance covers the first two density cases, while the third exceeds it. All three remnant-only losses remain below the initial \SI{5}{\meter\per\second} escape margin. Drag scales with $C_D A/m$; a deployed panel or trailing film increases the loss in proportion to its added effective area. Inbound drag before separation and any backward release impulse must be subtracted separately before concluding that a detached package escapes.

\paragraph{Departure direction and thermal attitude.}
For eccentricity $e = 1+r_i C_3/\mu$, the angle between the periapsis velocity and the outgoing asymptotic velocity is $\alpha = \arcsin(1/e)$. It is about \SI{86.5}{\degree} for the initial margin and \SI{84.0}{\degree} after the ideal reserve burn. A departure along Earth's solar orbital motion therefore does not imply the same heading at the plate. The necklace's thermal attitude wants its spin axis perpendicular to the Sun-line, while delivery wants that axis along the closing velocity (\autoref{sec:spin_reorientation}). Both can agree if the encounter plane is perpendicular to the Sun-line and contains Earth's solar orbital velocity. The approach then runs roughly perpendicular to Earth's solar orbital plane, and the rocket must share the corresponding encounter track. This is a joint route-and-timing choice, not an automatic saving for every prograde escape.

\section{Thermal Design For A 4 Solar Radii Periapsis}\label{sec:four_radii_thermal}
This appendix backs the claim in \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket} that dropping the periapsis from the Parker-class baseline to the 4 solar radii of the original 2005 Solar Probe design \cite{mccomas2005solar} holds the \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} Earth-crossing speed with a boost about 30\% smaller, and it sizes the projectile shield that survives the deeper dive.

A dive from far away reaches periapsis speed $v_p = \sqrt{2\mu_\odot/r_p}$, where $\mu_\odot = \SI{1.327e20}{\meter\cubed\per\second\squared}$ is the Sun's gravitational parameter and $r_p$ the periapsis radius. At the Parker Solar Probe's 9.86 solar radii this gives \SI{197}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, matching the roughly \SI{200}{\kilo\meter\per\second} used in \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket}. At 4 solar radii it gives \SI{309}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. Head-on closing speeds double these figures, \SI{618}{\kilo\meter\per\second} against \SI{393}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so effective exhaust velocity, which scales with collision speed (\autoref{sec:dv_effective}), rises by 57\%. The stronger Oberth effect is spent on economy rather than speed. The worked example at Parker depth, a boost from \SI{200}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{250}{\kilo\meter\per\second} at periapsis, coasts out to cross \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} at \SI{160}{\kilo\meter\per\second} heliocentric. At 4 solar radii a boost of only \SI{36}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, about 30\% smaller, leaves about \SI{147}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of hyperbolic excess, the speed kept after climbing out of the Sun's pull, because a fixed escape surplus costs less impulse the faster the periapsis where it is bought \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Holding the crossing speed near \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} rather than pushing higher is deliberate. That kinetic energy at Earth is already enough to throw the next payload onto its own low-periapsis orbit, and the 57\% higher exhaust velocity means each \si{\kilo\meter\per\second} of the smaller boost also spends less PuffSat mass.

Sunlight at 4 solar radii carries \SI{3.9}{\mega\watt\per\square\meter}, 2890 times the solar constant and 6.1 times the \SI{0.65}{\mega\watt\per\square\meter} at the Parker perihelion \cite{nasa2018parker_heat}. Radiative equilibrium temperature scales with the fourth root of flux, so the Parker probe's \SI{1377}{\celsius} sunward face \cite{parker_thermal_protection} maps to about \SI{2590}{\kelvin} at the same optical properties. That exceeds the \SI{2327}{\kelvin} melting point of alumina \cite{crc_handbook}, a representative white ceramic of the class NASA uses on Parker's sunward face \cite{parker_thermal_protection}. A sun-facing flat skin like the one in \autoref{fig:parker_cross_section} is marginal at best here, and vacuum-ultraviolet exposure drives its absorptance toward the gray-body limit where it fails outright, so geometry must shed the flux.

The fix is the one the 2005 design chose, a shield shaped as a cone pointed at the Sun \cite{mccomas2005solar}. A flank inclined at half-angle $\theta$, the angle between the sunward axis and the surface, intercepts only $\sin\theta$ of the normal flux. We take $\theta = 15^{\circ}$, which cuts the flank loading to \SI{1.0}{\mega\watt\per\square\meter}. An alumina skin, taking a representative darkened solar absorptance of 0.3 and infrared emissivity of 0.85, then equilibrates near \SI{1590}{\kelvin}, below the \SI{1650}{\kelvin} the Parker face is rated for \cite{parker_thermal_protection}. The design does not depend on the coating staying white. Vacuum-ultraviolet exposure darkens shield surfaces in ground tests \cite{balat2012_vuv_darkening}, so we also size the limit case with absorptance equal to emissivity, a fully blackened flank. It reaches \SI{2060}{\kelvin}, still about \SI{270}{\kelvin} below the alumina melting point and inside the \SIrange{1800}{2400}{\kelvin} band over which shield-grade carbon--carbon was characterized for the 4 solar radii mission \cite{balat2006_cc_emissivity}. The coating can degrade gracefully but cannot fail abruptly. Two effects set the angle. The Sun is no point source here.  Its disk subtends a half-angle of $\arcsin(1/4) \approx 14.5^{\circ}$, so a flank tilted below that still sees most of the disk and slimming stops paying. A slimmer cone also narrows the conduction path that cools the tip, treated next.

Flight experience says the darkening and dust budgets close, at least at 9.86 solar radii. The Parker probe's own instruments measured a continuous rain of sub-micron impacts through the near-Sun dust environment \cite{szalay2020_near_sun_dust}, and the spacecraft reported nominal health and communications after its closest perihelion \cite{nasa2024_parker_survives}. A projectile spends hours near 4 solar radii on a single expendable pass, and the blackened limit above already assumes the reflectance is entirely lost.

The apex is the one place the cone offers no relief, and it is smaller than it looks. An ideal cone has no sun-normal surface at all.  Every flank element down to the apex sees the same $\sin\theta$ loading. Only a blunted apex of radius $a$ intercepts normal flux, and conduction clamps a small one to the flank temperature. The intercepted power grows as $a^2$ while the conduction resistance into the widening cone falls as $1/a$, giving
\begin{equation}
\Delta T \approx \frac{\alpha S a}{k \tan\theta},
\end{equation}
where $S$ is the normal flux, $\alpha$ the absorptance, and $k$ the conductivity. For a graphite apex with $a = \SI{50}{\micro\meter}$ and $k \approx \SI{50}{\watt\per\meter\per\kelvin}$ at temperature, the rise is about \SI{15}{\kelvin}. The clamp weakens linearly with bluntness. Near $a \sim \SI{1}{\milli\meter}$ the rise passes \SI{250}{\kelvin}, and by a few millimeters the apex decouples and tends toward the \SI{2890}{\kelvin} equilibrium of an isolated sun-normal gray surface. Dust can do the blunting mid-pass. Sub-micron grains arriving at \SI{300}{\kilo\meter\per\second} would crater tens of times their own size, and with the near-Sun dust density Parker measured \cite{szalay2020_near_sun_dust} the tip's \SI{e-7}{\square\meter} frontal area sees of order one strike per pass, an extrapolation beyond the roughly \SI{100}{\kilo\meter\per\second} impacts Parker actually recorded. One unlucky strike could seed a runaway hot spot. A tungsten apex insert, seated in fine-grained graphite because woven carbon--carbon cannot hold a \SI{100}{\micro\meter} point, retires the risk. Sharp, tungsten enjoys the same conduction clamp. Blunted, its \SI{3695}{\kelvin} melting point \cite{crc_handbook} absorbs the \SI{2890}{\kelvin} worst case with \SI{800}{\kelvin} of margin, whatever happens to its surface finish. Hafnium and tantalum carbides melt about \SI{500}{\kelvin} higher still \cite{uhtcmc_review}, but the tip is not melting-limited, so that headroom goes unused. Tungsten is preferred for the properties the tip does exercise. Its conductivity runs several times that of those ceramics, which tightens the same clamp, and as a ductile metal it survives the dust cratering that would spall a brittle ceramic point. Tungsten's high atomic number also serves the opacity requirement of \autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats}, standing in for part of the iron dopant.

Bare carbon flanks would survive the blackened-limit temperature outright, so the alumina needs a different justification, and it is navigational. A surface subliming into vacuum recoils with a pressure of about half its vapor pressure. Carbon at \SI{2060}{\kelvin} sits near \SI{2e-4}{\pascal}, a recoil about 1\% of the \SI{0.013}{\newton\per\square\meter} of solar radiation pressure at this depth. The problem is not the size but the predictability. Carbon's vapor pressure doubles roughly every \SI{35}{\kelvin} at these temperatures, so a few-percent error in modeled flank temperature moves the recoil by tens of percent, while the solar output driving radiation pressure is known to a small fraction of a percent. Millimeter-scale terminal targeting (\autoref{sec:solid_PuffSats}) has no room for an acceleration that uncertain. Alumina at \SI{1590}{\kelvin} and tungsten at \SI{2100}{\kelvin} both hold vapor pressures below \SI{e-8}{\pascal}, removing the term from the guidance model.

Behind the skin, Parker provides a construction precedent and flight thermal data, though not a directly transferable calibration. Its flown thermal protection system uses an \SI{11.43}{\centi\meter} carbon-foam core between carbon--carbon face sheets. At perihelia 17 and 18, corresponding to 354 solar constants, a JHU/APL thermal model placed the sunward surface near \SI{840}{\celsius}, while sensors on the barrier blanket facing the shield's backside read \SIrange{225}{230}{\celsius}, and the propellant tank stayed between \SIrange[range-phrase={ and }]{20}{25}{\celsius} \cite{ercol2024_parker_thermal}. These are system-level results that depend on the shield, barrier blankets, and spacecraft geometry, so they do not by themselves fix an effective foam conductivity for the proposed cone. What they do show is that a passive stack of this class holds a near-room-temperature interior behind an \SI{840}{\celsius} face, which is the outcome the design needs. The last step is geometric, as it is on Parker. A box in the shadowed interior balances radiation from the hot rear face against its own emission to cold space, settling near $T_{\mathrm{box}} \approx F^{1/4}\, T_{\mathrm{rear}}$, where the view factor $F$ is the fraction of the box's sky the hot surface fills. Parker's barrier-blanket backside sits near \SI{500}{\kelvin}, so even a modest standoff, or one low-emissivity barrier sheet in between, holds the box under \SI{85}{\celsius}. That sheet can be a single aluminized polyimide layer of the kind stacked five deep in the JWST sunshield \cite{jwst_sunshield}. Aluminum reflects about 97\% of the rear face's mid-infrared emission, so with its bare polyimide face toward the box the layer floats near room temperature. A coupled thermal model and high-flux vacuum test must still fix the cone's rear-face temperature, foam thickness, and electronics view factors under both nominal and blackened-surface cases. The requirement, keeping the guidance electronics below \SI{85}{\celsius} throughout the pass, sits inside what Parker's stack already demonstrates.

The shadow extends beyond the interior. Behind the base plane of a cone of base radius $R_b$, the shield casts an umbra, the region from which the shield hides the whole solar disk. It narrows at the $14.5^{\circ}$ solar half-angle and closes about $3.9\,R_b$ aft, so a point at depth $z$ behind the base stays fully shadowed while its radial offset is under $R_b - 0.26\,z$. The 2005 design sheltered its whole spacecraft bus in this cone of shade \cite{mccomas2005solar}. Here it hosts the gram-scale terminal-guidance hardware of \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture}, a camera and a pulsed beacon tucked just inside the rim of the base. The corona itself does not relight the shadow. Thomson scattering off coronal electrons, at densities near \SI{e5}{\per\cubic\centi\meter} at this height \cite{leblanc1998corona}, redirects of order $10^{-9}$ of the local flux onto shadowed surfaces, tens of milliwatts per square meter. A shadowed device therefore sees the room-temperature barrier sheet on one side and cold space on the other. Its temperature is set by its own dissipation, and milliwatt heaters hold whatever setpoint the detector prefers. This also retires the thermal-distortion concern of \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture} for the periapsis leg. The radiative load on the optics sits nine orders of magnitude below what a sunlit aperture would absorb, so the smooth heating that star-differencing exists to cancel never develops.

The umbra's edge is a cliff, so the margin is geometric rather than thermal. A gray surface seeing a fraction $f$ of the disk equilibrates near $2890\,f^{1/4}$~\si{\kelvin}, about \SI{910}{\kelvin} at $f = 1\%$. A pointing error $\delta$ sweeps the shadow boundary sideways by $z\delta$ at depth $z$, so a device a few centimeters inside the boundary, mounted a fraction of a meter behind the base, tolerates pointing errors of several degrees, far looser than what the flank geometry already needs. Sight lines cooperate at periapsis, where the velocity vector runs perpendicular to the sun-line. Every camera in the terminal-guidance chain views a beacon along the approach corridor, about $90^{\circ}$ from the Sun, so the disk never enters a field of view, and keeping sunlight off the optics takes a straylight baffle rather than a coronagraph. The same baffle hides the one hot thing a rim-mounted device could see, the thin glowing edge where the sunlit flank meets the base. The coronal background behind a beacon, viewed at tangent heights of 4 solar radii and beyond, is what the narrowband filter and pulse gating of \autoref{sec:sensor_architecture} already reject.

\section{Earth Re-Intercept and the Phasing Loop}\label{sec:earth_reintercept}
The cycle in \autoref{sec:no_isru_rocket} sends a projectile from Earth to a low solar periapsis, boosts it, and brings it back across \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} at \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} or more. Crossing \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} is not the same as reaching Earth. This appendix shows why the two differ by a wide margin, why the miss cannot be fixed at periapsis, and what the three practical fixes cost. The numbers are computed in the companion calculations repository \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Every boost below is a PuffSat collision, as elsewhere in this cycle, not carried propellant.

A boost that lifts the periapsis speed past the local escape speed leaves the projectile on an escaping hyperbola. Falling from a \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} aphelion, the projectile reaches the 4-solar-radii periapsis at about \SI{306}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, just under the \SI{309}{\kilo\meter\per\second} local escape speed. A \SI{36}{\kilo\meter\per\second} periapsis boost lifts it to about \SI{342}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, so the outbound leg escapes with about \SI{147}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to spare, near the \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} Earth-crossing scale (\autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal}). The wider-aphelion resonant return below arrives faster at periapsis and keeps the full \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second}. The projectile crosses \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} exactly once on the way out, and Earth has to be there when it does. There is no second pass.

The natural crossing lands far from Earth. A minimum-energy dive from \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} to 4 solar radii is half the period of an \SI{0.509}{\astronomicalunit} ellipse, about 66 days, and the boosted climb-out adds about ten more. In that \SI{0.21}{\year} Earth advances about \SI{76}{\degree}. The projectile sweeps much farther. It covers \SI{180}{\degree} of heliocentric longitude falling from \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} to periapsis, then about \SI{130}{\degree} more whipping back out, roughly \SI{310}{\degree} in all. It re-crosses about \SI{50}{\degree} behind the longitude it launched from, while Earth has moved \SI{76}{\degree} ahead, so the gap is about \SI{125}{\degree}. The miss comes from the projectile whipping around the Sun, and it barely depends on periapsis depth.

Neither periapsis knob helps. Turning the velocity vector by an angle $\theta$ near periapsis costs about $2v_p\sin(\theta/2)$, which at the \SI{309}{\kilo\meter\per\second} periapsis speed is \SI{5.4}{\kilo\meter\per\second} per degree. The miss is tens of degrees, so a periapsis re-aim runs to hundreds of \si{\kilo\meter\per\second} against the roughly \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of heliocentric $\Delta V$ that sets up the dive. Direction changes want the slowest point on the orbit, and periapsis is the fastest. Rotating the orbit's long axis fails too on a fast dive. Launching from \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} onto an orbit whose periapsis is 4 solar radii forces Earth to sit near that orbit's aphelion at departure. That pins the periapsis direction opposite Earth, which pins the outbound crossing about \SI{50}{\degree} behind the launch longitude. The long axis is not a free parameter when the launch point is the aphelion of a deep diver, so the fix has to act on timing, not on aim.

The round trip is fixed near \SI{0.21}{\year}, but Earth returns to the crossing longitude only after it sweeps around to that point, which first happens near \SI{0.86}{\year}. Closing the gap means stretching the trip to that resonance, or bending it with a flyby. Three methods trade impulse, time, and network complexity differently.

\paragraph{Two-impulse phasing loop.} Insert a shallow dip before the deep dive. A PuffSat boost at Earth nudges the projectile onto an orbit with periapsis near \SI{0.50}{\astronomicalunit}. It returns to the launch point after \SI{0.65}{\year}, moving tangentially at the same speed it left, where a second PuffSat boost drops it into the deep dive. Both boosts point retrograde and lie along the same line, so the sequence \SI{29.78}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, \SI{24.4}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, \SI{5.7}{\kilo\meter\per\second} sums to the same \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} as a direct dive. The delay is free in total impulse, which holds the per-cycle doubling factor at two. Lowering periapsis beats raising aphelion, which points the two boosts in opposing directions and wastes about \SI{7.9}{\kilo\meter\per\second} for a longer \SI{1.86}{\year} resonance. The price is a second boost node. The loop returns to \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} far from Earth, so the network must field a retrograde PuffSat stream at that point and time. A mature network provides it, and an early one may not, which motivates the next two options.

\paragraph{Single-impulse resonant dive.} Fold the phasing into the single boost the projectile receives at Earth. Aim it outbound onto an orbit with aphelion near \SI{1.9}{\astronomicalunit} and periapsis at 4 solar radii. It coasts out, falls back through \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, dives, and re-crosses after about \SI{0.89}{\year} with Earth waiting. The aphelion is the free knob that closes the geometry. This needs only the Earth boost node, but the boost grows from \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{37.53}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, which decomposes into \SI{24.07}{\kilo\meter\per\second} retrograde, the same component a direct dive needs, plus \SI{28.80}{\kilo\meter\per\second} pointed radially outward to buy the phasing coast \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. The closing aphelion is \SI{1.9259}{\astronomicalunit}, so this option is already a raise-then-drop rather than a straight fall inward. A heavier boost spends more PuffSats per projectile, so the returning fleet launches a smaller next payload and the doubling factor falls below two. \autoref{sec:split_dive} takes that same boost and splits it across two nodes, which buys back most of the impulse at a cost in cycle time. It also notes what this figure does not cover. It places the payload at the dive perihelion and not the opposing stream that has to meet it there.

\paragraph{Gravity-assist resonant return.} Bend the return with a Venus or Earth flyby rather than a boost. A well-placed flyby rotates the trajectory and trims its period for no impulse, the tool the initial low-periapsis orbits already use (\autoref{sec:jupiter_gravity_initial}). The cost moves into scheduling. A flyby helps only when the assisting planet is in place, so the cycle stretches to perhaps one or two years and the launch windows thin. Unlike the two-impulse and single-impulse fixes above, this gravity-assist option is not modeled in the companion repository, so its cycle time is an estimate rather than a computed trajectory. This is the impulse-free option when the timing cooperates.

\paragraph{Split dive.} Fly the single-impulse dive's outbound ellipse, but do not buy the whole dive at Earth. Spend part of the boost there, coast to aphelion, and spend the rest out where the vehicle is slow and reshaping the orbit is cheap (\autoref{sec:split_dive}). Injecting a \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} payload onto a 4-solar-radii perihelion is a 54:1 radius ratio, far past the roughly 15.6:1 where a bi-elliptic transfer always beats a direct one, so the impulse falls from \SI{24.09}{\kilo\meter\per\second} to \SI{16.94}{\kilo\meter\per\second} through a \SI{3}{\astronomicalunit} aphelion \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Stopping partway is the interesting case. Deferring \SI{10.535}{\kilo\meter\per\second} of the Earth boost to a node at \SI{1.96}{\astronomicalunit} gives a \SI{1.0543}{\year} cycle that doubles in \SI{0.2969}{\year} against the single-impulse dive's \SI{0.3048}{\year}, on \num{1.536} kilograms of launched slug per delivered kilogram against \num{2.365}. Better on both axes the growth ledger scores. The cost sits outside that ledger, and it is the same cost the two-impulse loop carries: a second boost node away from Earth, at \SI{1.96}{\astronomicalunit} rather than at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, which the returning beam does not reach at this member of the family. What it takes to deliver impactors there has not been priced, so this is not yet a claim that the split dominates.

The cost lands on the growth rate. The cycle that launches the next, larger payload cannot close faster than one Earth return, so the doubling interval is the re-intercepting cycle, not the bare dive time, and its floor is near \SI{0.86}{\year}. Twenty doublings reach a millionfold, so the payload grows by a factor of a million in about 17 years rather than the under-a-decade a \SI{0.5}{\year} cycle would imply. Only the two-impulse loop holds the doubling factor at two and reaches that 17-year figure. The single-impulse dive stretches it through a smaller factor, and the gravity assist through a longer cycle. Every case is a floor, since it assumes each cycle catches its resonance with no turnaround at Earth.

\paragraph{Loops without the dive.} Every option above phases the same \SI{150}{\kilo\meter\per\second} dive. Two gentler cycles skip it entirely: the Jupiter return of \autoref{sec:jupiter_only_growth}, whose PuffSats cross Earth at about \SI{61}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and push about 8 times their weight per collision, and the apoapsis-raise loop of \autoref{sec:apoapsis_raise_loop}, which closes at about \SI{24}{\kilo\meter\per\second} on carried propellant and multiplies the fleet by 1.55 each cycle.

\section{The Three-Synodic Closure And The Split Push}\label{sec:jovian_dive_closure}
This appendix backs the two structural claims of \autoref{sec:jovian_dive_cycle}. The first is that letting Jupiter place the solar dive closes into a repeating cycle at three Earth--Jupiter synodic periods and at no other multiple. The second is that the Earth-side departure is better flown as two pulses than one. Every number is computed in the companion calculations repository \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}, on a circular coplanar model of both planets.

\subsection*{The Two Conditions And The Two Knobs}
A repeating cycle has to satisfy two things at once. It must take exactly $N$ synodic periods, so the next payload sees the geometry this one saw. And its \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} crossing on the way back out has to land where Earth actually is, which is the same re-intercept requirement \autoref{sec:earth_reintercept} imposes on the direct dive.

Two knobs meet them. The first is the departure hyperbolic excess speed, the speed the payload keeps after climbing out of Earth's gravity. The second is the departure aim, the angle between the departure velocity and Earth's own motion, which is free because the payload is leaving on a hyperbola whose asymptote can point anywhere in a cone. Two conditions and two knobs make the solution discrete rather than a family, so the Jovian bend the cycle demands is an output. Whether Jupiter can supply that bend at the \SI{4000}{\kilo\meter} perijove altitude floor is the feasibility test, and nothing in the search is allowed to choose it \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

Scanning the aim from \SIrange{-60}{85}{\degree} at half-degree steps produced exactly one sign change in the intercept residual at each depth, so the closure reported is a unique root rather than a search artifact \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

The worry that a boosted climb-out from a Sun-grazing perihelion has to pass through the Sun to reach Earth does not bite. The climb-out sweeps \SI{130.4}{\degree} of heliocentric longitude before re-crossing \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit}, which is the same whip-around \autoref{sec:earth_reintercept} works, and the three legs together sweep \SIrange{415}{443}{\degree}. Earth's advance over two synodic periods is \SI{786.3}{\degree}, or \SI{66.3}{\degree} modulo a full turn. Those are commensurate, and the solver drives the intercept residual to zero at both two and three synodic periods \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

\subsection*{Why Three, And Only Three}
\autoref{tab:synodic_closure} runs the four candidates at a 4 solar radii perihelion.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Closures at each synodic multiple for a 4 solar radii dive \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Bend required is what the trajectory demands of the flyby; bend available is what Jupiter supplies at the \SI{4000}{\kilo\meter} perijove altitude floor. A faster arrival bends less, which is why the short cycles fail. Reproduce with \texttt{make jovian-dive}.}
    \label{tab:synodic_closure}
    \begin{tabular}{|l|r|r|r|r|l|}\hline
        \textbf{Cycle} & \textbf{Period} & \textbf{Departure} & \textbf{Arrival} & \textbf{Bend req./avail.} & \textbf{Verdict} \\
         & (\si{\year}) & $v_\infty$ (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & $v_\infty$ (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & (\si{\degree}) & \\\hline
        1S & 1.0920 & 27.461 & 41.548 & 160.45 / 59.07 & short by 101.38 \\\hline
        2S & 2.1841 & 12.679 & 17.754 & 121.52 / 114.68 & short by 6.84 \\\hline
        3S & 3.2761 & 10.539 & 12.172 & 74.73 / 133.53 & margin $+58.80$ \\\hline
        4S & 4.3682 & & & & no transfer flies this slowly \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

The four-synodic row is a reachability result rather than a search failure. As the departure excess falls, the transfer's aphelion eventually stops reaching Jupiter's orbit, and the flight time approaches a ceiling on the way there. That ceiling is \SI{3.661}{\year}, approached from below as the aim bracket is widened, and four synodic periods needs \SI{4.3682}{\year} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Multi-revolution transfer arcs, which loop the Sun once or more before arriving, are the only route to a longer clock and are unexplored here.

Backing the dive out to 32 solar radii moves the numbers a little and the verdict not at all. Three synodic periods still closes prograde and unpowered, with \SI{54.48}{\degree} of margin against \SI{58.80}{\degree}. Two synodic periods gets worse, its deficit widening from \SI{6.84}{\degree} to \SI{18.10}{\degree}, and four is still unreachable. Three stays bracketed on both sides at every depth in \autoref{tab:dive_depth_dial} \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

\subsection*{Why A Burn At Jupiter Does Not Rescue The Shorter Clock}
Two synodic periods misses by \SI{6.84}{\degree}, which looks like a rounding error. It is not, and the reason is a clock rather than a geometry.

A burn at closest approach to Jupiter is the natural second knob. Sweeping the outgoing excess from \SIrange{12}{32}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, holding both closure conditions at every point, leaves a deficit that never reaches zero. It falls to an interior minimum of \SI{6.14}{\degree} at an outgoing excess of \SI{16}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, where the burn is \SI{1487}{\meter\per\second}, and rises on both sides \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. Braking lengthens the dive, so holding the cycle time forces a hotter outbound leg, which arrives faster and bends less, putting the demand straight back. Accelerating shortens the dive but stretches the outgoing hyperbola, which eats the outgoing half of the turn. Both directions lose.

Lowering the perijove floor does not rescue it either. Dropping from \SI{4000}{\kilo\meter} of altitude to \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} moves the available bend from \SI{114.68}{\degree} to \SI{116.13}{\degree}, cutting the deficit from \SI{6.84}{\degree} to \SI{5.39}{\degree}. Nor does a deeper dive, which runs the deficit from \SI{3.53}{\degree} at 2 solar radii to \SI{11.12}{\degree} at 9.86, so even abandoning the shield design of \autoref{sec:four_radii_thermal} leaves it short \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

What does close two synodic periods is a maneuver away from the flyby, costing about \SI{2}{\kilo\meter\per\second} wherever it is placed. A rotation at the edge of Jupiter's sphere of influence costs \SI{2119}{\meter\per\second} and the cheapest deep-space maneuver costs \SI{2268}{\meter\per\second} at \SI{4.391}{\astronomicalunit}. The deep-space result is worth reading twice, because the textbook intuition is that a mid-course burn buys the arrival direction cheaply. It does not here. The maneuver gets steadily cheaper the later it fires, running \SI{7766}{\meter\per\second} at \SI{1.48}{\astronomicalunit} and \SI{3353}{\meter\per\second} at \SI{3.04}{\astronomicalunit}, because it has to hold the two-synodic clock as well as the aim, and an early burn perturbs the whole leg. So \SI{2}{\kilo\meter\per\second} is the correction scale rather than a placement to be optimized away \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

\subsection*{The Parking Orbit Is The Real Trade}
\autoref{tab:parking_orbit} sweeps the period of the ellipse between the two pulses, which is also the gap between the two waves of PuffSats, at the 32 solar radii cycle.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{The two-wave departure against parking-orbit period, at 32 solar radii and $k = 8.5$ \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.
