Periapsis speed is what the overtaking push has to reach, against a \SI{11.01}{\kilo\meter\per\second} escape speed at \SI{200}{\kilo\meter}. Re-aim is the apoapsis rotation the split costs. Growth is kilograms returned per kilogram of impactor spent, and the last column is how far Earth moves along its orbit while the payload coasts. Reproduce with \texttt{make dive-depth}.}
    \label{tab:parking_orbit}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Period} (\si{\day}) & \textbf{$v_p$} (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & \textbf{Re-aim} (\si{\kilo\meter\per\second}) & \textbf{Growth} & \textbf{Doubling} (\si{\year}) & \textbf{Earth moves} (\si{\degree}) \\\hline
        0.125 & 9.135 & 7.325 & 1.07 & 34.03 & 0.12 \\\hline
        0.25 & 9.870 & 4.271 & 1.89 & 3.57 & 0.25 \\\hline
        1 & 10.571 & 1.582 & 2.96 & 2.09 & 0.99 \\\hline
        2 & 10.735 & 0.982 & 3.26 & 1.92 & 1.97 \\\hline
        5 & 10.861 & 0.527 & 3.49 & 1.82 & 4.93 \\\hline
        20 & 10.950 & 0.207 & 3.67 & 1.75 & 19.71 \\\hline
        40 & 10.972 & 0.130 & 3.71 & 1.73 & 39.43 \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Two features of the table make the split easier to fly than it first looks. Periapsis speed saturates almost immediately, because on an ellipse of semi-major axis $a$ the periapsis speed is $\sqrt{\mu(2/r_p - 1/a)}$, and at a \SI{6578}{\kilo\meter} periapsis radius the $2\mu/r_p$ term dominates until $a$ is very large. A six-hour ellipse already reaches 90\% of escape speed \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}. So the overtaking push does not need a long coast to do most of the lifting.

Growth saturates too. Five days captures 95\% of what forty days offers, while Earth advances \SI{4.93}{\degree} instead of \SI{39.43}{\degree}. That matters because the second wave has to reach Earth on its own trajectory, and a second wave arriving five days later finds Earth almost where the first one did. Below about half a day the argument reverses. The apoapsis speed rises as the orbit shortens, the re-aim cost $2v_a\sin(\phi/2)$ rises with it, and by three hours the rotation costs \SI{7.3}{\kilo\meter\per\second} and has eaten the entire advantage of the good arrival angle \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

Against a single canted push flying the whole \SI{13.06}{\kilo\meter\per\second}, the five-day split returns 3.49 kilograms per impactor kilogram rather than 2.53, a gain of 38\%. At 4 solar radii, where the cant lands near square and the bad angle is less bad, the same split is worth 8\% \cite{Katz_aim_is_all_you_need_2025}.

\section{Spin-Axis Reorientation Cost For A Tethered Ring}\label{sec:spin_reorientation}
This appendix backs the re-pointing figure quoted in \autoref{sec:tethered_ring}. Two attitudes compete for the ring's spin axis. Centrifugal stirring wants it perpendicular to the Sun-line, so that no part of a body holds a fixed sunward face (\autoref{sec:spherical_water_thermal}). The arriving disk wants the ring plane square to the closing velocity, which puts the spin axis along that velocity. Whether the two agree depends on where the Sun sits relative to the interception track, and the worst case has them a full \SI{90}{\degree} apart. Every result below is closed form.

\subsection*{The Impulse Bound}
A rigid assembly spinning at rate \(\omega\) about a principal axis carries angular momentum \(L = I\omega\). Re-pointing that axis through an angle \(\theta\) at unchanged spin rate leaves \(|L|\) fixed, so the required change in the angular-momentum vector is the chord of an isosceles triangle,
\begin{equation}
\left|\Delta \vec{L}\right| = 2 L \sin(\theta/2).
\label{eq:reorient_chord}
\end{equation}
Thrusters mounted a distance \(r\) from the axis generate a torque no larger than \(r\) times the force they exert. For a total expended linear impulse \(J = \int \lvert\vec{F}\rvert\,\mathrm{d}t\), summed over both thrusters,
\begin{equation}
\left|\Delta \vec{L}\right| = \left|\int \vec{\tau}\,\mathrm{d}t\right| \le \int \lvert\vec{\tau}\rvert\,\mathrm{d}t \le r\,J,
\qquad\text{so}\qquad
J \ge \frac{2 I \omega \sin(\theta/2)}{r}.
\label{eq:reorient_bound}
\end{equation}
Expressing that impulse as the velocity change the same propellant would have bought on the assembly's total mass \(M\), and writing \(k^2 = I/M\) for the squared radius of gyration,
\begin{equation}
\Delta v = \frac{J}{M} \ge 2\sin(\theta/2)\,\frac{\omega k^{2}}{r}.
\label{eq:reorient_dv}
\end{equation}
The cost is set by \(k^{2}/r\), the mass distribution measured against the thruster moment arm, and not by either alone. Compact mass on a long arm is cheap. Mass spread out as far as the arm is not, which is why the figures below depend on the water staying near \SI{2}{\meter} while the thrusters ride at the tether ends.

\subsection*{The Path Actually Flown}
The bound of \autoref{eq:reorient_bound} is reached only by driving \(\Delta\vec{L}\) straight along the chord. That drops the spin rate to \(\cos(\theta/2)\) of its starting value at the midpoint, which slackens the spokes, and it leaves \(\vec{L}\) off the symmetry axis so the assembly nutates, its spin axis wobbling around a cone instead of holding still. The standard spin-stabilized maneuver instead fires short pulses at a fixed body azimuth, the same clock angle around the spin on every turn, once per rotation \cite{wertz1978attitude}. Each pulse tips \(\vec{L}\) perpendicular to itself, so \(\lvert\mathrm{d}\vec{L}\rvert = L\,\mathrm{d}\theta\) and \(\lvert L\rvert\) never changes. That walks the great circle rather than the chord, at a cost of
\begin{equation}
J_{\mathrm{arc}} = \frac{I \omega \theta}{r},
\label{eq:reorient_arc}
\end{equation}
which for \(\theta = \pi/2\) is \(\pi/(2\sqrt{2}) = 1.11\) times the bound. Holding the spin rate fixed is worth that 11\%, because spoke tension is what holds the ring's shape. Firing over a finite azimuth window \(\Delta\phi\) rather than in an instant costs a further factor. Only the component along the wanted direction survives the average, so the useful fraction is
\begin{equation}
\eta_{\mathrm{duty}} = \frac{\sin(\Delta\phi/2)}{\Delta\phi/2}.
\label{eq:reorient_duty}
\end{equation}
A \SI{60}{\degree} window keeps \SI{95.5}{\percent} and a \SI{180}{\degree} window only \SI{63.7}{\percent}.

\subsection*{Moment Of Inertia Of The Ring}
Take the configuration of \autoref{sec:tethered_ring}, with ten \SI{9}{\kilo\gram} bags on a ring of radius \(R = \SI{2}{\meter}\) and one more on the axis. The two dry-mass packages of \autoref{sec:spinning_tension_detail} sit at the ends of the \SI{25}{\meter} tether bundle, so their moment arm is \(r = \SI{12.5}{\meter}\). Each carries half the \SI{250}{\gram} dry mass and half the \SI{500}{\gram} propellant load, or \(m_d = \SI{375}{\gram}\). Bags off the axis enter as point masses at their own radius, and the hub bag is a \SI{0.13}{\meter} sphere on the axis contributing \(\tfrac{2}{5}m a^{2} = \SI{0.06}{\kilo\gram\meter\squared}\), which rounds away. So
\begin{equation}
I = 10\,m_{\mathrm{bag}} R^{2} + 2 m_d r^{2} = 360 + 117 = \SI{477}{\kilo\gram\meter\squared},
\label{eq:reorient_inertia}
\end{equation}
against \(M = \SI{100}{\kilo\gram}\), a radius of gyration of \SI{2.18}{\meter}. The \SI{750}{\gram} of packages, under \SI{1}{\percent} of the mass, supply a quarter of the inertia, because inertia weights mass by the square of its radius.

\subsection*{Result}
At the \SI{1}{\radian\per\second} spin of \autoref{sec:tethered_ring}, a \SI{90}{\degree} re-point along the great circle needs \(I\omega\theta = \SI{749}{\newton\meter\second}\) of angular impulse. Divided by the \SI{12.5}{\meter} arm that is \SI{60}{\newton\second} of expended impulse, or \SI{63}{\newton\second} through a \SI{60}{\degree} firing window, an equivalent \SI{0.63}{\meter\per\second} on the full \SI{100}{\kilo\gram}. The drag-cancellation pass of \autoref{sec:estimate_cold_gas} models \SI{20}{\milli\newton} for \SI{300}{\second} plus a \SI{400}{\milli\newton} surge for \SI{30}{\second}, \SI{18}{\newton\second} before margins, so the worst-case re-point costs about \num{3.5} times that leg. The tank is the fairer measure, since that appendix carries wide margins. The \SI{500}{\gram} load of \autoref{sec:cold_gas_fluid_choice} holds \SI{123}{\newton\second} at the pessimistic \SI{25}{\second} specific impulse assumed there, and \SI{211}{\newton\second} at the \SI{43}{\second} flown propane systems reach, so the re-point takes about half the tank on the first figure and under a third on the second. \autoref{eq:reorient_arc} is linear in \(\theta\), so a \SI{30}{\degree} correction costs a third of the \SI{90}{\degree} figure.

Timing turns on how the \SI{400}{\milli\newton} of \autoref{sec:fine_control_thrusters} is read. That figure is the peak drag force on the whole vehicle, so the conservative reading shares it as \SI{200}{\milli\newton} at each end. The two packages then form a couple of \SI{5}{\newton\meter} while firing, with no net force on the assembly. A \SI{60}{\degree} window delivers \SI{5}{\newton\meter\second} per rotation, so the maneuver takes 150 rotations, or \SI{942}{\second}. Sizing each package for the full \SI{400}{\milli\newton} instead halves that to \SI{471}{\second}. Impulse and propellant are unchanged either way, because only the firing rate moves. Widening the window to \SI{180}{\degree} halves the wall-clock time again and raises the propellant by half.

The spokes take a modest transverse load while the axis moves. Precessing at \(\Omega_p = \theta/t\) requires an out-of-plane force on each bag with peak \(2 m \omega \Omega_p R\), against a spoke tension of \(m \omega^{2} R\), so the ring cones out of its plane by
\begin{equation}
\tan\psi = \frac{2\Omega_p}{\omega} = \frac{\theta}{\pi N},
\label{eq:reorient_coning}
\end{equation}
where \(N\) is the number of rotations the maneuver takes. At \(N = 150\) that is \SI{0.19}{\degree}, and at \(N = 75\) still only \SI{0.38}{\degree}. The tension rises by under a part in ten thousand in both cases.

\subsection*{Choosing The Moment Arm}
Pushing the packages further out lengthens the arm but adds inertia as \(r^{2}\). Writing \(I = I_0 + 2 m_d r^{2}\), with \(I_0\) the water ring's own inertia,
\begin{equation}
J_{\mathrm{arc}}(r) = \omega\theta\left(\frac{I_0}{r} + 2 m_d r\right),
\qquad
r_{*} = \sqrt{\frac{I_0}{2 m_d}} = \SI{21.9}{\meter},
\qquad
J_{\min} = 2\omega\theta\sqrt{2 m_d I_0} = \SI{52}{\newton\second}.
\label{eq:reorient_optimum}
\end{equation}
The minimum is shallow. The \SI{12.5}{\meter} arm the tether bundle already provides sits \SI{16}{\percent} above it, and any arm between \SI{10}{\meter} and \SI{45}{\meter} stays within \SI{35}{\percent}. Mounting the thrusters on the bags instead, at \(r = R = \SI{2}{\meter}\), would cost \SI{285}{\newton\second}, nearly five times as much. The long tether the dry-mass disposal scheme already carries is what makes this maneuver cheap.

\subsection*{Why The Maneuver Waits}
Flying the thermal attitude through the coast and re-pointing in the last hours costs almost nothing thermally. The hub bag is the only body whose stirring depends on the Sun angle. It straddles the axis rather than sitting off it, so its own field runs from zero on the axis out to \(\omega^{2}a = \SI{0.13}{\meter\per\second\squared}\) at its equator. Where the Sun lands decides whether the heating meets that field or the null at its center. The \SIrange{10}{15}{\watt} of \autoref{sec:lox_puffsat} is quoted for a \SI{25}{\kilo\gram} body, and scaling it by projected area puts the hub bag near \SIrange{5}{8}{\watt}. The same figure follows from the \SI{38}{\watt} the \SI{100}{\kilo\gram} body of \autoref{sec:spherical_water_thermal} radiates in balance. Even with nothing radiated, \SI{8}{\watt} raises its \SI{9}{\kilo\gram} of water by only \SI{2.2}{\kelvin} in three hours, against a tolerated band a hundred kelvin wide.

The mean is the easy bound, and it hides which attitude is at issue. Through the coast the Sun lies in the hub bag's equatorial plane, so the heating lands where its own field is strongest and sweeps around once per rotation. Warm water at the rim sits at the bottom of an outward field and overturns, and the permanently cold poles are what the aluminum-out caps of \autoref{sec:spherical_water_thermal} already answer. That is the good case, and it is the one flown for all but the last hours.

Re-pointing the axis at the Sun is the bad case. It moves the sunlit pole onto the centrifugal null, and warm water floats toward the axis, so the cap has nowhere left to float and sits conducting. Crossing the whole bag that way is slow, taking \(a^{2}/\kappa \approx \SI{32}{\hour}\) at a \SI{0.13}{\meter} radius, where \(\kappa\) is the thermal diffusivity of water. Three hours is far short of that, so the heat stays in a skin \(\sqrt{\kappa t} \approx \SI{4}{\centi\meter}\) deep and the surface rise follows \(2q\sqrt{\kappa t/\pi}/k\). Averaged over the lit hemisphere, \SI{8}{\watt} is \SI{76}{\watt\per\square\meter} and lifts the cap \SI{6}{\kelvin} above the interior. A \(\cos\theta\) distribution peaks at twice that, \SI{11}{\kelvin} at the sub-solar point, and that reading is pessimistic, because the water sees the bumper's inner face across the gap rather than the Sun. Both figures ignore the vapor space's latent transport and the roughly \SI{0.5}{\watt\per\kelvin} by which the cap's net intake falls as it warms. Neither threatens the liquid range.

The ten ring bags are indifferent to the change. A ring bag's centrifugal field points outward from the spin axis and never changes direction in the bag's own frame. The Sun direction does change, and it cannot be averaged as a vector, because a surface element of outward normal \(\vec{n}\) absorbs as \(\max(0,\,\vec{n}\cdot\vec{s})\). Average that quantity instead. Over one rotation \(\vec{s}\) sweeps a cone about the spin axis, so the mean depends on \(\vec{n}\) only through its angle to that axis. The bag's outward and inward faces share the same angle, so they collect identical mean flux at every Sun angle, and no mean gradient forms along the field. The bag stays a side-heated cavity with no onset value to clear (\autoref{sec:spherical_water_thermal}).

\section{Tensile-Fiber Selection for PuffSat Tension Members}\label{sec:fiber_selection}
Three PuffSat structures carry organic fiber under steady tension above most of the atmosphere: the vacuum launch column (\autoref{sec:tether_tapering}), the Mars skyhook tether (\autoref{sec:mars_skyhook}), and the polymer net behind the pusher-plate sail (\autoref{sec:lightweight_pusher_plates}). The same four fibers are candidates in each case, but a different property decides the choice. The column turns on self-support length, the skyhook on creep and specific strength, and the sail net on shock toughness within a temperature-ceiling limit. \autoref{tab:fiber_selection} collects the numbers, and this section gives the reasoning the three sites share.

Self-support breaking length scales with specific strength. PBO, the fiber sold as Zylon, reaches a self-support length near \SI{380}{\kilo\meter}, about \num{1.6} times Vectran's \cite{zylon_technical_info}. SK99 Dyneema's instantaneous figure is longer still, an estimated \SI{430}{\kilo\meter} from its \SI{20}{\percent} tenacity gain over SK78 \cite{dyneema_sk99_datasheet}, but it holds only for short loads.  Creep under sustained tension disqualifies polyethylene below. Kevlar, near \SI{250}{\kilo\meter} \cite{cambridge_materials_databook}, falls short of the \SIrange{300}{400}{\kilo\meter} a tall column spans, and Vectran is shorter still. Taper closes that gap, but its mass penalty climbs steeply as self-support length drops, so the column favors the fiber with the most margin (see \autoref{sec:tether_tapering}). Creep separates the fibers under permanent load. Drawn polyethylene creeps even when carbon-modified for creep resistance \cite{sk99_tension}. Vectran, a liquid-crystal polymer, all but eliminates creep \cite{vectran_brochure}, and PBO also creeps very little \cite{zylon_technical_info}. A structure that holds steady tension for years, such as a spinning skyhook, rules out polyethylene on creep alone.

Temperature ceiling forms a ladder. Drawn polyethylene like Dyneema melts near \SI{147}{\celsius} \cite{uhmwpe_fiber_datasheet}. Vectran retains about half its strength near \SI{150}{\celsius} and useful strength to roughly \SI{200}{\celsius}, melting above \SI{300}{\celsius} \cite{vectran_brochure}. Aramid does not melt, keeps most of its strength to about \SI{250}{\celsius}, and decomposes above \SI{425}{\celsius} \cite{kevlar_technical_guide}. PBO tops the ladder and decomposes only near \SI{650}{\celsius} \cite{zylon_technical_info}. A back ply that runs hot must climb the ladder, while one that radiates cool between widely spaced pulses can sit lower.

Cold sits at the safe end of that ladder. A drawn or rigid-rod fiber loses strength as it warms, not as it cools. PBO holds its strength down to cryogenic temperature. A Zylon/epoxy composite tested at \SI{77}{\kelvin} keeps a fiber tensile strength above \SI{4.3}{\giga\pascal}, which is why it reinforces high-field magnet windings \cite{zylon_cryogenic}. A night-side pass on an Earth column or a Mars tether stays far warmer than that, so the cold does not weaken the load-bearing fiber. The property to watch is brittleness, and it is not a cold effect. PBO is a low-strain fiber at every temperature. Its elongation at break is \SIrange{2.5}{3.5}{\percent}, and it fails by axial splitting with little plastic flow \cite{zylon_technical_info}. As a rigid-rod aromatic polymer, it has no conventional glass transition in this range, so it shows no cold ductile-to-brittle transition like the one that embrittles ordinary plastics. The transition it does show is set by strain rate, not temperature. A fast pull or a shock load fractures it with little warning \cite{pbo_strain_rate_dbt}.

A static tension member tolerates a brittle fiber. The launch column and the skyhook tether hold a steady axial load, where strength and low creep decide and elongation does not. Brittleness bites only where the fiber folds, knots, bends over a small radius, or takes a shock. The architecture keeps PBO clear of those cases. Terminations spread load by potting or capstan wraps rather than knots. Bend radii stay generous. The curvature actuators of \autoref{sec:vacuum_tube_details} ramp slowly, so the line never sees an impulsive spike. Redundant parallel cables cover a fiber that nicks rather than yields, since it cannot shed a defect by flowing around it. Parts that must fold or flex, such as the corrugated lower tube wall or the pusher-plate sail net, use Vectran instead. Vectran resists flex-cracking when cold, the property that won it over aramid for the Mars Pathfinder airbags, where folded aramid cracked along its creases \cite{pathfinder_airbag}.

The sail net pushes that logic furthest, because it is the one tension member loaded by shock rather than steady weight. Each pulse arrives as a brief, high strain-rate impulse, the regime where PBO turns brittle \cite{pbo_strain_rate_dbt}. The net exists to absorb that impulse, so shock toughness decides the choice and PBO's specific-strength lead does not apply. Vectran takes the role. It holds the impulse without creep, so the sail stays taut across a long pulse train \cite{vectran_brochure}. Temperature is the one limit on that choice. Vectran keeps useful strength only to about \SI{200}{\celsius}, far below PBO's \SI{650}{\celsius} \cite{vectran_brochure, zylon_technical_info}. The stitched laminate exists to keep the back ply under that ceiling. The ceramic felt insulates it, and a brief, widely spaced pulse radiates away before its heat reaches the net (see \autoref{sec:lightweight_pusher_plates}). While the back-ply equilibrium temperature stays below \SI{200}{\celsius}, Vectran wins outright. A hotter or more tightly spaced pulse train, or a thinner felt budget, forces the net up the ladder to aramid or coated PBO for thermal survival. That climb trades shock toughness for thermal headroom, paid back with a thicker net or wider pulse spacing.

Three environmental weaknesses shape whichever fiber gets exposed. Atomic oxygen erodes organic polymers in low orbit \cite{ao_protection_banks}. Ultraviolet weakens aramid, Vectran, and PBO. Moisture drives slow hydrolysis in aramid \cite{kevlar_technical_guide}, and PBO shares that weakness. PBO is the most exposed of the four. Bare PBO sheds about a third of its strength in a few hours of atomic oxygen \cite{pbo_atomic_oxygen}, and ultraviolet and visible light break its oxazole ring, and fielded PBO body armor lost roughly half its strength over a few years of service, combined with moisture, the failure that ended its use in police body armor \cite{zylon_body_armor}. One continuous silica or metallized barrier answers these weaknesses together. It reflects the ultraviolet and seals out the atomic oxygen at once, the same low-outgassing inorganic coating that has kept polyimide solar-array blankets intact in low orbit for years \cite{ao_protection_banks}, and behind it the fiber stays dry so the hydrolysis pathway is largely suppressed. A barrier helps only while it stays continuous. Micrometeoroid strikes and thermal cycling can open pinholes or hairline cracks, allowing atomic oxygen to undercut the fiber at each defect. A closer laboratory precedent is a UPy-functionalized polyhedral oligomeric silsesquioxane (UPy-POSS) coating on polyimide. It forms an atomic-oxygen-resistant silica surface and healed cracks at \SI{80}{\celsius} or during atomic-oxygen exposure while surviving repeated bending and thermal cycling \cite{wang2018_self_healing_ao}. The result does not qualify the coating on PBO or under tensile load and hypervelocity damage. A coated-PBO candidate therefore needs adhesion, ultraviolet and atomic-oxygen exposure, thermal-cycling, tension, and puncture tests before it can be credited with protecting the fiber. This coating remains the least-proven part of the PBO design. PBO is therefore the default where the environment is controlled and a liability where it is not, such as humid air near the surface.

PBO is not new to spaceflight. Metal-clad PBO is a stock product, and the fiber already carries the suspension loads of Crew Dragon's main parachutes as a space-rated tension member \cite{crew_dragon_parachute, zylon_technical_info}.

\begin{table}[htpb]
    \centering
    \begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{|l|L|L|L|L|}\hline
        \textbf{Fiber} & \textbf{Self-support length} & \textbf{Creep} & \textbf{Temperature ceiling} & \textbf{Main vulnerability} \\\hline
        UHMWPE (Dyneema) & $\sim$\SI{430}{\kilo\meter} instantaneous; creep-limited under sustained load & creeps, even carbon-modified & melts $\sim$\SI{147}{\celsius} & low temperature ceiling; creep \\\hline
        Aramid (Kevlar) & below \SIrange{300}{400}{\kilo\meter} & low & no melt; to $\sim$\SI{250}{\celsius}, decomposes above \SI{425}{\celsius} & moisture uptake; hydrolysis \\\hline
        Vectran (LCP) & $\sim$\SI{240}{\kilo\meter} & none & to $\sim$\SI{200}{\celsius}; melts above \SI{300}{\celsius} & lower specific strength \\\hline
        PBO (Zylon) & $\sim$\SI{380}{\kilo\meter} & none & decomposes $\sim$\SI{650}{\celsius} & photodegradation; atomic-oxygen erosion \\\hline
    \end{tabularx}
    \caption{Tensile-fiber candidates for PuffSat tension members. Self-support length is the breaking length under the fiber's own weight in Earth gravity, and it scales with specific strength.  Vectran's entry follows from PBO's \SI{380}{\kilo\meter} and the \num{1.6} ratio. PBO's two vulnerabilities close together: the coating that shields it from atomic oxygen also blocks the light that drives its photodegradation, and a dry environment suppresses its hydrolysis.}
    \label{tab:fiber_selection}
\end{table}

\section{Argon Slug Thermophysics}\label{sec:argon_slug_thermo}
\autoref{sec:watering_it_down} quotes an argon slug as the alternative to water and prices it in a few lines. This appendix works those numbers. One theme runs through all of them. Argon's handbook constants are quoted at one atmosphere, and there is no atmosphere, so the vacuum forms have to be used instead \cite{crc_handbook, nist_webbook_argon}.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Argon, with water for scale. The heat capacity of solid argon is the softest entry and the answers below barely move across its plausible range, as \autoref{eq:argon_flash} shows. Sublimation enthalpy is the sum of the other two, which is what it must be at the triple point.}
    \label{tab:argon_properties}
    \begin{tabular}{|l|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Quantity} & \textbf{Argon} & \textbf{Water} \\\hline
        Molar mass & \SI{39.95}{\gram\per\mole} & \SI{18.02}{\gram\per\mole} \\\hline
        Particles per kilogram, fully broken down & \SI{25.0}{\mole} & \SI{166.5}{\mole} \\\hline
        Triple point & \SI{83.81}{\kelvin}, \SI{68.9}{\kilo\pascal} & \SI{273.16}{\kelvin}, \SI{611}{\pascal} \\\hline
        Saturated vapor density there & \SI{3.95}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} & \SI{0.0048}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} \\\hline
        Critical point & \SI{150.7}{\kelvin}, \SI{4.86}{\mega\pascal} & \SI{647.1}{\kelvin}, \SI{22.1}{\mega\pascal} \\\hline
        Melting enthalpy & \SI{29.7}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} & \SI{334}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} \\\hline
        Vaporization enthalpy & \SI{163}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} & \SI{2260}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} \\\hline
        Sublimation enthalpy & \SI{193}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} & \SI{2830}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} \\\hline
        Condensed density near the triple point & \SI{1623}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} & \SI{917}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} \\\hline
        Solid heat capacity, \SIrange{60}{84}{\kelvin} & \SIrange{450}{600}{\joule\per\kilogram\per\kelvin} & \\\hline
        Bond energy to break the molecule & none & \SI{50.9}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection*{Vacuum Deletes The Boiling Point}
Argon's \SI{87.3}{\kelvin} boiling point is the temperature at which its vapor pressure reaches one atmosphere, which is a fact about the atmosphere rather than about argon. What survives in vacuum is the triple point. Below \SI{68.9}{\kilo\pascal} liquid argon does not exist at any temperature, and the substance goes between solid and vapor directly. Three places in the design care. The tank has to hold at least that pressure for its contents to be a liquid at all. The shell's cooling channel has to hold it as well, since below it the coolant sublimes at \SI{193}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} rather than melting at \SI{29.7}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram}, and the match described below is lost. The bag holds far less than it, which is why the slug arrives there as snow.

The same fact rules out one tempting simplification. A tank vented to space does not hold cold liquid argon. It holds solid argon that is subliming, and how fast it does that is the next calculation.

\subsection*{Free-Surface Sublimation}
A surface in vacuum loses mass at the rate its own vapor pressure would supply if nothing came back \cite{knudsen1915verdampfung}. The flux is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:hertz_knudsen}
\dot{m} = P_{\mathrm{sub}}(T)\sqrt{\frac{M}{2\pi R_g T}},
\end{equation}
where $P_{\mathrm{sub}}$ is the sublimation pressure. The square root is the mean speed at which molecules cross a plane, so the whole expression is a pressure divided by a speed. \autoref{tab:argon_sublimation} evaluates it for the \SI{0.37}{\square\meter} surface of a \SI{25}{\kilogram} argon rod at solid density.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Free-surface loss from a bare \SI{25}{\kilogram} argon rod, area \SI{0.37}{\square\meter}. The sublimation curve is anchored at the triple point with the \SI{7.71}{\kilo\joule\per\mole} sublimation enthalpy. Rates are per year of exposure, so the last two rows are statements that nothing is left.}
    \label{tab:argon_sublimation}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Temperature} & \textbf{$P_{\mathrm{sub}}$} & \textbf{Flux} & \textbf{Loss per year} \\\hline
        \SI{25}{\kelvin} & \SI{3.4e-7}{\pascal} & \SI{1.9e-9}{\kilogram\per\square\meter\per\second} & \SI{22}{\gram} \\\hline
        \SI{30}{\kelvin} & \SI{1.7e-4}{\pascal} & \SI{8.4e-7}{\kilogram\per\square\meter\per\second} & \SI{9.8}{\kilogram} \\\hline
        \SI{40}{\kelvin} & \SI{0.38}{\pascal} & \SI{1.6e-3}{\kilogram\per\square\meter\per\second} & \SI{19}{\tonne} \\\hline
        \SI{83.81}{\kelvin} & \SI{68.9}{\kilo\pascal} & \SI{208}{\kilogram\per\square\meter\per\second} & the rod lasts \SI{0.3}{\second} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

That table describes a bare body, and it is why an argon impactor is a sealed one. Free sublimation would need the rod below about \SI{28}{\kelvin} to survive two to three years, and nothing passive reaches that anywhere the cycle flies. Sealed, nothing is lost, and the vessel costs almost nothing. At \SI{60}{\kelvin} argon's vapor pressure is \SI{854}{\pascal}, which over a \SI{25}{\kilogram} rod needs under a gram of the fiber of \autoref{sec:fiber_selection}, and even at \SI{130}{\kelvin} it needs \SI{25}{\gram}. The container is never the binding cost.

What the vessel needs is shade, and the requirement is milder than it sounds. JWST holds the cold side of a five-layer aluminized Kapton sunshield near \SI{40}{\kelvin} at \SI{1.01}{\astronomicalunit} against a hot side near \SI{383}{\kelvin} \cite{jwst_sunshield}. Sixty kelvin asks for less. Five layers of \SI{6}{\micro\meter} Kapton over the \SI{1.5}{\square\meter} that shadows a \SI{0.87}{\meter} rod is \SI{64}{\gram} of film, so the shade and its spreaders run one to two percent of the projectile, the same order as the bag film. Two thermal objections turn out not to bind. A \SI{25}{\kilogram} argon rod holds \SI{13}{\kilo\joule\per\kelvin}, so collapsing the shade before it reaches the \SI{0.15}{\meter} aperture costs \SI{0.05}{\kelvin} across ten seconds of full sunlight at \SI{1}{\astronomicalunit} and \SI{3.3}{\kelvin} across ten minutes. And integrating Earth's reflected light along the approach from \SI{100000}{\kilo\meter} down to \SI{200}{\kilo\meter} at \SI{45}{\kilo\meter\per\second} delivers \SI{24}{\kilo\joule}, which is \SI{1.8}{\kelvin}.

What the shade prevents is a burst rather than a slow loss, and that is the asymmetry to design against. Ice that loses its shade warms and sublimes. Argon at solid density that loses its shade goes supercritical past \SI{150.7}{\kelvin}, and the vessel that was free at \SI{60}{\kelvin} is then asked to hold a fluid it cannot hold. Filling less densely survives that and costs the areal density \autoref{sec:needle_through_fog} needs, since the rod lengthens as the fill falls.

\subsection*{The Flash Into The Bag}
Argon sprayed into the bag cannot stay liquid, because the bag's mean density is \SI{213}{\kilogram} in \SI{660}{\cubic\meter}, or \SI{0.323}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}, and saturated argon vapor at the triple point is \SI{3.95}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}. There is not enough argon in the bag to supply the vapor that liquid argon demands over itself, so the spray flashes. Two conditions fix where it stops. The vapor has to sit on the saturation curve, and the heat to make that vapor has to come from the rest of the argon cooling and freezing:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:argon_flash}
x\,\rho_{\mathrm{bag}} = \rho_{\mathrm{sat}}(T), \qquad x\,\Delta H_{\mathrm{sub}} = \Delta H_{\mathrm{fus}} + c_s\,(T_t - T),
\end{equation}
where $x$ is the vapor fraction and $c_s$ is the solid heat capacity, taken here as \SI{525}{\joule\per\kilogram\per\kelvin}. Solving the pair from saturated solid at the triple point gives \SI{60}{\kelvin} and $x = 0.218$, so the bag holds \SI{22}{\percent} vapor over \SI{78}{\percent} solid snow at \SI{0.88}{\kilo\pascal}. Across the whole \SIrange{450}{600}{\joule\per\kilogram\per\kelvin} range of $c_s$ the temperature moves by \SI{0.3}{\kelvin} and the vapor fraction from \num{0.210} to \num{0.227}, because the saturation curve is far steeper than the energy balance.

Two numbers follow. The pressure is an eighth of the \SI{8.7}{\kilo\pascal} the steam bag holds, since pressure is $\rho R_g T/M$ and argon is heavier and five times colder, so \autoref{eq:bag_film_mass} returns \SI{0.5}{\kilogram} and the bag never leaves the handling floor of \autoref{tab:axial_bag}. And the slug arrives \SI{24}{\kelvin} colder than the tank it left, because the flash refrigerates it.

\subsection*{The Shell Load Against The Melting Enthalpy}
The eddy shell of \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} has no path to space of its own. Eight tonnes of aluminum at \SI{900}{\joule\per\kilogram\per\kelvin} reaching melting from room temperature is \SI{4.56}{\giga\joule}, and it does that in \SI{340}{\second} with the coolant stopped, so it collects \SI{13.4}{\mega\watt}. At two pulses per second a \SI{213}{\kilogram} slug is \SI{426}{\kilogram\per\second} of flow, and \SI{13.4}{\mega\watt} spread over it is \SI{31.5}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram}.

Fed liquid at \SI{87}{\kelvin} that heat has nowhere to go but latent heat, so it boils \SI{19}{\percent} of the flow and the channels run two-phase. Fed solid at the triple point it goes into melting instead, and melting costs \SI{29.7}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram}. The two agree to \SI{6}{\percent}, so the coolant enters solid and leaves as saturated liquid with \SI{1.8}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} of margin. Nothing was tuned to make that happen, and if the load moves the fallback below absorbs the difference.

\subsection*{Banking The Load In The Payload}
The cycle has two coolants and both of them are the propellant. A water slug warms and melts on the way to boiling, and \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} calls that the heat sponge. An argon slug melts at the triple point and boils past it, and the subsection above matches the shell's load to the melting step. There is a third, and it is not propellant at all. The ship carries a payload of water ice, and ice is the sponge that argon stopped being.

The three differ in what they are spending. Boiling either slug spends mass that leaves with the exhaust, so the sink is gone once the pulse is fired. Melting the payload spends nothing. The heat comes back out over the transfer and the ice is there for the next burn, which makes it a battery where the other two are a stream.

Write $\phi$ for the payload fraction, meaning the share of the mass at ignition that is neither slug nor dry structure. The shell deposits \SI{31.5}{\kilo\joule} per kilogram of slug, so per kilogram of payload it deposits $(1-\phi)/\phi$ times that. Charging the whole remainder as slug is the worst case, because dry mass displaces slug rather than adding to it. At $\phi = 1/4$ the payload owes \SI{94.4}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram}. \autoref{tab:payload_sink} sets that against what ice holds.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{The three sinks, against the \SI{94.4}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} of payload the eddy shell deposits at a payload fraction of \num{0.25}. Slug rows are multiplied by three in the payload column, since three kilograms of slug fly per kilogram of payload at that fraction. Ice enthalpies integrate the measured heat capacity of ice~Ih rather than a constant, which matters because that capacity falls by a factor of twelve between melting and \SI{30}{\kelvin} \cite{crc_handbook}. The water slug's apparent margin is not available in full. \autoref{tab:bag_state} spends nearly all of that sponge on the collision's own waste heat before the shell's share is counted, which is the difference between a sink the pulse is already using and one it is not.}
    \label{tab:payload_sink}
    \begin{tabular}{|l|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Sink} & \textbf{Per \si{\kilogram} of it} & \textbf{Per \si{\kilogram} payload} & \textbf{Margin} \\\hline
        Argon slug, melting only, the match above & 29.7 & 89 & \num{0.94} \\\hline
        Argon slug, melting and boiling & 193 & 578 & \num{6.1} \\\hline
        Water slug, warming and melting from \SI{122}{\kelvin} & 570 & 1710 & \num{18} \\\hline
        Water slug, and boiling as well & 2830 & 8490 & \num{90} \\\hline
        Ice payload, latent heat alone & 334 & 334 & \num{3.5} \\\hline
        Ice payload from \SI{243}{\kelvin} & 394 & 394 & \num{4.2} \\\hline
        Ice payload from \SI{122}{\kelvin}, shaded and passive & 570 & 570 & \num{6.0} \\\hline
        Ice payload from \SI{30}{\kelvin}, actively refrigerated & 624 & 624 & \num{6.6} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Two readings of the ice rows decide the design and neither of them is the margin. The first is that storing the payload cold is worth very little. Ice's heat capacity falls with temperature, from \SI{2110}{\joule\per\kilogram\per\kelvin} at melting to \SI{790}{} at \SI{100}{\kelvin} and \SI{175}{} at \SI{30}{\kelvin}, so the entire span from \SI{30}{\kelvin} up to the \SI{122}{\kelvin} a shaded body reaches unaided holds \SI{54}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram}. That is \SI{9.5}{\percent} of the total. Refrigerating the payload below where it already sits buys a tenth and costs a cryocooler on every projectile.

The second is that most of what remains is out of the loop's reach. A fluid that carries heat off an aluminum shell running several hundred kelvin has to be water, and water freezes at \SI{273}{\kelvin}, so the \SI{236}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} held below that point sits behind the coolant's own freezing point. The honest budget is the latent heat by itself, \SI{334}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram}, and that still clears the worst case by \num{3.5}. The cold the payload is stored at is not the asset. The phase change is.

\subsection*{Melting Fast Enough}
Holding the energy and taking it in time are different questions. Three links carry the heat and only the last is in doubt. In the reference cylindrical cooling layout, the shell hands \SI{13.4}{\mega\watt} to its channels over the \SI{225}{\square\meter} of a \SI{3}{\meter} bore \SI{23.8}{\meter} long, which is \SI{60}{\kilo\watt\per\square\meter}, about \SI{6}{\kelvin} of film drop at the \SI{10}{\kilo\watt\per\square\meter\per\kelvin} forced water reaches. The loop carries the same power on \SI{45.6}{\kilo\gram\per\second} at a \SI{70}{\kelvin} rise, and pumping that against a few bar costs \SI{15}{\kilo\watt} against \SI{13.4}{\mega\watt}.

The last link fails by insulation rather than by capacity. Liquid water conducts at \SI{0.57}{\watt\per\meter\per\kelvin} against \SI{2.2}{} for ice at melting and more for ice when cold, so meltwater left where it forms insulates better than the solid it came from. The burn takes \SI{1.26}{\centi\meter} off every wetted surface whatever the ship's size, since the melted fraction is fixed by the mass ratio at \SI{28}{\percent} and the burn lengthens in proportion to the payload. A \SI{142}{\tonne} magazine of the \SI{25}{\kilogram} rods of \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} carries \SI{3465}{\square\meter} of surface and burns for \SI{1000}{\second}, so it takes \SI{13.4}{\mega\watt} at \SI{3.9}{\kilo\watt\per\square\meter}. Circulated, that asks \SI{4}{\kelvin} across the film. Left standing as \SI{1.26}{\centi\meter} of still water it asks \SI{86}{\kelvin}, and on a \SI{27.5}{\tonne} magazine, where the same power crosses \SI{671}{\square\meter}, it asks \SI{442}{\kelvin} and the melt front stalls.

So the rate constraint binds at small payload where the energy constraint binds at large, and the answer to both is to make the meltwater the coolant rather than a barrier to it. Perforated feed lines discharge warm water into the melt cavity and a return draws it off, which is direct contact with no solid conduction path anywhere in it. That is how ice thermal storage already works on the ground. One transient survives. Water returning at \SI{340}{\kelvin} onto ice at \SI{122}{\kelvin} freezes before it melts anything, and the frost shell it grows relaxes in $\delta^2/\alpha$, a few seconds for the few millimeters involved. Against a burn of hundreds of seconds that is nothing, and pre-warming the magazine to just under freezing during the coast removes it outright.

\subsection*{The Melt Is The Casting Step}
Twenty-eight percent of the payload comes out of the burn as slush, which sounds like damage and is not. The projectiles are filled and sealed in flight rather than carried preformed, and the outbound leg to Jupiter runs a year or more. Melting the ice is the step that casting needs anyway, so the shell's \SI{13.4}{\giga\joule} pays for a quarter of it instead of being thrown away.

Casting has one direction it must not be done in. Freezing a \SI{0.87}{\meter} rod end to end drives the heat out along the ice already frozen, and \SI{173}{\watt} through \SI{0.87}{\meter} of ice at \SI{2.2}{\watt\per\meter\per\kelvin} across a \SI{0.19}{\meter} bore needs a gradient of \SI{2400}{\kelvin}. Freezing it radially drives the same heat across at most the \SI{9.5}{\centi\meter} radius, and \SI{334}{\watt\per\square\meter} across that needs \SI{14}{\kelvin}. Rods therefore freeze inward from the wall, which is also the direction that leaves the last liquid on the axis with a path to an open end. Water expands \SI{9}{\percent} on freezing, so that path matters. A \SI{40}{\tonne} melt wants \SI{3.9}{\cubic\meter} of ullage, and rods packed in a magazine leave several times that between them.

The schedule is generous. A \SI{25}{\kilogram} rod radiating from \SI{0.61}{\square\meter} at \SI{273}{\kelvin} sheds \SI{173}{\watt}, so its \SI{8.35}{\mega\joule} of fusion leaves in \SI{13}{\hour}. Casting the \num{1600} rods of a \SI{40}{\tonne} melt is then \num{21500} mold-hours against the \num{8760} in a year, which is two and a half molds working continuously. Liquefying the whole \SI{142}{\tonne} payload rather than the melted quarter costs \SI{80}{\giga\joule}, of which the shell donates \SI{13.4}{\giga\joule} and steady solar supplies the remaining \SI{2.1}{\kilo\watt}. That is the same shape of load as the cryocoolers \autoref{sec:epstein_drives} already runs across the cruise, kilowatts drawn steadily rather than megajoules delivered in a second.

\subsection*{What The Third Path Buys}
The margin is not the point. Three things change that the other two rejection paths do not touch.

The operating rule of \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} relaxes. That section ties the pulse rate to the coolant flow, because the shell has no path to space of its own and an eight tonne shell with the flow stopped reaches melting in \SI{340}{\second}. A shell that dumps into stored ice is no longer tied to the stream it is cooling, so throttling, coasting and firing in bursts stop being thermal decisions.

The radiator shrinks by three orders of magnitude and cools by nine hundred kelvin. \autoref{tab:argon_dump} needs \SI{114}{\square\meter} of graphite at \SI{1200}{\kelvin} to shed \SI{13.4}{\mega\watt} as it arrives. Banked instead, one \SI{1000}{\second} burn stores \SI{13.4}{\giga\joule}, and refreezing that across a two-year transfer is \SI{210}{\watt}, which \SI{0.7}{\square\meter} at \SI{273}{\kelvin} rejects. The burn is a ten-thousandth of the cycle, and a battery is what converts a duty cycle into a radiator. Two Earth-departure pulses five days apart need no recharge between them either, since \SI{28}{\percent} melts on each and \SI{57}{\percent} is still inside the latent budget.

And the slug stops having two jobs. Argon is chosen for what it does to the plume, where \autoref{tab:carrier_sigma} puts it above water by a factor of \numrange{4}{38} across the temperature range the plume works through. It cools the shell only because nothing else was available to. That second job is what forces the coolant channel above the \SI{68.9}{\kilo\pascal} triple point, and it is what leaves only \SI{1.8}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} of margin on the melting match above, with two-phase flow behind an eight tonne shell as the fallback. A payload that takes the whole load retires the job. What is left is a propellant chosen on plasma physics alone, sent from tank to bag without passing through the shell at all.

The cryogenic bill argon carries does not go away, but it stops being forced. Every projectile still needs a sealed vessel and a sunshade, and losing the shade drives the contents supercritical past \SI{150.7}{\kelvin}. The tank is cold at both ends of the cycle. The bag is a membrane that has to be handled and filled at \SI{60}{\kelvin}, which is a harder film problem than the \SI{423}{\kelvin} melting point of polyethylene makes it look. Where those bite hardest the water slug comes back, and the sponge it was giving up is now the payload's job rather than the propellant's.

\subsection*{The Resistive Dump}
Boiling the argon flow outright absorbs \SI{163}{\kilo\joule\per\kilogram} times \SI{426}{\kilogram\per\second}, or \SI{69.5}{\mega\watt}, five times the shell's load. Past that the induced current can be taken somewhere else. A pickup winding feeding a refractory resistor moves the same energy to a component chosen for temperature rather than for shielding, and radiated flux goes as $T^4$, so a hot dump is a small one. \autoref{tab:argon_dump} sizes it. The energy is dissipated in either case, so moving it costs no thrust.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Radiating area needed to shed the shell's \SI{13.4}{\mega\watt} from a black surface, one side. Graphite is the material assumed, since it emits near unity where polished refractory metals do not, and \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} already flies it as the nozzle liner. The \SI{800}{\kelvin} row is what a dump limited to the aluminum shell's own temperature would need, and is the reason the dump is a separate component.}
    \label{tab:argon_dump}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Element temperature} & \textbf{Flux} & \textbf{Area} \\\hline
        \SI{800}{\kelvin} & \SI{23.2}{\kilo\watt\per\square\meter} & \SI{577}{\square\meter} \\\hline
        \SI{1200}{\kelvin} & \SI{118}{\kilo\watt\per\square\meter} & \SI{114}{\square\meter} \\\hline
        \SI{1500}{\kelvin} & \SI{287}{\kilo\watt\per\square\meter} & \SI{47}{\square\meter} \\\hline
        \SI{2000}{\kelvin} & \SI{907}{\kilo\watt\per\square\meter} & \SI{15}{\square\meter} \\\hline
        \SI{2500}{\kelvin} & \SI{2215}{\kilo\watt\per\square\meter} & \SI{6}{\square\meter} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

The liner's booked \SI{4.9}{\kilogram} of carbon per pulse supplies about \SI{585}{\mega\watt} of sublimation capacity at two pulses per second. The former \SI{380}{\mega\watt} liner load came from scaling the bag's flash estimate by a cylindrical wall-area fraction. It is not an established upper bound. The water calculation in \autoref{sec:minimum_nozzle} supplies emitted energy, while the argon plume's absorbed liner load remains uncomputed. The conduction example below retains \SI{380}{\mega\watt} as an assumed absorbed load in its original cylindrical layout. The gap-radiation example separately assumes a \SI{1150}{\kelvin} liner. Neither is a solved water or argon thermal state. Heat removed by ablation must leave in the vapor rather than soak inward. Pyrolytic graphite conducts about \SI{1700}{\watt\per\meter\per\kelvin} along its sheets and about \SI{7}{} across them, so laying the sheets in the plane of the wall gives spreading a preference over conduction into the shell of more than two hundred to one.

\subsection*{Standing The Liner Off The Shell}
Spreading is not blocking, and the anisotropy above only buys the first. Seven watts per meter per kelvin across a centimeter of liner carries \SI{847}{\kilo\watt\per\square\meter} on a gradient of \SI{1210}{\kelvin}, and \SI{847}{\kilo\watt\per\square\meter} is what the assumed \SI{380}{\mega\watt} delivers to the \SI{449}{\square\meter} wall of a \SI{3}{\meter} bore \SI{23.8}{\meter} long. A liner bonded to the shell would hand it that whole load, twenty-eight times the \SI{13.4}{\mega\watt} the shell is sized for. The two hundred to one is a ratio between two paths rather than a claim that either one is shut.

Nothing requires the contact. The shell is there to take the induced current and the liner to take the flash, which are different jobs against different things. The liner therefore stands off on pads of insulating carbon foam, the carbon-bonded fiber kind near \SI{0.3}{\watt\per\meter\per\kelvin} rather than the graphitic foams that conduct like metal, and only radiation crosses the rest of the gap. Setting the shell back a few centimeters on a \SI{3}{\meter} bore changes what it shields by under two percent, so the gap is free on the electromagnetic side.

What crosses it is set by the aluminum rather than by the graphite. For this separate example, take an area-averaged liner temperature of \SI{1150}{\kelvin}, which is where \SI{98}{\kilo\watt\per\square\meter} balances $\sigma T^4$, and a blackbody there emits \SI{99}{\kilo\watt\per\square\meter}. Two facing surfaces exchange that divided by $1/\epsilon_1 + 1/\epsilon_2 - 1$, and with graphite at \num{0.85} against lightly oxidized aluminum at \num{0.1} the denominator is \num{10.2}. The leak is \SI{9.7}{\kilo\watt\per\square\meter}, or about \SI{4.4}{\mega\watt} over the reference bore. That is a third of what the shell already carries and well inside an argon loop that absorbs \SI{69}{\mega\watt} if it boils. The shell's own temperature barely enters, since it appears as a fourth power against a liner at \SI{1150}{\kelvin}. Its emissivity is the number to hold. Let the gap face darken to \num{0.3} and the leak reaches about \SI{13}{\mega\watt}, which nearly doubles the shell's load, so that face wants to stay bright.

The standoff earns its keep a second time on expansion. Aluminum grows at \SI{23.6e-6}{\per\kelvin} and pyrolytic graphite along its sheets at nearly zero, so across the range the shell works through, the two differ by centimeters along a \SI{23.8}{\meter} bore. No bond survives that, and tiles absorb it in their joints instead. The joints are lapped so no straight line runs from the bore to the aluminum, which keeps the unsteered neutrals of \autoref{sec:needle_through_fog} out of the gap.

\subsection*{Why The Carrier Is Argon And The Seed Stays A Trace}
A carrier gas interferes with the seed in three ways, and a good one does none of them. A permanent dipole scatters slow electrons hard, which is water's problem. An electron affinity binds free electrons into negative ions, which is oxygen's. And chemical bonds absorb collision energy that the plume never gets back, which is everyone's but a noble gas's. \autoref{tab:carrier_sigma} runs the seeded conductivity for three carriers, normalized to the water case so that the uncertain input cancels. That input is the electron-neutral momentum-transfer cross section, and the table is evaluated across a threefold spread in it because the ordering matters more than the value.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Seeded conductivity relative to water at the same density and temperature, at \SI{0.32}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter}. Electron density is a Saha solve on potassium's \SI{4.34}{\electronvolt} ionization potential, and the collision rate sums electron-neutral scattering against the carrier and against the alkali itself with Coulomb scattering off the ions. Ranges span cross sections from \SIrange{2e-21}{1e-20}{\square\meter} for argon, \SIrange{3e-19}{1e-18}{} for water and \SIrange{1e-18}{4e-18}{} for potassium. The ordering holds across the whole span, and argon's lead widens as the plume cools, which is where the \SI{3800}{\kelvin} leak floor of \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} binds.}
    \label{tab:carrier_sigma}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Temperature} & \textbf{Argon + 1\% K} & \textbf{Pure \ce{K2CO3}} & \textbf{Water + 1\% K} \\\hline
        \SI{2500}{\kelvin} & \numrange{29}{38} & \numrange{6.7}{7.3} & \num{1.0} \\\hline
        \SI{3000}{\kelvin} & \numrange{14}{25} & \numrange{5.8}{6.5} & \num{1.0} \\\hline
        \SI{4000}{\kelvin} & \numrange{4.0}{9.6} & \numrange{3.1}{5.0} & \num{1.0} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

The middle column answers a tempting simplification, which is to make the slug out of the seed. It beats water and loses to argon, and the reason is the square-root law \autoref{sec:watering_it_down} already states. Saha gives $n_e \propto \sqrt{n_K}$ while the collision rate rises as $n_K$, so densifying the alkali buys electrons at the square root and pays for mean free path at the first power. Potassium is a poor host for its own electrons because it is enormous. Its polarizability is \SI{43.4}{\cubic\angstrom} against argon's \SI{1.64}{\cubic\angstrom} \cite{crc_handbook}, and polarizability is what sets low-energy electron scattering, so a potassium atom scatters roughly a hundred times harder than an argon atom.

That fixes the seed fraction rather than leaving it free. Writing $f$ for the alkali's number fraction and $r$ for the ratio of the two cross sections, $\sigma \propto \sqrt{f}/(1 + (r-1)f)$, which is maximized at $f = 1/(r-1)$. That is the point where the alkali's own scattering equals the carrier's. For $r$ between 50 and 200 the optimum runs from \SI{2.0}{\percent} to \SI{0.5}{\percent} by number, and \SI{1}{\percent} potassium by mass in argon is \SI{1}{\percent} by number, so the seed this paper already carries sits on the optimum rather than near it. Over-seeding is a real cost. A plug made of pure carbonate would put \SI{7.9}{\percent} potassium by number into the blob and return \SIrange{48}{81}{\percent} of the conductivity available at the optimum, so the plug stays a diluent with the seed dissolved in it.

Two other carriers deserve a line each, because both are better than argon on one axis and disqualified on another. Krypton is thermally the right answer. It boils at \SI{119.8}{\kelvin} and is critical at \SI{209.4}{\kelvin} \cite{crc_handbook}, so it sits as a liquid at Jupiter's \SI{122}{\kelvin} with no refrigeration at all, which is argon's one remaining problem solved outright. Both gases come out of the same air-separation plant, and dry air is \num{9340} parts per million argon against \num{1.14} parts per million krypton \cite{crc_handbook}, so the same throughput yields eight thousand times less of it. Oxygen is the answer the lunar mining of \autoref{sec:lunar_mining} would prefer, and it has the lowest bond energy of anything here at \SI{15.6}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram}. It is refused on electron affinity rather than on anything thermal. Atomic oxygen binds an electron at \SI{1.46}{\electronvolt} \cite{crc_handbook}, and a plume that turns its free electrons into \ce{O-} does not conduct.


\autoref{tab:carrier_sigma} compares pure carriers. What a flown blob gets is a different number, because the collision rate is a sum over everything in the fireball and the fireball is never one substance. \autoref{tab:argon_scatter} runs that sum for the configurations the design can actually choose between. Two features of it are worth reading off. Water dominates far out of proportion to its mass, so an argon slug flying alongside an ice projectile and plug returns about an eighth of what argon can give. And the floor is not zero, because the seed and the ship's own consumables are in the sum too. At the bottom row the potassium seed is \SI{34}{\percent} of the collision rate, the argon \SI{33}{\percent}, the polyethylene bag \SI{18}{\percent} and the ablating graphite liner \SI{14}{\percent}. The projectile's electronics are under a thousandth of it, since \SI{50}{\gram} of silicon, oxide and metal against a \SI{275}{\kilogram} blob is too little to matter at any plausible cross section. None of the four large terms carries a permanent dipole, which is why together they cost a factor of one and a half rather than the factor of eight the ice costs.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Plume conductivity for the whole blob, relative to the best the design can reach, which is an all-argon blob carrying the seed it must carry plus the bag and liner it destroys each pulse. Computed as $1/\sum_i n_i \sigma_i$ at \SI{3000}{\kelvin}, where the water is recombined and scatters as a dipolar molecule. Cross sections are scaled from polarizability against argon's \SI{1.64}{\cubic\angstrom} \cite{crc_handbook}, except water, whose permanent dipole puts it near fifty times argon and which is the one species that does not follow that scaling. Reading the ratio a factor of two higher, which the Ramsauer minimum in argon's cross section near the plume's mean electron energy would justify, spreads the rows further apart without reordering them.}
    \label{tab:argon_scatter}
    \begin{tabular}{|l|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Configuration} & \textbf{Conductivity} \\\hline
        All-water slug & \num{0.027} \\\hline
        Argon slug, ice projectile and ice plug & \num{0.115} \\\hline
        Argon slug, argon plug, ice projectile & \num{0.272} \\\hline
        All argon, seed dissolved in a saturated brine pellet & \num{0.625} \\\hline
        All argon, dry carbonate powder seed & \num{1.000} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection*{What Carries The Seed}
Argon dissolves nothing, so the seed cannot ride in the slug the way it rides in water droplets. It does not have to be dissolved to ride. A slurry of fine carbonate powder in liquid argon sprays and flashes like the pure liquid, and the \SI{78}{\percent} of the slug that stays condensed carries the powder the way the droplets carried the solution. That is the baseline, and it is the bottom row of \autoref{tab:argon_scatter}, because it puts no water in the blob at all. If a dry seed proves impractical, the fallback is to dissolve it in the projectile and plug, and what those are then made of is a live question. \autoref{tab:seed_carrier} compares the three candidates.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{Carriers for the seed, at \SI{57.6}{\kilogram} of diluent plus \SI{4.87}{\kilogram} of \ce{K2CO3} in a \SI{275.5}{\kilogram} blob. Atomization is every bond broken; the parenthetical for \ce{CO2} is what stays broken if carbon monoxide re-forms, which its \SI{11.1}{\electronvolt} bond makes likely. The jet column is \autoref{eq:eta_chem} at \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} with the blob-averaged bond energy, against \num{0.731} for the all-water slug. The storage floor is the temperature at which a bare \SI{25}{\kilogram} rod loses under \SI{1}{\percent} in three years by \autoref{eq:hertz_knudsen}. The last column is negative ions per free electron at \SI{3000}{\kelvin} from the affinity of whatever the diluent leaves loose, and it is an estimate rather than a chemistry solve.}
    \label{tab:seed_carrier}
    \begin{tabular}{|l|r|r|r|}\hline
         & \textbf{Water ice} & \textbf{Dry ice} & \textbf{Polyethylene} \\\hline
        Atomization & \SI{50.9}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} & \SI{36.6}{} (\num{12.1}) & \SI{84.0}{} \\\hline
        Particles per kilogram & \SI{166.5}{\mole} & \SI{68.2}{\mole} & \SI{213.9}{\mole} \\\hline
        $\eta_{\mathrm{chem}}$ for the blob & \num{0.948} & \numrange{0.963}{0.987} & \num{0.914} \\\hline
        Bare storage floor & \SI{147}{\kelvin} & \SI{74}{\kelvin} & none \\\hline
        Density & \SI{917}{\kilogram\per\cubic\meter} & \SI{1560}{} & \SI{970}{} \\\hline
        Negative ions per electron & \num{0.36} & \num{0.15} & \num{0.23} \\\hline
        Loading the seed & dissolves & will not & mineral filler \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Polyethylene is the interesting entry, because the reason \autoref{sec:needle_through_fog} refuses it stops applying. That reason is that its carbon scavenges the slug's oxygen into carbon monoxide and breaks water's recombination loan. An argon slug has no oxygen to steal, and the only oxygen in the blob is the seed's own, which was a mild liability rather than an asset. The carbon eating it is a gain. Polyethylene also has no storage floor at all, since it holds no vapor pressure below its \SI{423}{\kelvin} melting point, so a seeded rod needs no sunshade, no blanket and no enclosure anywhere in the cycle. Water ice needs shading inside \SI{147}{\kelvin} and dry ice inside \SI{74}{\kelvin}. Loading is easier as well, because \SI{8}{\percent} mineral filler in polyethylene is ordinary compounding, while potash cannot be put into dry ice at any concentration. Its density is within \SI{6}{\percent} of ice, so the matched-density penetration argument of \autoref{sec:needle_through_fog} carries over unchanged if the plug is made of the same stuff. All of this is the fallback rather than the design. The baseline makes the plug frozen slug argon, since it is deployed from the ship rather than flown in, and the projectile shielded argon, which removes the water entirely. Taking only the plug and leaving an ice projectile is the halfway house, worth $\eta_{\mathrm{chem}} = \num{0.981}$ and \num{0.272} on \autoref{tab:argon_scatter}, at the cost of turning matched-density penetration into ice against argon at $\sqrt{\rho_p/\rho_t} = 0.82$.

What it costs is the bond energy. At \SI{84.0}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} polyethylene is the worst of the three per kilogram, though the blob only feels a third of that because the diluent is under a quarter of it, and \num{0.914} against water's \num{0.948} is a \SI{3.6}{\percent} difference in a term that the all-water slug ran at \num{0.731}. One chemistry item is unresolved, and it belongs with the radiation-hydrodynamic calculation already owed. Polyethylene trades one electron sink for another. It cuts the blob's oxygen from \SI{19}{\percent} to \SI{0.6}{\percent}, taking the hydroxide sink and \ce{O-} attachment with it, and puts \SI{53.6}{\kilogram} of carbon in their place at an affinity of \SI{1.26}{\electronvolt} \cite{crc_handbook}. The last row of \autoref{tab:seed_carrier} makes that a net gain, \num{0.23} negative ions per free electron against ice's \num{0.37}. A condensing fraction of the carbon would erode it.

\subsection*{The Split Slug}
If the heat will not allow an all-argon slug, the slug can be part water. \autoref{tab:argon_water_split} prices that compromise on both of the axes argon is chosen for, and they do not behave alike. The bond energy is a mass average, because every kilogram of water in the fireball pays its own \SI{50.9}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} and every kilogram of argon pays nothing. The electron collision rate is a sum instead, $\nu = \sum_i n_i \sigma_i v$, so the species that scatters hardest sets it no matter how little of it there is. Water loses twice over in that sum. It scatters roughly fifty times harder than argon per molecule because it has a permanent dipole, and once dissociated it supplies \num{6.7} times as many particles per kilogram because it is lighter. The product is why the crossing point sits at \SI{0.3}{\percent} of the slug rather than somewhere a designer would choose.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{A slug split between water and argon, with the \SI{62.5}{\kilogram} of ice projectile and plug and the \SI{4.87}{\kilogram} of seed held fixed. $\eta_{\mathrm{chem}}$ is \autoref{eq:eta_chem} at \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} on the blob-averaged bond energy. The collision-rate column is $\sum_i n_i \sigma_i$ normalized to the all-argon slug, taking water's electron-neutral cross section as fifty times argon's. It is the reciprocal of a conductivity, so larger is worse.}
    \label{tab:argon_water_split}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|r|r|}\hline
        \textbf{Water share of the slug} & \textbf{Bond energy of the blob} & \textbf{$\eta_{\mathrm{chem}}$} & \textbf{Collision rate} \\\hline
        \SI{0}{\percent} & \SI{11.0}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} & \num{0.948} & \num{1.0} \\\hline
        \SI{25}{\percent} & \SI{20.8}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} & \num{0.900} & \num{84} \\\hline
        \SI{50}{\percent} & \SI{30.7}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} & \num{0.848} & \num{167} \\\hline
        \SI{75}{\percent} & \SI{40.5}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} & \num{0.793} & \num{250} \\\hline
        \SI{100}{\percent} & \SI{50.4}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} & \num{0.735} & \num{333} \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

Read as a design rule, the table says to spend the heat budget on argon and take the chemistry as the return, rather than expecting the conductivity to come with it. A half-and-half slug recovers \num{0.848} against water's \num{0.735}, which is about half the distance to the all-argon \num{0.948} on the axis \autoref{sec:jet_efficiency} charges, as a mass average has to be. On the other axis it recovers a factor of two out of a factor of three hundred. The one arrangement that might recover more is geometric rather than compositional, since the field couples through a current sheet at the plume's surface, and a sprayed argon sheath around a water core puts the conducting species there. That depends entirely on how fast the fireball mixes, which this paper does not compute.

\subsection*{The Chemistry Toll Against Closing Speed}
\autoref{eq:eta_chem} charges water a fixed \SI{50.9}{\mega\joule\per\kilogram} of bond energy against a collision budget that grows as the square of the closing speed. The toll is therefore a function of speed alone at fixed $k$, and \autoref{tab:eta_chem_speed} evaluates it. Argon's column is \num{1.000} at every row, which is the whole argument for it and also the whole limit on where that argument applies.

\begin{table}[!htpb]
    \centering
    \caption{$\eta_{\mathrm{chem}}$ from \autoref{eq:eta_chem} at $k = 8.5$, the fraction of the ideal one-axis exhaust speed that survives paying water's bond energy. The first three rows are the growth push of \autoref{sec:jupiter_only_growth}. The last two bracket a head-on pulse at the \SIrange{200}{309}{\kilo\meter\per\second} periapsis speeds of \autoref{sec:jupiter_gravity_initial}, where the prograde and retrograde payloads close at twice their own speed.}
    \label{tab:eta_chem_speed}
    \begin{tabular}{|r|r|l|}\hline
        \textbf{Closing speed} & \textbf{$\eta_{\mathrm{chem}}$} & \textbf{Where} \\\hline
        \SI{45.58}{\kilo\meter\per\second} & \num{0.731} & cold end of the growth push \\\hline
        \SI{56.53}{\kilo\meter\per\second} & \num{0.835} & start of the growth push \\\hline
        \SI{75}{\kilo\meter\per\second} & \num{0.910} & hottest pulse the fleet flies \\\hline
        \SI{400}{\kilo\meter\per\second} & \num{0.997} & periapsis pulse, \SI{200}{\kilo\meter\per\second} orbit \\\hline
        \SI{618}{\kilo\meter\per\second} & \num{0.999} & periapsis pulse, 4 solar radii \\\hline
    \end{tabular}
\end{table}

 This paper's ideas are my own. While I used AI tools to create some artwork and suggest writing improvements, any potential AI regurgitation of others' work is unintentional. Please inform me if you find any uncited material. In addition to ChatGPT \cite{chatgpt} and Grok \cite{grok}, I used Claude Code \cite{claude_code} to assist with editing this paper and with code in the companion calculations repository.

As a non-expert, I may have also overlooked relevant prior work or failed to choose the most appropriate citations. I previously worked at Netflix, which produces content related to some popular culture titles referenced in the paper. However, I am no longer employed there and am not a major investor. My blog is not monetized and exists primarily to explain this paper's ideas. I do not believe I have any current financial conflicts of interest.
